# Thesis gate — How we can know our own minds, given how much interpretation appears to be doing. The empirical literature on confabulation, motivated reasoning and implicit attitudes (Nisbett and Wilson; Schwitzgebel) undermines a simple observational or inner-perception model of introspection. But the interpretive alternative — that we read ourselves off behavioural and contextual evidence much as we read others (Carruthers, Gopnik) — struggles to explain first-person authority, the asymmetry between self-ascription and other-ascription that Moran's agentialist account and Bar-On's neo-expressivism were built to capture. At issue is whether first-person authority survives the interpretive turn, and if so what grounds it.

59 candidates. **2 in the top-right quadrant**, 5 repairable, 31 not attacked.

Ranked by promise, then by unanswered damage. Nothing has been eliminated — the verdict is a field, not a filter.

**Promise** is scored blind, before any attack. **Residual** is the strongest objection left unanswered — 0 is best, and it is the ranking number. **Worst** is the strongest objection mounted at all: when Worst is also low, the thesis was not seriously tested rather than sturdy.

## Look here first

*Promise 4+ with nothing above a 2 left unanswered. Ambitious and hard to break. Expect a short list, and an empty one on some runs.*

### P1. Accepting that we interpret ourselves commits you to rejecting the concept-possession link for phenomenal concepts: if I must interpret whether I'm in pain the way I interpret whether you are, I cannot possess the concept PAIN through direct phenomenal acquaintance, making phenomenal concept strategy responses to the knowledge argument fail.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 4/5 · survives
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Physicalist interpretivists like Carruthers who need phenomenal concept strategy but endorse thoroughgoing interpretivism about all mental states
- **Sketch:** The phenomenal concept strategy (Loar, Papineau) solves the knowledge argument by claiming that phenomenal concepts are uniquely grounded in direct acquaintance with phenomenal qualities—Mary learns something new because she deploys a new recognitional concept available only from the first-person experiential perspective. But if Carruthers or Schwitzgebel are right that self-knowledge of phenomenal states is interpretive—we infer we're in pain from behavior, context, functional role, much as we attribute pain to others—then phenomenal concepts can't be grounded in privileged acquaintance. They must be grounded the same way our concepts of others' phenomenal states are: in third-personally accessible features. This means either (a) rejecting the phenomenal concept strategy, leaving physicalists without their best reply to Jackson, or (b) exempting phenomenal states from the interpretive model, thereby limiting the scope of interpretivism in a way that restores partial first-person privilege.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Connects interpretivism about self-knowledge to phenomenal concept strategy's failure—genuine cross-debate implications.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. The defence showed the commitment reveals strategic instability in actual theorists' positions, not mere conceptual incompatibility
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. The defence demonstrated that interpretivism worthy of the name requires the strong reading, collapsing the weak/strong distinction
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, answered. The defence showed boundary-drawing follows functional analysis of theoretical work, not arbitrary stipulation, and specified what would falsify
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. The defence distinguished inter-camp debate from intra-camp tension, showing the thesis identifies unstable positions not mapped in cited literature

<details><summary>7 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) First-person authority survives the interpretive turn only for currently attended mental states; Schwitzgebel-style interpretivism is correct for everything else, and this split explains why the debate has seemed intractable.
- (3/5) The interpretation model and first-person authority are both correct because they apply to different temporal phases: we lack privileged access to occurrent mental states (hence confabulation), but we do have authority over sustained dispositional attitudes because these are stabilized by our own ongoing self-interpretation.
- (2/5) Interpretive self-knowledge and transparent self-knowledge target different determinables: interpretation reveals what attitudes you have, transparency reveals their force or direction of fit.
- (2/5) The confabulation literature undermines not the observational model but the assumption that self-knowledge is a unified phenomenon—we have observational access to some states and only interpretive access to others, with no general theory of 'introspection' possible.
- (3/5) The confabulation literature actually provides a counterexample to a premise both sides accept: that there's a unified phenomenon called 'self-knowledge' or 'introspection' — instead, 'Why did I do that?' involves interpretation while 'What do I think about abortion?' involves something else entirely, and conflating them has made the debate unsolvable.
- (3/5) The confabulation literature undermines not first-person authority but first-person exhaustiveness: we have genuine non-interpretive access to a narrow range of conscious states, while confabulating about the causes, connections, and unconscious attitudes that interpretivists wrongly treat as the primary data.
- (3/5) First-person authority persists because confabulation studies misidentify their own results: subjects aren't confabulating about pre-existing mental states they fail to access, but rather exhibiting a distinctive kind of mental state—narrative self-model attitudes—that are precisely what they seem to be and to which subjects have genuine introspective access.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims that interpretivism about self-knowledge entails rejection of the concept-possession link central to phenomenal concept strategies. But this is merely unpacking what "interpretivism" means when applied to phenomenal states. If interpretivism is the view that we know our mental states through interpretation rather than direct acquaintance, and phenomenal concept strategies require concept-possession through direct acquaintance, then of course they're incompatible—this follows analytically from the definitions. The thesis presents as a substantive discovery what is actually a terminological consequence: "interpretivism applied to phenomenal states excludes direct acquaintance-based concept possession" is true by stipulation of what these positions mean. The weak point is the implicit claim that this constitutes a dialectically significant commitment rather than an obvious conceptual truth. Anyone endorsing interpretivism about phenomenal states already knows they're rejecting direct acquaintance; anyone deploying phenomenal concept strategies already knows they're rejecting interpretivism about qualia. The thesis simply restates this mutual exclusion in commitment-revealing language, like saying "accepting that bachelors are unmarried commits you to rejecting that bachelors have spouses." No one holding both views was unaware of the tension; the "commitment" reveals nothing not already transparent in the position descriptions.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in that the incompatibility between thoroughgoing interpretivism and acquaintance-based phenomenal concepts is fairly obvious once stated. However, it doesn't fully succeed because: (1) the thesis identifies a *specific target* (physicalist interpretivists like Carruthers) who may not have explicitly recognized this tension in their own position, and (2) the dialectical work isn't just noting incompatibility but forcing a choice that undermines a common physicalist strategy. The commitment may be "obvious in retrospect" but still represent genuine theoretical pressure if the target figures haven't addressed it.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes this commitment dialectically significant. The thesis doesn't merely point out that "interpretivism excludes acquaintance" by definition—it identifies a *strategic bind* that specific theorists face in their actual dialectical position.

Consider Carruthers' situation concretely. He endorses interpretivism about phenomenal self-knowledge to solve various problems (explaining self-ignorance, unifying self- and other-attribution). Separately, as a physicalist, he needs *some* response to the knowledge argument, and the phenomenal concept strategy is the leading physicalist option. The thesis reveals that these two commitments—each independently motivated, each adopted for separate theoretical reasons—cannot coexist.

This isn't trivial for three reasons:

First, the tension is *non-obvious in practice* because the commitments arise in different literatures addressing different problems. Interpretivism emerges from debates about introspection, self-knowledge, and cognitive architecture. Phenomenal concept strategy emerges from debates about Mary, zombies, and the explanatory gap. A theorist might adopt interpretivism for its explanatory power regarding introspective error while separately relying on phenomenal concepts to block Jackson's argument, without recognizing these tools undermine each other.

Second, the commitment forces a *revealing choice*: either abandon your best anti-dualist weapon (phenomenal concept strategy) or exempt phenomenal states from interpretivism (thereby conceding first-person privilege for the very states where physicalists most need to avoid it). This is like revealing that someone's independently-motivated premises entail an unwanted conclusion—not trivial even if the entailment is deductively simple.

Third, "bachelor/spouse" cases are trivial because no one *actually holds* both. But physicalist interpretivists like Carruthers *do* occupy both positions simultaneously in their published work—Carruthers defends interpretivism extensively while remaining a physicalist who needs responses to anti-physicalist arguments. The thesis exposes genuine theoretical instability, not merely conceptual incompatibility.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. While the incompatibility is indeed straightforward once explicitly stated, this doesn't make it trivial. The dialectical significance lies in catching specific theorists in a bind created by independently-motivated commitments adopted for separate reasons. The objection conflates "simple to see once revealed" with "trivial"—but forcing theorists to choose between two tools they need for different purposes is precisely what commitment-revealing moves do. The fact that the entailment is analytic doesn't drain it of dialectical force when the theorists in question haven't recognized or addressed it.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "interpretive" between a weak reading (self-knowledge involves cognitive processing, inference, or construction rather than being purely receptive) and a strong reading (self-knowledge has no privileged access whatsoever and operates identically to other-attribution). The argument needs both readings to work.

The thesis is *plausible* on the weak reading: if knowing I'm in pain involves any interpretive element—integrating multiple information sources, applying concepts, making judgments—this might seem incompatible with the "direct acquaintance" picture where phenomenal concepts are simply triggered by raw feels. Carruthers and Schwitzgebel do establish that self-knowledge involves interpretation in this weak sense.

But the thesis is only *interesting* on the strong reading: it only threatens phenomenal concept strategies if interpretivism means we have literally *no* privileged phenomenal access at all, that self-attribution works *exactly* like other-attribution. Otherwise, phenomenal concept theorists can simply accommodate weak interpretivism—yes, there's cognitive processing involved in self-knowledge, but it operates on proprietary first-person phenomenal data unavailable to third-person attributors, preserving the asymmetry needed for phenomenal concepts.

The weak point is the slide between these readings in the phrase "interpret whether I'm in pain the way I interpret whether you are." On the weak reading, this means both involve interpretation (true but compatible with phenomenal concepts). On the strong reading, it means both involve *identical* interpretive processes with *identical* evidential access (interesting but not established by Carruthers/Schwitzgebel, who show self-knowledge is interpretive without proving it's *identical* to other-knowledge).

STRENGTH: 4/5

This equivocation does serious damage. The thesis cannot survive without showing that interpretivism entails the *strong* reading, but the cited theorists only establish the *weak* reading.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection's distinction collapses under examination. There is no stable "weak interpretivism" that preserves phenomenal concept strategies while accommodating Carruthers-style interpretivism.

The objector claims phenomenal concept theorists can accept that "there's cognitive processing" operating on "proprietary first-person phenomenal data." But this misunderstands what interpretivism *is*. Interpretivism isn't merely the claim that self-knowledge involves "processing"—even pure acquaintance theorists can grant that concepts must be applied, judgments formed. What makes interpretivism *interpretivism* is the specific claim about *what gets processed*.

For Carruthers and Schwitzgebel, the interpretive process operates on the *same kinds of evidence* available for other-attribution: behavioral dispositions, functional roles, contextual cues, bodily states. Carruthers explicitly argues we self-attribute pain by monitoring our pain-behavior tendencies and pain-typical cognitive effects. Schwitzgebel argues we infer our experiences from the same outward-facing evidence others use. The "interpretation" isn't neutral cognitive processing—it's *inference from third-personally available data*.

This directly excludes "proprietary first-person phenomenal data" as an epistemic input. If there were such proprietary data serving as evidence, it wouldn't be *interpretivism*—it would be acquaintance-plus-processing. The interpretive model's explanatory power (accounting for introspective error, explaining parity with other-attribution) comes precisely from *denying* that we have special phenomenal evidence unavailable to external observers.

The objector's "weak reading" thus isn't interpretivism at all—it's standard acquaintance theory with the trivial addition that applying concepts counts as "interpretation." No interpretivist would recognize this as their view.

Consider the dialectical work: Carruthers uses interpretivism to explain how we can be *wrong* about our phenomenal states, how introspection can *fail*. If we had proprietary phenomenal access, these errors would be mysterious—how do you misinterpret what's directly given? The interpretivist explanation works precisely because the evidence is *indirect*, requiring inference that can go wrong. This is incompatible with phenomenal concepts grounded in direct acquaintance with phenomenal character.

The strong reading isn't an interpretation—it's what interpretivism *means*. The thesis stands.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds. The objection's "weak/strong" distinction tries to carve out space that doesn't exist. Interpretivism worthy of the name requires that self-knowledge operates on third-personally accessible evidence, not on "proprietary phenomenal data." The moment you grant proprietary phenomenal access, you've abandoned interpretivism for a hybrid view that inherits all the problems (explaining introspective error, explaining self-ignorance) that motivated interpretivism in the first place.

The objector is right that the thesis needs the "strong" reading to threaten phenomenal concepts. But wrong that this reading isn't established by Carruthers/Schwitzgebel—their entire explanatory project *requires* denying privileged phenomenal access. A "weak interpretivism" that preserves such access would be interpretivism in name only, unable to do the theoretical work these authors need it to do.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through strategic redefinition of what counts as "genuine interpretivism" or "genuine phenomenal concept strategy." 

Consider potential counterexamples: A theorist might claim to be both an interpretivist AND to accept phenomenal concept strategies by proposing a hybrid view—perhaps phenomenal concepts are grounded in acquaintance but *accessed* through interpretive processes, or interpretivism applies to *knowing that* one is in pain while acquaintance grounds concept-possession itself. 

The thesis can dismiss any such hybrid as either "not really interpretivism" (if it preserves any privileged phenomenal access) or "not really phenomenal concept strategy" (if it abandons direct acquaintance). We see this move already in the defence against EQUIVOCATION: any interpretivism that allows "proprietary phenomenal data" gets reclassified as "acquaintance-plus-processing" and ruled "not interpretivism at all."

The weak point is this definitional flexibility: the thesis can redraw boundaries around "interpretivism" and "phenomenal concept strategy" to exclude any apparent reconciliation as a bastardized version of one or both positions. A theorist like Carruthers who claims to hold both gets ruled out not by showing internal incoherence in his *actual* position, but by stipulating that the terms, properly understood, cannot both apply. Any case of someone seemingly holding both views gets reinterpreted as confusion about what the views really require.

This makes the commitment claim unfalsifiable—not because it's necessarily false, but because the definitional apparatus ensures no actual theoretical position can serve as a counterexample.
**STRENGTH: 3/5**
This attack has genuine force but isn't devastating. The thesis does employ definitional policing that could insulate it from cases, particularly in how it treats "interpretivism." However, the defence has some legitimate grounds for its stipulations—Carruthers and Schwitzgebel do make specific claims about evidence-bases that genuinely exclude certain hybrid positions. The attack succeeds in showing the thesis has some protection through redefinition, but doesn't fully establish that *any* counterexample would be dismissed rather than genuinely failing to instantiate the relevant positions.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses principled boundary-drawing with unfalsifiable gerrymandering. The thesis doesn't immunize itself through arbitrary redefinition—it identifies what features make interpretivism and phenomenal concept strategy *do the theoretical work* they're designed to do, then shows these features conflict.

Consider what would count as a *genuine* counterexample: a theorist who holds both positions in their substantive, work-doing forms. The thesis allows for this test: Does the theorist's "interpretivism" explain introspective error and self-ignorance through evidential indirectness? Does their "phenomenal concept strategy" block the knowledge argument through concepts grounded in phenomenal acquaintance? If yes to both, we have a counterexample. If no to either, we don't have the relevant positions.

This isn't unfalsifiable gerrymandering—it's demanding that counterexamples involve the *actual views* under discussion, not hollowed-out labels. Compare: "Accepting libertarian free will commits you to rejecting causal closure" isn't unfalsifiable just because it dismisses "compatibilist free will" as not the relevant kind. The thesis identifies specific theoretical machinery, not arbitrary stipulations.

The objector's proposed hybrids fail not by stipulation but by *functional analysis*:

**Hybrid 1**: "Phenomenal concepts grounded in acquaintance but accessed through interpretation." This fails because if the grounding is in acquaintance, the concept-possession link to phenomenal character is preserved—exactly what threatens physicalism in the knowledge argument. Adding interpretive *access* doesn't help the physicalist, because Mary's problem is that she couldn't *possess* phenomenal concepts before experiencing red, not that she couldn't access already-possessed concepts. The hybrid preserves the anti-physicalist threat.

**Hybrid 2**: "Interpretivism about knowing-that, acquaintance grounds concepts." This fails because phenomenal concept strategies need acquaintance to ground *epistemic* access to phenomenal properties, not just concept-possession. If Mary knows-that she's seeing red only through interpretation of third-person evidence, she doesn't gain the distinctive first-person epistemic access that explains why she learns something new. The hybrid abandons what phenomenal concept strategy needs.

These aren't arbitrary dismissals—they're demonstrations that the hybrids don't do the work. A position is falsifiable when we can specify what would refute it. Here: show me interpretivism that explains introspective error without evidential indirectness, or phenomenal concepts that block Jackson without acquaintance-based epistemic access. That's not unfalsifiable—it's demanding theoretical coherence.

The Carruthers case proves this isn't gerrymandering: he's a real theorist with real commitments, and the thesis claims he faces a real choice. If he published explicit reconciliation of these commitments that preserved both their theoretical functions, that would falsify the thesis. He hasn't. The thesis predicts he can't. That's falsifiable.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds. The objection mistakes substantive theoretical analysis for definitional immunization. The thesis does draw boundaries around "interpretivism" and "phenomenal concept strategy," but does so by identifying their essential theoretical functions—the work they do in their home debates. This is legitimate philosophical method, not unfalsifiable gerrymandering.

The proposed hybrids fail not because they're ruled out by stipulation, but because they demonstrably don't preserve both positions' theoretical utility. A view that allows phenomenal concept strategy to work (by grounding concepts in acquaintance) thereby loses interpretivism's explanatory power (which requires evidential indirectness). A view that preserves interpretivism's explanatory power (through evidential indirectness) thereby loses phenomenal concept strategy's anti-dualist force (which requires acquaintance-based epistemic access).

The thesis remains falsifiable: produce a theorist whose interpretivism genuinely explains introspective error through evidential indirectness AND whose phenomenal concept strategy genuinely blocks the knowledge argument through acquaintance-based concepts. The thesis predicts this cannot be done. That's a substantive, falsifiable claim—not an unfalsifiable definitional fortress.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis presents itself as revealing a novel tension, but this exact objection to phenomenal concept strategies has been extensively developed in the literature on introspective access. Ned Block's work on phenomenal overflow and the limits of introspective access (2007, 2011) argues that if we lack direct introspective access to all phenomenal properties, phenomenal concept strategies face serious problems. Eric Schwitzgebel's "The Unreliability of Naive Introspection" (2008) explicitly argues against the privileged access assumption underlying phenomenal concept strategies. More directly, Amy Kind's "How to Believe in Qualia" (2008) and David Papineau's own later work acknowledge that phenomenal concept strategies require a strong introspective access assumption that interpretivists reject. The "interpretivism undermines phenomenal concept strategy" move appears in Keith Frankish's illusionist work, where he argues that rejecting introspective transparency (closely related to interpretivism) removes the motivation for phenomenal realism that drives phenomenal concept strategies. The weak point is the implicit novelty claim: this isn't a newly-revealed commitment but a well-mapped battleground where the tension between interpretivism and acquaintance-based approaches has been explicitly debated for over a decade. The thesis restates known incompatibilities rather than discovering new dialectical pressure.
**STRENGTH: 2/5**
The attack has limited force. While there is indeed literature discussing tensions between introspective access assumptions and phenomenal concept strategies, the thesis makes a more *specific* claim: that physicalist interpretivists like Carruthers who *need* phenomenal concept strategy for anti-dualist purposes face a particular bind. The existing literature mostly features these as competing camps (introspective skeptics vs. phenomenal concept defenders), not as commitments held *simultaneously* by single theorists who need both tools. The thesis's contribution is identifying this as an *internal* tension for certain physicalists, not just a known inter-camp disagreement.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies what the thesis contributes. The existing literature it cites represents *inter-camp* debate—introspective skeptics attacking phenomenal concept strategies, phenomenal concept defenders requiring strong access assumptions. The thesis reveals an *intra-camp* problem: physicalists who need both tools simultaneously.

Consider the dialectical landscape more carefully:

**Block, Schwitzgebel, Kind** present introspective skepticism as *undermining* phenomenal concept strategies—they're opponents of that approach. They argue: "If you accept our view of introspection, you must reject phenomenal concepts." This is external critique, not internal tension.

**Frankish's illusionism** explicitly *abandons* phenomenal realism entirely—he's not trying to save physicalism while preserving genuine phenomenal properties. His position is coherent precisely because he bites the bullet: no real qualia, no need for phenomenal concept strategies.

The thesis targets a different position: physicalists like **Carruthers** who are:
1. **Phenomenal realists** (unlike Frankish)—they grant genuine phenomenal properties exist
2. **Interpretivists** about self-knowledge—they adopt Schwitzgebel-style skepticism about introspective access
3. **Anti-dualists** who need responses to the knowledge argument

This creates a *specific bind* not addressed in the cited literature: How do you defend physicalism about genuine phenomenal properties against the knowledge argument when you've rejected the privileged access that phenomenal concept strategies require?

The existing literature offers three positions:
- **Phenomenal concept defenders** (Loar, Papineau early): Accept privileged access, reject interpretivism
- **Introspective skeptics** (Schwitzgebel, Block): Reject privileged access, attack phenomenal concept strategies from outside
- **Illusionists** (Frankish): Reject phenomenal realism entirely, don't need phenomenal concept strategies

The thesis identifies a *fourth* position that appears in actual theorists but hasn't been adequately mapped: **Physicalist interpretivist realists** who accept phenomenal properties are real, adopt interpretivism, but still need to answer Jackson. This position faces unique pressure because it cannot adopt any of the three stable solutions above.

Carruthers exemplifies this unstable position: his 2011 *The Opacity of Mind* defends thoroughgoing interpretivism while his broader physicalist commitments require responses to anti-physicalist arguments. He doesn't explicitly address how to answer the knowledge argument without phenomenal concept strategies, nor does he explain what alternative anti-dualist strategy interpretivists should use.

The thesis's contribution isn't discovering that interpretivism and phenomenal concepts conflict—that's known. It's identifying that specific physicalist interpretivists occupy an unstable position requiring both, and forcing them to choose: either exempt phenomenal states from interpretivism (contradicting their thoroughgoing interpretivism) or abandon phenomenal concept strategies (leaving them without their best anti-dualist weapon).

This is novel dialectical work. The existing literature documents the incompatibility. The thesis identifies theorists caught between both commitments and shows they must move.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds. The objection correctly notes that the incompatibility between interpretivism and phenomenal concept strategies is discussed in the literature, but fails to recognize what the thesis adds: identifying this as an *internal* problem for specific physicalist interpretivists rather than an *external* debate between camps.

The cited authors either attack phenomenal concept strategies from outside (Block, Schwitzgebel, Kind) or abandon phenomenal realism entirely (Frankish). None address the position of physicalists who need to preserve both phenomenal realism (against eliminativism) and physicalism (against dualism) while adopting interpretivism. This is the unstable position the thesis targets.

The thesis isn't "already taken" because the existing literature hasn't identified this as a problem *internal* to certain physicalists' theoretical commitments. Showing that two views conflict in the literature is different from showing that specific theorists need both views for their broader project. The former is conceptual mapping; the latter is dialectical pressure on actual positions.

Carruthers and similar theorists face a genuine choice the literature hasn't forced them to make explicitly: which commitment do you abandon? The thesis does novel work by identifying this bind and demanding a response from those caught in it.

</details>

---

### P2. Interpretation-based accounts cannot explain first-person authority because they miss that interpretation requires fixing a person across time, and self-interpretation is unstable: there is no fact about which interpretation of my past self is correct that doesn't depend on who my present self chooses to be continuous with.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 4/5 · survives
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Carruthers and Gopnik's interpretivist accounts that assimilate first-person and third-person cases
- **Sketch:** Carruthers and Gopnik say we interpret ourselves from behavioral evidence like we interpret others. But interpretation requires identifying the subject—deciding which past states belong to the person whose current state we're explaining. When interpreting others, the subject is fixed independently. When interpreting ourselves, we face a circularity: which of my past mental states I should count as evidence depends on which past self I identify with, but which past self I identify with depends on how I interpret those mental states. The key premise: self-interpretation collapses because it requires solving the diachronic identity question and answering it simultaneously. This instability doesn't arise in the other-directed case. Far from explaining away first-person authority, interpretivism needs to presuppose it—we need authoritative self-ascription to stabilize the subject that interpretation is about. The interpretive position thus requires what it aimed to eliminate.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Novel circularity argument that interpretation requires fixing the subject diachronically, creating instability unique to self-interpretation that hasn't been adequately addressed.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 2/5, answered. The objection offered Sarah's straightforward anxiety identification, but the defence showed this mistakes episodic state-identification for the diachronic interpretive project the thesis actually targets
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, answered. The objection correctly identified dramatic language but failed to engage the structural asymmetry: other-interpretation has a fixed subject while self-interpretation must fix its own subject
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. The thin/thick distinction was real and serious, but the defence showed Carruthers and Gopnik's theoretical ambitions commit them to thick interpretation, making the equivocation charge fail
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, answered. The objection identified a protective pattern in responses, but the defence showed the thesis makes falsifiable claims about what thick interpretation requires and holds interpretivism to its own theoretical scope
- **Survives as:** Interpretation-based accounts that aim to explain first-person authority generally (not just episodic state-identification) cannot succeed because thick interpretation requires fixing which past psychological states belong to the subject being interpreted, and this subject-fixing cannot be done interpretively without circularity or non-interpretively without presupposing the first-person authority interpretivism aimed to explain away

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider Sarah, who yesterday experienced intense anxiety before a presentation. Today, reflecting on this, she must interpret whether that anxiety represented her "true self" or was merely situational stress. According to the thesis, there's no fact about which interpretation is correct independent of who Sarah now chooses to be continuous with.

But here's a concrete counterexample: Sarah can simply observe that yesterday-Sarah had racing heart, sweating palms, catastrophic thoughts about failure, and avoidance behavior. She interprets these behavioral/physiological signs exactly as she would interpret them in another person—as anxiety. This interpretation succeeds without any prior decision about "which past self to identify with." The evidence (physical symptoms, behavioral patterns) is simply there, publicly accessible even to Sarah herself, and supports the anxiety interpretation regardless of whether Sarah now wishes to see herself as "an anxious person" or "someone who had a situational response."

The interpretation doesn't collapse into circularity—it proceeds straightforwardly from observable evidence to mental state attribution, just as Carruthers and Gopnik claim. Sarah's present identity choices don't determine which past states "belong" to her; temporal continuity of the organism does that unproblematically.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in showing that basic mental state attribution can proceed from straightforward behavioral evidence, but it doesn't fully engage the thesis's deeper point about diachronic identity and which past states count as relevant evidence for interpretation.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The counterexample smuggles in precisely what it needs to assume: that yesterday-Sarah's states are unproblematically Sarah's states to interpret. But this begs the question against the instability argument.

Consider the case properly: Sarah today faces not just yesterday's anxiety episode but a lifetime of mental states. Which subset of these states constitutes the relevant evidence base for interpreting her current mental life? The thesis claims this selection cannot be made neutrally.

Suppose Sarah experienced social anxiety throughout adolescence, then had a confident period in her twenties, and now faces this presentation anxiety at thirty. Is today's anxiety a resurgence of her "true" anxious disposition, or a situational aberration in an otherwise confident person? The interpretation depends on which past states we weight as evidentially relevant.

The objector says "temporal continuity of the organism does that unproblematically." But this is exactly what fails. The organism has continuous existence through all these states—anxious adolescent, confident twentysomething, currently anxious thirty-year-old. Mere organism-continuity doesn't tell us which states to privilege in interpretation. If Sarah identifies as fundamentally confident (choosing continuity with her twenties self), she interprets today's anxiety as situational noise. If she identifies as fundamentally anxious (choosing continuity with her adolescent self), she interprets today's anxiety as her true nature reasserting itself.

The objection's "racing heart, sweating palms" evidence only identifies that an anxiety episode occurred yesterday. It doesn't answer the interpretive question: what does this episode mean about Sarah's mental life more broadly? And that question requires already knowing which Sarah—which selection of past states—we're interpreting.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection mistakes episodic state-identification for the deeper interpretive project. Yes, Sarah can identify that yesterday involved anxiety using behavioral evidence. But interpretation in Carruthers and Gopnik's sense means constructing a coherent psychological profile that explains patterns across time. This requires selecting which temporal slices of mental life constitute the person whose coherent psychology we're reconstructing—and that selection is precisely where the instability enters. The organism-continuity move fails because one organism houses multiple possible psychological continuities, and choosing between them is what self-interpretation cannot do neutrally.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims interpretation-based accounts "cannot explain first-person authority" because self-interpretation faces an instability problem. But examine what's actually being established here. The thesis observes that when interpreting ourselves diachronically, we face questions about which past states to privilege—whether Sarah should identify with her anxious adolescent self or confident twenties self. This is presented as a devastating "instability" that interpretation "cannot" handle.

But this is merely a verbal repackaging of the uncontested fact that self-interpretation involves interpretive choices. Of course when we interpret ourselves across time, we must decide which patterns matter, which past states are relevant, how to weight evidence. That's what interpretation *is*—making sense of data by selecting what's significant. The thesis dresses up this obvious feature of interpretation as though it were a discovered problem ("instability," "circularity," "collapse").

The supposedly problematic "instability" is just: interpreting yourself requires making interpretive choices. The supposedly devastating "circularity" is just: interpretation involves judgment calls about what to emphasize. These aren't discoveries about interpretation—they're stipulative redescriptions of interpretation's ordinary features as if they were bugs rather than features.

The thesis hasn't shown interpretation *fails* at explaining first-person authority. It's just insisted we call interpretation's normal operation "unstable" and declared victory. This is verbal legislation, not philosophical insight.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force—the thesis does dramatize interpretation's ordinary features with loaded terms like "collapse" and "instability." However, the thesis may be making a genuine point: that interpretation requires a stable subject, and self-interpretation cannot provide this stability without presupposing what it aims to explain. That's potentially substantive, not merely verbal.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies what makes the instability problem substantive. The thesis is not merely observing that "interpretation involves interpretive choices"—it's identifying a specific structural defect that appears in the self-directed case but not the other-directed case, and this asymmetry is precisely what interpretation-based accounts cannot explain.

Here's the structural point: Interpretation requires a fixed subject as input. When I interpret you, the subject is independently fixed—you are that organism, with that continuous spatiotemporal trajectory, and the interpretive question is: what mental states best explain that organism's behavior? The subject (you) is settled before interpretation begins.

But when I interpret myself diachronically, the subject cannot be fixed independently of the interpretation itself. The question "which past mental states belong to the subject I'm interpreting?" has no answer prior to the interpretive act. Am I the anxious person who had a confident period, or the confident person who had anxious periods? This isn't an "interpretive choice" within a fixed interpretive project—it's a choice that determines what the interpretive project is about.

The objector says this is just "interpretation's normal operation." But that's precisely wrong. In normal interpretation (other-directed), the subject is fixed and we interpret their states. In self-interpretation, we must simultaneously fix the subject and interpret its states. These are not the same operation.

The substantive problem: Carruthers and Gopnik claim first-person and third-person access work the same way—both are interpretive. But if self-interpretation requires an additional step (fixing the subject) that other-interpretation doesn't require, and if this step cannot itself be done interpretively without vicious regress, then the cases are not parallel. The interpretive account cannot explain first-person authority because it cannot explain how we get the stable subject that interpretation requires.

This isn't verbal legislation. It's identifying that interpretation presupposes subject-identity, and self-interpretation cannot supply this presupposition without circularity. The "instability" terminology captures something real: an interpretation that must determine its own subject matter cannot get started without some non-interpretive ground.

The objector might reply: "Just use organism-continuity to fix the subject." But the previous defence already showed this fails—one organism contains multiple possible psychological continuities. Or they might say: "Just interpret all the organism's states." But then we haven't explained first-person authority—we've made self-knowledge as indirect and uncertain as other-knowledge, which is exactly what interpretation-based accounts aimed to show but what our phenomenology contradicts.
**VERDICT**
The defence holds. The objection correctly notices that the thesis uses dramatic language ("collapse," "instability"), but incorrectly concludes this is merely verbal. The thesis identifies a genuine structural difference: other-interpretation has an independently fixed subject; self-interpretation must fix its own subject. This isn't just "interpretation involves choices"—it's that self-interpretation faces a constitutive problem (determining what it's about) that other-interpretation doesn't face.

The objection fails to engage this structural asymmetry. Calling it "interpretation's normal operation" simply ignores that the operation works differently in the first-person and third-person cases—which is exactly what an account claiming to assimilate these cases needs to address but cannot. The thesis survives: interpretation-based accounts cannot explain first-person authority because they require a stable subject that self-interpretation cannot provide without presupposing the very authority it aimed to explain away.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "interpretation" between a thin sense where it's plausible and a thick sense where it's interesting, and the argument requires both.

Thin sense: Interpretation as simply inferring mental states from evidence (behavioral, physiological, etc.). On this reading, the Carruthers-Gopnik claim is plausible—we do sometimes infer our own mental states from evidence, just as we infer others' mental states.

Thick sense: Interpretation as a holistic project of constructing a coherent psychological narrative across time, requiring prior selection of which temporal slices constitute "the subject" whose coherent psychology we're reconstructing.

The thesis is plausible only on the thin reading: yes, if interpretation meant this thick holistic project, it would face the diachronic identity problem. But Carruthers and Gopnik aren't committed to interpretation in this thick sense—they can simply claim we interpret our current or recent mental states from evidence, without any grand narrative-construction project.

However, the thesis is interesting only on the thick reading. If "interpretation" just means "sometimes we infer our mental states from evidence," then showing it faces problems with diachronic identity doesn't threaten first-person authority generally—it just shows this particular inference method has limits. The dramatic conclusion that "interpretation-based accounts cannot explain first-person authority" requires that interpretation be doing the heavy theoretical lifting of the thick sense.

The argument needs both: plausibility from thin (Carruthers and Gopnik are actually committed to this) and interest from thick (this actually threatens first-person authority generally).

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis gains force by sliding between what Carruthers and Gopnik actually claim (episodic mental state inference from evidence) and a much more ambitious interpretive project (diachronic narrative construction) that they needn't endorse.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misunderstands what Carruthers and Gopnik are committed to. Their accounts are not merely about episodic state-identification but about explaining the systematic features of self-knowledge—including our sense of psychological continuity and our ability to self-ascribe dispositional states. This commits them to the thick sense, not just the thin.

Consider what interpretivism must explain to be a genuine competitor to traditional first-person authority accounts:

**First**, Carruthers and Gopnik explicitly aim to explain not just how we know "I'm anxious right now" but how we know things like "I'm a generous person," "I believe in democracy," "I want to be a good parent"—dispositional and standing states that inherently involve diachronic integration. These aren't episodic inferences from current evidence; they're interpretive constructions from patterns across time. If interpretivism retreats to only explaining episodic state-identification, it abandons its ambition to be a general account of self-knowledge.

**Second**, even episodic self-interpretation requires the thick sense when we move beyond brute sensation. When Sarah interprets her racing heart as "anxiety about the presentation" rather than "excitement about the presentation" or "caffeine effects," she's not just identifying a current state—she's placing it in a meaningful psychological context. Is this anxiety characteristic of her? Is it rational given her history? These questions require determining which past states are relevant evidence, which is precisely the diachronic identity problem.

**Third**, the objector's thin sense cannot explain what makes self-interpretation distinctively first-personal. If interpretation were just "infer mental states from current behavioral/physiological evidence," then my interpretation of myself would be exactly as authoritative (or unauthoritative) as your interpretation of me based on observing my current behavior. But Carruthers and Gopnik claim to explain first-person authority—the special status of self-ascription. To do this, they must explain why my interpretation of my mental life has a privileged status, and this requires appeal to something like: I have access to more evidence (my historical patterns) or I'm better positioned to construct the coherent narrative. Either way, we're in the thick sense.

**Fourth**, the philosophical dialectic requires the thick sense. Interpretivism is offered as an alternative to traditional accounts that grant first-person authority special epistemic status. If interpretivism only claims "sometimes we infer our current states from evidence," it's not competing with these accounts—it's just describing one limited mechanism among others. To be a rival theory, interpretivism must claim to explain the full range of self-knowledge, including knowledge of our enduring psychological characteristics.

The objection tries to save interpretivism by limiting its scope, but this saves it only by making it irrelevant to the debate about first-person authority. The thesis correctly targets interpretivism as a general account—and any general account must face the diachronic identity problem.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds. The objection's thin/thick distinction is real, but the thick sense is not optional for Carruthers and Gopnik—it's required by their theoretical ambitions. They explicitly aim to explain dispositional self-knowledge, the special authority of self-ascription, and systematic features of self-understanding. All of these require interpreting patterns across time, which requires solving the subject-fixing problem.

The objection's proposed retreat—limiting interpretation to episodic state-inference from current evidence—would indeed avoid the diachronic identity problem. But it would also abandon the claim to explain first-person authority generally. An account that only explains "sometimes I infer my current anxiety from my racing heart" is not competing with traditional accounts of self-knowledge's special status.

The thesis stands: interpretation-based accounts that aim to explain first-person authority (not just describe one limited mechanism) must engage in thick interpretation, and thick interpretation faces the instability problem. The objection fails because it offers interpretivism a Pyrrhic victory—escape the problem by abandoning the ambition.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as "interpretation." 

Consider any apparent counterexample where self-interpretation seems to work without instability. The thesis can respond in two ways, both protective:

**First protective move**: If someone offers a case of successful self-interpretation using straightforward evidence (like Sarah identifying her anxiety from physiological symptoms), the thesis redefines this as "mere episodic state-identification" rather than "real interpretation." We saw this in the defense against TRIVIAL: suddenly "interpretation" means the thick holistic project, not the thin inference. But if someone then shows problems with thick interpretation don't touch thin interpretation, the thesis insists thin interpretation isn't what Carruthers and Gopnik really mean—so it's not a genuine counterexample to the thesis about "interpretation-based accounts."

**Second protective move**: Any case where diachronic identity seems unproblematic gets redefined as "presupposing non-interpretive authority." If Sarah straightforwardly identifies with her organism's continuous existence, the thesis declares this "begs the question" because it assumes a stable subject. If she uses social/legal identity, that's dismissed as not genuinely interpretive. The thesis has made "interpretation" mean specifically: determining mental states without any prior subject-fixing. But this is definitional—any successful interpretation that uses a fixed subject is automatically "not really interpretation" by the thesis's lights.

The thesis cannot lose: working self-interpretation is redefined as either "not thick enough to matter" or "presupposes what it should explain," while failing self-interpretation confirms the instability claim. Every case gets sorted into categories that protect the thesis.

**STRENGTH: 3/5**

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis does seem to define "interpretation" in a way that makes it necessarily unstable (must fix subject interpretively, cannot use prior subject-fixing), then claims victory when interpretation so defined fails. However, the thesis might respond that this definition captures what Carruthers and Gopnik are actually committed to, making this a substantive rather than protective move.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses principled distinction-drawing with ad hoc maneuvering. The thesis is not redefining "interpretation" to avoid counterexamples—it's holding interpretivism to its own theoretical commitments and showing these commitments generate internal tension.

**On the "protective moves" charge:**

The objection claims the thesis unfairly shifts between thin and thick interpretation. But this gets the dialectic backwards. The thesis doesn't arbitrarily impose the thick reading—it derives it from what Carruthers and Gopnik must claim to be doing genuine theoretical work. Here's why this isn't protective:

The thin reading (episodic state-inference from current evidence) is genuinely available, but it's *interpretivism's* problem that it can't stop there. Carruthers and Gopnik explicitly present their accounts as explaining first-person authority broadly—including dispositional states, standing beliefs, character traits. They don't say "we only explain how you know your current anxiety from your racing heart." They say self-knowledge generally works through interpretation of evidence. 

When the thesis insists on the thick reading for these cases, that's not redefining to avoid counterexamples—it's identifying what scope interpretivism actually needs to do its theoretical job. The thesis can say: "Fine, thin interpretation works for episodic states, but that's not enough to vindicate interpretivism as an account of first-person authority." This isn't unfalsifiable; it's a substantive claim about explanatory adequacy that could be wrong (if someone showed thin interpretation actually suffices).

**On the "presupposing authority" charge:**

The objection says any successful subject-fixing gets dismissed as "presupposing what it should explain." But this is exactly the substantive point, not a protective maneuver. Here's the structure:

- Interpretivism claims: first-person authority is explained by self-interpretation from evidence (no special first-person access needed)
- The thesis asks: but what fixes which evidence is relevant to interpreting *me*?
- If the answer invokes organism-continuity, social identity, or similar, the thesis responds: these don't determine psychological continuity without further interpretation
- If the answer invokes authoritative self-identification ("I just know which past states are mine"), the thesis responds: this presupposes first-person authority

This isn't unfalsifiable—it's a dilemma. Interpretivism could escape by showing either: (a) subject-fixing can be done neutrally without interpretive choice, or (b) interpretive choice doesn't require prior authority. These are falsifiable empirical/conceptual claims. The thesis argues both fail, but that's substantive argument, not definitional protection.

**The falsifiable core:**

The thesis makes these falsifiable claims:

1. Thick interpretation (needed for dispositional self-knowledge) requires selecting which past states constitute the relevant subject
2. This selection cannot be made on purely organism-continuity grounds (one organism, multiple psychological continuities)
3. Making this selection interpretively creates circularity (must interpret to fix subject, must fix subject to interpret)
4. Making this selection non-interpretively requires first-person authority that interpretivism denies

Each of these could be false. Someone might show: organism-continuity does determine psychological continuity uniquely (falsifies 2), or interpretive subject-fixing doesn't create circularity (falsifies 3), or there's a third option beyond interpretive/non-interpretive selection (falsifies 4).

**What would actually falsify the thesis:**

Show a case where: (a) thick self-interpretation succeeds (explains dispositional/standing states, not just episodic), (b) without presupposing which past psychological states belong to the subject being interpreted, (c) using only resources available to other-interpretation (no special first-person authority), and (d) delivering the kind of certainty/authority that first-person self-ascription actually has.

This is a demanding standard, but that's because the thesis is making a strong claim. It's not unfalsifiable—it's just that meeting this standard is hard, which is the thesis's point.

**The objection's error:**

The objection mistakes "high falsification bar" for "unfalsifiable." Yes, the thesis can respond to many putative counterexamples by showing they either don't engage thick interpretation (so don't threaten the thesis's scope claim) or do presuppose authority (so don't vindicate pure interpretivism). But these responses aren't protective redefinitions—they're applications of the thesis's substantive claims about what interpretivism needs to accomplish and what resources it has available.

An analogy: If I claim "pure empiricism cannot explain mathematical knowledge," and you offer sense-experience of physical objects as a counterexample, I can respond "that doesn't explain abstract mathematics, only applied." This isn't unfalsifiable maneuvering—it's holding empiricism to what it needs to explain. Similarly, the thesis holds interpretivism to explaining the full scope of first-person authority, not just easy cases.
**VERDICT**
The defence holds, but barely. The thesis survives the unfalsifiability charge because it makes substantive falsifiable claims about what thick interpretation requires and what resources are available. The objection correctly identifies that the thesis has demanding standards for what counts as a successful counterexample, but this reflects the thesis's strong claim rather than protective definition.

However, the objection exposes a real tension: the thesis's responses to counterexamples do follow a pattern (dismiss as too thin, or as presupposing authority), and this pattern could indicate the thesis has defined "pure interpretation" so narrowly that nothing could satisfy it. The defence succeeds by showing this narrow definition is principled—it captures what interpretivism must be to compete with traditional accounts—but the thesis would be stronger if it engaged more explicitly with whether this standard is fair or whether interpretivism might reasonably limit its ambitions.

The core insight survives: if interpretivism aims to fully explain first-person authority, it faces the diachronic identity problem. If it retreats to limited claims, it's no longer a rival to traditional accounts. This is substantive, not unfalsifiable, but the objection rightly pushes the thesis to be clearer about whether it's attacking interpretivism's ambitions or its execution.

</details>
