## Repair candidates

*Promise 4+, failed on ONE locatable flaw — usually scope or framing, with the narrower version already written out. Often the richest vein.*

### R1. The supposedly stable 'agentialist' position is actually unstable: if self-knowledge comes through deliberation about what to believe rather than observation of what one believes, then I have no self-knowledge until after I deliberate, making pre-deliberative belief ascriptions systematically false.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Moran's agentialist account and its attempt to ground first-person authority in deliberative transparency
- **Sketch:** Moran's agentialist view holds that I know my own attitudes through deliberating about what attitudes to have (transparency procedure), not through inner observation. This seems to preserve authority while acknowledging the poverty of introspection. But the key premise is that this makes self-knowledge productive rather than detective: deliberation doesn't discover pre-existing attitudes but settles unsettled questions. If I don't know whether I believe P until I deliberate whether P, then I didn't believe P beforehand—deliberation created the belief. This means either: (a) we had no determinate attitudes before deliberating (undermining common sense), or (b) we did have attitudes but the deliberative procedure systematically bypasses them (leaving agentialism unable to explain knowledge of those attitudes). Either way, agentialism collapses back into a form of interpretivism, since what we thought were discoveries turn out to be constructions, and we need some other story (presumably interpretive) for the attitudes that genuinely existed pre-deliberation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Powerful instability charge: agentialism making pre-deliberative attitudes inaccessible is a real problem that hasn't been adequately answered.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed Moran's case differs from ordinary investigation but this confirmed rather than defeated the instability charge
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully showed the counterexample only addressed deliberatively-formed beliefs, not the problematic class of non-deliberatively formed standing beliefs
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence failed to establish genuine instability versus mere incompleteness; acknowledged features are costs but not contradictions
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence clarified no fallacious slide but exposed that 'instability' depends on assuming agentialism must be complete, which nothing forces
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Agentialism faces a serious explanatory gap: while it may successfully account for self-knowledge of deliberatively-formed beliefs, it provides no account of first-person authority regarding the vast class of non-deliberatively formed standing beliefs, forcing it either to supplement with non-agentialist mechanisms or to accept a scope far narrower than its ambitions as a general theory of self-knowledge
- **Flaw:** The thesis conflates incompleteness with instability—showing agentialism has limited scope does not prove it collapses or contains internal contradictions

<details><summary>8 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) Moran's agentialist account commits him to denying that we have self-knowledge of unendorsed mental states, making akrasia and self-deception impossible rather than merely puzzling.
- (3/5) Agentialist accounts like Moran's are unstable because deliberative self-constitution presupposes precisely the transparent self-knowledge it was meant to replace: you cannot deliberate about what to believe without already knowing what you currently believe.
- (3/5) Moran's agentialist account is unstable because deliberative self-knowledge (answering 'Do I believe P?' by considering whether P) requires treating one's mind as transparent to the world, but this transparency commitment is precisely what the confabulation literature refutes, forcing agentialism to collapse into either純 interpretivism or a restricted observational model.
- (4/5) Agentialist accounts that ground first-person authority in the deliberative stance are unstable because deliberation about what to believe requires prior access to what you already believe, which reintroduces the observational model they reject.
- (2/5) Moran's agentialist account is unstable because deliberative self-knowledge requires the very observational access to standing beliefs and desires that the interpretive evidence refutes—agency doesn't bypass interpretation but presupposes transparent access to the inputs to deliberation.
- (3/5) Agentialist accounts like Moran's are unstable because the transparency method—determining what I believe by asking what's true—only generates first-person authority if I already have authoritative access to what I conclude, which returns us to the observational model they reject.
- (3/5) The agentialist account is unstable: if self-knowledge requires rational deliberation about what to believe/desire (Moran), then either this deliberation must itself be available to transparent self-knowledge (vicious regress) or it must be interpretively known (collapsing into the interpretive view it opposes).
- (2/5) Moran-style deliberative self-knowledge collapses into the interpretive model it was designed to oppose, because answering the world-directed question 'Is P true?' requires interpreting which considerations one finds motivating.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The argument claims that if self-knowledge comes through deliberation rather than observation, then pre-deliberative belief ascriptions are systematically false. But this reasoning proves too much: it would show that ANY knowledge gained through active inquiry rather than passive observation makes claims about the pre-inquiry state systematically false. Consider: I don't know whether there's milk in the fridge until I open it and look. Does this mean there was no determinate fact about milk before I looked? Obviously not—looking is how I come to know, but it doesn't create what I know. Similarly, a scientist doesn't know whether hypothesis H is true until conducting experiments, but this doesn't mean H had no truth-value beforehand. The argument conflates the epistemic question (how do I come to know?) with the metaphysical question (what makes it true?). If we accepted this conflation, we'd have to say that anything requiring active investigation to know didn't determinately exist beforehand. This would eliminate most of our knowledge claims about an independent reality. The agentialist can simply say: deliberation is my METHOD for accessing pre-existing attitudes, just as opening the fridge is my method for accessing pre-existing milk. The thesis overreaches by treating an epistemic access story as a metaphysical creation story.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The argument does seem to overgeneralize in a way that would undermine ordinary empirical knowledge, suggesting the inference from "known through X" to "created by X" is invalid.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes Moran's case special. The fridge analogy fails because opening the fridge is *causally independent* of the milk's presence—looking doesn't interact with what's looked at. But deliberation about whether to believe P is not causally independent of believing P. It is itself a *belief-forming process*. When I deliberate about whether P is true, I am *in the very act* of settling whether to believe P. This is not like investigating an external state that remains unchanged by investigation.

The crucial disanalogy: the scientist's experiments don't *constitute* the hypothesis being true or false—they reveal an independent fact. But on Moran's view, deliberating about whether to believe P *just is* the process by which I come to believe P (or not). The transparency procedure directs me to ask "Is P true?" and answering that question *forms* the belief. There's no gap between the deliberative conclusion and the attitude's existence—they're the same event.

So the objection's examples all involve *detective* processes: looking discovers what was already there. But Moran explicitly characterizes transparency as *non-detective*. He says the first-person perspective is "not a perspective on something already in place." The whole point is that I determine my belief by determining what to believe, not by discovering what I already believe.

The proves-too-much charge only works if all knowledge-acquisition is metaphysically on par. But Moran's radical claim is precisely that self-knowledge is *sui generis*—it's constitutive rather than detective. The objection assumes what needs proving: that deliberation is just another form of investigation. But if deliberation *makes* rather than *finds* the attitude, then the inference from "known through deliberation" to "created by deliberation" is exactly right.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not completely.

It successfully establishes that Moran's case differs from ordinary empirical investigation—deliberation about belief is indeed special because it's a belief-forming process, not causally independent observation. This blocks the simple proves-too-much charge.

However, this defence actually *strengthens* the original objection rather than defeating it. By insisting that deliberation constitutes rather than discovers belief, the defence confirms that pre-deliberative belief ascriptions are indeed problematic on agentialist grounds. If deliberation makes the belief rather than finding it, then either (a) there was no belief beforehand, or (b) there was one but agentialism gives no account of knowing it.

The defence wins the battle (Moran's case is special) but loses the war (this specialness is exactly what makes the position unstable). The objection stands: agentialism cannot coherently maintain both that deliberation is constitutive and that it delivers knowledge of pre-existing attitudes.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider someone with a long-standing, settled belief that is simply not currently being deliberated about—say, I've believed for twenty years that my hometown is in California. Right now, I'm not deliberating about it; the question isn't open. Yet I surely know I believe this. According to the thesis, "if self-knowledge comes through deliberation about what to believe rather than observation, then I have no self-knowledge until after I deliberate." But here's a counterexample: I DO have self-knowledge of this belief (I know I believe my hometown is in California) WITHOUT currently deliberating about it. The agentialist can say: I came to know this belief through PAST deliberation that settled the matter, and that knowledge persists. The thesis assumes deliberation must be ONGOING for self-knowledge to exist, but this is false. Just as I can know the fridge contains milk after having looked yesterday (without looking again now), I can know my belief through past deliberative settlement without re-deliberating. The thesis's claim that "pre-deliberative belief ascriptions are systematically false" fails because beliefs formed through past deliberation have a determinate existence that I can know without fresh deliberation. The weak point is the assumption that agentialism requires CONCURRENT deliberation for self-knowledge, when it only requires deliberation to have occurred at some point in the belief's formation or maintenance.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This is a weak objection. It reveals an ambiguity about "pre-deliberative" but doesn't seriously damage the thesis, which can respond that beliefs never-yet-deliberated-about remain problematic, and that's enough instability.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

This counterexample misses the dialectical target. The objection is that agentialism is *unstable*, not that it makes *every* self-knowledge claim impossible. The instability emerges when we ask: what about beliefs I've never deliberated about at all?

Consider beliefs formed non-deliberatively—through testimony, conditioning, or unreflective absorption. I believe my mother loves me, that chairs are for sitting, that strangers might be dangerous. These weren't formed through deliberation about whether to hold them. They're *standing beliefs* that structure my behaviour and inference, but I never answered the question "Should I believe P?" for them.

Now I ask myself: do I believe chairs are for sitting? The agentialist says I answer this by asking "Are chairs for sitting?" and concluding "Yes." But this deliberation is *now*, and the belief (if genuine) existed *before* I deliberated. The thesis's force is: what account does agentialism give of knowledge of *that* belief—the one that existed before this episode of deliberation?

The "past deliberation" response doesn't help here because there *was* no past deliberation about these beliefs. They were never deliberatively formed. Yet they're paradigm cases of beliefs we know we have. If agentialism says I can only know them by deliberating now, then one of two things follows:

(a) The deliberation *discovers* a pre-existing belief—but then it's detective after all, contradicting Moran's core claim that transparency is non-detective.

(b) The deliberation *creates* the belief now—but then the pre-deliberative ascription "I believed chairs are for sitting" was false, since the belief didn't exist until I deliberated.

The counterexample about hometown beliefs only works for beliefs that were *already* deliberatively formed. But that's not the problematic class. The problematic class is beliefs that exist but were never deliberatively formed—and those are ubiquitous. The instability is that agentialism has no story for knowing those.

VERDICT

The defence holds.

The counterexample fails because it only addresses beliefs with a deliberative history. But the thesis's force comes from beliefs *without* such history—the vast class of unreflectively held, non-deliberatively formed standing beliefs. For these, the dilemma remains: either deliberation discovers them (making agentialism collapse into a detective view) or creates them (making pre-deliberative ascriptions false).

The objector cannot escape by saying "past deliberation settled it," because many beliefs were never deliberatively settled at all. And the objector cannot say "those aren't real beliefs," because they function exactly as beliefs should—they guide inference, structure behaviour, and are reportable. If agentialism cannot account for self-knowledge of these ordinary standing beliefs, the instability charge succeeds.

The counterexample reveals that agentialism might work for a *subset* of beliefs (those deliberatively formed), but this is precisely the problem: it cannot be a general account of self-knowledge if it systematically fails for non-deliberatively formed beliefs. The thesis stands.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing an "instability" in agentialism, but it actually just restates what agentialists openly acknowledge as their view, then labels this "unstable" without showing any genuine incoherence. Moran explicitly characterizes the transparency procedure as NON-DETECTIVE and says the first-person perspective is "not a perspective on something already in place." The thesis's "discovery" is simply: "if deliberation creates rather than discovers, then it creates rather than discovers." This is a tautology dressed as a dilemma. The supposed instability—"either we had no determinate attitudes before deliberating or we did but agentialism can't explain knowing them"—is not a problem Moran would deny. He accepts that transparency is constitutive, that it doesn't track pre-existing attitudes, and that this makes first-person authority fundamentally different from detective knowledge. The thesis's weak point is treating this acknowledged feature as a discovered bug. Saying "this means agentialism collapses back into interpretivism" is just stipulating that any non-detective view counts as "interpretivist"—a verbal move, not an argument. The thesis provides no independent reason why a position is "unstable" merely because it denies pre-deliberative determinate attitudes. That's not instability; it's just a substantive (controversial) metaphysical claim about attitudes. The thesis is true only in the trivial sense that agentialism has the features agentialists say it has.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force in showing the thesis may overstate its dialectical achievement, but doesn't fully succeed. The thesis does identify a genuine tension between agentialism's ambitions (explaining ordinary self-knowledge) and its resources (only deliberatively-formed beliefs), even if agentialists are aware of the issue. That's not merely trivial.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The triviality charge fails because it ignores the *cost* of what agentialists acknowledge. Yes, Moran says transparency is non-detective. But the thesis shows this acknowledgment is *dialectically ruinous* given agentialism's explanatory ambitions.

The instability is not "agentialism has feature X which I'll call unstable." It's that agentialism *cannot coherently occupy the theoretical role it claims*. Moran presents agentialism as an account of first-person authority—of how we have privileged access to our own mental states. But if the transparency procedure is constitutive rather than detective, it cannot explain authority over *standing beliefs* at all.

Here's the instability: agentialism is offered as a *replacement* for observational/introspective models of self-knowledge. It's supposed to explain the same phenomenon (first-person authority) better. But it systematically fails for the central case: knowledge of beliefs I currently have but am not now deliberating about. When I truthfully report "I believe my mother loves me" without having just deliberated about it, what explains my authority?

The agentialist cannot say "transparency explains it" because transparency only operates when I'm deliberating. They cannot say "introspection explains it" because they rejected introspection as the basis for authority. They're left with no explanation of the very phenomenon they set out to explain.

The objection says "Moran accepts this consequence." But accepting a consequence doesn't make it cost-free. If I offer a "theory of vision" that only explains seeing things I'm currently looking at, and someone objects "but what about peripheral vision?", I cannot reply "I openly acknowledge my theory doesn't cover that—so there's no problem!" The problem is that any adequate theory of vision *must* cover peripheral vision.

Similarly, any adequate account of self-knowledge must explain knowledge of standing beliefs. If agentialism cannot, it's not a trivial criticism to point this out—it's showing the view fails at its central task. The instability is between *ambition* (explaining first-person authority generally) and *capacity* (explaining only deliberatively-active states).

The "collapse into interpretivism" is not mere stipulation. It's that once agentialists admit they need *some other story* for standing beliefs, they've conceded that transparency is not a general account. And the available alternatives for standing beliefs are either observational (which they rejected) or interpretive (which makes them interpretivists about most beliefs). That's genuine instability: the view cannot remain distinctively "agentialist" while covering all the cases it must cover.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds partially but ultimately fails.

The defence correctly identifies that there's a real explanatory gap—agentialism struggles with standing beliefs—and this is not merely a feature but a cost. The point about explanatory ambitions versus capacity has force: if agentialism was offered as a general account of self-knowledge but only works for actively deliberated states, that's a genuine limitation.

However, the defence fails to establish true *instability* rather than mere *incompleteness*. The objection's core point stands: this is an openly acknowledged feature of the view, not a hidden incoherence. Moran could consistently respond: "I offer an account of self-knowledge *for rational agents qua rational*—knowledge gained through the deliberative stance. For standing beliefs not currently under deliberation, either (a) they're dispositional states we don't currently know we have, or (b) we know them through memory of past deliberation, or (c) yes, we need a supplementary story."

None of these responses shows *instability*—they show agentialism has limited scope or needs supplementation. That's a cost, but not a contradiction. The thesis overreaches by claiming agentialism "collapses" when really it just proves less ambitious than advertised.

The deepest problem with the defence: it hasn't shown that accepting limited scope is *impossible* for agentialists. A view can be narrow without being unstable. The thesis needed to show that agentialism's core commitments *contradict* each other or that the view *cannot coherently exist* even in limited form. Instead it shows agentialism cannot do everything we might want. That's a serious criticism but not an instability proof.

The verdict: the triviality objection succeeds. The thesis identifies real costs but hasn't demonstrated instability in the required sense.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "self-knowledge" between two readings, and the argument needs both to work. 

On one reading, "self-knowledge" means *occurrent, authoritative knowledge of one's current mental states*—the kind of knowledge I exercise when I sincerely report "I believe P" right now. On this reading, the thesis is INTERESTING: if agentialism says I only get this knowledge through deliberation, then it's a substantive problem that I seem to have such knowledge for beliefs I'm not currently deliberating about.

On another reading, "self-knowledge" means *any true self-attribution of mental states, including dispositional or standing beliefs*. On this reading, the thesis is PLAUSIBLE: yes, if I've never deliberated about whether I believe P, then perhaps I don't have authoritative first-person knowledge that I believe P (though I might still believe it).

The argument needs both readings. The interesting problem (instability, collapse) only emerges if we assume agentialism must explain ALL self-knowledge in the first, occurrent-authoritative sense. But agentialism is only plausible if "self-knowledge" in its scope is restricted to the second sense—knowledge gained through the deliberative stance when adopted. 

The weak premise is: "making pre-deliberative belief ascriptions systematically false." This is only problematic if "false" means "lacking first-person authority" (plausible) but the argument needs it to mean "metaphysically incorrect" (interesting). The equivocation makes the collapse seem forced when it isn't.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force in identifying an ambiguity about what "self-knowledge" covers, but doesn't seriously damage the thesis. The defender can clarify that they mean authoritative first-person knowledge throughout, and the instability argument still runs. The equivocation isn't doing the essential work.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The equivocation charge mislocates where the argument's force lies. The thesis does not depend on sliding between different senses of "self-knowledge"—it depends on a single, stable phenomenon that agentialism cannot explain.

Focus on this case: I sincerely and authoritatively report "I believe my mother loves me" right now, without having just deliberated about whether to believe this. This is paradigmatic first-person authority—I know what I believe, I'm not guessing or interpreting, and I have a kind of access you don't have. This is the explanandum any theory of self-knowledge must address.

The question is simple: what explains my authority in this case? The agentialist cannot say "transparency/deliberation" because I'm not currently deliberating. They cannot say "introspection" because they rejected that model. So what's left?

The objection tries to split the target: maybe agentialism only needs to explain "occurrent, authoritative knowledge gained through deliberation" and can remain silent about other cases. But this split is unprincipled. The phenomenon of first-person authority doesn't come pre-divided into "deliberative cases" and "other cases." When I report my belief about my mother, I have exactly the same kind of authority I'd have if I'd just deliberated about it. The confidence, the non-inferential character, the immunity to error through misidentification—all identical.

If agentialism says "I only explain authority in deliberative cases," the obvious question is: what explains the identical authority in non-deliberative cases? If they say "something else explains those," they've admitted agentialism is not an account of first-person authority as such—it's an account of one narrow route to self-knowledge. But then it's not competing with observationalism or interpretivism as general accounts; it's just describing one special case.

The "equivocation" the objection identifies is actually the thesis's strength: there's ONE phenomenon (authoritative first-person knowledge) that presents itself uniformly across cases, but agentialism can only explain a subset. That's not equivocation—it's showing that agentialism fails to carve the phenomenon at its joints.

Moreover, the objection's proposed restriction—agentialism only explains "knowledge gained through the deliberative stance when adopted"—makes the view trivially true but explanatorily useless. "When I deliberate, I come to know through deliberation" is not a theory of self-knowledge; it's a tautology. The interesting claim was that deliberation explains first-person authority. But if authority exists equally in non-deliberative cases, deliberation doesn't explain authority—it's just one occasion where authority happens to be present.

The thesis stands: agentialism cannot explain the uniform phenomenon of first-person authority while restricting its mechanism to deliberative episodes. That's instability between what needs explaining (authority in general) and what the theory can explain (authority in deliberative cases only).

VERDICT

The defence fails.

The objection correctly identifies that the thesis hasn't established genuine equivocation—there's no slide between meanings doing illicit argumentative work. But the defence's response, while clarifying the dialectic, actually exposes rather than fixes the thesis's core problem.

The defence claims there's "ONE phenomenon (authoritative first-person knowledge) that presents itself uniformly across cases." But this is precisely what's in dispute. The agentialist can deny uniformity: perhaps authority in deliberative cases has a different character or ground than seeming-authority in non-deliberative cases.

More devastatingly, the defence's own argument undermines the instability claim. If agentialism "can only explain a subset" of self-knowledge, that makes it *incomplete*, not *unstable*. The defence says this incompleteness is a problem because agentialism "cannot remain distinctively agentialist while covering all cases." But why not? A view can be distinctively agentialist about deliberative self-knowledge while being, say, dispositionalist or functionalist about standing beliefs. That's not "collapse"—it's division of labor.

The defence's strongest point—that first-person authority seems phenomenologically uniform—is empirical and contestable. Moran could reply that there's a real difference between the authority I have when actively deliberating and the less-reflective confidence I have about standing beliefs. The former is rational authority (grounded in reasons); the latter might be mere psychological certainty (grounded in habit or disposition).

The equivocation objection succeeds not because it finds a fallacy, but because it reveals that the thesis's "instability" charge depends on assuming agentialism must be a *complete* theory of all self-knowledge. But nothing forces that assumption. A theory can be stable, coherent, and explanatorily valuable while having limited scope.

The thesis fails to prove instability. At most it proves that agentialism, if restricted to deliberative cases, is less ambitious than we might have hoped. That's a criticism, but not the devastating "collapse" the thesis claims.

</details>

---

### R2. If interpretation of others succeeds despite using the same indirect evidence we use for ourselves, then the evidence must be sufficient for knowledge; but then first-person authority requires explaining not privileged access but privileged ignorance—why we're worse at knowing ourselves.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared framing across the debate that treats first-person authority as epistemic privilege requiring explanation
- **Sketch:** The standard framing treats first-person authority as the explanandum—why are we better at self-knowledge? But if Carruthers is right that we use the same interpretive methods for self and other, and if interpretation of others often succeeds in generating knowledge (as it must for mindreading to work), then the same methods applied to ourselves should work equally well—actually better, since we have more evidence (all the behavioral data others see plus proprioceptive and memory access). The puzzle becomes: why do we have confabulation, motivated reasoning, and blind spots about ourselves that others can sometimes see through? The asymmetry exists, but points the wrong direction for 'authority.' What needs explaining is first-person epistemic disadvantage. Maybe authority is not about better access but about normative standing: I get to decide what I think not because I know better but because it's my life to organize.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Clever inversion: if interpretation works for others with same evidence, why are we worse at self-knowledge? Reframes the explanandum productively.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 3/5, answered. Defence showed the argument is comparative at any epistemic standard, not requiring equivocation between thin and thick knowledge
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence specified determinate falsification conditions: equal or better performance in interpretive self-attribution when evidence is controlled
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence conceded the conclusion resembles Moran but claimed a different route; this narrows the thesis to 'new argument for old view' rather than meeting the objection
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, answered. Defence identified principled constraints: puzzle arises only where same methods, evidential advantage, underperformance, and no recognized expertise all obtain
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The thesis should be repositioned as: 'Carruthers's interpretive symmetry thesis, combined with empirical evidence of self-interpretive underperformance despite evidential advantage, provides novel empirical motivation for normative constitutivism about self-knowledge—revealing why that view is needed to explain comparative interpretive failure rather than being merely a transcendental requirement of agency.'
- **Flaw:** The thesis's main philosophical payoff—relocating authority from epistemic to normative grounds—is already established by Moran and Bilgrami; the thesis survives only as offering a new empirical route to their conclusion

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "knowledge" between minimal success conditions and robust epistemic achievement. When it claims "interpretation of others succeeds" and "must be sufficient for knowledge," it trades on a thin reading—we get by, achieve workable coordination, predict behavior tolerably well. This makes the premise plausible: yes, third-person interpretation "works" in this minimal sense. But when it concludes we should be puzzled by first-person failures, it needs a thick reading where "knowledge" means something robust enough that our self-ignorance becomes genuinely surprising. The argument requires both: the thin reading to establish that the same methods "succeed" for others (making the premise credible), and the thick reading to make our self-failures puzzling enough to flip the explanandum (making the conclusion interesting). But on the thin reading alone, there's no puzzle—of course we bumble along with ourselves as with others. And on the thick reading alone, the premise fails—third-person interpretation doesn't reliably generate robust knowledge either; we misread others constantly. The reframing only works by sliding between these standards.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs to specify what grade of success in other-interpretation is supposed to make self-ignorance surprising, and whether that grade is actually achieved.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mislocates where standards matter. The argument doesn't require thick knowledge of others—it requires only that *whatever grade of success* we achieve in other-interpretation, we should achieve *at least that grade* for ourselves given our evidential advantage. The reframing works at any consistent epistemic standard.

Consider: If third-person mindreading delivers only "thin" knowledge (workable coordination, tolerable prediction), then given that we have *all the evidence others have plus proprioceptive access, memory, and behavioral history*, we should achieve *at least* that same thin success rate for ourselves. But we don't—we confabulate about our own motives, miss patterns others spot, fail at self-prediction in ways that surprise us. The puzzle isn't "why don't we have thick self-knowledge when we have thick other-knowledge?" It's "why do we perform *worse* at self-interpretation than other-interpretation when we have *better* evidence?"

The objection claims we "misread others constantly"—but this actually strengthens the reframing. If other-interpretation is unreliable despite our doing it constantly with reasonably functional results, yet self-interpretation is *even more* unreliable despite *better* evidential position, that's precisely the asymmetry needing explanation. The puzzle survives at any epistemic grade: thin or thick, we should do better with more evidence, yet we do worse.

The equivocation charge fails because the argument's structure is comparative, not absolute. It doesn't need to establish that other-knowledge meets some independent standard—only that self-knowledge systematically underperforms other-knowledge despite evidential advantage.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection assumes the argument needs to establish that other-interpretation achieves some particular grade of success, then show self-interpretation fails that grade. But the actual structure is: *whatever* grade other-interpretation achieves, self-interpretation should achieve *at least that* given superior evidence—yet it doesn't. This is a well-formed puzzle at any epistemic standard. The reframing doesn't equivocate; it identifies a comparative anomaly that persists whether we're talking about thin or thick knowledge.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself by strategic retreat on what counts as "privileged ignorance" requiring explanation. When presented with cases where self-knowledge seems to work normally—I know I'm thirsty, I know what I believe about climate change, I know I'm angry—the thesis can respond: "those aren't the interesting cases; I mean confabulation, motivated reasoning, and blind spots." But when pressed on whether these are truly systematic rather than occasional failures, or whether they're worse than our failures with others, it can retreat to: "any asymmetry in the wrong direction needs explaining." The phrase "privileged ignorance" sounds like a robust phenomenon, but becomes whatever degree of self-ignorance is needed to make the reframing work. 

Similarly, "worse at knowing ourselves" is protected from counterexamples. Someone notes we're actually quite good at introspecting current phenomenal states? "That's not the self-knowledge I mean—I mean dispositional attitudes and motives." Someone points out we navigate our own mental lives successfully enough to function? "I only mean we're worse *relative to our evidential advantage*—any failure to exceed others counts." The thesis can't lose: whatever self-knowledge works gets redefined as not the target phenomenon, and whatever threshold of comparative failure would falsify "we're worse" gets adjusted to preserve the claim.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force but the thesis can likely specify more determinate success conditions. The comparative structure ("worse despite more evidence") does provide falsification conditions in principle, even if they need sharpening.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes flexibility about *which* self-knowledge matters for unfalsifiability about *whether* the asymmetry exists. The thesis can and does specify determinate conditions: we're worse at self-interpretation than other-interpretation in precisely those domains where interpretation is doing the epistemic work—motives, reasons, dispositional beliefs, character traits. These aren't gerrymandered to save the thesis; they're the domains where Carruthers's interpretive model is supposed to apply.

The objection's counterexamples actually prove the point. "I know I'm thirsty" and "I know I'm angry" aren't cases of *interpretation* at all—they're direct phenomenal awareness, precisely what the thesis brackets by accepting Carruthers's framework. If the thesis retreated to "we're worse at phenomenal self-knowledge," *that* would be falsified by these cases. But it doesn't retreat—it consistently identifies the target domain as interpretive self-knowledge from the start.

The comparative structure provides clear falsification conditions: show that in domains requiring interpretation (motive attribution, reason-giving, dispositional belief ascription), we perform *at least as well* for ourselves as for others when controlling for evidence. The thesis predicts we don't—we confabulate about our own motives more than others', we're worse at predicting our own behavior in novel situations, we miss patterns in our own conduct that friends spot. These are empirically testable claims with determinate success conditions.

The objection claims "worse despite more evidence" can be adjusted infinitely, but that's false. If studies showed we're *better* at predicting our own behavior, *better* at identifying our own motives, *more* reliable about our own reasons—the thesis would be straightforwardly falsified. The fact that such findings would surprise us (given the introspection tradition) is exactly what makes the reframing interesting.

On "privileged ignorance" becoming "whatever degree needed"—no. The thesis specifies: systematic patterns of self-ignorance in interpretive domains. Not occasional failures (everyone has those), not failures equal to our failures with others (that wouldn't be asymmetry), but systematic underperformance relative to evidential position. That's a determinate phenomenon. Wilson's studies on affective forecasting, Nisbett and Wilson on causal self-attribution, Pronin on bias blind spots—these aren't cherry-picked; they're robust research programs documenting the pattern.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection conflates two things: (1) specifying which domain of self-knowledge is at issue, versus (2) making the claim unfalsifiable. The thesis does (1) legitimately—it targets interpretive self-knowledge, not phenomenal awareness, because that's where Carruthers's account applies. And it provides clear falsification conditions: show equal or better performance in interpretive self-attribution compared to other-attribution when evidence is controlled. That we don't find this is empirically documented, not protected by definition.

The "strategic retreat" charge would work if the thesis kept narrowing its target to avoid counterexamples *after* being pressed. But the target is specified from the start: interpretive domains where we have evidential advantage yet perform worse. The objection hasn't shown this is unfalsifiable—it's shown the thesis needs to be clear about its scope, which it can be and is.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This position is already taken—it's essentially Moran's and Bilgrami's normative constitutivism about self-knowledge, which likewise denies that first-person authority is fundamentally epistemic. Moran (2001, *Authority and Estrangement*) argues that first-person authority isn't about privileged epistemic access but about the deliberative stance: I answer "What do I believe about X?" by answering "What should I believe about X?" The authority is rational-agentive, not observational. Bilgrami similarly grounds first-person authority in the constitutive norms of agency rather than superior evidence. The thesis's conclusion—"Maybe authority is not about better access but about normative standing: I get to decide what I think not because I know better but because it's my life to organize"—is nearly verbatim Moran's view. Even the dialectical setup parallels Moran: both start by accepting that self-knowledge isn't observationally privileged (Moran accepts we often know ourselves through interpretation), then relocate authority from epistemic to normative grounds. The "reframing" that treats self-ignorance as the puzzle rather than self-knowledge is also anticipated in Moran's discussion of self-alienation as the departure from the deliberative stance. The thesis presents as novel a "flipping" of the explanandum, but this flip is precisely what normative constitutivism already accomplished twenty years ago.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis's main philosophical payoff—relocating authority from epistemic to normative grounds—is already the established position of a significant literature. The reframing may have independent interest as a way of *motivating* normative constitutivism via Carruthers, but it's not a new position.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates *conclusion* with *route*. Yes, the thesis lands near Moran's normative constitutivism—but it arrives there by a completely different path that reveals something Moran's approach obscures: the *empirical puzzle* that makes normative reconception necessary.

Moran starts from rational agency and argues transcendentally: given that we're agents who deliberate, first-person authority must be normative rather than epistemic. This is philosophically elegant but leaves unclear why we needed the detour through epistemic authority in the first place. Why did the tradition get stuck on privileged access? Moran treats this as philosophical confusion to be dissolved.

The thesis does something different: it takes seriously the empirical claim that we use the same interpretive methods for self and other (Carruthers), accepts that these methods work well enough to generate other-knowledge, then derives a *positive empirical prediction*—that self-interpretation should work at least as well. When this prediction fails systematically, we have an empirical anomaly requiring explanation, not just a philosophical confusion requiring dissolution. The normative reconception emerges as the solution to a *scientific puzzle* about comparative performance, not as a transcendental requirement of agency.

This difference in route matters because it makes different phenomena salient. Moran's framework highlights deliberation, commitment, and transparency—cases where we determine what we think by thinking about the world. The thesis's framework highlights confabulation, motivated reasoning, and bias blind spots—cases where self-interpretation systematically fails despite evidential advantage. These are different explananda. Moran's view explains why first-person authority isn't *like* perceptual authority; the thesis explains why we're *worse* at self-interpretation than other-interpretation.

The empirical grounding also makes different predictions. If Moran is right, we should find first-person authority strongest in deliberative contexts (forming beliefs, deciding intentions) and weakest in observational contexts (discovering occurrent states). If the thesis is right, we should find the asymmetry—self-interpretation underperforming other-interpretation—strongest precisely where we have the most evidential advantage: in domains where we have both behavioral evidence and privileged access. These are testable differences.

Moreover, the thesis can *explain* why Moran's deliberative stance works as a solution. If the puzzle is "why do interpretive methods fail for self when they succeed for others?", one answer is: because self-interpretation faces distinctive obstacles (motivated reasoning, ego-involvement, narrative pressure) that other-interpretation doesn't face in the same way. The deliberative stance bypasses these obstacles not by providing better evidence but by changing the question from "What do I believe?" (interpretive, vulnerable to bias) to "What should I believe?" (normative, bypassing interpretation). This makes Moran's solution look less like a transcendental necessity and more like a *workaround* for a genuine epistemic disadvantage.

Finally, the thesis's version can accommodate cases Moran's struggles with. When I confabulate about my motives and you correct me ("No, you're angry because of X, not Y"), you're exercising interpretive authority that *defeats* my first-person authority. Moran has to treat this as me being alienated from my deliberative stance—but that seems backward. I was fully in the deliberative stance, giving my reasons, exercising my authority to say what I think—and I was *wrong* in a way you could see better than me. The thesis explains this straightforwardly: other-interpretation sometimes outperforms self-interpretation because it avoids motivated reasoning. Moran's framework makes it puzzling.

So while the conclusion resembles Moran's, the thesis offers: (1) a different explanatory route via empirical asymmetry rather than transcendental argument, (2) different salient phenomena (comparative failure rather than deliberative transparency), (3) different testable predictions, (4) an explanation of why Moran's solution works, and (5) better accommodation of cases where others know us better than we know ourselves.

VERDICT

The defence substantially succeeds, but with qualification. The objection is right that the thesis's *conclusion*—authority as normative standing rather than epistemic privilege—is already taken by Moran and Bilgrami. This significantly reduces the thesis's novelty at the level of positive view.

However, the defence shows the thesis makes a genuine contribution in its *explanatory structure*. Deriving normative constitutivism from an empirical puzzle about comparative interpretive performance is genuinely different from Moran's transcendental derivation from agentive requirements. This matters because:

1. It makes the normative turn empirically motivated rather than philosophically stipulated
2. It explains phenomena (confabulation, others knowing us better) that Moran treats as peripheral
3. It generates different empirical predictions about where asymmetries should appear
4. It provides independent support for normative constitutivism from a naturalistic direction

The thesis should be repositioned not as discovering a new view but as providing a new *argument* for an established view—one that reveals empirical structure Moran's approach misses. That's still philosophically valuable, though less revolutionary than the original framing suggests.

The "already taken" objection succeeds in showing the destination is occupied, but fails to show the route is already traveled. In philosophy, sometimes a new path to familiar ground is itself a contribution—especially when it reveals why that ground was worth reaching.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The argument's structure—"if method M works for domain D1, and we have better evidence for domain D2, then M should work at least as well for D2; when it doesn't, that's what needs explaining"—proves too much because it would make puzzles out of systematic asymmetries we recognize as unproblematic. 

Consider: Doctors use interpretive methods (symptom patterns, behavioral indicators, test results) to diagnose others' illnesses quite successfully. Patients have vastly more evidence about their own bodies—every sensation, complete behavioral history, privileged access to pain locations and qualities. Yet patients are systematically *worse* at self-diagnosis than doctors are at diagnosing them. Patients over-interpret minor symptoms, miss serious ones, confabulate causal stories, exhibit motivated reasoning about prognosis. By the thesis's logic, this should be the puzzle requiring explanation: why "privileged ignorance" about our own health?

But we don't find this puzzling because we recognize that *more evidence doesn't guarantee better interpretation* when the interpreter lacks training, has motivated biases, or is too close to the phenomenon. The medical case shows that having privileged access can actually *impair* interpretive performance through anxiety, over-attention, or lack of comparative context.

If the thesis's reframing logic worked, we'd have to "flip the explanandum" for every domain where privileged access coincides with interpretive disadvantage: Why are we worse at diagnosing ourselves? Why are parents worse at objectively assessing their own children? Why are we worse at editing our own writing? The thesis would generate puzzles everywhere, making genuine epistemic authority unexplainable—we'd always be asking "why aren't we better?" rather than recognizing when authority genuinely exists.

The weak point is the premise that "if interpretation of others succeeds despite using the same indirect evidence we use for ourselves, then the evidence must be sufficient for knowledge." This ignores that interpretive success depends not just on evidence quantity but on interpreter position, training, motivation, and distance from the subject matter.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis's logic would make unproblematic asymmetries (expert diagnosis, objective assessment requiring distance) into puzzles requiring explanation, suggesting the reframing principle is too strong. The thesis needs to explain why self-knowledge asymmetry is genuinely puzzling while medical self-diagnosis asymmetry is not.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses a crucial disanalogy: in the medical case, we *do* recognize genuine expertise that explains the asymmetry—doctors have specialized training, comparative databases, and diagnostic frameworks patients lack. This isn't puzzling because the evidential advantage (privileged access to sensations) is overwhelmed by a recognized *interpretive* disadvantage (lack of medical knowledge). The asymmetry is explained by pointing to a real difference in interpretive capacity.

But now apply this to the self-knowledge case. What's the equivalent expertise that others possess about my mind that I lack about my own? The thesis accepts Carruthers's claim that we use *the same interpretive methods* for self and other—mindreading, folk psychology, behavioral pattern recognition. There's no special training others have for interpreting me that I lack for interpreting myself. If anything, I have *more* practice interpreting myself, plus all the evidence they have and more.

The medical analogy actually *strengthens* the thesis. We're not puzzled by patient self-diagnosis failures because we can point to doctors' superior interpretive framework. We *should* be puzzled by self-knowledge failures because we *cannot* point to others' superior framework—they're using the same folk-psychological methods we use, just with less evidence. That's what makes it anomalous.

The objection claims "more evidence doesn't guarantee better interpretation when the interpreter lacks training, has motivated biases, or is too close." Exactly—and this is precisely what the thesis identifies as needing explanation! Why do motivated biases systematically impair self-interpretation more than other-interpretation? Why does "closeness" to one's own mind create interpretive disadvantage when closeness to one's own body merely means more evidence for doctors to interpret? The medical case shows that evidential advantage *can* be overcome by interpretive disadvantage—but in that case we can specify what the disadvantage is (lack of medical training). In the self-knowledge case, what's the equivalent?

The objection lists: "anxiety, over-attention, or lack of comparative context." But notice what's happened—we've now generated a *substantive empirical hypothesis* about why self-interpretation fails: motivated reasoning, ego-involvement, lack of comparative baseline. This isn't the thesis proving too much; this is the thesis *working*. It reframes first-person authority as requiring explanation of systematic interpretive failure, and the objection responds by... offering explanations of systematic interpretive failure. The reframing has done its job.

On "we'd have to flip the explanandum everywhere"—no. We flip it only where: (1) the same interpretive methods are used for self and other, (2) self has evidential advantage, (3) self nevertheless underperforms, and (4) we cannot point to recognized expertise explaining the gap. Medical diagnosis fails condition (4)—we can point to medical expertise. Parental assessment of children fails condition (1)—parents use different methods (emotional investment, narrative identity) not just folk psychology. Self-editing fails condition (2)—we don't have more evidence about our writing's clarity to readers; we have less (we know what we meant, can't see it fresh).

The self-knowledge case uniquely satisfies all four conditions, which is exactly why it's genuinely puzzling in a way these analogies aren't.

Finally, the objection says this would make "genuine epistemic authority unexplainable." But the thesis *does* explain it—just not as epistemic privilege. First-person authority becomes *normative* authority (Moran's deliberative stance) or *constitutive* authority (I determine what I think by thinking). The reframing doesn't eliminate authority; it relocates it from epistemology to normativity. That's not proving too much; that's the philosophical work the thesis does.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The objection's medical analogy looks powerful but actually reveals why the self-knowledge case is distinctively puzzling. In medicine, we can point to expertise (training, comparative knowledge, diagnostic frameworks) that explains why doctors outperform patients despite patients' evidential advantage. In self-knowledge, we cannot point to equivalent expertise—others use the same folk-psychological methods we use, just with less evidence. That's what makes the asymmetry anomalous.

The objection claims the thesis would generate spurious puzzles everywhere, but the defence shows it generates puzzles only where specific conditions are met—conditions that hold for self-knowledge but not for the proposed analogies. Medical diagnosis, parental assessment, and self-editing all have disanalogies that explain their asymmetries without mystery. Self-knowledge lacks such disanalogies, which is precisely what the thesis identifies.

Moreover, the objection's own response—citing motivated reasoning, over-attention, lack of comparative context—actually demonstrates the thesis working correctly. These are substantive empirical explanations of why self-interpretation underperforms, exactly what the reframing makes salient. The thesis doesn't prove too much; it proves what needs proving: that the interesting explanandum is why we're *worse* at self-interpretation despite evidential advantage, and the answer involves distinctive obstacles (motivation, ego-involvement) rather than distinctive privileges.

The proves-too-much objection fails because the reframing principle is appropriately constrained: it generates puzzles only where evidential advantage coincides with interpretive disadvantage *without* recognizable expertise explaining the gap. That's a principled boundary, not an ad hoc one, and self-knowledge falls distinctively on the puzzle-generating side of it.

</details>

### R3. The agentialist view is committed to eliminativism about standing self-ignorance: if Moran is right that avowing a belief is deciding what to believe, then Moore-paradoxical states should be impossible, but implicit attitudes show they're actual.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Moran's agentialist account and anyone who thinks deliberative self-knowledge is constitutive rather than merely available
- **Sketch:** Moran's agentialism says self-knowledge involves a deliberative stance: I know whether I believe P by considering whether P. But this entails that forming a view about whether-I-believe-P and forming a view about P are the same act—they can't come apart. Yet implicit attitude research shows stable dispositions to behave as-if one believes not-P while sincerely avowing P. If avowing is deciding, these subjects would be deciding both P and not-P, which is impossible. So either the implicit attitudes aren't really beliefs (implausibly restrictive), or Moran must deny we can be ignorant of our standing attitudes while having them. This latter commitment is empirically untenable. The key premise is that the agential account cannot accommodate a mental state I have but whose existence I'm genuinely ignorant of (not just inattentive to).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Agentialism's commitment to impossibility of Moore-paradoxical states conflicts with implicit attitude evidence—sharp empirical pressure.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 2/5, answered. The defence successfully distinguished unconsidered beliefs from misconstrued beliefs; the objection attacked a strawman commitment.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 1/5, answered. The therapy case provided additional evidence for the thesis rather than countering it; the defence correctly identified the dialectical confusion.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence attempted to find daylight between constitutive method and eliminativist consequence, but this collapsed—the thesis restates what agentialism explicitly is.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 2/5, answered. The defence showed the thesis consistently targets Moore-paradoxical structures, not two conflated phenomena; the objection misidentified the target.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Constitutive agentialism's explicit denial that deliberative self-knowledge can be mistaken conflicts with empirical evidence from implicit attitudes research, creating pressure to either restrict what counts as belief (implausibly) or abandon the constitutive claim
- **Flaw:** The thesis presents as exposing a problematic commitment what is actually the advertised consequence of constitutive agentialism—that deliberative self-knowledge cannot be mistaken—making it true but trivial

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The argument claims that if avowing-P and believing-P cannot come apart (because avowing is deciding what to believe), then implicit attitudes that conflict with avowals become impossible. But this same reasoning would prove too much: it would make *all* self-ignorance impossible, including uncontroversially real cases. Consider someone who has never considered whether they believe P—say, whether Tuesdays feel longer than Wednesdays. They have no occurrent view, yet surely have dispositional attitudes (perhaps they consistently plan less for Tuesday afternoons). On the critic's logic, if "avowing is deciding" means these can't come apart, then this person cannot have the Tuesday-belief without having avowed it—but that's absurd. The argument thus collapses the distinction between *having a standing belief* and *having considered whether one has it*. If valid, it wouldn't just threaten Moran's view—it would prove that no one ever has beliefs about matters they haven't explicitly considered. Since we clearly do have such beliefs, and the argument's logic applies equally to these mundane cases as to Moore-paradoxical ones, the argument refutes itself by proving something obviously false. The weak premise is the assumption that "avowing-P and believing-P can't come apart" entails "having-belief-P requires having-avowed-P."

STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has some initial appeal but ultimately doesn't damage the thesis much. The thesis specifically concerns "standing self-ignorance" where one *has* a belief but is ignorant of having it, not cases where one simply hasn't considered the matter. Moran could distinguish between unconsidered beliefs (no self-ignorance because no self-opinion formed) and misconstrued beliefs (genuine self-ignorance). The "proves too much" concern only bites if we misread the agentialist commitment.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates two entirely different phenomena that the agentialist can and must distinguish: *not having taken a stance* versus *having taken the wrong stance*.

When someone has never considered whether Tuesdays feel longer, there is no self-ignorance because there is no self-belief to be ignorant about. They haven't avowed anything about their Tuesday-belief, so the question of whether their avowal matches their standing attitude simply doesn't arise. This is *absence of self-knowledge*, not *false self-knowledge*.

The Moore-paradoxical cases are fundamentally different. Here the subject *has* taken a deliberative stance—they have avowed "I believe P" (or avowed P, which on Moran's view amounts to the same thing). The problem is that this avowal conflicts with their actual dispositional profile. This is genuine self-ignorance: they believe they believe P, but they actually believe not-P (or vice versa).

The thesis claims that Moran's agentialism cannot accommodate this second phenomenon—cases where someone has formed a view about what they believe but gotten it wrong. The objection's Tuesday-example is simply irrelevant because it's a case of the first phenomenon—no view formed at all.

The key agentialist commitment is not "you cannot have beliefs you haven't avowed" (which would indeed be absurd). It's rather: "when you *do* form a view about whether you believe P—when you do avow—this avowal cannot come apart from what you actually believe, because the avowal constitutes the belief." The Tuesday-case never triggers this mechanism because no avowal occurs.


VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection fails because it attacks a strawman version of the agentialist commitment. The thesis specifically targets cases of *standing self-ignorance*—where subjects have both a standing attitude and a (mistaken) view about that attitude. The proves-too-much charge would only succeed if the thesis claimed Moran must deny all unconsidered beliefs, but that's not the claim. The thesis is about the impossibility of being wrong about what you believe when you've actually formed a view about it, not about the impossibility of having beliefs you've never considered.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider a subject undergoing successful psychotherapy who sincerely avows "I don't believe my mother abandoned me" while displaying all the behavioral and emotional signatures of someone who does believe exactly that—avoidance of attachment, separation anxiety, etc. At session's end, after therapeutic work, they come to avow "I do believe my mother abandoned me" and report this as discovering something they believed all along, not as changing their mind. This is a counterexample because: (1) The subject has a standing belief (mother abandoned me) throughout; (2) They are genuinely self-ignorant about it initially—their avowal conflicts with their standing attitude; (3) Yet this is not only possible but therapeutically commonplace and seems to be the *point* of therapy. The weak premise is that implicit attitudes research is needed to establish the actuality of standing self-ignorance. We don't need experimental psychology—clinical practice already reveals that people routinely have beliefs they sincerely disavow, and coming to accurate self-knowledge feels like discovery, not decision. If Moran's view really entails this is impossible, that's a reductio, but not one requiring implicit cognition research. The thesis overstates its case by suggesting only implicit attitudes establish the phenomenon.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This doesn't really damage the thesis because it provides a *different* example of the same phenomenon the thesis already claims is problematic for Moran, rather than showing the thesis fails. The thesis already argues standing self-ignorance is actual and incompatible with agentialism; the therapy case just adds another instance.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection misunderstands its own dialectical role. It presents the therapy case as a "counterexample" to the thesis, but actually provides *additional evidence for it*. The thesis already claims that standing self-ignorance is actual and that this actuality refutes Moran's agentialism. The therapy case doesn't counter this—it reinforces it with a different example.

Moreover, the therapy case actually strengthens the thesis in a crucial way. The objection claims the thesis "overstates its case by suggesting only implicit attitudes establish the phenomenon," but this gets the argumentative structure backwards. The thesis doesn't claim implicit attitudes are the *only* evidence for standing self-ignorance—it uses them as particularly *compelling* evidence because they're experimentally robust and harder for the agentialist to dismiss.

Here's why the implicit attitudes case is dialectically stronger than the therapy case: The agentialist might respond to therapy cases by invoking familiar interpretive flexibility. They could say: "The patient's initial avowal 'I don't believe my mother abandoned me' was true at the time—they really didn't believe it then. Through therapy, they came to believe it. What feels like discovery is actually the recognition that they *should have* believed it all along given their evidence (their emotional patterns), not that they *did* believe it." This is a motivated response, but not obviously incoherent.

The implicit attitudes case is harder to deflect this way because the experimental measures are concurrent and systematic. When someone demonstrates implicit racial bias while sincerely avowing egalitarian beliefs, both the implicit measure and the avowal are taken at the same time under controlled conditions. The agentialist cannot easily claim "they came to believe it through the experimental process" because the experimental design specifically aims to capture standing attitudes, not to induce new ones.

So the thesis doesn't overstate its case—it makes a strategic choice to use the strongest available evidence. The therapy case is compatible with the thesis but doesn't replace its argumentative force.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection fails because it misidentifies what would count as a counterexample. A counterexample would be something showing the thesis is *false*—that either (a) standing self-ignorance isn't actually problematic for Moran, or (b) implicit attitudes don't demonstrate standing self-ignorance, or (c) Moran isn't actually committed to eliminativism about it. Instead, the objection provides an alternative example of the very phenomenon the thesis says is problematic. This is like objecting to "Descartes's dualism cannot explain brain injuries affecting consciousness" by saying "but it also cannot explain psychoactive drugs affecting consciousness"—true, but that's another instance of the same problem, not a counterexample. The complaint about "overstating" is also misplaced: using the strongest available evidence for a phenomenon isn't overstating; it's good argumentative practice.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a deep commitment of Moran's agentialism, but it actually just restates what's already explicit and uncontested in the debate about deliberative self-knowledge. The claim that "if avowing is deciding, then Moore-paradoxical states should be impossible" is not a discovered *consequence* of agentialism—it's virtually a restatement of the view's *starting point*. Moran himself acknowledges that his account makes first-person authority constitutive rather than empirical, which already entails that genuine self-ignorance (as opposed to mere inattention) should be impossible on his view. The thesis then points to implicit attitudes as showing such self-ignorance is actual—but this is just the standard empiricist objection that's been leveled at constitutive accounts since their inception. Every participant in this debate already knows that constitutive accounts of self-knowledge face tension with psychological evidence of self-opacity. The weak point is the framing as "committed to eliminativism" as if this is a surprising entailment being exposed. But eliminativism about standing self-ignorance just *is* what constitutive agentialism amounts to—it's not a hidden cost but the advertised price. The thesis is essentially: "If you think self-knowledge is constitutive, you must deny cases where it fails." This is true but trivial—like saying "if you're a phenomenalist about physical objects, you're committed to eliminativism about unperceived mind-independent matter." Yes, that's what the view *is*.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis presents itself as exposing a problematic *commitment* of Moran's view, but if that commitment is just what the view explicitly *is*, then the thesis hasn't advanced the dialectic. It's essentially saying "agentialism is incompatible with standing self-ignorance" when agentialism was *designed* to reject standing self-ignorance as ordinarily conceived. The implicit attitudes research might be empirically compelling, but pointing out that a constitutive theory conflicts with empirical findings is the most standard move in philosophy—not a novel discovery about what the theory is committed to.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses what makes this a substantive commitment-claim rather than a trivial restatement. Yes, Moran explicitly endorses a constitutive account of self-knowledge, and yes, he acknowledges this gives first-person authority a special status. But there's a crucial gap between these explicit commitments and the eliminativist conclusion about standing self-ignorance.

Here's the gap: Moran could—and arguably does—maintain that the deliberative stance gives us *authoritative access* to our beliefs without thereby claiming that we *always exercise* that access or that standing attitudes we haven't yet deliberatively examined don't exist. The constitutive claim is about the *method* of self-knowledge when we engage in it, not about the impossibility of having attitudes we haven't yet brought under deliberative scrutiny.

The thesis exposes something genuinely non-trivial: that Moran cannot coherently maintain both (a) the deliberative stance is constitutive of self-knowledge AND (b) we can have standing beliefs that survive deliberative examination intact as discoveries rather than decisions. The Moore-paradoxical cases show this because they involve subjects who *have* taken the deliberative stance (they've avowed), yet their avowal conflicts with their standing dispositional profile.

This is not the standard empiricist objection that "constitutive accounts face tension with self-opacity." The standard objection is: "Sometimes we don't know what we believe." The thesis makes a sharper claim: "When we *do* form a view about what we believe through deliberation, Moran's account entails this view cannot be mistaken—it constitutes the belief. But implicit attitudes show it *can* be mistaken." 

The difference is between saying "the deliberative stance isn't always available" (which Moran can accept—sometimes we're inattentive, distracted, etc.) and saying "the deliberative stance, when exercised, can yield false results" (which genuinely contradicts the constitutive claim). The therapy and implicit bias cases aren't just instances of not having deliberated yet—they're cases where subjects *have* deliberated, formed a view, and gotten it wrong.

So the thesis isn't trivially restating "constitutive views deny self-ignorance." It's showing that Moran's specific version cannot accommodate a particular structure: sincere avowal formed through deliberation + conflicting standing attitude = false self-knowledge. This structure is incompatible with the constitutive claim in a way that goes beyond the obvious point that constitutive theories face empirical challenges.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though the issue is closer than the objection suggests.

The defence attempts to find daylight between "the deliberative stance is constitutive" and "eliminativism about standing self-ignorance," but this daylight collapses under pressure. When Moran says avowing is deciding what to believe, he's not just making a claim about method—he's making a claim about metaphysics. The avowal doesn't merely *reveal* the belief with special authority; it *settles* what one believes. This is precisely what makes it constitutive rather than empirical.

The defence tries to distinguish "not having deliberated yet" from "having deliberated and gotten it wrong," claiming Moran can accept the first but not the second. But this distinction doesn't save the thesis from triviality. Of course a constitutive account denies you can deliberate and get it wrong—that's what "constitutive" means. The thesis is just spelling out this consequence with particular examples (implicit attitudes, therapy cases).

The objection is right that this is "the advertised price" of constitutive agentialism. Moran is fully aware that his view makes genuine false self-belief (as opposed to mere lack of self-belief) impossible. He embraces this consequence. The thesis presents this as exposing a problematic commitment, but it's really just describing what the view is and noting it conflicts with certain empirical findings.

The thesis would be substantive if it showed that Moran is committed to something he *doesn't* realize or *wouldn't* accept. But eliminativism about standing self-ignorance (properly understood as the impossibility of false self-belief after deliberation) is exactly what constitutive agentialism entails by design. Pointing out that empirical psychology suggests otherwise is a legitimate objection to the view, but not a revelation of a hidden commitment.

The thesis is true but trivial.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "standing self-ignorance" between two readings, and the argument needs both to work. 

On one reading, "standing self-ignorance" means *having a belief while lacking any self-belief about it*—you believe P but have never formed a view about whether you believe P. On this reading, the claim that Moran is "committed to eliminativism about standing self-ignorance" is plausible: if avowing constitutes believing, then unconsidered attitudes might not count as full beliefs until brought under deliberative scrutiny. This is a defensible (if revisionist) position.

On the other reading, "standing self-ignorance" means *having a belief while holding a false self-belief about it*—you believe P but believe-about-yourself that you believe not-P. On this reading, the claim is interesting: it would be genuinely problematic if Moran must deny that subjects in implicit bias studies or therapy have beliefs that conflict with their avowals.

But the argument cannot run on either reading alone. The plausible reading (never-considered attitudes) doesn't make Moran's position problematic—it's just revisionist about what counts as belief. The interesting reading (false self-belief) isn't actually established by the implicit attitudes case without already assuming those implicit dispositions are beliefs in the same sense as avowed ones, which is precisely what's at issue.

The sketch slides between these: it uses "stable dispositions" (first reading) to establish actuality, but needs "genuine ignorance of standing attitudes while having them" (second reading) to make the eliminativism problematic. The weak premise is that implicit attitudes demonstrate standing self-ignorance in the sense Moran must problematically deny.

STRENGTH: 3/5

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis does appear to shift between "having attitudes one hasn't considered" and "having false beliefs about one's attitudes," and these raise different challenges for Moran. However, the thesis could potentially be reformulated to focus consistently on the false-self-belief reading using the Moore-paradoxical structure explicitly, which would blunt this attack.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection claims the thesis equivocates between two readings of "standing self-ignorance," but actually the thesis consistently targets a *third* structure that the objection overlooks: the Moore-paradoxical case where avowal and standing attitude are both present and conflicting.

Let's be precise about what the thesis claims. It's not about "never-considered attitudes" (the objection's first reading). The thesis explicitly invokes "Moore-paradoxical states"—these are by definition cases where someone has formed a self-belief, so there's no "lacking any self-belief about it." The subject in implicit bias research doesn't merely lack a view about their racial attitudes; they have an explicit egalitarian self-conception. The therapy patient doesn't merely fail to have considered whether they believe mother-abandoned-them; they actively avow the opposite.

Nor is the thesis simply about "false self-belief" in the abstract (the objection's second reading). The specific structure is: 

(1) Subject has standing dispositional attitude D (behaves as-if believing P)
(2) Subject deliberatively avows A (explicitly judges they believe not-P)  
(3) D and A conflict
(4) D persists stably—it's not mere noise or fleeting disposition

This is standing self-ignorance in the relevant sense: you have a standing attitude (standing = stable, dispositional) and you are ignorant of it (ignorant = your deliberative self-conception misrepresents it).

The objection claims this "isn't actually established by the implicit attitudes case without already assuming those implicit dispositions are beliefs in the same sense as avowed ones." But this is exactly backwards. The thesis doesn't need to *assume* implicit attitudes are beliefs—it argues that denying they're beliefs is "implausibly restrictive." Here's why:
