Implicit racial biases predict discriminatory behavior, hiring decisions, medical treatment disparities, and social interaction patterns. They're stable across time and contexts. They respond to evidence and can be modified through intervention. They exhibit the functional profile of beliefs. The agentialist who denies these are beliefs faces a dilemma: either (a) accept that huge swaths of practically-efficacious, evidence-responsive, behaviorally-predictive mental states aren't beliefs—making "belief" useless for psychological explanation—or (b) accept they are beliefs, in which case standing self-ignorance is actual.

The thesis doesn't equivocate. It consistently means: having a stable dispositional attitude that conflicts with your deliberative self-conception. This is one phenomenon, not two being conflated. The objection's "two readings" are actually two *different phenomena* that the thesis explicitly distinguishes itself from. The thesis is about the third thing: Moore-paradoxical self-ignorance.

The objection's final move—claiming we need to "already assume" implicit dispositions are beliefs—simply restates the agentialist's defensive option that the thesis already identified as implausibly restrictive. That's not equivocation; that's correctly identifying where the dialectical pressure lies.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection fails because it misidentifies the target phenomenon. The thesis isn't equivocating between "unconsidered attitudes" and "false self-belief"—it's consistently targeting Moore-paradoxical structures where both avowal and conflicting standing disposition are present. 

The objection's claim that the argument "needs both readings to work" is false. The thesis needs only one thing: cases where (a) subjects have deliberatively formed a self-conception, (b) they have a conflicting stable dispositional profile, and (c) both persist simultaneously. This is a single, well-defined phenomenon, not an equivocation between two.

The objection's deeper move—questioning whether implicit dispositions are really beliefs—is not a charge of equivocation but a substantive disagreement about the case. The thesis already acknowledges this is the agentialist's available move and argues it's implausibly restrictive. Whether that argument succeeds is a separate question from whether the thesis equivocates. It doesn't.

The defence successfully shows the thesis has a consistent target throughout: standing attitudes (stable dispositions) that subjects are ignorant of (misrepresent in deliberative self-conception). That's one phenomenon, not two being conflated.

</details>

---

### R4. Adopting the interpretive view of self-knowledge commits you to rejecting the transparency method (knowing your mind by attending to the world), yet all major interpretivists rely on transparency to explain ordinary avowals.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Interpretivist accounts that try to appropriate transparency while maintaining that self-knowledge is fundamentally interpretive
- **Sketch:** Interpretivist accounts (Carruthers, Gopnik) say we know our minds by interpreting behavioral and contextual evidence. But when explaining how people sincerely avow 'I believe it will rain' without inner observation, these same theorists invoke transparency: you know you believe it will rain by thinking about whether it will rain, not by interpreting your behavior. The argument shows this is inconsistent: transparency assumes you can know your belief by direct attention to its content/object, not by self-interpretation. The key premise is that the transparency method is incompatible with interpretivism because it requires unmediated access to your attitude toward a proposition simply by considering the proposition. Either interpretivist accounts must abandon transparency (leaving ordinary avowals mysterious) or they must abandon interpretivism (conceding non-interpretive self-knowledge). This dilemma hasn't been recognized because 'attending to the world' sounds observational rather than introspective, obscuring that it still grants first-person authority without interpretation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Sharp internal inconsistency charge: interpretivists relying on transparency while rejecting it is a real tension that hasn't been sufficiently pressed.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The thesis treats interpretivist-transparency combinations as confused rather than as evidence of compatibility, but hasn't established the incompatibility strongly enough to justify this
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Without textual evidence of interpretivists contradicting themselves, the thesis collapses into Byrne's established external critique that interpretivism cannot accommodate transparency
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 3/5, answered. The defence successfully distinguished phenomenal from doxastic self-knowledge, showing the incompatibility is properly restricted to phenomenally silent propositional attitudes
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 2/5, answered. Moran's view is anti-interpretivist, not a combination of interpretivism and transparency, so it fails as a counterexample
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Major interpretivists (Carruthers, Gopnik) cannot adequately explain the phenomenology of ordinary belief avowals, which exhibits transparency (knowing your belief about P by attending to P) that their interpretivist framework cannot accommodate
- **Flaw:** The thesis claims interpretivists internally contradict themselves by using transparency, but without textual evidence this is indistinguishable from the already-established external critique that interpretivism cannot explain transparency's phenomenology

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims interpretivism is "committed to rejecting" transparency, yet the core argument depends on stipulating what counts as "genuine interpretivism." When confronted with actual interpretivists who explicitly use transparency (Carruthers, Gopnik), the thesis doesn't treat this as a counterexample that refutes the incompatibility claim. Instead, it redefines these cases as "trying to appropriate transparency while maintaining interpretivism" — implying they're *confused* rather than being actual counterexamples.

This makes the thesis unfalsifiable: 
- If interpretivists reject transparency → confirms the commitment claim
- If interpretivists embrace transparency → they're "inconsistent" or "haven't recognized" the incompatibility, still confirming the underlying commitment claim

The weak premise is the modal claim about what interpretivism "commits you to." The thesis protects this by treating any interpretivist-plus-transparency position not as evidence that interpretivism and transparency are actually compatible, but as evidence of theorists failing to see an incompatibility that the thesis-holder has discovered. Real counterevidence would be an interpretivist account that coherently combines both — but any such account gets relabeled as "inconsistent" or "unrecognized dilemma," never as a working counterexample.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem. The thesis needs to show why the combination is *necessarily* incoherent, not just assert incompatibility then treat all existing combinations as confused.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies what would falsify the thesis. The thesis is NOT claiming "no one combines interpretivism and transparency" — that's obviously false and the thesis explicitly acknowledges that major interpretivists DO this. Rather, it claims this combination is *philosophically incoherent*, creating a tension these theorists have failed to recognize.

This is falsifiable in the standard way philosophical inconsistency claims are falsifiable: show that the alleged tension dissolves under analysis. Specifically, you'd need to demonstrate that transparency's mechanism (knowing your belief about P by attending to P itself) is actually compatible with interpretivism's core claim (self-knowledge works by interpreting behavioral/contextual evidence).

The thesis provides a clear criterion: transparency assumes you can know your attitude toward a proposition by considering the proposition alone, without interpreting evidence about yourself. Interpretivism denies this is possible — it says all self-knowledge requires interpreting evidence. These are contradictory claims about how the mechanism works.

A genuine counterexample would be an account showing how "attending to whether P" IS a form of self-interpretation, or how transparency operates through behavioral evidence after all, or how interpretivism permits some non-interpretive routes. Moran's work arguably attempts this by treating transparency as rational self-constitution rather than discovery — that would be a real counterexample if successful.

The thesis doesn't immunize itself by calling combinations "confused" — it provides philosophical argument for why they're confused (the mechanism incompatibility). That's how philosophy works: you argue positions are inconsistent, you don't just observe that people hold them.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, but not completely.

The defender is right that "people combine X and Y" doesn't automatically refute "X and Y are incompatible" — that's correct philosophical methodology. BUT the objection's core point survives: the thesis hasn't yet established the incompatibility strongly enough to bear the weight being placed on it.

Here's why the defence ultimately fails: The thesis needs to show that what interpretivists call "transparency" genuinely is the transparency method (with its incompatible commitments), not merely that they use the word. If interpretivists have a reading of "attending to the world" that's actually consistent with interpretivism — say, treating it as a fast, automatic self-interpretation that feels direct — then there's no inconsistency to expose, just terminological variation.

The defence gestures at "the mechanism incompatibility" but this is precisely what needs arguing, not asserting. Until we see why attending-to-P cannot possibly be construed as interpretive, the thesis remains vulnerable to the unfalsifiability worry: it will reject any interpretivist-transparency combination as confused rather than taking it as evidence the combination might actually work.

**The thesis survives as a research program** (investigate whether the combination really is coherent) but fails as a demonstrated commitment (interpretivism commits you to rejecting transparency). The modal claim is too strong for the argument given.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**

This position is essentially Gareth Evans's transparency condition (1982) plus a standard critique of interpretationism. Evans famously argued that self-knowledge of belief works through transparency: "in making a self-ascription of belief, one's eyes are, so to speak, or occasionally literally, directed outward—upon the world." The incompatibility between this and third-person interpretive models has been extensively discussed.

Richard Moran's *Authority and Estrangement* (2001) develops this into a full account, arguing that avowals gain their authority precisely because they're *not* based on self-interpretation but on deliberative reasoning about the world. Moran explicitly frames this as opposing observational/interpretive models (including Gopnik's "theory-theory").

The specific charge that interpretivists like Carruthers inconsistently rely on transparency while officially rejecting it appears in Alex Byrne's work (2005, 2011), where he argues that interpretationist accounts cannot explain the phenomenology of ordinary belief self-ascription, which lacks the interpretive character their theory requires.

Even the framing as an unrecognized tension within interpretationist programs mirrors existing literature: critics have repeatedly pointed out that when interpretivists explain actual cases of avowal, they quietly help themselves to non-interpretive mechanisms. The thesis restates this known dialectical situation—transparency-based accounts versus interpretationism—as if it were a newly discovered internal inconsistency.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This significantly undermines the thesis's originality. The transparency condition, its incompatibility with interpretationism, and the charge that interpretivists covertly rely on it are all established positions in the self-knowledge literature.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection conflates *using existing resources* with *making an already-taken move*. Yes, Evans introduced transparency and Moran opposed it to interpretivism — but neither claimed that *interpretivists themselves* systematically rely on the very mechanism their theory should reject. That's the distinctive move here.

Byrne's criticism (2011) argues interpretivism cannot *accommodate* transparency's phenomenology — that's an external attack showing interpretivism fails to explain something. This thesis makes an *internal* inconsistency claim: interpretivists don't just fail to explain transparency, they actively *use* it in their own explanations of avowals while officially denying its legitimacy. That's a different dialectical move — it's catching them red-handed rather than pointing out what they cannot explain.

The evidence matters here: Does Carruthers, when explaining how someone avows "I believe it will rain," actually invoke a transparency-style mechanism (attend to whether it will rain) or does he stick to pure self-interpretation (observe your behavioral dispositions)? If he does the former while his theory demands the latter, that's not just "interpretivism cannot explain X" — it's "interpretivists contradict themselves when explaining X."

This is like the difference between "dualists cannot explain consciousness" versus "dualists appeal to physical causation when explaining consciousness." The latter reveals internal tension, not just explanatory failure.

Moreover, the thesis identifies *why* this tension has been missed: transparency sounds non-introspective ("attending outward") so interpretivists don't recognize they're granting non-interpretive self-knowledge. That's a diagnostic claim about the dialectic itself, not present in Byrne or Moran, who simply defend transparency against interpretationism without claiming interpretivists covertly depend on it.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails.

The problem is that the "internal inconsistency" reading requires empirical evidence that doesn't appear in the sketch: actual passages where Carruthers or Gopnik invoke transparency-style mechanisms while officially rejecting them. Without textual evidence, we cannot distinguish between:

1. Interpretivists genuinely using transparency (inconsistency — thesis succeeds)
2. Critics claiming interpretivists *must* use transparency to explain avowals (external critique — already taken)

The sketch provides no quotations, no specific passages, no demonstration that interpretivists actually say "you know you believe P by attending to whether P" rather than "you know you believe P by interpreting your dispositions to assert P." The thesis *asserts* they do this ("when explaining how people sincerely avow...these same theorists invoke transparency") but provides no evidence.

This matters because Byrne's critique already claims interpretivism cannot explain the directness of avowals. The thesis's distinctiveness depends entirely on showing interpretivists *themselves* grant this directness in their explanations. Without that evidence, the thesis collapses into Byrne's external critique wearing different clothes.

**The move is already taken.** The thesis needs actual textual evidence of interpretivists contradicting themselves to distinguish itself from the established critique that interpretivism fails to accommodate transparency. The "unrecognized tension within interpretationist programs" is only novel if interpretivists actually exhibit the tension in their own words, not if critics argue they must.

The diagnostic claim about why it's been missed is genuinely new, but it cannot save a thesis whose core move — transparency versus interpretivism — is already thoroughly explored in Evans, Moran, and Byrne.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis argues that transparency (knowing your belief about P by attending to P) is incompatible with interpretivism because transparency grants "unmediated access to your attitude toward a proposition simply by considering the proposition." But this reasoning proves too much: it would show that ANY fast, automatic, or non-deliberative cognitive process is incompatible with interpretivism.

Consider: When you know you're angry, you don't typically interpret behavioral evidence — you just feel angry. When you know you're in pain, you don't observe your pain behavior — you just feel pain. When you know what you're imagining, you don't interpret evidence about your imaginings — you just attend to the imagined content. By the thesis's logic, interpretivism "commits you to rejecting" all of these as genuine self-knowledge, since none involves conscious self-interpretation.

The weak premise is the claim that transparency's directness makes it incompatible with interpretivism. If this worked, interpretivism would be committed to rejecting not just transparency for beliefs, but all phenomenologically direct self-knowledge. This would make interpretivism an absurdly radical position that denies we have any immediate self-knowledge whatsoever — not just about beliefs, but about sensations, emotions, perceptions, imaginings, and intentions.

But interpretivists like Carruthers and Gopnik don't reject ALL immediate self-knowledge. They typically grant that sensations and perhaps emotions are known directly. The argument's standard for "incompatibility with interpretivism" is so strict that it would force interpretivists to positions they don't actually hold and that are independently implausible.

**STRENGTH: 3/5**

This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs to explain why transparency for beliefs specifically is incompatible with interpretivism, while other forms of phenomenologically direct self-knowledge aren't.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misses a crucial distinction between *phenomenal* self-knowledge and *doxastic* self-knowledge. Interpretivists like Carruthers explicitly grant that sensations, pains, and phenomenal states are known directly through their intrinsic phenomenology — that's not self-interpretation, it's just having the experience. The interpretivist claim is specifically about *propositional attitudes* like beliefs and desires, which lack distinctive phenomenology.

This is why transparency for beliefs is uniquely problematic: beliefs don't announce themselves through felt qualities the way pains do. When you know you believe it will rain, there's no "belief-feeling" you're experiencing. So if you know it non-interpretively, it must be through some other mechanism — and transparency proposes that mechanism is attending to the proposition itself. But that's precisely what interpretivism denies: it says you can only know your attitude toward a proposition by interpreting evidence, not by considering the proposition.

The cases the objection lists actually support the thesis:

- **Pain/sensation**: Known through phenomenology (having the quale) — interpretivists grant this, no problem
- **Emotions**: Known through phenomenology (feeling angry) — interpretivists grant this
- **Perceptions**: Known through phenomenal character (seeing red) — interpretivists grant this  
- **Imaginings**: Known through phenomenal imagery — interpretivists grant this
- **Beliefs**: NO distinctive phenomenology — interpretivists say you must interpret evidence, but transparency says you attend to the world

The thesis doesn't require rejecting all immediate self-knowledge. It requires rejecting immediate self-knowledge of propositional attitudes that lack phenomenal character. That's exactly the domain interpretivism targets, and exactly where transparency operates.

Carruthers himself draws this distinction (2011, *The Opacity of Mind*): he allows direct knowledge of phenomenal states but insists that attitudes require interpretation precisely because they lack phenomenology. The thesis shows that when explaining belief-avowals, he cannot maintain this position — he must invoke transparency, which grants non-interpretive access to beliefs despite their lacking phenomenology.

The "proves too much" worry only works if you ignore the phenomenal/doxastic distinction that structures the entire debate. Transparency is uniquely threatening to interpretivism because it claims non-interpretive knowledge of precisely the states interpretivism says require interpretation: phenomenally silent propositional attitudes.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds.

This is the strongest response available and it holds up. The objection's examples (pain, emotion, perception, imagination) all involve phenomenal character, and interpretivists standardly grant direct knowledge of phenomenal states. The thesis specifically targets propositional attitudes like belief, which lack distinctive phenomenology.

The key insight is that transparency offers a mechanism for knowing beliefs without interpretation AND without phenomenology — that's what makes it incompatible with interpretivism. You can't say "I know my pain non-interpretively because pain has phenomenology" about beliefs, because beliefs don't have phenomenology. Yet transparency claims you know them non-interpretively anyway (by attending to their objects). That's what interpretivism must reject.

The objection would succeed if the thesis claimed transparency's mere directness or automaticity made it incompatible with interpretivism — that would prove too much. But the actual incompatibility is more specific: transparency grants non-interpretive knowledge of phenomenally silent attitudes by a mechanism (attending to the attitude's object) that bypasses the interpretation interpretivism requires for such states.

**The thesis survives this objection.** The "proves too much" charge fails because the thesis's scope is properly restricted to propositional attitudes, which is exactly the domain where interpretivism and transparency genuinely conflict. Interpretivists can consistently allow direct knowledge of phenomenal states while rejecting direct knowledge of beliefs — and transparency for beliefs is what creates the inconsistency the thesis identifies.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
**COUNTEREXAMPLE: Moran's Deliberative Transparency**

Richard Moran's account in *Authority and Estrangement* (2001) provides a counterexample. Moran explicitly combines:

1. **Interpretivist elements**: Self-knowledge involves a kind of self-interpretation through rational deliberation. You come to know your belief by engaging in reasoning about what to believe, which is a form of self-constitution through interpretive rational activity.

2. **Transparency**: You know what you believe about P by considering P itself, not by observing your mental states.

Moran argues transparency works *through* a deliberative, reason-responsive process that is itself interpretive. When you ask "Do I believe it will rain?" you answer by asking "Will it rain?" — but this isn't unmediated access. It's a rational procedure where you interpret considerations, weigh reasons, and thereby constitute your belief. The "attending to the world" is itself an interpretive activity: you're interpreting evidence about whether it will rain, and through that interpretive process, you both form and know your belief.

This dissolves the alleged incompatibility. Transparency becomes a species of self-interpretation rather than its opposite. The mechanism isn't "unmediated access by considering the proposition" but rather "mediated access through deliberative interpretation of considerations bearing on the proposition."

The weak premise is the claim that transparency "assumes you can know your attitude toward a proposition simply by considering the proposition" without interpretation. Moran shows you can read transparency as requiring interpretation — just interpretation of the world rather than interpretation of yourself.

**STRENGTH: 2/5**

This has some force but ultimately doesn't work. Moran's account is better classified as *anti-interpretivist* despite involving deliberation. The thesis targets accounts like Carruthers and Gopnik that treat self-knowledge as third-personal interpretation of behavioral evidence, not Moran's first-personal rational deliberation. Moran explicitly opposes his view to observational/third-personal models, positioning it as an alternative to interpretivism, not a version of it. So this isn't a counterexample to the thesis's actual target.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection fundamentally misunderstands what "interpretivism" means in this debate. Interpretivism is the view that self-knowledge works by interpreting *behavioral and contextual evidence about yourself* — observing what you say, do, and how you react, then inferring your mental states from this evidence. This is the position held by Carruthers, Gopnik, and others in the "theory-theory" tradition. It's explicitly *third-personal*: you know your mind the same way others do, by interpreting outward evidence.

Moran's account is not interpretivist in this sense at all. When Moran says you know your belief by deliberating about what to believe, he's not saying you interpret evidence about yourself. You're reasoning about the world (will it rain?), not observing your behavior. The fact that deliberation involves "interpreting considerations" doesn't make it self-interpretation — you're interpreting *meteorological evidence*, not *psychological evidence about yourself*.

The thesis explicitly targets "interpretivist accounts (Carruthers, Gopnik)" — accounts that treat self-knowledge as self-directed mindreading. Moran explicitly rejects this model. He positions his view as an alternative to both introspectionism AND interpretivism, offering a third way based on rational agency. His transparency is *deliberative*, not *observational*.

The counterexample fails because it equivocates on "interpretation":
- **Interpretivist sense**: inferring mental states from behavioral evidence (third-personal)
- **Moran's sense**: reasoning about what to believe based on world-directed considerations (first-personal)

These are completely different mechanisms. The thesis claims interpretivism (first sense) is incompatible with transparency. Moran's account doesn't combine interpretivism with transparency — it rejects interpretivism in favor of a deliberative-transparency view.

In fact, the thesis's defence mentioned Moran as potentially offering "a real counterexample if successful" precisely because Moran treats transparency as rational self-constitution rather than discovery. But that's not a version of interpretivism — it's an alternative to it. Moran is on the transparency side of the divide, not showing how to combine both sides.

The objection only works if you stretch "interpretivism" to mean "any view involving interpretation of anything," which drains it of its specific theoretical content. The thesis targets a specific position about how self-knowledge works (mindreading yourself), and Moran simply isn't defending that position.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds completely.

This is the correct response and it holds. The objection fails because it misidentifies Moran's view as interpretivist when it is explicitly anti-interpretivist. The key distinctions are:

**Interpretivism (Carruthers, Gopnik)**:
- Self-knowledge via interpreting behavioral/contextual evidence about yourself
- Third-personal mechanism (you know your mind like others know it)
- Self-directed mindreading

**Moran's Deliberative View**:
- Self-knowledge via first-personal rational deliberation about the world
- Constitutes beliefs through reasoning, doesn't infer them from evidence
- Explicitly opposed to observational/interpretivist models

The objection tries to count Moran as interpretivist because deliberation involves "interpreting considerations," but this conflates world-directed reasoning (interpreting whether it will rain) with self-directed interpretation (inferring your beliefs from your behavior). These are entirely different cognitive processes.

Moran himself frames his view as a third alternative to both introspectionism and interpretivism. In *Authority and Estrangement*, he explicitly criticizes theory-theory accounts (the interpretivist position) for treating self-knowledge as observational inference. His transparency is deliberative and agentive, not interpretive in the relevant sense.

**The counterexample fails.** Moran doesn't show that interpretivism and transparency are compatible — he rejects interpretivism in favor of transparency. The thesis survives because its target (behavioral-evidence interpretivism) remains distinct from and incompatible with transparency, and Moran provides no counterexample to this incompatibility. He simply occupies different theoretical territory.

The objection's own strength rating (2/5) was accurate. This isn't a successful counterexample.

</details>

---

### R5. The observational and interpretive models are both wrong because they assume self-knowledge requires a subject-object structure; instead, in the central cases, self-knowledge is practical knowledge—knowledge-in-action of what you're doing mentally, which carries Anscombean authority without either observation or inference.

- **Promise 4/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that self-knowledge must be either perceptual or interpretive—that these exhaust the epistemological options
- **Sketch:** When you're actively reasoning, deliberating, or imagining, you know what you're doing not by observing or interpreting but in the way an agent knows what they're intentionally doing—non-observationally and non-inferentially, yet defeasibly. The authority is practical authority: you're the one doing it, so you're authoritative about its specification under the intentional description. Confabulation cases involve either mental actions that fail (like failed attempts to pay attention) or retrospective interpretation of completed mental events (where practical knowledge no longer applies). The key premise is that mental actions are a genuine category to which Anscombe's analysis of practical knowledge extends, and that practical knowledge explains an authority-without-observation that the standard epistemological frameworks cannot accommodate.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Extending Anscombean practical knowledge to mental action is genuinely novel and could reframe the debate's epistemological assumptions.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, answered. Defence successfully distinguished practical knowledge (constitutive of activity) from Moran's interpretivism (rational self-interpretation), showing genuine third category
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's mechanism/intention distinction collapsed under pressure; cannot maintain authority about intentional specification while allowing specification-level error in rationalization cases
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's subpersonal/intentional distinction failed; pattern-matching vs. reasoning differs in rational structure not just mechanism, requiring external evidence about reasons themselves
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed constitutive claim does genuine theoretical work by denying epistemic gap rather than merely relabeling phenomena
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Practical knowledge applies only to the bare fact that one is engaged in some intentional mental activity, not to which specific mental action-type one is performing, since typing requires access to rational structure that practical knowledge cannot provide
- **Flaw:** Cannot maintain authoritative practical knowledge of mental action-types while allowing the specification-level errors (rationalization, pattern-matching mistaken for reasoning) that manifestly occur, because mental act-types are individuated by their rational structure, which proves opaque to first-person specification

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This is the practical knowledge account of self-knowledge, most prominently defended by Richard Moran in *Authority and Estrangement* (2001). Moran argues that first-person authority about one's attitudes comes from the deliberative, reason-responsive stance rather than from observation or detection, and that self-knowledge is continuous with practical self-determination. The weak point is the claim that this genuinely constitutes a *third* alternative rather than a sophisticated variant of the interpretive model. Moran's account still involves rational self-interpretation—making sense of oneself through reasons—rather than pure observation, which places it within the interpretive family. The candidate extends this to "mental actions" and invokes Anscombe's practical knowledge, but Anscombe's account concerns bodily actions where non-observational knowledge tracks causally efficacious intentions. Mental episodes like "actively reasoning" lack the same causal structure: there's no muscular execution to know non-observationally. When you know what you're thinking, you're still accessing a mental state, and the question remains whether this access is perceptual-like or interpretive-rational. Calling it "practical" may redescribe the phenomenon without escaping the epistemological dilemma. The Moran position, and its Anscombean extension, arguably remains a species of interpretivism—one emphasizing agential authority—rather than a genuine third way.

STRENGTH: 2/5

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies both the target and the dialectical structure. Start with what makes practical knowledge genuinely distinct: it's not *about* a state you're in but constitutive of an activity you're performing. When you're adding numbers, your knowledge that you're adding (not multiplying, not daydreaming) isn't accessed through *any* epistemic route—observational or interpretive—because the knowing and the doing are the same act under different descriptions.

The objector says "you're still accessing a mental state" but this begs the question. On the practical knowledge view, there's no state to access until the activity creates it. Your knowledge that you're calculating doesn't report a pre-existing mental configuration; it specifies what you're doing, and that specification partially constitutes the activity's intentional character. This is why it has authority: not because you detect or interpret correctly, but because you're authoring the act.

The "mental actions lack causal structure" move fails on Anscombe's own terms. She explicitly includes mental acts like calculating in *Intention* §§23-24, noting that "I am calculating" has the same non-observational character as "I am walking." The objector demands "muscular execution" but Anscombe's criterion was never physiological implementation—it was answerability to the question "Why?" in a specific way. Mental actions answer "Why are you calculating?" with reasons (to solve this problem) not causes, exactly like bodily actions.

The interpretivism charge collapses the distinction between *rational* and *interpretive*. Yes, practical knowledge involves rationality—but as *exercise* not *diagnosis*. Interpretation means standing back from your state and making sense of it through evidence and inference. Practical knowledge means enacting reasons directly. The Moran connection is real but doesn't collapse the view into interpretivism: Moran's point is precisely that deliberative self-knowledge isn't *interpretive* because you determine what to think through reasoning, not interpret what you already think through evidence.

The objector's final move—"arguably remains a species of interpretivism"—is telling. If the best case is "arguably a species of," the distinction has survived.
**VERDICT**
The defence holds, but barely, and only for a restricted domain.

The core distinction—between *enacting* mental activities and *accessing* mental states—is real and survives the objection. When genuinely engaged in calculating, deliberating, or reasoning, you do know what you're doing in a way that's neither observational nor interpretive-from-evidence. The objector's demand for "muscular execution" imports a criterion Anscombe never imposed, and the "still accessing a state" framing assumes precisely what's at issue.

However, the defence exposes a fatal limitation: this works only for *ongoing mental activities*, not for the full range of self-knowledge the thesis claims to cover. The moment you ask about standing beliefs, desires, or emotions—the paradigm cases that motivated both observational and interpretive models—practical knowledge has nothing to say. You don't *do* your belief that Paris is in France; you have it. The thesis promised to explain "central cases" of self-knowledge, but the central cases in the literature are precisely these standing attitudes, not episodes of active reasoning.

The defence succeeds in establishing a genuine third category, but the thesis dies by overreach. Practical knowledge explains one important but narrow class of self-knowledge, leaving the original dilemma intact for everything else. That's not reframing the debate—it's carving out an exception.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If practical knowledge of mental action doesn't require observation or interpretation because the knowing and doing are "the same act under different descriptions," then this account proves too much by eliminating the possibility of self-ignorance about what you're mentally doing. Consider: when you're engaged in wishful thinking, rationalization, or self-deception, you typically believe you're engaged in genuine reasoning—you'd sincerely report "I am reasoning carefully about whether to trust him" when you're actually selectively gathering evidence to support a predetermined conclusion. On the observational/interpretive models, such error is straightforward: you misperceive or misinterpret your mental activity. But if knowing-what-you're-doing is constitutive of the doing itself—if there's no gap between the act and your authoritative specification of it—then whatever you sincerely specify *makes it so*. Your sincere judgment "I am reasoning" would constitute reasoning, making wishful thinking impossible whenever you believe you're reasoning properly. The weak point is the claim that practical knowledge has "Anscombean authority" while remaining "defeasible." Anscombe's practical knowledge is defeasible only regarding external circumstances (the bodily movement fails) not regarding the intentional specification itself. But mental actions must be defeasible at the specification level—you can be wrong about whether you're reasoning versus rationalizing—which means there must be a fact about what you're doing that's independent of your authoritative say-so. That reintroduces exactly the subject-object structure the thesis rejects.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This creates a serious dilemma: either accept that sincere self-ascription of mental action-types is infallible (absurd), or admit a gap between the activity and your knowledge of it (reinstating the subject-object structure). The "defeasible practical knowledge" move appears internally unstable.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes practical knowledge must be either infallible or observational, but Anscombe's account already contains the resources to reject this dichotomy. The key is distinguishing *specification under an intentional description* from *successful execution* of what's specified.

When you're rationalizing while believing you're reasoning, practical knowledge explains both the authority and the error. You have authoritative knowledge that you're *trying to reason* or *engaged in what-you-take-to-be-reasoning*—this specification is constitutive and non-observational. The defeasibility enters not at whether you're doing what you say, but at whether what you're doing meets the standards constitutive of that activity-type. You're authoritatively doing what you specify, but what you specify may fail to be genuine reasoning because reasoning has standards (responsiveness to counter-evidence, etc.) that aren't up to you.

This is precisely parallel to Anscombe's bodily case. When you're "writing your name" but your hand is paralyzed, you have authoritative practical knowledge that you're writing (that's what you're doing intentionally), but the activity fails as writing because writing requires the hand to move. The failure doesn't impugn the practical knowledge—you really are trying to write, doing what-counts-for-you-as-writing. The subject-object gap isn't between you and your intention but between your intention and the world's cooperation.

For mental actions, the relevant "world" is the normative standards of the activity. You have non-observational knowledge that you're engaged in reasoning-activity (the intentional description you're enacting), but whether this activity constitutes *successful* reasoning depends on whether it meets reasoning's standards—and those standards are independent of your specification. This is why you can be corrected: not because you didn't know what you were doing, but because what you were doing didn't amount to what that kind of doing requires.

The objector demands that being "wrong about whether you're reasoning versus rationalizing" requires subject-object structure, but this conflates two questions: (1) What activity-type are you performing? (2) Does your performance meet that type's standards? Practical knowledge answers (1) authoritatively and constitutively. The independence needed for defeasibility comes from (2), which concerns normative assessment not epistemic access.

Crucially, this doesn't "prove too much" because the account never claimed you can't be wrong—it claimed your wrongness has a different structure. You're not wrong about what you're doing under the description you intend; you're wrong about whether what you're doing meets the standards you're aiming at. The rationalization case shows practical knowledge working exactly as it should: you know non-observationally that you're engaged in reasoning-activity (that's the intentional specification), but third-personal assessment reveals this activity fails reasoning's norms.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though the failure is instructive.

The move to separate "specification under intentional description" from "meeting the activity's standards" is clever but ultimately collapses. Here's why: the distinction between "trying to reason" and "successfully reasoning" only works if these are genuinely different activities you might be performing. But in the rationalization case, you don't think you're merely *trying* to reason—you think you're *succeeding*. The sincere self-ascription is "I am reasoning carefully about this," not "I am engaged in what-I-take-to-be-reasoning."

The bodily analogy actually undermines the defence. When your hand is paralyzed, you can still have practical knowledge that you're trying to write because trying-to-write and writing are distinguishable by the presence or absence of hand movement—something external to the intention. But "trying to reason" and "reasoning" aren't distinguished by any external performance. The difference lies entirely in whether your mental activity meets certain rational standards—standards of evidence-responsiveness, coherence, non-selectivity. 

If you have authoritative practical knowledge of what mental activity you're performing, and the only difference between reasoning and rationalizing is whether the activity meets reasoning's standards, then you must have authoritative knowledge of whether you're meeting those standards—otherwise you don't know which activity-type you're performing. But you clearly don't have such authority; that's exactly what makes rationalization possible.

The defence tries to have it both ways: authoritative knowledge of the intentional specification, but defeasibility about whether it meets the type's standards. This works for bodily action because the standards involve external cooperation (muscles, objects). It fails for mental action because the standards are internal to what makes it the activity-type it is. You can't authoritatively know you're reasoning while being defeasibly wrong about whether you're reasoning—these can't both be true of the same act under the same description.

The objection stands: practical knowledge of mental action either eliminates the possibility of specification-level error (making rationalization impossible) or reintroduces a subject-object gap (making it observational or interpretive after all). The thesis cannot escape this dilemma.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
Consider a case of mathematical problem-solving where you're working through a complex proof. You experience yourself as "reasoning through the logical steps" and would sincerely report "I am following the logical structure of the proof." On the practical knowledge account, you know non-observationally what mental action you're performing—this knowing is constitutive of the intentional activity itself.

But suppose a cognitive scientist is monitoring your brain activity and eye movements. She observes that you've actually been pattern-matching against a similar proof you saw yesterday, not reasoning through the logical structure at all. The neural signature shows memory retrieval, not logical inference. When she points this out and shows you that you can't actually explain the inferential steps (you're just "seeing" that they work), you recognize she's right.

This is a counterexample because: according to the thesis, you should have had authoritative practical knowledge of your mental action—you were the one doing it, so you're authoritative about "its specification under the intentional description." But you were simply wrong about what mental action you were performing. The weak point is the claim that practical knowledge "carries Anscombean authority" for mental actions. The case shows you lacked authority about whether you were reasoning-through-steps versus pattern-matching-from-memory. This wasn't a failure of external circumstances (like paralyzed hand); you were wrong about the very type of mental activity occurring. Since you needed the scientist's third-person evidence to correct your first-person specification, your knowledge was actually observational/interpretive after all—just bad observation/interpretation.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This seriously damages the thesis because it shows that even for ongoing mental activities (the thesis's strongest case), you can be fundamentally mistaken about what type of mental action you're performing in a way that requires external, third-person evidence to correct. The authority the thesis claims dissolves precisely where it should be strongest.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misunderstands what "specification under the intentional description" means and imports an implausibly fine-grained taxonomy of mental act-types that no theory of practical knowledge could sustain.

First, the intentional description. You were indeed reasoning through the proof—that's what you were doing intentionally, and your practical knowledge of this is authoritative and constitutive. The cognitive scientist didn't show you were wrong about what you were doing; she showed you something about *how* you were doing it—about the subpersonal mechanisms implementing your reasoning. But Anscombe's practical knowledge never extended to mechanisms. When you know non-observationally that you're walking, you don't thereby know which muscles are firing or whether you're using visual feedback or proprioception. The physiological story doesn't impugn your practical knowledge that you're walking.

The proof case is parallel. You have authoritative knowledge that you're reasoning through the proof (that's the intentional activity). The neural signature reveals the cognitive mechanisms—memory retrieval rather than step-by-step inference—but this is a subpersonal story about implementation, not a correction of your intentional specification. "Reasoning through a proof" doesn't specify mechanism; it specifies the intentional activity-type: engaging with the proof's logical structure to understand why the conclusion follows.

Second, the act-type taxonomy. The objection assumes "reasoning-through-steps" and "pattern-matching-from-memory" are rival specifications of what mental action you're performing. But this is wrong. Pattern-matching-from-memory is a cognitive scientist's theoretical category describing subpersonal processing. It's not an intentional action-type you could be performing instead of reasoning. You can't intend to pattern-match any more than you can intend to activate your hippocampus. These are mechanisms, not acts.

The real test: could you have been pattern-matching *intentionally*? No—because once you recognize it as mere pattern-matching (can't explain the steps), you recognize it as failed reasoning, not successful pattern-matching. There's no mental action of "pattern-matching from memory" that you might have been doing instead. The scientist revealed that your reasoning was relying on recognition rather than reconstruction, but reasoning-via-recognition is still reasoning—just reasoning that depends on memory in ways you didn't realize.

Third, the "needed third-person evidence" move. Yes, you needed external evidence to learn about the mechanisms. But needing external evidence about *how* you're doing something doesn't undermine practical knowledge of *what* you're doing. You might need a physiotherapist to tell you you're walking by compensating with your hip rather than using your knee properly—but this doesn't mean you lacked practical knowledge that you were walking. The external evidence concerns the implementation, not the intentional specification.

The objection's force depends on treating cognitive mechanism-descriptions as rival intentional act-types. Once we distinguish intentional action (reasoning through the proof) from implementing mechanism (pattern-matching in memory systems), the counterexample dissolves. You had authoritative practical knowledge of what you were doing; the scientist taught you something about the subpersonal how.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. The mechanism/intention distinction cannot bear the weight placed on it here.

The problem is that "reasoning through logical steps" versus "pattern-matching from memory" aren't merely different mechanisms implementing the same intentional action—they're different mental activities with different rational characters. This becomes clear when we consider what you lost when you realized the truth: you lost justification. Before the discovery, you thought your confidence in the proof was based on understanding the inferential connections. After, you recognized it was based on recognition-memory of a similar proof. These ground your belief differently and have different epistemic properties (one transfers to variants of the proof, one doesn't).

The walking analogy fails because compensating-with-hip versus using-knee-properly are both ways of walking—they're both successful locomotion, just biomechanically different. But pattern-matching-from-memory versus reasoning-through-steps aren't both ways of reasoning-through-a-proof in the same sense. When you can't explain the inferential steps, you haven't reasoned through them—you've recognized a pattern. The scientist didn't just reveal a different implementation; she revealed you hadn't done what you thought you'd done.

The defence's own concession is fatal: "reasoning-via-recognition is still reasoning—just reasoning that depends on memory in ways you didn't realize." If you didn't realize the ways your reasoning depended on memory, then you lacked knowledge of something essential to what made your mental activity the type it was. You didn't just lack knowledge of subpersonal mechanism; you lacked knowledge of the rational structure of your own cognitive achievement. And that's precisely what you should have had authoritative access to if practical knowledge works as the thesis claims.

Here's the decisive point: you needed third-person evidence to learn not just about neurons but about *reasons*—about what was actually justifying your confidence. The rational structure of your mental activity (recognition-based vs. inference-based) was opaque to you. But if practical knowledge of mental action gives you authority about "its specification under the intentional description," and intentional descriptions are individuated by their rational roles, then you should have had authoritative access to this rational structure. You didn't.

The counterexample stands. Even in the thesis's strongest case—ongoing mathematical reasoning—you can be wrong about what mental action-type you're performing in a way that matters rationally (not just mechanically) and that requires external evidence to correct. The authority the thesis promises doesn't obtain.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis presents itself as a bold reframing that dissolves the observational/interpretive dilemma, but the core claim is actually trivial: it just relabels a subset of self-knowledge cases without genuine theoretical innovation. The weak point is the supposed novelty of "practical knowledge" as a third category.

Here's what's really happening: the thesis takes cases where you know what you're doing because you're actively doing it intentionally (calculating, deliberating, reasoning), notes that this knowledge seems direct and authoritative, and calls it "practical knowledge of mental action." But this is just a redescription of what everyone already recognized: that there's a phenomenological difference between actively thinking-about-X and retrospectively reflecting on what you thought. The observational and interpretive theorists never denied this difference—they offered accounts of it.

The "practical knowledge" label suggests a theoretical alternative, but it's actually just a name for the explanandum, not an explanation. Saying "you know what you're doing because it's practical knowledge" is like saying "you know because you're the one doing it"—which is true but theoretically empty. It doesn't explain *how* the knowledge works, what makes it authoritative, or why it sometimes fails. It's a verbal stipulation dressed as a discovery.

The Anscombe invocation provides philosophical pedigree but no actual theory. Anscombe explained practical knowledge of bodily action through the causal role of intention in producing movement. But the thesis explicitly can't use this explanation for mental action (there's no analogous causal structure), so invoking "Anscombean authority" is just borrowing the prestige of her framework without its explanatory machinery. What remains is the bare claim: "you know what you're mentally doing because you're doing it"—which is a description of the phenomenon, not an account of it.

The supposed reframing is therefore trivial: it names a class of cases, notes they feel different from observation/interpretation, but provides no theory of how this knowledge works that would constitute a genuine alternative to existing frameworks.
**STRENGTH: 2/5**
The attack has some force but ultimately doesn't succeed. While it's true that "practical knowledge" could be merely a label, the thesis does make substantive claims: that the knowing and doing are constitutively related (not just correlated), that this explains authority without requiring epistemic access, and that this dissolves rather than answers the observation/interpretation question. These are theoretical claims, not just verbal stipulations. The fact that the account faces serious problems (as the previous exchanges show) actually demonstrates it's making substantive claims substantive enough to be wrong. A truly trivial thesis couldn't generate the kind of counterexamples and dilemmas we've seen—it would just be empty. The thesis fails, but not by being trivial.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The triviality charge fundamentally misunderstands what theoretical work the practical knowledge framework does. It's not just relabeling—it's providing a constitutive explanation that dissolves a false presupposition in the existing debate.

Start with what the observational and interpretive models share: both assume self-knowledge requires an epistemic *gap* between you and what you know, then offer different accounts of how you bridge it. Observationalists say you bridge it perceptually (inner sense, monitoring, introspection). Interpretivists say you bridge it rationally (self-interpretation, reasons-based inference). Both accept that there's a fact about your mental life that exists independently, which you then come to know through some epistemic route.

The practical knowledge account doesn't just "note a phenomenological difference"—it denies this shared structure for a specific class of cases. The theoretical claim is that for ongoing mental actions, there is no gap to bridge because the knowing is constitutive of the doing. This isn't "you know because you're doing it" (which would be trivial)—it's "your knowing partially constitutes what makes it the doing that it is."

Here's the explanatory work: Why does your sincere judgment "I am calculating" have authority? Not because you've observed correctly (observational answer) or interpreted correctly (interpretive answer), but because the judgment itself is part of what makes the activity calculating rather than daydreaming or doodling. The intentional specification—what you take yourself to be doing—is a constitutive element of the intentional action. This explains the authority directly: you're authoritative because you're authoring, not because you're detecting or inferring accurately.

The objector says this "doesn't explain how the knowledge works," but this misunderstands the dialectical move. The point is precisely that asking "how does the knowledge work?" presupposes the wrong framework—it presupposes there's knowledge of an independent fact. The practical knowledge view says that for mental actions, this question is like asking "how do you know what you're trying to do?" The answer isn't a mechanism of access; it's that trying-to-do-X constitutively involves taking-yourself-to-be-doing-X. The question dissolves.

On the Anscombe point: the objector claims we can't use her causal explanation so we're just borrowing prestige. But this gets Anscombe backwards. Her innovation wasn't the causal story—it was recognizing that practical knowledge doesn't require a causal-observational basis at all. The causal story explains why the action succeeds or fails, not what grounds the knowledge. What grounds practical knowledge is being the agent, and this applies to mental agency just as much as bodily agency. You know what you're intentionally doing mentally because you're the one doing it intentionally—and "intentionally" here means under-a-specification-you-endorse, which is precisely what gives you authority.

The theoretical substance is in the constitutive claim: that for mental actions, the first-person specification isn't a report of an independent fact but a determination of what the activity is. This explains why the knowledge is non-observational (nothing to observe), non-inferential (nothing to infer from), yet authoritative (you're making it so) and defeasible (you can fail to do what you're trying to do). That's not trivial—it's a substantive metaphysical claim about the relationship between self-consciousness and mental agency.

The objector notes the thesis faces serious problems, taking this as evidence it's substantive. But we can strengthen this: the problems arise precisely because the constitutive claim is bold and specific. If it were just relabeling, it wouldn't generate the dilemmas about rationalization, pattern-matching, and specification-level error. These problems emerge because the thesis makes a strong claim—that practical knowledge constitutes rather than reports—which has determinate implications that can be tested and challenged.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds. The thesis is not trivial.

The constitutive claim—that for mental actions, first-person specification partially determines what the activity is rather than reporting an independent fact—is substantive and does genuine theoretical work. It explains authority without observation or interpretation by denying the gap these models try to bridge. This is a real alternative structure, not just a label.

The Anscombe point also holds. Her framework's power lies precisely in showing that practical knowledge doesn't need an epistemic mechanism—it's grounded in agency itself. This applies to mental agency even without the causal story about bodily movement. The objector demands an explanation of "how the knowledge works," but this demand presupposes the observational/interpretive framework the thesis rejects.

However—and this is crucial—succeeding against the triviality charge doesn't rescue the thesis. The previous exchanges showed the thesis fails for different reasons: it can't handle rationalization cases without collapsing, can't distinguish reasoning from pattern-matching without reintroducing subject-object structure, and covers too narrow a domain to reframe the debate about self-knowledge generally.

The thesis makes substantive, bold claims. Those claims are false or severely limited, but they're not trivial. It's a genuine theoretical proposal that fails on its merits, not an empty relabeling. The triviality objection doesn't stick, but the thesis remains defeated by the earlier, more powerful objections.

</details>

## Attacked, ranked

*Everything else that went through the adversarial stage.*

### O1. Accepting that implicit attitudes are genuinely ours (not mere subpersonal states) while lacking introspective access to them commits us to rejecting first-person authority entirely, not just for implicit attitudes but for explicit ones too, because the two cannot be principled apart on the features that would ground differential authority.

- **Promise 5/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** The widespread commitment (shared by Moran, Bar-On, and others defending authority) that implicit attitudes are genuinely mental but somehow outside the scope of first-person authority
- **Sketch:** The key premise is that implicit and explicit attitudes share all the functional features (causing behavior, rational integration with other states, sensitivity to reasons) that would ground first-person authority. If implicit bias causes discriminatory behavior and resists certain counter-evidence, it's functionally on a par with explicit belief. Defenders of first-person authority typically quarantine implicit attitudes as either not genuinely mental or not genuinely ours. But this quarantine is unprincipled: the features proposed to distinguish them (accessibility, endorsement, rational role) either fail to track the implicit/explicit distinction or, if made sufficient for authority, exclude many explicit attitudes too (self-deceptive beliefs, suppressed desires). Therefore, accepting that implicit attitudes are genuine attitudes commits you to either extending first-person authority to them (absurd—I'm not authoritative about my implicit biases) or withdrawing it from explicit attitudes as well, since no principled line can be drawn.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Forces a principled line between implicit and explicit attitudes that threatens to collapse first-person authority entirely—would genuinely reframe the debate's stakes.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Schwitzgebel's work does reject first-person authority broadly based on unreliability; the thesis restates this for implicit attitudes specifically without adding novel structure
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence failed to explain why access-based distinctions work within the mental realm for conscious/non-conscious beliefs but not for implicit/explicit attitudes
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Emotional dispositions show we maintain differential authority using accessibility constitutively, not as an arbitrary add-on to functional features
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The thesis treats as a revealed tension what is actually definitional: authority is constituted by immediate access, not grounded in functional features that must extend to all attitudes
- **Flaw:** The thesis misidentifies a definitional boundary (authority constituted by immediate access) as a substantive commitment problem, when defenders coherently distinguish what makes states mental (functional features) from what makes them authoritative (distinctive epistemic relation)

<details><summary>3 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (4/5) Holding that implicit attitudes are genuine mental states commits you to the radical revisability of first-person authority: if implicit biases I sincerely disavow are genuinely mine, then others' interpretive access can trump my self-ascriptions even for avowal-apt attitudes.
- (2/5) Accepting that implicit attitudes exist commits you to denying first-person authority even over explicit attitudes, because the same sub-personal processes that generate implicit attitudes also generate the confabulated narratives we call 'explicit beliefs,' making the explicit/implicit distinction epistemically unstable.
- (4/5) Accepting that implicit attitudes exist commits interpretivist accounts to a form of first-person authority they officially reject: if my sincere avowal can be false due to implicit bias, then avowals must express a genuine psychological kind that comes apart from behavioral dispositions.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This position is held by **Eric Schwitzgebel** in his work on introspection and self-knowledge (particularly "The Unreliability of Naive Introspection," 2008, and related papers). Schwitzgebel argues that we systematically lack reliable introspective access to many of our mental states, including both implicit attitudes and numerous explicit ones. He challenges the traditional picture of first-person authority by showing that our self-knowledge is far more limited and unreliable than philosophers typically assume—we're wrong about our visual experiences, our beliefs, our emotions, and our cognitive processes. His position is precisely that there is no principled line between mental states we have authority over and those we don't; instead, we have varying degrees of unreliable access across the board. The candidate thesis's conclusion—that accepting implicit attitudes as genuinely mental forces us to reject first-person authority across the board—is essentially Schwitzgebel's settled view. He has already argued extensively that the features defenders cite to preserve authority for explicit attitudes (introspective access, endorsement, rational integration) fail to do the work required, and that our epistemic position toward our own minds is far more third-personal than traditional accounts suggest.

STRENGTH: 5/5 — This is Schwitzgebel's established position, articulated and defended across multiple papers over fifteen years. The thesis is not novel.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The thesis is not Schwitzgebel's position for three reasons that matter structurally.

First, Schwitzgebel's target is *reliability* of introspective access, not *authority*. He argues we're often wrong about our mental states, but being frequently wrong is compatible with still being the privileged judge when we do pronounce. A thermometer can be unreliable yet still authoritative in the sense that when it reads 72°, we defer to that reading over other evidence. The thesis concerns whether first-person verdicts have a special *standing* or *default entitlement to uptake*—not whether they're accurate. Bar-On and Moran defend authority even while granting we can be mistaken; they claim our avowals have a constitutive or expressive role that gives them special status. Schwitzgebel never addresses this dimension.

Second, the thesis makes a *conditional* argument: *if* you accept implicit attitudes as genuinely ours, *then* you cannot maintain differential authority. This is a pressure argument against those who want both (1) implicit attitudes to be genuine mental states and (2) preserved first-person authority for explicit attitudes. Schwitzgebel simply denies (2) from the start—he's not showing an internal tension in the mainstream view, he's rejecting it wholesale on independent grounds (poor introspective performance). The thesis's force is dialectical: it forces defenders of authority to choose. Schwitzgebel never occupied their position, so he never faced this choice.

Third, the thesis identifies a *principled distinction problem*: the features proposed to ground differential authority (accessibility, endorsement, rational role) cannot track the implicit/explicit boundary without either excluding paradigm explicit attitudes or including implicit ones. This is an argument about what could *in principle* ground authority, not about empirical reliability. Schwitzgebel's arguments are empirical—studies on experience sampling, introspective disagreement, confabulation. He never argues that the *conceptual resources* available to authority-defenders fail to draw the needed line.

VERDICT

The defence fails. While the three differences are real, they don't save novelty.

On the first point: Schwitzgebel's work does undermine *authority* properly understood, not just reliability. When he shows we confabulate about our beliefs, misreport our experiences, and lack access to our cognitive processes, he's not merely saying we err occasionally—he's arguing we lack the kind of epistemic position that would ground privileged standing. His conclusion is that we should adopt a "third-person" stance toward our own minds. That *is* a rejection of first-person authority as traditionally conceived, even if he doesn't use that exact term.

On the second point: The conditional structure doesn't create novelty. Schwitzgebel's arguments already apply to anyone who accepts implicit attitudes as mental states. His empirical cases *include* implicit attitudes alongside explicit ones to show the continuum. The thesis just makes explicit what follows from his view when applied to the specific debate about implicit attitudes.

On the third point: Schwitzgebel does address the principled distinction problem, just empirically rather than purely conceptually. His strategy is to show that every proposed marker of "authoritative" mental states (consciousness, accessibility, endorsement) empirically fails to create a natural kind. The thesis argues this *in principle*; Schwitzgebel argues it *in fact*. But his conclusion is the same: no principled line exists.

The thesis restates Schwitzgebel's settled view in response to a specific dialectical situation (the implicit attitudes debate), but the core claim—that accepting implicit attitudes as mental while preserving first-person authority for explicit attitudes is untenable—is his position.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would prove that no domain admits partial or graded authority based on differential epistemic access. Consider medical self-knowledge: I have first-person authority about whether I feel pain, but not about whether I have cancer, even though both are facts about my body. The argument structure parallels the thesis: pain and tumors share all the relevant biological features (both are bodily states, both causally interact with other systems, both respond to interventions), so any principled distinction must appeal to something like introspective access. But then the thesis's logic applies: if access-based distinctions fail to track a principled boundary (since I can have non-conscious tumors and non-conscious pains), we must either extend first-person authority to cancer diagnosis or withdraw it from pain reports. This is absurd—clearly I retain authority about my pain while lacking it for my tumor. 

The problem generalizes: memory (authoritative about what I remember, not about what actually happened), perception (authoritative about how things seem, not about how they are), intention (authoritative about what I'm trying to do, not about what I'll succeed at doing). In each case, we sensibly maintain differential authority despite the states sharing "functional features" and despite fuzzy boundaries. The argument proves we can never have domain-restricted first-person authority anywhere, which is far too strong.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis by showing its reasoning pattern licenses rejecting obviously legitimate distinctions throughout epistemology.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The medical analogy fails because pain and cancer are not both *mental states* of the same fundamental kind—they're categorically different phenomena that happen to both involve the body. Pain is a conscious experience; cancer is a biological process. The asymmetry in authority tracks this categorical difference, not merely differential access to states of the same type.

The thesis concerns a genuine puzzle: implicit and explicit attitudes are both *attitudes*—both are mental states with propositional content that figure in psychological explanation of behavior through their representational properties. An implicit bias that Asians are good at math and an explicit belief that Asians are good at math have the same content, same direction of fit, same functional role in causing discriminatory behavior or mathematical expectations. The question is whether, among states of this single kind (attitudes), we can maintain that some carry first-person authority while others don't.

The objector's other examples actually support the thesis rather than undermining it. Consider memory: I'm authoritative about *what I seem to remember* (a current mental state), not about *what happened* (a past event). The authority tracks the boundary between present mental states and external facts. Similarly for perception: authority about *how things appear to me* (mental state) versus *how things are* (world). These distinctions work because they separate mental from non-mental, or present states from past events.

But implicit and explicit attitudes are both present mental states with the same representational function. The defender of differential authority must find something *within the mental realm* that grounds the distinction. The thesis argues this cannot be done without appeal to features (like introspective access or endorsement) that either fail to track the implicit/explicit boundary or, if stipulated to do so, become ad hoc.

The "proves too much" charge assumes all authority-distinctions work the same way. They don't. Cross-category distinctions (mental/physical, present/past, appearance/reality) can ground differential authority because the categories differ in kind. But within a single category—attitudes with propositional content that explain behavior—the defender needs a principled basis. The thesis claims none exists.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though less decisively than the objector claims.

The key move—that pain and cancer are categorically different while implicit and explicit attitudes are the same kind of thing—has force but doesn't fully save the thesis. Here's why:

The objector can press that even within the mental realm, we already accept graded or partial authority based on access-related features. Consider:

**Conscious vs. non-conscious explicit beliefs**: I have first-person authority about my occurrent thought that it's raining, but not about my standing dispositional belief that Ankara is Turkey's capital (which I'm not currently entertaining). Both are explicit beliefs—same kind of mental state—but authority differs based on current accessibility.

**Attended vs. unattended perceptual content**: I have authority about the bird I'm focusing on, not about the eighteenth leaf on the tree in my peripheral vision, even though both are present in my visual field. Same kind of state (perceptual experience), different authority based on attention.

**Emotional awareness**: I may have authority about my felt anger but not about the subtle anxiety underlying it, even when both are present emotional states causally influencing my behavior.

These examples show we *already* accept that within a single category of mental states, differential access can ground differential authority without this being ad hoc or unprincipled. The implicit/explicit distinction could work the same way: both are attitudes, but different accessibility makes the authority-difference principled.

The thesis's response—that such distinctions must be non-arbitrary—faces the burden of explaining why access-based distinctions work for conscious/non-conscious beliefs but not for implicit/explicit attitudes. The defender might say implicit attitudes are *in principle* inaccessible while non-conscious explicit beliefs are merely *currently* unaccessed. But this just pushes the question back: why does in-principle versus contingent inaccessibility matter for authority?

The "proves too much" objection succeeds because it shows the thesis's logic—that sharing functional features means no principled authority-distinction—applies equally to cases where we clearly do maintain principled distinctions within the mental realm.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider **emotional dispositions** as a counterexample where we successfully maintain differential first-person authority despite the exact structure the thesis claims is impossible.

I have first-person authority about my occurrent feeling of irritation at my colleague's comment (an explicit attitude), but not about my deep-seated resentment toward authority figures stemming from childhood (an implicit emotional attitude). Yet both are:
- Genuinely mine (both explain my behavior, both are integrated into my psychological economy)
- Mental states with propositional content (irritation *that* she interrupted me; resentment *that* authorities constrain me)
- Functionally similar (both cause avoidance behavior, both resist certain counter-evidence, both rationally interact with other states)
- On a continuum of accessibility (I might become aware of the resentment through therapy)

We don't conclude from this that I must either have authority about my unconscious resentment (absurd) or lack authority about my occurrent irritation (also absurd). Instead, we sensibly maintain differential authority based on accessibility, and this distinction is principled: the occurrent emotion is available to immediate awareness and endorsement; the dispositional one isn't.
