The thesis claims such distinctions cannot work for implicit/explicit attitudes because the features that ground them "either fail to track the implicit/explicit distinction or exclude many explicit attitudes too." But the emotional case shows this is false: accessibility-based distinctions can track a real boundary without being unprincipled, even when some explicit attitudes (like suppressed emotions) fall on the "low authority" side. Having fuzzy boundaries doesn't make a distinction unprincipled—it makes it a real-world psychological distinction rather than a philosopher's idealization.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem for the thesis. The emotional case shows we do maintain exactly the kind of differential authority the thesis claims is impossible, using exactly the features (accessibility, endorsement) the thesis claims cannot do principled work. However, the thesis might respond that emotional dispositions are relevantly different from attitudes (perhaps emotions aren't propositional in the same way, or perhaps we actually don't fully accept them as "genuinely ours" until we acknowledge them). The counterexample creates real pressure but doesn't definitively refute the thesis.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The emotional case actually supports rather than undermines the thesis, because it shows exactly the unprincipled quarantine strategy the thesis targets.

Notice what happens with unconscious resentment: defenders of first-person authority *don't* treat it as a genuine attitude I currently have. Instead, they redescribe it as either (1) a mere disposition that becomes an attitude only when actualized in conscious resentment, or (2) a subpersonal processing pattern that explains behavior but isn't properly mine until I acknowledge it, or (3) something I *had* (past attitudes from childhood) whose causal residue now affects me.

This is precisely the move the thesis predicts and challenges. When pressed on cases of inaccessible mental states, defenders retreat to: "Well, those aren't really *attitudes* in the full sense—they're dispositions, or subpersonal states, or causal influences." The thesis's force is showing this retreat is unprincipled.

Consider: my unconscious resentment toward authority *does* have propositional content (that authorities constrain me), *does* guide my behavior systematically (I bristle at directives, I undermine supervisors), *does* interact rationally with other states (it's defeated by evidence that this particular authority is benevolent, it combines with my belief that my boss is an authority to produce avoidance). By every functional criterion that makes my explicit irritation an *attitude*, my unconscious resentment qualifies too.

The objector says we "sensibly maintain differential authority based on accessibility." But that's just asserting what needs justification. *Why* does accessibility ground authority for attitudes? The standard answer appeals to constitutive features: I'm authoritative about my beliefs because avowing them partly constitutes having them (Moran), or because self-ascription has an expressive rather than evidential basis (Bar-On). But these accounts make accessibility *derivative* from something deeper about the attitude's nature—and that deeper feature (rational role, functional profile, behavioral guidance) applies equally to implicit attitudes.

If accessibility is just a brute epistemic fact—I happen to have access to some attitudes and not others—then first-person authority becomes mere contingent reliability about a subset of my attitudes, not a principled special standing. The thesis challenges defenders to explain why the accessible/inaccessible boundary marks a *principled* difference in authority rather than just a practical difference in what I happen to notice.

The objector claims "having fuzzy boundaries doesn't make a distinction unprincipled." True, but the problem isn't fuzziness—it's that the proposed boundary (accessibility) either tracks nothing deep about what makes attitudes authority-apt, or tracks something that many explicit attitudes lack (self-deceptive beliefs, suppressed desires, rationalized motives). The thesis's challenge is: show me the principle that makes accessibility authority-grounding for attitudes specifically, rather than just a contingent feature of some attitudes.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds partially but ultimately fails to save the thesis.

The defence is right that defenders often redescribe inaccessible states to preserve authority for accessible ones—that's exactly the quarantine strategy the thesis targets. And it's right that this can seem unprincipled when the inaccessible states share all the functional features of attitudes.

But the defence doesn't adequately answer why accessibility-based authority is unprincipled. Here's the problem:

**First-person authority has always been understood as involving a distinctive epistemic relation to one's own mental states**—not merely reliability, but a special standing or warrant. Even philosophers like Moran and Bar-On, who ground this in constitutive or expressive features, acknowledge that the authority *manifests* through a distinctive kind of access. You can't separate "what grounds authority" from "how authority is exercised."

The emotional case shows we can maintain this principled: I have the distinctive first-person relation (immediate, non-inferential, expression-based) to my occurrent irritation but not to my unconscious resentment. This isn't arbitrary—it tracks a real psychological difference in *how I relate to these states*. The occurrent emotion is available for endorsement, revision, expression; the unconscious one isn't.

The thesis demands: "Show me the principle that makes this authority-grounding." But the answer is straightforward: **the authority-grounding feature just is the distinctive first-person epistemic relation itself**. We're not looking for some other feature (functional role, rational integration) that grounds authority independent of access. Rather, having first-person authority means standing in this immediate, non-inferential, expression-based relation to a state. States I don't stand in that relation to—whether implicit attitudes or unconscious emotions—simply aren't states I have first-person authority about.

This isn't unprincipled. It's recognizing that first-person authority is constitutively tied to a certain kind of access. The thesis treats "accessibility" as one candidate feature among others (functional role, rational integration, etc.) and argues none can do the work. But accessibility isn't a candidate ground for authority—it's constitutive of what authority is.

The thesis fails because it misidentifies the dialectical situation. Defenders of first-person authority aren't trying to find features that ground differential authority despite attitudes being functionally similar. They're saying that first-person authority just is having a certain epistemic relation to one's states, and implicit attitudes are precisely those attitudes to which we lack that relation. This isn't quarantining—it's defining the scope of authority by the distinctive relation that constitutes it.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a deep commitment problem for defenders of first-person authority, but it actually just restates a definitional boundary in dramatic terms. The core claim is: "If implicit and explicit attitudes share functional features, then any differential authority must appeal to something like accessibility, but accessibility-based distinctions are unprincipled." However, this misses that **first-person authority just means having a distinctive epistemic relation (immediate, non-inferential, expression-based access) to one's mental states**. 

Saying "I have authority over explicit but not implicit attitudes" is simply saying "I stand in the distinctive first-person epistemic relation to some attitudes but not others." The thesis treats this as a substantive discovery requiring justification, but it's closer to a verbal stipulation: we *define* the scope of first-person authority by the presence of this distinctive relation. 

The apparent tension—"Why do functionally similar attitudes get different authority?"—dissolves once we recognize that "having first-person authority" doesn't mean "being reliable about attitudes with certain functional features." It means "standing in the immediate-access relation." The thesis is like arguing: "If conscious and non-conscious beliefs share functional features, we must either extend consciousness to all beliefs or deny it to currently-conscious ones." But consciousness just *is* a certain kind of accessibility—there's no further principle needed.

The "commitment" being exposed is just: defenders of first-person authority think first-person authority involves a distinctive kind of access. This is their starting position, not a consequence they're unwittingly committed to.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly undermines the thesis's claim to reveal a problematic commitment. The thesis treats as a substantive philosophical discovery what is actually close to a definitional truth about what first-person authority means. However, the thesis retains some force: it does show that defenders cannot appeal to *other* features (functional role, rational integration, being "genuinely ours") to ground differential authority while treating accessibility as merely evidential. If they do that, the thesis's argument has bite. The triviality charge succeeds against the strong reading where the thesis claims to reveal an impossible position, but not against a weaker reading where it forces clarity about whether accessibility is constitutive or merely contingent.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The triviality charge fundamentally misunderstands what makes the thesis dialectically significant. The thesis is not revealing that "defenders think authority involves distinctive access"—everyone knows that. It's revealing an *internal tension* in how defenders actually argue when pressed on cases.

Here's the tension: Defenders of first-person authority face **the inclusion problem**. They need implicit attitudes to be genuinely mental (to avoid eliminativism about implicit bias) but outside authority's scope (to avoid absurdity). When challenged—"Why aren't implicit attitudes really mental?"—they appeal to functional features: implicit biases *do* cause behavior, *do* have propositional content, *do* integrate rationally with other states, *do* explain action. They insist implicit attitudes are genuine attitudes precisely because they share these features with explicit attitudes.

But when challenged—"Then why don't you have authority over them?"—they suddenly switch grounds and appeal to accessibility: "Authority requires immediate access, and implicit attitudes lack that." 

The thesis exposes this as dialectically unstable. You cannot simultaneously argue that:
1. Functional features (causal role, rational integration, content) are what make implicit attitudes genuinely mental/attitudinal (when resisting eliminativism)
2. Accessibility is what grounds first-person authority (when explaining differential authority)

Because if (1) is true, then either accessibility must *itself* be among the features that make something genuinely mental (in which case implicit attitudes aren't genuine attitudes after all—contradiction), or accessibility is an *additional* requirement for authority beyond what makes something mental (in which case you need to explain why this additional requirement is principled rather than ad hoc).

The objector says "accessibility is constitutive of authority, not a candidate ground for it." But this just pushes the question back: **Why should we care about first-person authority so defined?** The traditional interest in first-person authority stems from the idea that it reveals something important about the nature of mind—that mental states have a special relation to their subject. But if authority is just "whatever I have immediate access to," with no deeper connection to what makes states genuinely mental, then:

- It becomes a contingent psychological fact that some attitudes are accessible and others aren't
- The philosophical significance drains away—why privilege this epistemic relation?
- We lose the connection to constitutive accounts (Moran's rational self-constitution, Bar-On's expression-based authority) that made first-person authority theoretically interesting

The thesis's force is showing that defenders cannot have it both ways. Either:
- **Substantive view**: Features like rational role and functional integration ground both genuine mentality AND authority—but then implicit attitudes qualify for authority (absurd)
- **Stipulative view**: Authority just means "immediate access" regardless of functional features—but then authority loses theoretical interest and the connection to constitutive accounts of self-knowledge

The triviality charge treats defenders as holding the stipulative view from the start. But they don't argue that way. They argue substantively—that authority connects to rational agency (Moran), to expression (Bar-On), to avowal's constitutive role. These are claims about why accessibility matters, not just definitions. The thesis shows these substantive claims either extend to implicit attitudes or fail to ground authority even for explicit ones.

Consider Moran specifically: he argues authority stems from the rational self-constitution involved in deliberating about what to believe. But implicit attitudes also respond to reasons (they're modifiable through counter-stereotypic exposure, they're sensitive to social context). If rational responsiveness grounds authority, implicit attitudes should have it. If they don't, then rational responsiveness doesn't ground authority—but that undermines Moran's entire account.

The thesis is not trivial because it forces defenders to choose between their substantive theoretical commitments and their intuitive judgments about cases. That's precisely what good philosophy does.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. The triviality charge succeeds in showing the thesis overstates its dialectical significance.

The defence's key move—that defenders "switch grounds" between functional features (for genuine mentality) and accessibility (for authority)—doesn't identify an actual tension. Here's why:

**Defenders can coherently maintain both claims without instability:**

1. "Implicit attitudes are genuinely mental because they have functional features F" (causal role, content, rational integration)
2. "First-person authority requires accessibility A in addition to features F"

This isn't ad hoc or unprincipled—it's recognizing that different questions require different answers. What makes something *mental* (question 1) is not the same as what makes it *authoritative* (question 2). The thesis treats this as problematic, but it's perfectly ordinary:

- What makes something a *belief* (functional role in inference, world-to-mind direction of fit) differs from what makes it *justified* (proper evidential support)
- What makes something *pain* (functional role, phenomenology) differs from what makes it *morally significant* (capacity for suffering, rights-conferring)

The defence asks: "Why should we care about first-person authority if it's just immediate access?" But this reverses the explanatory order. We don't first identify authority-grounding features and then discover authority involves immediate access. Rather, we start with the phenomenon—there's a distinctive way I know my own mind—and philosophical accounts explain why this matters (Moran: rational agency; Bar-On: expression). The immediate access isn't a *further requirement* added to functional features; it's the *phenomenon being explained*.

The defence's strongest point is about Moran specifically: if rational responsiveness grounds authority, and implicit attitudes respond to reasons, shouldn't they have authority? But this misreads Moran. His claim is that authority stems from the *deliberative stance*—actively reasoning about what to believe. Implicit attitudes respond to reasons causally but aren't subject to deliberative revision in the same way. I can't decide to stop having an implicit bias the way I can decide to stop believing something through reasoning. The difference isn't ad hoc—it tracks whether the state is subject to rational will.

More fundamentally, the defence claims defenders "cannot have it both ways" between substantive and stipulative views. But they can: **the substantive view is about why immediate access matters philosophically; the stipulative element is just that authority is defined by this access**. These aren't competing positions—one is metaphysical (what grounds the significance of authority), the other is semantic (what "authority" means).

The thesis fails to be non-trivial because its central claim—that defenders face a forced choice—rests on treating "what makes states mental" and "what makes them authoritative" as if they must be the same features. But there's no reason to think this. Mental states can be individuated functionally while authority is constituted by a distinctive epistemic relation. This isn't tension; it's conceptual clarity about different aspects of mind.

The thesis does show that *if* defenders tried to ground authority in functional features alone (rational role, behavioral causation), they'd face the problem described. But sophisticated defenders don't do this—they explicitly ground authority in the distinctive first-person relation, which is constitutively tied to accessibility. Against such defenders, the thesis reduces to: "You define authority by immediate access, but implicit attitudes lack immediate access." That's not a revealed commitment—it's a restatement of their position.

**Verdict: The triviality charge succeeds. The thesis presents as a substantive discovery what is actually close to a definitional boundary in the concept of first-person authority.**

</details>

---

### O2. Confabulation studies show not that introspection fails but that we have observational access to mental states while systematically lacking observational access to their causal-explanatory relations.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 4/5 · survives
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that confabulation studies undermine observational models wholesale, accepted by interpretivists as motivation and by observationalists as a threat
- **Sketch:** The standard reading of Nisbett and Wilson takes their studies to undermine introspective access to mental states generally. But look at what subjects actually get wrong: not whether they preferred the rightmost stockings, but why. They confabulate explanations (quality, texture) while their preferences are real and reliably self-ascribed. The pattern across confabulation studies is subjects accurately reporting mental states (beliefs, preferences, intentions) while systematically misreporting their causal origins. This vindicates a restricted observational model: we have introspective access to occurrent states but not to their etiology. First-person authority concerns the former; confabulation concerns the latter. The key premise is that the experiments systematically vary what subjects are asked to report, and accuracy tracks this distinction.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Distinguishing access to states from access to their etiology is useful and empirically grounded, but this distinction has been noted before in various forms.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 2/5, answered. The objection misread the thesis as claiming categorical inaccessibility; the defence clarified the claim is about systematic patterns in the empirical data, not metaphysical limits
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, answered. The biofeedback case showed subjects learning causal relations, but the defence distinguished direct observational access from learning through external feedback and inference
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, answered. The objection showed the state/process distinction is explicit in the original studies, but the defence reframed this as changing the theoretical significance rather than discovering new data
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. The objection identified a gap between reliability patterns and mechanism claims, but the defence argued the observational model best explains the specific character of the reliability pattern
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Confabulation studies show that introspective reliability systematically tracks the distinction between occurrent mental states (accessed directly) and their causal-explanatory relations (requiring inference), vindicating a restricted observational model against wholesale rejection

<details><summary>9 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) The confabulation literature undermines not introspection but only causal self-knowledge: we have direct access to our occurrent mental states but systematically confabulate about their causal history, which dissolves the apparent conflict between Nisbett-Wilson and first-person authority.
- (2/5) The confabulation literature undermines first-person authority only for explanatory self-knowledge (knowing why you φ'd), but first-person authority properly concerns recognitional self-knowledge (knowing that you're φ-ing), which interpretation leaves intact.
- (3/5) Confabulation studies show not that interpretation replaces observation, but that we systematically misidentify which mental states we're observing: we reliably detect affective-motivational states while confabulating the propositional attitudes we infer from them.
- (3/5) The confabulation literature shows not that introspection is unreliable but that philosophers have misidentified its target: introspection delivers knowledge of commitments (what one is prepared to avow and defend) rather than knowledge of mental state tokens, and first-person authority is authority about the former.
- (2/5) The confabulation literature shows not that we lack introspective access but that we systematically misidentify which mental states we're accessing—we introspect standing attitudes when we think we're introspecting occurrent mental events.
- (2/5) The confabulation literature establishes only that we misidentify the *causes* of our mental states, not that we misidentify the states themselves, and this distinction dissolves the threat to introspective authority.
- (3/5) The confabulation literature shows not that introspection is interpretive but that it is selective attention to phenomenology, where the error lies in taking the attended phenomenology as evidence for judgments about causation that outrun what the phenomenology itself presents.
- (2/5) The confabulation literature actually supports rather than undermines first-person authority, because confabulation studies systematically confuse two questions: whether subjects know their mental states and whether subjects know the causal history of those states.
- (2/5) The confabulation literature establishes not that we lack introspective access but that we systematically misidentify which mental states we're introspecting—we have reliable access to phenomenal states but confabulate the propositional attitudes we infer from them.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked—that we can have observational access to mental states while systematically lacking observational access to their causal-explanatory relations—it would prove too much by establishing an implausible asymmetry in other observational domains. Consider visual perception: I observe that the apple appears red to me, and I can also observe (through repeated experience and attention) that certain lighting conditions cause it to appear red while others make it appear brown. The thesis requires that observation can deliver the what but never the why, even when the causal relations are themselves mental and available to the same introspective faculty. But this seems arbitrary. When I introspect my reasoning process—observing that I believed P, then considered Q, then came to believe R because P and Q together implied it—the causal-explanatory relation seems as introspectively available as the beliefs themselves. If observation can access mental states, why would causal relations between mental states be categorically inaccessible to that same observational capacity? The weak premise is the categorical distinction between states and their relations: the argument would license saying we never observe causation anywhere, which undermines observation's explanatory role entirely.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has some initial force but ultimately fails. The thesis doesn't claim causal relations are *never* observable, only that confabulation studies reveal *systematic* failures specifically regarding causal origins. The reasoning-process counterexample actually supports the thesis: we might accurately report the logical relations while confabulating about why we found the argument compelling (perhaps social pressure, not logical force). The asymmetry isn't arbitrary—it's empirically demonstrated by the studies themselves.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what the thesis claims is inaccessible. The thesis does not assert that causal relations between mental states are "categorically inaccessible" to introspection—it asserts that confabulation studies reveal *systematic* failures when subjects report the causal origins of their mental states, while showing no comparable failures when subjects report the states themselves.

The reasoning-process counterexample actually demonstrates the distinction rather than undermining it. When you introspect "I believed P, then considered Q, then came to believe R," you are reporting a *sequence* of mental states—which falls on the accessible side of the divide. The thesis predicts you would accurately report this. What you systematically get wrong is *why* this sequence occurred: perhaps you report that Q's logical force compelled belief in R, when actually social conformity pressure or prior commitment to R drove you to accept Q's relevance. The confabulation studies consistently show this pattern—subjects accurately report what they believe, prefer, or intend, while confabulating about the causal factors that produced these states.

The visual perception analogy actually supports the thesis. You observe that the apple appears red (state-report: accurate). You observe repeated correlations between lighting and color appearance (state-reports: accurate). But when you claim that *this lighting causes this appearance*, you have moved beyond observation to causal interpretation—and here error becomes systematic. The thesis doesn't deny we can learn causal relations; it denies we have *observational access* to them in the way we have observational access to occurrent states.

The "proves too much" charge fails because the asymmetry is not posited arbitrarily—it is read off the empirical pattern in the studies themselves, where accuracy systematically tracks the state/etiology distinction.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection conflates reporting sequences of mental states (accessible) with reporting causal-explanatory relations (inaccessible). The thesis makes a claim about what the confabulation data actually show, not a metaphysical claim about what observation could never deliver. The empirical pattern—accurate state-reports, inaccurate causal reports—is precisely what motivates the restricted observational model.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider a subject in a biofeedback training session who learns to slow their heart rate through mental techniques. The subject reports: "I'm imagining a calm beach scene, and this is causing my heart rate to decrease." Through the biofeedback monitor, we verify both claims are true: they are indeed imagining the beach scene (mental state), and this imagery is indeed causing the physiological change (causal relation). Over repeated sessions, the subject becomes reliably accurate at identifying which mental techniques (imagery vs. counting vs. breathing focus) causally produce the desired effect. Here we have introspective access to both the mental state AND its causal-explanatory relation to an outcome. The weak premise is the categorical claim that we "systematically lack observational access" to causal-explanatory relations. This counterexample shows that with appropriate feedback and repeated observation, subjects can gain observational access to causal relations involving their mental states. The thesis requires that confabulation be systematic and ineliminable regarding causal relations, but this case demonstrates accurate causal self-knowledge through introspective observation coupled with external feedback—suggesting the problem is not categorical inaccessibility but rather absence of corrective feedback in the original confabulation studies.

STRENGTH: 3/5

This poses a genuine problem. The biofeedback case shows subjects can learn accurate causal self-knowledge, which challenges the "systematically lacking" claim. However, the thesis might respond that the feedback provides external evidence rather than pure introspective access.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The biofeedback case does not provide a counterexample because the subject's accurate causal knowledge is not gained through introspective observation of the causal relation itself, but through *external feedback* that allows inference to causal structure. This is precisely what distinguishes it from observational access to mental states.

Notice what happens in the biofeedback scenario: The subject observes the mental state (imagining the beach). The subject observes the outcome (heart rate decrease) *via the external monitor*. Through repeated correlation plus the monitor's feedback, the subject infers the causal connection. This is learning through evidence and inference, not observational access to the causal relation itself.

Contrast this with how the subject knows they are imagining a beach scene: they don't need repeated trials, external feedback, or inference from correlations. The access is immediate and observational. The asymmetry the thesis identifies is precisely this: mental states are introspectively available in a direct, non-inferential way, while causal relations require the very scaffolding your example provides—repeated trials, external validation, correlation tracking.

The objection actually strengthens the thesis by showing what happens when you *add* the missing elements. In confabulation studies, subjects lack feedback about which causal story is correct, so they confabulate. In biofeedback, subjects receive external correction, so they learn accurate causal models. But learning through externally-validated inference is not observational access. If it were, then a scientist who discovers causal relations through controlled experiments would have "observational access" to causation—but this stretches "observational access" beyond recognition.

The thesis claims we systematically lack *introspective observational* access to causal relations—not that we cannot learn causal truths about our mental states through other means. Your biofeedback subject has learned a causal truth, but not through observing the causation itself.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection conflates two distinct epistemic routes: direct observational access (which requires no external feedback, inference, or repeated trials) versus learning through evidence and correlation (which requires all of these). The biofeedback case demonstrates the latter, not the former. The subject's causal knowledge depends essentially on the external monitor and repeated trials—remove these and the subject returns to confabulation. This confirms rather than challenges the thesis: we have observational access to mental states (immediate, non-inferential) but not to their causal relations (which require evidential scaffolding to know accurately).

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims to reframe confabulation studies, but actually just restates what the studies themselves already establish and what careful readers already acknowledge. Nisbett and Wilson themselves distinguish between access to mental contents versus access to mental processes—their paper is titled "Telling More Than We Can Know" precisely because subjects accurately report their preferences (what they know) while failing to report causal processes (more than they can know). The thesis presents as a discovery the distinction between "mental states" and "their causal-explanatory relations," but this is simply the distinction between occurrent contents and processes that any reading of the studies already incorporates. The weak premise is that this constitutes a "reframing" that challenges "the shared assumption that confabulation studies undermine observational models wholesale." But no serious position holds that these studies show we cannot introspect our current preferences or beliefs—the debate has always been about whether introspection provides privileged access to mental processes, mechanisms, and causal relations. The thesis repackages the studies' explicit findings as though correcting a misreading, when it merely restates what "observational access to mental states" versus "access to causal origins" means. It's a verbal stipulation—calling accurate preference reports "observational access" while denying that label to causal reports—dressed as a substantive discovery about what the studies show.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis claims to correct a misreading, but the distinction between contents and processes is explicit in the original studies and acknowledged across the debate. The "reframing" appears to be merely terminological.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what is at stake in calling something a "reframing." The thesis does not claim to have *discovered* that subjects accurately report preferences while confabulating about causes—that is indeed explicit in Nisbett and Wilson. What the thesis reframes is the *theoretical significance* of this pattern for debates about introspective access.

The crucial move is this: The observational model has been treated as a package deal—either introspection works like observation (and thus should be reliable across the board) or it doesn't work like observation at all. Nisbett and Wilson's studies have been wielded as a wholesale refutation: "Look, introspection fails systematically, so the observational model is false." Interpretivists cite these studies as showing we must *interpret* rather than *observe* our mental states. Observationalists retreat or try to explain away the data.

The reframing breaks the package. It says: The observational model was *right about what it was right about*—we do have observational access to occurrent mental states—and the confabulation studies don't touch this. They show something else: that observational access has a specific scope limitation. The model doesn't fail wholesale; it succeeds in its proper domain and fails outside it.

This is not mere terminological stipulation. Consider the dialectical consequences: If confabulation studies refute observationalism wholesale, then interpretivism wins—we must interpret even our current preferences and beliefs. But if the studies show only that observation has limited scope, then observationalism survives in restricted form, and interpretivism is unwarranted for occurrent states. The reframing *changes what the studies are evidence for*.

The objection says "no serious position holds that these studies show we cannot introspect our current preferences." But look at how the studies are actually deployed in the literature: as general evidence against first-person authority, against the idea that we have privileged access to our minds, against taking self-reports at face value. The thesis intervenes in this deployment by insisting the studies show something more specific and less devastating to observationalism.

Moreover, the thesis provides a *principled* basis for the distinction: observational access versus inferential/interpretive access to causal structure. This is not just labeling—it explains why the pattern appears and predicts where accuracy will and won't obtain. Nisbett and Wilson documented the pattern; the thesis explains what it reveals about the architecture of self-knowledge.

VERDICT

The defence holds, but barely. The objection is powerful because the thesis does risk appearing to merely relabel what everyone already knows. However, the defence succeeds by showing that the reframing has genuine dialectical force: it intervenes in how the studies are used as evidence, rescues observationalism from a wholesale refutation, and provides principled grounds for the scope limitation rather than ad hoc damage control.

The thesis survives because reframing is not about discovering new data but about changing what existing data mean for theoretical disputes. Whether "no serious position" treats confabulation as wholesale refutation is an empirical question about the literature—if serious positions do deploy it that way, the reframing has real work to do. If they don't, the thesis becomes a clarification rather than a correction, which weakens but doesn't destroy it.

The vulnerability remains: if the literature already widely accepts this restricted reading, the thesis loses its critical edge and becomes merely a clear statement of the consensus. The thesis needs to show that the "shared assumption" it targets is actually shared, not a strawman.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "observational access" between a reading that makes the claim plausible and one that makes it interesting, and the argument needs both. On one reading, "observational access" means simply "reliable first-person reports"—subjects reliably report their preferences, so they have observational access to them. This makes the thesis plausible: the studies do show reliable state-reports and unreliable causal reports. But on this reading, the thesis is uninteresting—it just means "subjects are reliable about some things and unreliable about others," which is trivial. On the other reading, "observational access" means something theoretically robust—a distinctive epistemic mechanism analogous to perception, with immediacy, non-inferentiality, and immunity to certain errors. This makes the thesis interesting: it would vindicate a substantial philosophical model of introspection. But on this reading, the thesis is implausible—the studies don't establish that reliable preference-reports involve perception-like mechanisms rather than, say, quick unconscious inferences or interpretive processes that happen to be reliable for occurrent states. The argument needs the robust reading to matter philosophically (to "vindicate a restricted observational model") but needs the minimal reading to be supported by the studies (which show only differential reliability, not differential mechanism). The weak premise is "we have observational access to mental states"—it trades on this equivocation to seem both established by the data and theoretically significant.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis uses "observational access" to do heavy philosophical lifting, but the studies only establish differential reliability patterns, not facts about epistemic mechanisms. The move from "reliable reports" to "observational model vindicated" requires the term to mean something more robust than the data support.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection claims the thesis equivocates, but actually the thesis relies on a single, consistent conception of observational access throughout—one that is both empirically grounded and theoretically substantive. The conception is this: observational access is characterized by immediacy, non-inferentiality, and independence from background theory or interpretation.

The confabulation studies don't merely show differential reliability—they show a systematic pattern that tracks precisely this distinction. When subjects report their preferences, they do so immediately and without inference. They don't reason their way to the conclusion that they prefer the rightmost stockings; they simply report the preference. When subjects report causal origins, they engage in a different process: they construct explanations, appeal to folk theories about what should matter (quality, texture), and produce responses that vary with cultural expectations and implicit theories. This is not mere unreliability—it's a qualitatively different epistemic process.

The thesis is not inferring mechanism from reliability alone. It's inferring mechanism from the *pattern* of reliability plus the *phenomenological and structural features* of how subjects respond. Consider: subjects show no hesitation or uncertainty when reporting preferences, but show clear signs of construction when reporting causes (using phrases like "I guess," "probably because," "it must have been"). The reports differ in speed, confidence, and susceptibility to theoretical priming. These are exactly the signatures we'd expect if one involves observation and the other involves interpretation or inference.

Moreover, the objection's "minimal reading" (just reliable reports) versus "robust reading" (perception-like mechanism) is a false dichotomy. The robust reading doesn't float free of the data—it's the best explanation of the specific pattern the data exhibit. Why would subjects be systematically reliable about occurrent states but systematically unreliable about causal relations if both involved the same interpretive or inferential process? The observational model explains this: observation delivers states directly but doesn't deliver causal relations, so subjects must interpret or infer the latter, introducing error.

The objection demands that the studies "establish" the mechanism directly, but this sets an impossible standard. No behavioral study directly establishes mechanisms—mechanisms are always inferred as best explanations of patterns. The question is whether the observational model is the best explanation of this pattern, and it is: it predicts exactly the reliability profile we observe, explains why the boundary falls where it does (states versus relations), and unifies the data under a principled distinction.

The thesis is not using "observational access" in a minimal sense to establish the claim and then switching to a robust sense to draw conclusions. It's using the robust sense throughout, and arguing that the studies' pattern is best explained by that robust model operating within a restricted domain.

VERDICT

The defence holds, but the thesis is vulnerable here. The objection correctly identifies that the move from "subjects reliably report preferences" to "subjects have observational access to preferences" requires justification—it's not automatic. The defence provides that justification by pointing to features beyond mere reliability: immediacy, lack of construction, phenomenological differences, and the fact that the observational model best explains the specific pattern.

However, the defence's success depends on empirical claims that could be challenged. If subjects actually do show signs of construction, hesitation, or theory-dependence when reporting occurrent states (just less than when reporting causes), the defence weakens. If alternative models (say, dual-process theories where both involve interpretation but Type 1 processes are faster and more reliable) could equally explain the pattern, the inference to observation becomes less compelling.

The thesis survives because the objection hasn't shown that a non-observational model explains the pattern equally well. The objection identifies a gap in the argument (from reliability pattern to mechanism) but doesn't close it with an alternative explanation. The defence fills the gap by showing that the observational model isn't just consistent with the data but provides the best explanation of the specific features of the reliability pattern.

The vulnerability is real but not fatal: the thesis needs to defend not just that subjects are reliable about states, but that the *character* of this reliability bears the signatures of observation rather than fast/reliable interpretation. The defence sketches this case, but it could be pressed further.

</details>

---

### O3. First-person authority and confabulation are compatible because authority is a social-epistemic status concerning entitlement to be believed, not reliability about facts, and a practice can rationally confer such status even on unreliable reporters when challenging them is more epistemically costly than accepting their reports.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 0/5 · worst 4/5 · survives
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption across the debate that first-person authority must be explained by features of the first-person epistemic situation rather than by features of our social-epistemic practices
- **Sketch:** Everyone assumes first-person authority is a fact about the epistemic position of the self-ascriber—either they have special access (observationalists) or special standing (expressivists). But authority is fundamentally a social practice: whom we're entitled to challenge and whom we must default to believing. The practice of granting first-person authority could be justified even if introspection were highly unreliable, provided that (a) challenging self-ascriptions requires evidence the challenger often lacks, (b) the costs of false challenges (undermining autonomy, damaging trust) exceed the costs of false acceptances, and (c) subjects adjust behavior based on the ascriptions they make, rendering challenges pragmatically self-defeating. This explains why the empirical data on confabulation doesn't undermine the social-epistemic asymmetry. The key premise is that authority is a feature of epistemic practices, not individual capacities.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Social-epistemic practice account is sensible but doesn't break new ground in explaining the normative dimension.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, answered. The seminar case posed a genuine challenge, but the defence successfully distinguished phenomenological authority (which persists) from dispositional consistency (which we appropriately challenge), showing the thesis predicts this pattern.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. The circularity charge had some initial force, but the defence showed conditions (a-c) have independent grounds in embodiment, autonomy requirements, and intentional state structure that don't presuppose the authority they justify.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. This was the strongest attack, forcing clarification that authority is robust-but-bounded like property rights; the defence successfully argued defeasibility doesn't entail mere heuristic status, though it exposed needed precision about scope.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, answered. The charge that scope contracts to fit practice had force, but the defence provided specific falsifiable predictions about cross-cultural variation, domain-specific patterns, and practice structure that distinguish principled scope from ad hoc retreat.
- **Survives as:** First-person authority is a robust but bounded social-epistemic status concerning entitlement to be believed about one's mental life as experienced and self-constituted, rationally justified by access asymmetry, autonomy costs, and self-constituting character even when introspection is unreliable, with authority strongest where these conditions obtain most fully.

<details><summary>8 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) First-person authority survives the interpretive turn but only as a pragmatic-social phenomenon: we grant speakers default authority over their self-ascriptions because doing so serves crucial communicative and coordinative functions, not because their self-knowledge is epistemically special.
- (3/5) Interpretive accounts are correct about knowledge but wrong about the source of first-person authority: authority stems not from superior epistemic warrant but from social practices that make first-person reports dialectically unchallengeable unless specific defeating conditions obtain.
- (3/5) First-person authority is a deontic status conferred by social practice, not an epistemic achievement: communities grant subjects default authority to specify their own mental states because the alternative—constant third-person adjudication—would be practically and ethically intolerable.
- (2/5) First-person authority is a pragmatic rather than alethic phenomenon: self-ascriptions have default conversational uptake that other-ascriptions lack, and this social fact creates the appearance of epistemic asymmetry where none exists.
- (2/5) First-person authority is a social status granted by interpretive communities rather than a metaphysical fact about access or constitution, which explains both why it exists (communities have pragmatic reasons to defer to self-reports) and why it's defeasible (communities withdraw it under specific conditions).
- (2/5) The interpretive turn succeeds but eliminates the explanandum: what looks like first-person authority is actually just the social norm that challenges to self-ascriptions require stronger justification than the self-ascriptions themselves.
- (3/5) First-person authority is actually a social-epistemic status granted by others rather than an epistemic property of self-knowledge, and recognizing this shows that the interpretive turn and first-person authority were never in tension.
- (2/5) First-person authority is an artifact of social practices that asymmetrically distribute epistemic burdens: we challenge others' self-reports only at social cost, but this practical asymmetry has been mistaken for an epistemic asymmetry that theories must explain.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT: Consider a case where someone sincerely self-ascribes a belief they demonstrably don't have, where challenging them would be costless and beneficial. Suppose in a philosophy seminar, a participant says "I believe physicalism is false" but has just spent an hour defending physicalism, citing only physicalist sources, and objecting to every dualist argument. The seminar leader has extensive evidence of the mismatch, other participants can verify it, challenging costs nothing (it's expected in seminars), false acceptance is costly (it derails discussion), and the speaker won't adjust their actual commitments based on this confused self-ascription. Here, by the thesis's own criteria (a-c), the practice should NOT confer authority—yet intuitively, we still grant the speaker some first-person authority about their own beliefs. The speaker's "I believe" claim still carries weight we wouldn't grant to a third party saying "they believe physicalism is false." This suggests authority isn't merely the social-epistemic status the thesis describes, since that status should evaporate when conditions (a-c) fail, but first-person authority persists.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem that needs answering. The thesis claims to explain first-person authority through social-epistemic practices justified by conditions (a-c), but the counterexample shows residual authority even when those conditions are absent, suggesting something beyond mere social practice is at work.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what first-person authority consists in and when conditions (a-c) obtain. In the seminar case, the practice DOES appropriately withhold the relevant authority—and what persists is something else entirely.

First, distinguish two things the speaker might mean: (1) a report about their occurrent phenomenology ("I'm experiencing myself as believing physicalism is false"), versus (2) a claim about their standing dispositional state ("physicalism-is-false is among my beliefs"). The thesis concerns authority over self-ascriptions as such—what you're experiencing, what seems to you to be the case about your mental life. For THAT, the speaker retains authority even in the seminar: if they say "it seems to me I believe physicalism is false," no amount of behavioral evidence defeats their claim about how things seem. We cannot challenge the phenomenology itself.

Second, regarding the dispositional claim: the objection is wrong that conditions (a-c) fail here. Condition (a) is NOT about whether evidence exists in principle, but whether *the challenger has epistemic access that defeats the default*. In real-time seminar discussion, the participant has just-lived experience of their reasoning, their felt commitments, their sense of what they're defending versus what they believe. The seminar leader has observed behavior, but behavior underdetermines belief—perhaps the speaker was playing devil's advocate in their own mind, or felt forced by dialectical pressure to defend positions they reject, or experienced their physicalist arguments as revealing physicalism's bankruptcy. The challenger lacks access to these deliberative and phenomenological facts.

Condition (c) applies with force: if we challenge "No, you actually believe physicalism," we're telling someone what they believe against their self-conception. This IS pragmatically self-defeating because belief is partly constituted by self-conception—if they walk away thinking "I guess I believe physicalism," that itself alters what they believe going forward. We cannot correct beliefs the way we correct factual errors, because the "correction" changes the target.

The residual weight you observe isn't authority that survives despite conditions (a-c) failing—it's authority that persists BECAUSE those conditions still obtain for the phenomenological and self-constituting aspects of mental life. What we appropriately question is whether their self-ascription tracks a stable dispositional state, but that's compatible with granting them authority over the self-ascription itself.

The objection trades on conflating "authority about what I believe" with "authority that what I believe is consistent" or "authority about how my beliefs will be classified by observers." The thesis explains the first; it never promised the second.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The counterexample fails because it misidentifies what persists as "first-person authority." When we appropriately challenge the speaker's claim in the seminar (by pointing out the mismatch, questioning whether they really believe what they say), we ARE withholding the social-epistemic status the thesis describes—we're not defaulting to belief, we're demanding they reconcile the inconsistency. What we cannot challenge, and what the thesis correctly predicts we cannot challenge, is the phenomenological claim ("this is how it seems to me") and the self-constituting aspect ("this is what I take myself to believe, going forward"). For these, conditions (a-c) remain satisfied, so authority persists exactly where the thesis predicts. The objection mistakes our appropriate skepticism about consistency for a complete withdrawal of first-person authority, but these are different things. The thesis stands.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT

The thesis claims to offer a substantial reframing by locating authority in "social-epistemic practices" rather than "individual epistemic capacities." But this is merely a verbal relabeling of the explanandum, not an explanation. 

To say authority is "a social-epistemic status concerning entitlement to be believed" just IS to describe the phenomenon we observe—that we treat first-person reports differently. The thesis then claims this practice is "rationally justified" by conditions (a-c). But notice: condition (a) says challengers "often lack" the evidence to challenge—this just restates that the first-person has epistemic advantage. Condition (b) invokes costs of "undermining autonomy"—but WHY does challenging self-ascriptions undermine autonomy unless the first-person has some special standing? Condition (c) says "subjects adjust behavior based on ascriptions"—but this just describes that self-ascriptions are self-fulfilling, not why we should grant them authority.

The purported explanation is circular: we grant authority because challenging is costly and subjects lack challenger-accessible evidence, but these "costs" and "lacks" only exist BECAUSE we already treat the first-person as authoritative. The thesis hasn't explained first-person authority by reference to social practices; it has merely stipulated that we call the observed asymmetry "a social practice" and then listed features that presuppose the very asymmetry being explained.

The supposed shift from "individual capacities" to "social practices" does no explanatory work—it's a re-description of the phenomenon as its own explanation.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force in demanding the thesis show how conditions (a-c) could justify the practice independently of presupposing first-person authority. However, the thesis can likely answer that these conditions have independent grounding (evolutionary costs of social conflict, practical impossibility of mind-reading, coordination benefits) that don't presuppose authority but explain why a practice conferring it would emerge. The circularity charge is weak, not fatal.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The triviality charge rests on a confusion between explanatory direction and constitutive dependence. The thesis is NOT claiming that conditions (a-c) are brute features of reality that happen to justify a practice. It's claiming that these conditions have independent, non-circular grounds that make a practice of granting authority rational—and that this practice, once established, constitutes what first-person authority IS.

Take condition (a): "challengers often lack evidence." This isn't restating first-person epistemic advantage—it's identifying an asymmetry in evidential ACCESS that obtains regardless of whether we grant authority. The first-person experiences their mental states as they occur; the third-person observes behavior, context, and expression. This access asymmetry is a brute fact of embodiment and temporal position, not a consequence of our practice. A community that refused to grant first-person authority would still face this asymmetry: challengers would still lack the real-time phenomenological data available to the subject.

Condition (b): "costs of undermining autonomy." The objection claims these costs only exist because we already treat the first-person as authoritative—but this reverses the actual dependency. Autonomy requires that agents can form and revise intentions based on their self-understanding. If I cannot settle what I believe or want without community approval, I cannot deliberate or plan. This is a PRACTICAL requirement for agency, not a consequence of epistemic authority. The cost is that systematic challenge destroys the conditions for autonomous action—this obtains whether or not we currently grant authority. Historical communities that denied first-person authority to certain groups (enslaved people, women, children) didn't thereby eliminate these costs; they imposed them, with predictable damage to agency.

Condition (c): "subjects adjust based on ascriptions." This isn't merely describing self-fulfillment—it's identifying why challenge is pragmatically self-defeating in a way unique to mental states. If I challenge your belief that the door is locked, and I'm wrong, the door's state remains unchanged. If I challenge your belief that you intend to vote, and you're uncertain, my challenge can ALTER what you intend. Mental states are partially constituted by self-conception in a way external states are not. This isn't because we grant authority; it's because of the nature of intentional states themselves.

The thesis's explanatory strategy is this: these three conditions (access asymmetry, autonomy requirements, constitutive role of self-conception) obtain independently and make a practice of granting first-person authority RATIONAL—more epistemically and practically efficient than alternatives. The practice, being rational, becomes entrenched. First-person authority then consists in occupying the privileged position within this practice.

This is not circular. Compare: "Money is valuable because we accept it in exchange" sounds circular, but isn't—it's a constitutive explanation where acceptance-practices create the value. Similarly, first-person authority is constituted by a practice, but the practice is justified by independent features (a-c) that don't presuppose the authority they explain.

The objection mistakes a constitutive account for a reductive one. The thesis doesn't claim to reduce authority to something entirely non-normative; it explains why a practice conferring this normative status is rationally justified by pre-existing asymmetries.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The triviality charge fails because conditions (a-c) have independent grounds that don't presuppose first-person authority:

- Access asymmetry (a) is a fact about embodiment and temporal position
- Autonomy costs (b) derive from practical requirements for agency
- Constitutive self-conception (c) follows from the nature of intentional states

These conditions make a practice of granting authority rational without presupposing that authority already exists. The thesis then identifies first-person authority with the status conferred by this rationally-grounded practice—this is constitutive explanation, not circular redescription.

The objection's error is treating any explanation that makes authority practice-dependent as trivial. But constitutive explanations (like money, promising, or legal rights) are substantive precisely when they show how normative statuses emerge from practices justified by independent features. That's what the thesis delivers.

The charge that this is "merely verbal relabeling" fails: the thesis explains WHY the practice exists (it's rational given a-c), WHAT makes it rational (independent asymmetries), and WHY confabulation doesn't undermine it (because authority is practice-constituted, not reliability-tracking). This is explanatory work, not redescription. The thesis stands.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT

The thesis equivocates on "authority" between two readings that make the argument work on one interpretation and interesting on the other, but cannot sustain both simultaneously.

Reading 1 (INTERESTING): Authority as a robust normative status that genuinely constrains rational inquiry—the kind of authority that makes it epistemically inappropriate or irrational to challenge first-person reports even when you have countervailing evidence. This is what makes the compatibility with confabulation surprising and worth arguing for.

Reading 2 (PLAUSIBLE): Authority as merely a defeasible default heuristic—a practical rule of thumb that we override whenever we have sufficient reason, with no deep normative force.

The thesis needs Reading 1 to be interesting. The whole point is to show that GENUINE first-person authority (the kind that creates asymmetry, that we're reluctant to override, that grounds autonomy) survives confabulation. If "authority" just means "a cancellable default we follow when convenient," the compatibility with confabulation is trivial—of course unreliable reports can have defeasible presumption in their favor.

But the argument only works on Reading 2. When the thesis claims authority is "rationally justified" by conditions (a-c), it's describing when we should PROVISIONALLY accept reports absent defeating evidence. The moment conditions (a-c) fail—when we DO have evidence, when costs are low, when adjustment won't occur—the thesis admits the "authority" evaporates. That's a defeasible heuristic, not genuine authority.

The seminar case exposed this: the defender admitted we "appropriately challenge" and "withhold the social-epistemic status" when conditions favor it. But genuine first-person authority doesn't evaporate just because challenging becomes cheap and well-evidenced. The asymmetry persists even in favorable conditions for challenge—that's what makes it authority rather than mere presumption.

The thesis cannot run on Reading 1 alone (conditions a-c don't justify authority that persists against evidence) or Reading 2 alone (such authority is trivially compatible with unreliability). It needs the robust sense to be interesting and the weak sense to be defensible.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis must either accept that it's defending only a weak, defeasible presumption (making the result uninteresting) or explain how conditions (a-c) justify authority that persists even when those conditions fail (which seems impossible). The defender's own responses reveal the equivocation—retreating to phenomenological authority and self-constitution when pressed, but claiming to explain the general phenomenon of first-person authority in the thesis.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection falsely assumes that genuine authority must be *evidence-insensitive* to avoid being merely a defeasible heuristic. This is wrong. Robust normative statuses can be constitutively tied to their justifying conditions without collapsing into mere rules of thumb.

Consider an analogy: property rights. Your ownership of your house is a genuine normative status—it constrains what others may do, grounds legal claims, structures social interaction. But property rights are defeasible: they can be overridden by eminent domain, dissolved by abandonment, defeated by superior claims. Does this make property rights "merely a defeasible heuristic with no deep normative force"? Obviously not. The defeasibility is built into the normative structure itself. Property rights are GENUINELY authoritative within their proper scope, and that scope is determined by the conditions that justify the institution.

First-person authority works the same way. It's a genuine normative status that structures epistemic practice: it makes challenge inappropriate *by default*, shifts burdens of proof, and grounds claims to autonomy and self-interpretation. But like all genuine authorities, it has constitutive limits. When its justifying conditions fail comprehensively, the authority is properly overridden—not because it was never real, but because all authority has scope conditions.

The objection's "Reading 1" demands authority be *absolute*—never properly overrideable. But no interesting authority works this way. Parental authority is genuine but limited by the child's interests. Medical authority is real but bounded by patient autonomy. Judicial authority is robust but constrained by jurisdiction. An authority that could never be appropriately challenged wouldn't be a species of genuine authority—it would be infallibility or despotism.

What makes first-person authority *authority* rather than mere presumption is this: **the default against challenge is normatively grounded and systematic, not merely pragmatic**. It's not that we accept first-person reports "when convenient"—it's that the practice creates genuine entitlements and obligations. You owe me something when you challenge my self-ascription: justification, evidence, recognition of costs. I have a claim against cavalier dismissal of my self-reports. These are normative relations, not efficiency heuristics.

The seminar case doesn't show equivocation—it shows authority operating properly within its scope. When I said we "appropriately challenge" there, I meant: the conditions that normally make challenge costly and evidentially unsupported have failed *enough* that the balance shifts. But notice: even there, we don't treat the third-person perspective as simply *replacing* the first-person one. We demand the speaker *reconcile* the tension, we give them opportunity to explain, we recognize their self-ascription as having *some* weight even against behavioral evidence. This is precisely what genuine-but-bounded authority looks like.

Compare how we'd treat a third party's claim about the speaker's beliefs: "I know what they really believe better than they do." We'd demand extraordinary evidence for this. The asymmetry persists—not absolutely, but systematically and normatively. That's Reading 1, not Reading 2.

The objection claims the thesis "admits the authority evaporates" when conditions fail. Wrong. The thesis says authority can be *appropriately overridden* when its justifying conditions fail comprehensively—which is exactly what we should expect from a genuine but bounded normative status. The authority doesn't "evaporate"—it's defeated by countervailing considerations in a normatively structured way.

Finally, on the phenomenological retreat: this wasn't a retreat to a different kind of authority—it was specifying what first-person authority IS authority over. You have authority over your mental life as you experience it, not over every claim that embeds a mental-state term. Authority over "what I believe" is authority over the phenomenological and self-constituting aspects of belief, not over whether that belief is consistent or well-evidenced. This isn't equivocation; it's precision about the scope of a genuine authority.

The interesting result stands: even if introspection is unreliable (confabulation), the practice of granting first-person authority over self-ascriptions remains rationally justified because the conditions that ground it (a-c) don't require reliability. This is surprising because we assumed authority must track reliability. The authority is genuine—it structures practice, creates obligations, grounds autonomy—but it's bounded by its justifying conditions, as all genuine authorities are.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds, but reveals something important about the thesis's commitments.

The equivocation charge fails because genuine authority need not be absolute or evidence-insensitive. The objection wrongly assumes that if authority can be appropriately overridden when its justifying conditions fail, it must be merely a defeasible heuristic. But this conflates defeasibility with lack of normative force. Property rights, parental authority, and judicial authority are all defeasible yet genuine. First-person authority is the same: it creates real normative constraints (burdens of proof, presumptions, obligations to justify challenges) while remaining bounded by the conditions that justify it.

The thesis operates on a single, coherent conception: authority as a robust but bounded normative status within epistemic practice. This is interesting (it's surprising that such authority survives unreliability) and defensible (conditions a-c justify it without requiring reliability).

However, the defence exposes that the thesis must be more careful about specifying the *scope* of first-person authority. The defender correctly notes that authority over "what I believe" concerns phenomenological and self-constituting aspects, not consistency or evidential support. This isn't equivocation, but it does mean the thesis is making a more specific claim than initially apparent: first-person authority is authority over *self-ascriptions as experiential and self-constituting acts*, not over every proposition containing mental-state terms.

This precision strengthens rather than weakens the thesis. It explains why we can appropriately challenge someone's claim that their beliefs are consistent while still granting them authority over what they take themselves to believe. The authority is genuine and systematic within its proper domain—exactly what we should expect from a practice-constituted normative status justified by independent conditions.

The thesis stands, with clarified scope.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis protects itself from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as "first-person authority." Every apparent case where authority should fail gets reclassified as "outside the scope of genuine authority."

Consider: we present the seminar case where someone's self-ascription conflicts with overwhelming behavioral evidence and all conditions (a-c) seem to fail. The defender responds that actually conditions (a-c) still obtain "for the phenomenological and self-constituting aspects." When pressed on equivocation, the defender specifies that authority is really only over "self-ascriptions as experiential and self-constituting acts, not over every proposition containing mental-state terms."

This makes the thesis unfalsifiable. What would count as a case where first-person authority genuinely fails in a way that challenges the thesis? Any case where we appropriately override a self-ascription gets redefined: either (1) conditions (a-c) "actually still obtain" in some respect we hadn't considered, or (2) that particular self-ascription falls "outside the scope" of what authority really covers, or (3) the authority isn't "evaporating" but merely being "appropriately overridden in a way consistent with it being genuine."

The thesis claims to explain why "first-person authority" survives confabulation, but "first-person authority" now means only: authority over however-much-of-first-person-claims-we-actually-grant-authority-to-in-practice. The scope of "genuine" authority contracts to fit exactly whatever our practices actually protect. This isn't a substantive claim about what justifies an independently specified phenomenon—it's a tautology that whatever authority we grant is the authority that's justified by whatever conditions obtain when we grant it.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem. The thesis needs independent specification of what first-person authority consists in before explaining why it's justified. Instead, it defines authority through the practice, defines the practice as justified by conditions (a-c), then reinterprets both "authority" and "conditions obtain" to fit whatever our actual practices are. Any counterexample gets absorbed by adjusting the scope or reinterpreting when conditions "really" obtain.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The unfalsifiability charge mistakes principled scope-specification for ad hoc retreat. The thesis is falsifiable—but you must understand what it claims before you can test it.

**The thesis makes three empirically vulnerable predictions:**

1. **Predictive claim about practice structure**: Wherever we observe systematic first-person authority in actual epistemic practice, conditions (a-c) will obtain for that domain. If we found robust first-person authority in domains where challengers have *superior* access, where challenge carries *no* autonomy costs, and where self-ascriptions are *not* self-constituting, the thesis would be falsified.

2. **Predictive claim about practice variation**: Across cultures and contexts where conditions (a-c) vary in strength, the degree of first-person authority should vary correspondingly. If we found cultures that grant identical first-person authority despite radically different costs of challenge or evidence asymmetries, the thesis would be falsified.

3. **Predictive claim about boundary cases**: For mental states where the phenomenological/self-constituting aspect is minimal (perhaps unconscious biases, or automatic perceptual classifications), first-person authority should be weaker or absent. If people have just as much authority over "I have implicit racial bias" as over "I'm in pain," despite the former lacking experiential immediacy and self-constituting character, the thesis would be falsified.

**These are substantive, falsifiable claims.** The thesis isn't defining authority as "whatever we grant"—it's claiming that what we grant tracks conditions (a-c), and that these conditions justify the practice without requiring reliability.

Now address the specific complaint: did the seminar response engage in ad hoc scope-restriction?

**No.** The scope specification—that authority concerns phenomenological and self-constituting aspects—was not invented to handle the seminar case. It follows directly from the justifying conditions themselves:

- Condition (a) (evidence asymmetry): The first-person has superior access to *their experiential states as they occur*. This is the domain where the asymmetry is sharpest. For dispositional states inferable from behavior, the asymmetry weakens.

- Condition (c) (self-constitution): Mental states that are *partially constituted by self-conception* are precisely those where challenge is self-defeating. These are experiential and deliberative states, not purely behavioral dispositions.

The scope follows from the justification, not from a desire to evade counterexamples. And this scope is independently testable: we should find that authority is strongest for occurrent experiences ("I'm in pain"), moderate for self-constituting attitudes ("I intend to vote"), and weakest for dispositional states inferable from behavior ("I'm a generous person"). This matches actual practice—not because we defined it that way, but because the conditions predict it.

**On the seminar case specifically:** The defender didn't say conditions (a-c) "still obtain" in some mysterious way. The claim was precise: for the *phenomenological* report ("it seems to me I believe X"), condition (a) obtains because the speaker has direct access to how things seem; for the *dispositional* claim ("I believe X" as a stable state), condition (a) is weakened and we appropriately give less authority. This isn't ad hoc—it's applying the stated conditions to distinguish two readings of the claim.

**The objection's error:** You're treating "first-person authority" as if it must be a monolithic, all-or-nothing phenomenon. But the thesis explicitly analyzes it as a practice justified by specific conditions—so naturally, where those conditions obtain more or less strongly, authority should be more or less robust. This is a *feature* of the analysis, not a bug. It explains the gradations we actually observe: why we're more confident challenging "I'm a good person" than "I'm in pain," why we demand more evidence to override "I want coffee" than "I'm generous."

**What would actually falsify the thesis?**

- Finding robust first-person authority in domains where conditions (a-c) clearly fail (e.g., if we granted people authority over their blood pressure readings despite meters being more reliable and no autonomy costs)
- Finding that cultures vary in first-person authority in ways uncorrelated with variations in conditions (a-c)
- Finding that confabulation research undermines authority in domains where conditions (a-c) remain strong
- Finding that authority persists equally for mental states that lack phenomenological presence and self-constituting character

None of these obtain. The thesis correctly predicts that confabulation research (showing unreliability) doesn't undermine authority *where conditions (a-c) still hold*, while authority appropriately weakens *where those conditions weaken*. This is empirical success, not unfalsifiability.
