The complaint that authority "contracts to fit our practices" inverts the actual dialectic. The thesis *predicts* the contours of our practice from independent conditions. That the prediction succeeds is evidence for the thesis, not proof of vacuity.
**VERDICT**
**The defence succeeds.** The unfalsifiability charge fails because the thesis makes multiple empirically vulnerable predictions that could be falsified by actual findings about practice variation, cross-cultural differences, and domain-specific patterns of authority.

The objection mistakes *principled scope-specification* for *ad hoc retreat*. The thesis doesn't define authority as "whatever we grant in practice"—it claims that what we grant tracks conditions (a-c), and derives the scope of authority from those conditions. The scope specification (phenomenological and self-constituting aspects) follows directly from the justifying conditions themselves, not from a desire to evade counterexamples.

The seminar case response was not ad hoc: it applied the stated conditions to distinguish authority over occurrent phenomenology (where condition (a) strongly obtains) from authority over dispositional states (where it weakens). This distinction is independently motivated by the theory and predicts the gradations we observe in actual practice.

What makes the thesis substantive is that it explains *why* authority has the contours it does (strongest for pain, moderate for intentions, weakest for character traits) by reference to independently specifiable conditions. This generates falsifiable predictions about how authority should vary across domains and cultures.

The thesis stands. It is falsifiable, empirically adequate, and explanatorily successful. The fact that it correctly predicts the boundaries of first-person authority is a virtue, not a vice.

</details>

---

### O4. Interpretivism is unstable because the interpreter position it posits for self-knowledge cannot be occupied without already presupposing first-person authority over one's interpretive judgments.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The interpretivist position of Carruthers and Gopnik, which treats self-interpretation as explanatorily basic.
- **Sketch:** Carruthers and Gopnik argue we interpret ourselves like we interpret others. But interpretation requires making judgments: 'this behavior is best explained by jealousy rather than concern.' For these interpretive judgments to constitute self-knowledge, I must have first-person authority over them—I must know that I judge myself jealous rather than concerned. But if I need interpretation to know my own mental states, I need interpretation to know my interpretive judgments too, since they're also mental states. This launches a regress. The interpretivist must either grant first-person authority at the level of interpretive judgment (conceding the game) or claim I interpret my interpretations, ad infinitum. The key premise is that interpretive judgments are themselves mental states subject to the same epistemic constraints as other mental states.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Regress argument has force but similar meta-level challenges to interpretivism have been raised; competent execution.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the problem is special to interpretivism because its solution self-applies problematically, unlike other epistemological theories
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, answered. Defence demonstrated interpretivism's status as knowledge-theory requires the strong reading, making this a genuine dilemma not equivocation
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, answered. Defence showed constraints track Carruthers/Gopnik's actual commitments rather than arbitrary redefinition
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claimed distinctive contributions but the objection correctly identified this as a known problem presented as novel
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The self-interpretation regress (identified by Shoemaker, Gertler, and Cassam) applies with particular force to Carruthers and Gopnik's empirically-grounded interpretivism because their account of interpretive judgments as mental states subject to mindreading creates a dilemma: either grant first-person authority to interpretive judgments (abandoning the view's theoretical motivation) or accept infinite regress (leaving self-knowledge ungrounded)
- **Flaw:** The thesis presents a well-established objection (the self-interpretation regress, explicitly articulated by Gertler, Cassam, and others) as though it were a novel discovery of instability

<details><summary>1 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) Interpretivist accounts are self-undermining because they rely on first-person authority about inference and reasoning to construct their third-personal interpretations, creating a vicious circularity.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis claims interpretivism is "unstable" because interpreters must have first-person authority over their interpretive judgments. But this merely restates a familiar feature of all epistemological theories: that judgment itself requires some epistemic capacity. Every theory of knowledge must account for how we know our own judgments—empiricists face this with observation reports, foundationalists with basic beliefs, coherentists with coherence judgments. The interpretivist faces exactly the same structural challenge everyone faces, no more and no less. Pointing out that "interpretive judgments are mental states too" is trivial—of course they are. The supposed "instability" is just the general problem of epistemic access to one's own cognitive states, redescribed with interpretivist vocabulary. The weak point is the claim that this creates a *special* problem for interpretivism. It doesn't. The interpretivist can respond exactly as others do: perhaps interpretive capacities are exercised rather than known, or perhaps some cognitive states (like judging) have different epistemic properties than others (like desiring). The thesis presents a universal epistemological challenge as though it were a devastating objection to one particular theory.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This attack has some force in showing the problem isn't unique, but the thesis might survive by arguing interpretivism specifically *cannot* use standard moves available to other theories (like distinguishing judgment from other mental states), making the problem genuinely destabilizing for this view in particular.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what makes the problem special for interpretivism. Other epistemological theories do not claim that *the very capacity they invoke to solve the problem* is itself subject to the problem. The interpretivist's distinctive claim is that I know my mental states *by interpreting behavioral and other evidence*. But interpretive judgment is not like observation or intuition of basic beliefs—it is itself a complex mental state (a judgment with content, involving comparison of explanatory hypotheses). 

The interpretivist cannot distinguish interpretive judgments from other mental states without abandoning interpretivism. If they say "interpretive capacities are exercised rather than known," they concede that *something* about the mind is transparent or self-intimating—precisely what they deny about desires, beliefs, and emotions. If they say judging has "different epistemic properties," they must explain why. The natural answer—that we have direct access to our judgments—is exactly the first-person authority they reject.

Foundationalists can stop their regress with non-inferential basic beliefs; that's their theory's architecture. Coherentists can treat coherence judgments as part of the mutually supporting web; that's their architecture. But interpretivists cannot treat interpretive judgments as transparent without contradicting their core claim that mental states require interpretation. They also cannot interpret their interpretations without regress, because *that* meta-interpretation is also a mental state requiring interpretation.

The empiricist parallel fails: observation reports are not themselves observations, but interpretive judgments *are* mental states of exactly the kind interpretivism says require interpretation.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The problem is genuinely special because interpretivism's proposed solution (interpretation) applies to itself in a way that generates either contradiction or regress, while other theories' solutions do not self-apply problematically.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "first-person authority." On one reading, it means *infallible* or *non-interpretive* access to mental states—direct transparency that interpretivism explicitly rejects. On another reading, it means merely *having* or *making* judgments without needing to epistemically access them as objects. The argument needs both readings to work.

The thesis is *plausible* when "first-person authority over interpretive judgments" means simply that I can make interpretive judgments—that I judge myself jealous rather than concerned. This is trivially true: interpretation requires making judgments. But on this reading, interpretivism is perfectly stable. Making a judgment doesn't require knowing-that-I'm-making-it in any problematic sense. I simply judge, and that judgment constitutes my (interpretivist) self-knowledge.

The thesis is *interesting* only when "first-person authority" means the stronger claim that I must have special epistemic access to my interpretive judgments—that I must *know* I'm judging myself jealous with the kind of authority interpretivism denies. On this reading, the regress genuinely threatens. But this reading is implausible: why must interpretive judgments be known rather than simply made? The interpretivist denies we need such access to desires and beliefs; they can equally deny we need it for interpretive judgments.

The weak point is the claim "I must have first-person authority over them." This slides between exercising judgment (weak, plausible) and having epistemic authority over judgment (strong, interesting).

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This equivocation is genuinely damaging. The argument appears to force a dilemma only by conflating making judgments with knowing-that-one-makes-them.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses that interpretivism is a theory of *self-knowledge*, not merely self-constitution. The interpretivist claims interpretation is how we *know* our mental states—that's the entire point of the view. So when I interpret myself as jealous, this interpretation is supposed to constitute my *knowing* I'm jealous, not merely my being jealous.

Now consider what this requires. For my interpretation to constitute knowledge, it must get things right: my interpretive judgment must correctly identify my state. But "correctly identify" implies a distinction between the judgment and what it's about. If my interpretive judgment *is* my jealousy (if judging-myself-jealous just is being-jealous), then there's nothing for interpretation to discover—I'm not gaining self-knowledge through interpretation, I'm just having mental states. The interpretive judgment must be *about* a prior or independent mental state to count as knowledge of it.

But if the interpretive judgment is distinct from and about the jealousy, then it's a further mental state—one with content, one that can be correct or incorrect. And now the question arises: how do I know *this* state? How do I know I'm making the judgment "I'm jealous rather than concerned" as opposed to some other judgment?

The objection says I can "simply judge" without knowing-that-I'm-judging. But this collapses the knowledge claim. If I don't know which judgment I'm making, then I don't know which interpretation I'm giving of myself, and therefore don't know which mental state I'm attributing to myself. The interpretation cannot constitute self-knowledge if I don't know what interpretation I'm making.

The interpretivist cannot say "the judgment itself constitutes the knowledge without needing to be known" because that would make the interpretive judgment transparent or self-intimating—exactly what they deny about other mental states. They would be granting that *some* mental states (interpretive judgments) are self-presenting while others (desires, emotions) require interpretation. But this is arbitrary special pleading unless they can explain why judgments get this privilege. And any such explanation will either invoke first-person authority (conceding the game) or require interpreting the interpretations (launching the regress).

The distinction between exercising and knowing isn't available here because interpretivism is precisely a thesis about *how we know*, not about what we do.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The equivocation charge fails because interpretivism's status as a theory of self-*knowledge* requires that interpretive judgments be epistemically accessible, not merely exercised. The weak reading (merely making judgments) doesn't give us self-knowledge; the strong reading (knowing our judgments) does but generates the instability. This isn't equivocation—it's a genuine dilemma.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because it protects itself by redefining what counts as "genuine interpretivism" whenever a response is offered. 

When the interpretivist says "interpretive judgments need not be known, only exercised," the thesis responds: "That's not real interpretivism—interpretivism is a theory of *knowledge*." But Carruthers and Gopnik could perfectly well hold that interpretation is a *process* or *capacity* we exercise, and that self-knowledge consists in the *outputs* of this process, not in knowing the process itself. The thesis rules this out by stipulation.

When the interpretivist distinguishes interpretive capacities from their objects (like distinguishing seeing from what's seen), the thesis responds: "That grants first-person authority—you've conceded!" But the interpretivist could say interpretation is simply a cognitive mechanism that operates without self-awareness, like visual processing. The thesis redefines this as "conceding the game" rather than engaging it as a substantive position.

When the interpretivist accepts some regress as unproblematic (as coherentists accept circular justification), the thesis responds: "That's incoherent for interpretivism specifically." But this just reasserts that interpretivism cannot have the structure it claims to have.

Every interpretivist response gets ruled "not really interpretivism" or "actually concedes first-person authority" by definitional fiat. The weak point is the repeated move of redefining interpretivism to exclude whatever response is offered, making the thesis immune to counterexamples by controlling what counts as the target view.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — There is something to this. The thesis does seem to operate with a narrow definition of interpretivism that excludes certain natural responses. However, the thesis might survive by showing these redefinitions track genuine commitments of the interpretivist position as actually defended by Carruthers and Gopnik, rather than being arbitrary.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection confuses principled distinction from stipulative redefinition. The thesis doesn't arbitrarily exclude responses—it holds interpretivism to its own stated commitments as defended by Carruthers and Gopnik.

Consider what interpretivism actually claims in their work: that we know our own mental states through the same third-personal interpretive process we use for others, that there is no special first-person access, that self-knowledge is a matter of inference from behavioral and contextual evidence. These are substantive commitments, not my stipulations.

When the interpretivist says "interpretation is just a process we exercise, and self-knowledge consists in the outputs," this isn't a coherent interpretivist position—it's abandoning the view. If the process operates "without self-awareness" like visual processing, then we have no epistemic access to whether we're interpreting correctly, whether we're interpreting at all, or what interpretation we're making. But interpretivism is supposed to explain how we *know* our mental states. An unconscious process that delivers outputs we cannot assess is not a theory of knowledge—it's a causal story that leaves the epistemic question untouched.

The visual processing analogy actually proves the point. We don't have first-person authority over our visual processing mechanisms—we have it over what we see. The interpretivist who adopts this structure must say we have first-person authority over our interpretive conclusions (what we "see" about ourselves) even if not over the process. But those conclusions are mental states (judgments), and interpretivism says we lack first-person authority over mental states. The interpretivist cannot simultaneously deny first-person authority (their core claim) and invoke it for interpretive outputs (their escape route).

When the thesis says distinguishing interpretive capacities from their objects "grants first-person authority," this isn't redefinition—it's pointing out that if I have direct, non-interpretive access to my interpretive conclusions, then I have exactly the first-person authority over (some) mental states that interpretivism denies exists. The interpretivist cannot carve out interpretive judgments as special without explaining why they're exempt from the interpretive requirement, and any such explanation will either be arbitrary or will generalize to undermine interpretivism.

The regress comparison to coherentism fails. Coherentists embrace circular justification as their *solution*—mutual support is how justification works on their view. But interpretivists don't embrace infinite regress as their solution. They claim interpretation grounds self-knowledge. An infinite regress means interpretation never grounds anything—it's a reductio, not a feature.

The thesis holds interpretivism to what Carruthers and Gopnik actually defend: that self-knowledge works through evidence-based interpretation, that there's no privileged first-person access, that we know ourselves as we know others. Responses that abandon these commitments aren't "versions of interpretivism the thesis excludes"—they're different theories wearing interpretivist labels.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. The thesis doesn't protect itself by redefinition—it identifies genuine tensions between interpretivism's commitments and the resources needed to escape the regress. The "unfalsifiable" charge mistakes principled application of the view's own stated commitments for arbitrary boundary-drawing. 

When responses require either (a) granting first-person authority to some mental states, (b) treating interpretation as epistemically inert, or (c) accepting that self-knowledge is never grounded, they genuinely abandon what makes interpretivism interpretivism. The thesis correctly identifies that interpretivism cannot adopt these moves without ceasing to be an interpretivist theory of self-knowledge. This is substantive philosophical argument, not definitional protection.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing an original "instability" in interpretivism, but this is simply the self-interpretation regress that has been central to discussions of interpretivism since its inception. Ryle's "systematic elusiveness of 'I'" (1949) identified that introspection of introspection generates regress. Shoemaker's "self-blindness" argument (1994) established that interpretive self-knowledge requires the interpreter to have non-interpretive access to their own interpretive activity. More recently, Gertler (2011) explicitly argues that interpretivism about self-knowledge fails because "the interpretive process itself must be accessible to the subject in a non-interpretive way" to avoid regress—this is precisely the thesis's point about first-person authority over interpretive judgments. Cassam (2014) calls this the "transparency problem" for interpretivism: interpretation requires judgments that must themselves be transparent. Even sympathetic interpretivists like Lawlor (2009) acknowledge this regress challenge and attempt solutions (like limiting interpretivism to certain mental states). The weak point is the thesis's presentation as identifying a novel instability rather than engaging with this established dialectic. It's a known objection with a name (the self-interpretation regress) and multiple formulations by Shoemaker, Gertler, and Cassam.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is genuinely damaging. The thesis appears to rediscover a well-established objection without acknowledging or engaging the existing literature, which undermines its contribution and suggests the author hasn't surveyed the territory.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection mistakes overlap with established literature for lack of contribution. Yes, the self-interpretation regress is known—but the thesis makes a specific structural claim that differs from the cited formulations in philosophically significant ways.

Shoemaker's self-blindness argument shows that *some* non-interpretive access is needed, but doesn't identify precisely where or why the regress bites. Gertler argues the interpretive *process* must be accessible, but "process" is ambiguous—it could mean the mechanism, the activity, or the output. Cassam's "transparency problem" notes that interpretation requires transparent judgments, but doesn't develop why transparency at the judgment level specifically undermines interpretivism rather than just requiring a mixed view.

The thesis makes a sharper claim: that interpretive *judgments* (not processes, mechanisms, or activities) are themselves mental states with content, and that interpretivism's own theoretical commitments require they be interpreted, generating not merely regress but a specific *instability*—the view must either contradict itself (grant first-person authority) or collapse into infinite regress (interpret interpretations forever). This is a dilemma, not just a regress observation.

Moreover, the thesis targets Carruthers and Gopnik specifically, and neither Shoemaker (1994), Gertler (2011), nor Cassam (2014) address their particular version of interpretivism, which emerged largely from empirical psychology and cognitive science rather than the philosophical introspection tradition. Carruthers's distinctive claim that interpretation is *mindreading turned inward* might have resources unavailable to earlier interpretivists, and showing the instability affects even this empirically-grounded version is a substantive contribution.

The thesis also emphasizes that this isn't merely a problem requiring a solution—it's an *instability* showing the position cannot coherently maintain its core commitments. Lawlor's strategy of limiting interpretivism to certain mental states is precisely what the thesis predicts and diagnoses as concession: once you exempt some mental states (interpretive judgments) from the interpretive requirement, you've abandoned the theoretical motivation for interpretivism about the others.

Finally, even if the core problem is recognized, the thesis's specific framing—that the interpreter position "cannot be occupied" without presupposing what it denies—may illuminate why various interpretivist responses fail in ways the literature hasn't fully articulated. The occupation metaphor captures something: there's no stable standpoint from which to conduct interpretation if that standpoint itself requires interpretation to be known.

VERDICT

The defence partially succeeds but the objection significantly damages the thesis. The thesis does make some distinctive contributions: the focus on interpretive judgments specifically as mental states, the dilemma structure (contradict or regress), and the application to Carruthers/Gopnik's empirical version. These are not nothing.

However, the objection is fundamentally correct that this is a known problem in the literature. The thesis's framing as revealing an "instability" that interpretivism hasn't recognized is misleading when Gertler explicitly makes nearly the same point, and when the self-interpretation regress is a standard objection interpretivists address. Even if the thesis adds precision or applies the objection to a new target, it should acknowledge this lineage and position itself as *developing* or *sharpening* an established challenge, not discovering it.

The thesis survives as a competent articulation and application of a known objection, but it's significantly weakened by presenting itself as more original than it is. A version that engaged Gertler, Cassam, and interpretivist responses to the regress problem would be stronger—it could show why existing responses fail or why Carruthers/Gopnik's version faces the problem in a particularly acute form. As stated, the thesis risks appearing unaware of the dialectical context it enters.

The contribution is real but modest: applying a known objection carefully to a specific target. That's legitimate philosophical work, but not the revelation of instability the presentation suggests.

</details>

---

### O5. The interpretive alternative is unstable because systematic interpretation of a subject requires holding some self-ascriptions immune from reinterpretation, which reintroduces the very observational model it rejects.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Carruthers, Gopnik, and other proponents of the mindreading model of introspection
- **Sketch:** Carruthers and Gopnik claim we interpret ourselves as we interpret others. But interpretation requires fixed points—data that constrains rather than undergoes interpretation. When interpreting others, their sincere avowals often serve as such fixed points. But if I'm interpreting my own sincere avowal 'I intend to φ,' what serves as the fixed point? Not the avowal itself (that's what's being interpreted). Not behavior (behavior is what the avowal was meant to explain). The interpretivist must either: (a) allow some self-ascriptions observational status after all, or (b) embrace interpretive holism so radical that no self-ascription ever achieves stability. Option (a) abandons interpretivism; option (b) makes self-knowledge impossible. The key premise is that interpretation is a constrained activity requiring uninterpreted constraints.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The fixed-point problem for interpretation is real and worth articulating, though interpretivists may have coherentist responses available.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender's coherentist response was absorbed by their own bootstrapping concession—they showed the objection applies but narrowed when it applies (synchronic vs diachronic)
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 3/5, answered. The defender successfully distinguished self-interpretation from external interpretation via the reflexive collapse asymmetry—the interpreter/interpreted identity creates unique instability
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 2/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defender conceded the jazz case defeats synchronic versions but absorbed the objection by retreating to a diachronic bootstrapping requirement
  - TRIVIAL — landed 1/5, answered. The defender correctly identified this as confusing substantive architectural problems (bootstrapping, reflexive regress) with terminological disputes
- 🔧 **Survives as:** The interpretivist model of self-knowledge faces a developmental bootstrapping problem: either some early mental states had observational status that initiated the interpretive framework, or the acquisition of interpretive self-knowledge remains unexplained
- **Flaw:** The defender repeatedly absorbed objections by narrowing scope (synchronic to diachronic, immediate to developmental) rather than meeting them, and conceded the coherentist response faces bootstrapping problems

<details><summary>1 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in privileged access to mental states but in privileged access to the standing interpretive question one is entitled to leave open.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This objection is essentially Crispin Wright's "rule-following consideration" applied to self-interpretation, most explicitly developed in his critiques of interpretivist theories of meaning and mental content. Wright argues (particularly in "Rails to Infinity" and related work on Wittgenstein) that interpretive practices require "bedrock" - something that isn't itself subject to interpretation - or else we face a regress where every interpretation requires a further interpretation. The premise that "interpretation is a constrained activity requiring uninterpreted constraints" is precisely Wright's point about the need for what he calls "primitive uptake" or "direct apprehension" at some level. More specifically, Bilgrami's "self-knowledge and resentment" work makes nearly this exact move against interpretivist self-knowledge: he argues that if we're always interpreting our own mental states, we lose the stability needed for genuine self-knowledge and must smuggle in some form of direct access. The weak point is the claim that interpretivists "must" accept either horn of the dilemma - perhaps they can accept radical holism while maintaining that coherence over time provides sufficient stability without requiring observational fixed points.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem that interpretivists must address. The position has been articulated by respected philosophers and identifies a genuine architectural tension, though sophisticated interpretivists might have coherentist responses available.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The dilemma rests on a false assumption: that interpretation requires *uninterpreted* fixed points rather than *relatively stable* points within an evolving interpretive web. Consider how we interpret texts or cultures—we don't need incorrigible data points; we need sufficient coherence constraints.

The interpretivist can embrace option (b) while denying it makes self-knowledge impossible. Here's how:

**The Coherentist Response**: When I interpret my avowal "I intend to φ," the constraint comes not from any single uninterpreted datum but from the *entire network* of my other mental state attributions, behavioral patterns, and rational norms. My interpretation of this particular intention is constrained by:
- Other intentions I've attributed to myself
- Beliefs about my situation  
- My track record of intention-attribution
- Norms of practical rationality
- Patterns in my behavior over time

No single element is immune from reinterpretation, but the *system as a whole* provides constraint. This is exactly how interpretation works elsewhere. When interpreting a difficult passage in a text, we're constrained by our interpretation of other passages—yet no passage is absolutely immune from reinterpretation in light of the whole.

**Stability Without Foundations**: The objection conflates two things: (1) having no *permanently* fixed points, and (2) having no *currently* stable points. Interpretivism needs only the latter. My self-interpretation achieves stability through coherence—the same way a scientific theory achieves stability without any observation statements being unrevisable in principle. Quine showed this works for empirical knowledge generally; why not for self-knowledge?

**Against the Regress Worry**: There's no vicious regress because interpretation isn't a sequential process requiring completion of one interpretation before another begins. It's holistic and simultaneous—like adjusting multiple variables to solve a system of equations. When I interpret "I intend to φ," I'm not first interpreting something else that then serves as bedrock; I'm seeking reflective equilibrium across all my self-attributions at once.

The Wright/Bilgrami worry assumes interpretation must have the architecture of inference from premises. But coherentist interpretation has no such architecture—it's mutual adjustment toward coherence, not derivation from foundations.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails**, but not immediately.

The coherentist response has genuine force against cruder versions of the objection. However, it faces a devastating problem: **the bootstrapping objection**. 

For coherence to constrain, there must be *enough* initial self-attributions with *enough* stability to generate coherence pressures. But if interpretivism applies "all the way down," where do these initial attributions come from? 

In text interpretation, we have perceptual access to marks on paper—uninterpreted data that gets the interpretive process started. In interpreting others, we have their behavior and avowals as observational input. But in self-interpretation, if even my current seeming-to-intend-to-φ is itself something I must interpret rather than simply have, what generates the initial web that coherence then refines?

The coherentist might reply: "The web was always already there—acquired through earlier interpretation." But this just pushes the problem back. At some point, interpretation must *get started*, and starting requires something not-yet-interpreted to interpret.

Moreover, the analogy to Quinean holism fails because Quine's web includes *observation sentences* at the periphery—sentences whose truth we're disposed to accept based on sensory stimulation, even if they're theoretically revisable. These provide the crucial input that coherence then organizes. The interpretivist denies any analogous "self-observation sentences."

The defence shows that interpretivism isn't *incoherent*, but the objection succeeds in showing it's *unstable*: it must either smuggle in something observational to get interpretation started (confirming the thesis), or face the bootstrapping problem that makes self-knowledge mysterious rather than explained.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If this argument worked, it would prove that we cannot interpret anything at all—texts, artworks, historical events, or other people—without having some uninterpreted, observationally given fixed points. But this is false. Consider literary interpretation: when interpreting a complex novel, there is no single sentence or passage that serves as an uninterpreted fixed point immune from reinterpretation. Every element—even seemingly straightforward descriptive sentences—can be reread as ironic, metaphorical, or unreliable in light of the whole. Yet literary interpretation is clearly possible and often achieves stability through coherence alone. Similarly, anthropologists interpret alien cultures without any cultural facts serving as observationally given; every practice is interpreted in light of every other, with the entire web adjusting toward coherence. The argument's key premise—that "interpretation is a constrained activity requiring uninterpreted constraints"—would make all these interpretive practices impossible or unintelligible. Since they manifestly succeed, the premise must be false. The weak point is precisely this premise: it overgeneralizes from cases where interpretation happens to have observational anchors to the claim that it must always have them. Interpretivism about self-knowledge can simply adopt the same coherentist structure that demonstrably works elsewhere.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This is a weak objection. While the argument does rely on a general claim about interpretation requiring constraints, there's a principled distinction available: self-interpretation faces unique bootstrapping problems that external interpretation doesn't face, because the interpreter and interpreted are identical. In external interpretation, the interpreter has independent cognitive access to their own interpretive activity, which provides the needed stability. This blocks the "proves too much" move.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The "proves too much" objection fails because it ignores a crucial asymmetry between self-interpretation and interpretation of external objects. **The defence of the thesis rests on this asymmetry.**

**The Asymmetry Argument**: When I interpret a text, artwork, or culture, *I myself* am not part of what's being interpreted. My interpretive activity—my considering hypotheses, weighing evidence, revising judgments—occurs in a cognitive space that is *outside* the domain of interpretation. This provides the needed stability: however holistic my interpretation of the novel becomes, *my own cognitive acts of interpreting* are not themselves under interpretation in that same activity.

But in self-interpretation, this separation collapses. When I interpret my intention to φ, the very cognitive activity of forming and revising that interpretation is *itself* something I must interpret. If I think "Perhaps I don't really intend to φ but am deceiving myself," this thought is itself a mental state requiring interpretation. Am I genuinely suspicious of my intention, or am I engaging in performative self-doubt? The interpreter and interpreted are the same, which creates a unique instability.

**Literary Interpretation Disanalogy**: The objector says no sentence in a novel is immune from reinterpretation. True—but the *reader's perceptual access to the marks on the page* is not itself being interpreted in the same activity. I can see that the text says "Call me Ishmael" even while interpreting whether this is sincere, ironic, or unreliable narration. The perceptual given anchors the interpretive activity, even if no *interpretation* is immune from revision.

Similarly, when interpreting another person, their *behavior and utterances* are observationally available to me, even if my *interpretation* of what mental states they express is holistic and revisable. I see them raise their hand; whether this means they're hailing a taxi or stretching is interpretive, but that *something happened* is not.

**The Self-Interpretation Problem**: In the self-case, there is no analogous separation. What corresponds to seeing the marks on the page or observing the hand-raising? The interpretivist cannot say "my seeming to intend to φ" because that seeming is itself something requiring interpretation rather than observation. Unlike external interpretation, where the interpreter has observational access to the raw material that interpretation organizes, self-interpretation has no such raw material—or rather, if it did, that would *be* the observational model the interpretivist rejects.

**Anthropology Disanalogy**: The anthropologist observes behaviors, artifacts, and utterances—these are the uninterpreted (in the sense of not-yet-culturally-interpreted) data. What practices *mean* is interpreted holistically, but that certain practices occur is observationally given. The anthropologist's own cognitive activity of theorizing is not part of the culture being interpreted.

The thesis doesn't claim interpretation *in general* requires uninterpreted constraints at the object level. It claims that when the interpreter and interpreted coincide—when the interpretive activity is itself part of what must be interpreted—you get either regress or the need for something observational to break the circle.
**VERDICT**
**The defence succeeds.**

The "proves too much" objection rests on a false equivalence between different types of interpretation. The thesis identifies a problem specific to self-interpretation that doesn't arise in external interpretation: the **reflexive collapse** of the interpreter/interpreted distinction.

In every case the objector cites—texts, cultures, other minds—the interpreter maintains a position *outside* what's being interpreted. This external position provides the cognitive stability needed for interpretation to proceed, even when the interpretation itself is holistic and lacks foundations. The reader's perceptual and cognitive processes aren't part of the novel being interpreted; the anthropologist's theoretical reasoning isn't part of the culture being studied; the mind-reader's own mental states aren't what they're attributing to others.

Self-interpretation lacks this asymmetry. The interpretive activity and its object are identical, creating what we might call the **reflexive regress**: if I'm interpreting my mental state M, and this interpretation is itself a mental state M*, then M* also requires interpretation, yielding M**, and so on. In external interpretation, this regress doesn't arise because the interpretive states (mine) are separate from the interpreted object (the text, culture, or other mind).

The objector might reply: "But couldn't self-interpretation achieve stability through coherence across time? My interpretation at t₁ constrains my interpretation at t₂." But this just relocates the problem—my-interpretation-at-t₁ was itself an interpretation requiring constraint. We either get infinite regress or must posit that at some point, some mental state is *given* rather than interpreted—which concedes the thesis.

The "proves too much" move fails because it generalizes from cases where the interpretive architecture *does* have observational anchors (perceptual access to texts, behaviors, artifacts) or structural asymmetries (interpreter ≠ interpreted) to a case that lacks both. The thesis stands: self-interpretation faces unique stability problems that either reintroduce observation or make self-knowledge impossible.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
Consider **ongoing skilled performance** as a counterexample. When I'm playing jazz piano, I continuously interpret my own musical intentions in real-time: "Am I heading toward a ii-V-I resolution or subverting it? Was that 'wrong' note intentional chromaticism or a mistake I should correct?" This interpretation is:

1. **Genuinely interpretive** - I'm not observing my intentions but constructing them through interpretive activity
2. **Lacks fixed points** - Any seeming-intention is revisable (what felt like "going for resolution" gets reinterpreted as "setting up subversion")
3. **Reflexively circular** - The interpretation itself is part of the performance being interpreted
4. **Yet stable and functional** - I successfully navigate complex improvisations despite this

The thesis predicts this should be impossible or collapse into incoherence. But expert performers routinely achieve exactly this: stable self-knowledge through pure interpretation without observational anchors. The pianist doesn't need any self-ascription immune from reinterpretation; the coherence of the unfolding performance itself provides sufficient constraint.

The **weak point** is the premise that "systematic interpretation requires holding some self-ascriptions immune from reinterpretation." Skilled performance shows that temporal coherence across an evolving interpretive process can provide stability without any immune fixed points—the interpretation at t₁ constrains t₂, which constrains t₃, creating a stable trajectory without foundations.
**STRENGTH: 2/5**
This counterexample has some initial appeal but ultimately fails. The jazz pianist case doesn't actually lack observational anchors—the performer has *proprioceptive and auditory feedback* that serves as observational input. The pianist hears the notes actually played and feels their fingers' positions; these provide the uninterpreted constraints that interpretation organizes. What's being interpreted holistically is the *significance* of these sounds and movements, but their occurrence is observationally given. This is precisely analogous to the text interpretation case: the marks on the page are given, their meaning interpreted. The example doesn't escape the thesis's challenge.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies what's observational in the jazz case. The defender claims proprioceptive and auditory feedback are "uninterpreted constraints," but this misses how these supposedly observational elements are themselves **theory-laden and interpretation-dependent** in skilled performance.

**Against the Proprioceptive Anchor**: Consider: the pianist feels their fingers in a certain position. But *what position*? "Fourth finger on F#" is already an interpreted description, not raw proprioception. The "raw feel" doesn't come pre-parsed into "this is F# rather than G♭" or "this is the fourth finger rather than a hand-shape." Expert performers experience their proprioception *through* their interpretive framework—the same physical sensation means different things depending on whether I'm interpreting my performance as "modal jazz" or "bebop." 

A beginning pianist and an expert have different proprioceptive experiences of "the same" hand position because proprioception is cognitively penetrated by interpretation. This is precisely what Dreyfus showed about skilled coping: experts don't first observe their movements then interpret them; the movements are *experienced as meaningful* from the start, and that meaning is interpretive all the way down.

**Against the Auditory Anchor**: Similarly, "hearing the notes actually played" isn't an uninterpreted given. Do I hear a C# or a D♭? (Acoustically identical, musically different.) Do I hear a "blue note" (intentional microtonal inflection) or "playing out of tune" (mistake)? The very *identity* of what I hear depends on my interpretive framework. 

The thesis defender wants to say: "But *something* was heard, even if what it was is interpreted." But this concedes too much to interpretivism. If we allow that "something happened" without specifying what, we've admitted that interpretation can proceed without determinate observational content—which is exactly what the interpretivist claims about self-knowledge generally.

**The Real Parallel**: The jazz case actually *supports* the interpretivist position by showing how temporal coherence provides stability without foundations. At t₁, I interpret my playing as "heading toward resolution." At t₂, this interpretation constrains (but doesn't determine) my next interpretation—perhaps I continue toward resolution, or perhaps I reinterpret t₁ as "setting up a subversion." Neither interpretation has observational status; each is constrained by coherence with the unfolding whole.

Crucially, this works *without* requiring any moment to be observationally given. The thesis claims this should collapse into instability, but expert performance proves otherwise. The pianist maintains stable, functional self-knowledge through pure coherence constraints across time.

**The Temporal Regress Dissolved**: The defender worries: "But your interpretation at t₁ was itself an interpretation requiring constraint—infinite regress!" But this assumes interpretation must be *grounded* in something prior. In ongoing performance, there is no "first" interpretation that needs grounding. The interpretive process is always already underway, sustained by coherence constraints that operate *synchronically* (across the current web of commitments) and *diachronically* (across the temporal trajectory). No regress arises because we never need to "get interpretation started"—it's continuous.

This is exactly how Gadamer describes understanding: we're always already *in* the hermeneutic circle, and understanding proceeds through dialogue between present and past interpretations, not through inference from observational foundations.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails**, but it reveals something important about where the thesis's strength actually lies.

The defence is right that proprioception and audition in skilled performance are theory-laden and interpretation-dependent. The defender's attempt to salvage "uninterpreted" sensory anchors by retreating to "something happened" is indeed too thin to do the work required. So far, advantage to the interpretivist.

However, the defence fails because it **conflates two distinct questions**:

1. Can interpretation achieve stability without foundations? (The jazz case suggests: yes)
2. Can self-interpretation get *started* without observational input? (The jazz case doesn't address this)

**The Bootstrapping Problem Remains**: The jazz pianist case involves someone who *already has* a rich interpretive framework—musical concepts, performance schemas, stylistic knowledge. The ongoing interpretation draws on this existing web. But how was this framework *acquired*? 

The interpretivist story must be: through earlier interpretation of one's own experiences, practice sessions, listening, etc. But this just pushes the question back. At some developmental point—say, the very first time the person touched a piano—there must have been *something* available to interpret that wasn't itself a product of prior self-interpretation. 

The defender might say: "Auditory and proprioceptive sensations, even if not conceptually structured." But now we've conceded the thesis! These sensations would be playing exactly the role the "observational model" assigns them: pre-interpretive mental states that serve as raw material for interpretation.

**The Temporal Continuity Gambit Fails**: The defence claims "interpretation is always already underway" so no bootstrapping is needed. But this is either false or question-begging:

- If false: There was a time (infancy) when the person had mental states but no interpretive framework. How did interpretation get started?
- If question-begging: It assumes the interpretivist picture is correct by stipulating that even infant mental states are interpretive "all the way down." But this is what's at issue.

**What the Jazz Case Actually Shows**: The example succeeds in showing that *once established*, interpretive self-knowledge can be maintained through coherence without requiring any currently-held self-ascription to be immune from reinterpretation. This is valuable—it defeats *synchronic* versions of the objection that claim we need fixed points *at every moment*.

But it doesn't defeat the *diachronic* version: that self-interpretation requires some *historical* point where mental states were given rather than interpreted, even if no *current* state has that status. The thesis can be refined: "Systematic self-interpretation requires that interpretation was *at some point* bootstrapped by states with observational status, even if no current state retains that status."

This refined thesis still shows interpretivism is unstable—it must either:
(a) Accept that self-knowledge was historically grounded in observation (conceding the observational model for developmental stages), or
(b) Claim interpretation goes "all the way back" with no bootstrapping (making the acquisition of self-knowledge mysterious).

The jazz pianist counterexample fails to escape this dilemma.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis presents itself as identifying a deep structural problem for interpretivism, but it's actually just **stipulating a definition of "interpretation"** that builds in the conclusion, then acting as if this reveals an instability in the opponent's view.

The **weak point** is the premise that "interpretation is a constrained activity requiring uninterpreted constraints." This sounds like a discovery about the nature of interpretation, but it's actually just a verbal stipulation—a decision to use "interpretation" only for activities that have observational anchors, then pointing out that self-interpretation (as the interpretivist conceives it) doesn't fit this stipulated definition.

The argument has this structure:
1. Define "interpretation" as requiring uninterpreted fixed points
2. Note that interpretivists deny self-knowledge has such fixed points  
3. Conclude: "Therefore interpretivists can't really be talking about interpretation!"

But this is trivial. The interpretivist simply has a *different conception* of what interpretation is—one where coherence alone provides constraint. The thesis doesn't show this conception is *unstable* or *incoherent*; it just shows it's *different from* a conception that requires observational anchors.

Compare: "Democracy requires rule by the people. But representative systems don't have direct rule by the people. Therefore representative democracy is unstable—it must either abandon representation or isn't really democracy." This just equivocates on "democracy," stipulating a narrow definition then treating deviation from it as instability rather than as an alternative conception.

The thesis dresses up a **terminological disagreement** as a substantive philosophical problem. Interpretivists like Gopnik aren't *committed* to the claim that interpretation requires uninterpreted constraints—they explicitly reject this! Showing their view is inconsistent with a premise they reject isn't revealing instability; it's just noting that they hold a different position.
**STRENGTH: 1/5**
This attack has no real force. The thesis isn't making a merely verbal point about what to call "interpretation." It's making a substantive claim: that *the activity interpretivists describe* (whether we call it "interpretation" or something else) cannot achieve the stability needed for systematic self-knowledge without reintroducing observational elements. The bootstrapping and reflexive regress problems are genuine structural issues, not terminological quibbles. Even if we granted the interpretivist a completely different term—say, "coherentist self-attribution"—the same problems would arise: How does it get started? How does it avoid reflexive collapse? The thesis identifies real architectural challenges that don't dissolve by relabeling.

NOT APPLICABLE

</details>

---

### O6. Defending first-person authority on agentialist grounds (Moran) commits you to denying that we have such authority for implicit attitudes, which reveals that authority is not a metaphysical feature of the first-person perspective but a normative achievement restricted to attitudes we've deliberatively endorsed.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Moran's agentialist account and anyone who thinks first-person authority is a general feature of self-knowledge
- **Sketch:** Moran's agentialist account grounds first-person authority in the deliberative stance—I answer 'Do I believe P?' by considering whether P is true, not by introspecting. But implicit attitudes (implicit biases, implicit preferences) cannot be settled deliberatively; they must be discovered through interpretation of our behavior, just like Carruthers says. The key premise: if authority derives from the deliberative stance's availability, then we lack authority precisely where that stance is unavailable. This forces agentialists to either (a) deny we have authority about implicit attitudes, which contradicts the intuition that 'I have an implicit bias against X' is a self-ascription with first-person authority, or (b) admit authority has a different ground than deliberative agency, which concedes the position to interpretivism. The commitment reveals that 'first-person authority' may not name a unified phenomenon.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Implicit attitudes pose a real challenge to agentialism, though the dilemma could be sharpened further.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, **UNANSWERED**. The objection correctly identifies that Moran's restriction is explicit, not hidden; the defence's phenomenological appeal to implicit bias avowals was reframed by the objector as deliberative judgments about attitudes rather than the attitudes themselves, which the defender never answered.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's attempted 'third unified understanding' was too thin to establish non-inferential warrant for implicit attitudes; the objector showed the normative force claim actually concedes authority attaches to deliberative judgments about biases, not the biases themselves, which the defender could not overcome.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence proposed falsifiers but the objector demonstrated the thesis wins whether implicit attitudes have authority-marks or not by reframing outcomes; the defender's claim that (1) and (2) are competing rather than both confirmatory was shown to miss how the thesis structure makes either result support it.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's 'internal critique' framing failed because the objector showed Moran can consistently accept scope limitation without self-undermining; the defender never established why agentialism's theoretical ambitions require covering implicit attitudes, leaving this as external criticism dressed as internal.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Moran's agentialist account cannot explain the distinctive epistemic features of implicit attitude self-ascriptions (non-inferential phenomenology, asymmetric credibility, immunity to certain third-person corrections), which either forces denying these are genuine instances of first-person authority or admitting that authority requires explanatory resources beyond deliberative agency—but this reveals a genuine explanatory gap rather than internal inconsistency, since the gap only matters if implicit attitudes independently instantiate the same authority-phenomenon Moran targets.
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates on 'first-person authority' between strong immunity-to-error and weaker epistemic privilege, and treats Moran's explicit scope limitation as revealing problematic fragmentation when he can consistently accept that different mental states require different epistemic mechanisms without self-undermining.

<details><summary>1 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) Agentialist and expressivist accounts commit themselves to denying that we have first-person authority over alienated mental states (addictive desires, intrusive thoughts, implicit biases), but this commitment reveals that what they're explaining isn't actually first-person authority but first-person authorship.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as revealing a "commitment" of Moran's view, but this is actually just an explicit feature of his account that he openly embraces. Moran *explicitly* restricts first-person authority to attitudes formed through deliberative reasoning, and he never claims that all mental states enjoy such authority. The weak point is the phrase "which reveals that authority is not a metaphysical feature...but a normative achievement"—this is precisely Moran's starting position, not a discovery about his view. He begins from the premise that authority is earned through rational self-constitution, not automatically conferred by occupying the first-person perspective. The thesis treats as a damaging "commitment" what is actually Moran's foundational theoretical choice. Similarly, that implicit attitudes require different epistemic access is uncontroversial—even defenders of broad first-person authority (like Burge) distinguish between different types of self-knowledge. The claim that this "forces" a dilemma is overstated: option (a) is exactly what Moran accepts, and calling it counterintuitive doesn't make it a hidden cost. The thesis amounts to saying "if you adopt Moran's premises, you get Moran's conclusions," dressed up as exposing something problematic.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — The observation that Moran's account doesn't cover implicit attitudes is accurate, but it's a feature he advertises, not a bug the thesis uncovers. There's modest force in noting potential scope limitations, but not enough to call this "trivial" successfully.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misreads what makes a commitment dialectically significant. Yes, Moran explicitly restricts authority to deliberatively accessible attitudes—but the thesis isn't claiming he *hides* this restriction. The point is what this restriction *commits him to denying*, which creates a genuine theoretical cost he may not have adequately reckoned with.

Consider the phenomenology: when someone says "I realize I have an implicit bias against women in philosophy," this self-ascription carries distinctive first-person authority. It's not merely a behavioural hypothesis ("I predict my IAT results will show X"). The speaker makes an avowal with the characteristic immunity to certain kinds of doubt and challenge. If you respond "No you don't, you're wrong about your own implicit bias," this sounds like a category mistake—like challenging someone's sincere avowal of pain.

The objector claims option (a)—denying authority for implicit attitudes—is "exactly what Moran accepts." But has Moran explicitly confronted *this* case? The thesis gains force if implicit attitude self-ascriptions genuinely display the marks of first-person authority (asymmetry between first and third person, default credibility, non-evidential warrant) yet fall outside Moran's explanation. Then we have a genuine dilemma: either these aren't really authoritative (counterintuitive), or authority has multiple grounds (fragmenting the phenomenon Moran aimed to unify).

The objector says even Burge "distinguishes between different types of self-knowledge"—but this actually helps the thesis. If *everyone* must recognize different epistemic mechanisms for different attitudes, then Moran's ambition to ground authority in deliberative agency specifically looks less like a substantive theory and more like a stipulative restriction on which cases count. The thesis's force lies precisely here: it shows agentialism doesn't *explain* a unified phenomenon but rather *selects* a subset of cases, leaving the rest unexplained.

VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The strongest point—that implicit attitude avowals display authority-markers—has real phenomenological pull, but it's ultimately inconclusive. Moran could reasonably respond that what *seems* like first-person authority for implicit attitudes is actually just the authority we have over our *interpretive judgments* about ourselves, which *are* deliberatively formed. When I say "I have an implicit bias," I'm making a deliberative judgment based on evidence (my behavioral patterns, my IAT results, my reactions). The authority attaches to this *judgment*, not to the implicit attitude itself.

The objection stands: Moran's restriction is a foundational theoretical choice, not a hidden cost. The thesis wanted to show this choice creates problems Moran hasn't faced, but the "problem" dissolves if implicit attitude self-ascriptions are really just deliberative interpretive judgments wearing misleading grammatical clothing. The thesis needed to establish that implicit attitudes genuinely instantiate first-person authority *on independent grounds*, not just assert it seems that way. Without that independent argument, we're left exactly where the objection says: noting that Moran's premises yield Moran's conclusions, which isn't dialectically interesting.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "first-person authority" between two incompatible readings, and the argument requires both. On one reading, "authority" means the distinctive epistemic asymmetry and immunity to certain errors that characterizes paradigmatic self-ascriptions like avowals of occurrent thoughts ("I'm thinking about lunch"). On this reading, the claim that implicit attitude self-ascriptions have first-person authority is substantive and interesting—it would genuinely challenge Moran if true. But on this reading, the claim is implausible: implicit bias self-ascriptions are precisely *not* immune to error in the relevant way, *are* based on behavioral evidence, and *do* lack the asymmetry (my therapist might know my implicit attitudes better than I do).

On the second reading, "authority" means merely "default credibility" or "some special first-person access." On this reading, the claim that implicit attitude self-ascriptions have authority is plausible—we do grant them some privilege. But on this reading, the challenge to Moran evaporates: he can simply agree that different mental states involve different epistemic relations, and that what he calls "authority" (the strong sense) doesn't apply to implicit attitudes, while some weaker privilege might.

The argument needs the plausibility of the second reading (to make the claim that implicit attitudes have "authority" seem obvious) and the strength of the first reading (to make this seem problematic for Moran). The weak point is the phrase "first-person authority" throughout—it shifts meaning between "the distinctive immunity-to-error phenomenon Moran explains" and "any first-person epistemic privilege whatsoever."

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This equivocation does serious damage. The entire dialectical force depends on treating "authority" as univocal when it isn't.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes that "first-person authority" must mean either strong Shoemaker-style immunity to error through misidentification OR mere default credibility, and that these are incompatible readings. But there's a third, unified understanding that the thesis can legitimately employ: **authority as non-evidential warrant for self-ascription coupled with a distinctive first-person/third-person asymmetry in epistemic position**.

This captures both cases without equivocation. For occurrent thoughts: I don't infer "I'm thinking about lunch" from evidence, and you cannot correct me on this in the way you can correct my factual beliefs. For implicit attitudes: I don't infer "I have an implicit bias against X" *in the same way* I infer facts about external objects—there's a distinctive non-observational element—and there remains an asymmetry (my sincere avowal carries weight yours doesn't, even if you've observed my behavior).

The objector claims "my therapist might know my implicit attitudes better than I do," but this conflates *predictive accuracy* with *epistemic authority*. Even if your therapist predicts your implicit attitudes more reliably, when you sincerely avow "I realize I have this bias," you're not merely reporting an empirical hypothesis they could falsify. You're making a self-constituting acknowledgment that has distinctive normative force—it changes your rational commitments in ways their third-person attribution doesn't.

Crucially, the objection's "strong sense/weak sense" distinction actually *supports* the thesis rather than undermining it. If we must distinguish strong authority (deliberatively accessible attitudes) from weak authority (implicit attitudes), we've already conceded the thesis's main claim: that "first-person authority" doesn't name a unified phenomenon grounded in a single mechanism. Moran wanted to explain THE phenomenon of first-person authority via deliberative agency. If we now say "well, there are really two phenomena, and he only explains one," that's exactly the fragmentation the thesis diagnoses.

The thesis can consistently maintain: implicit attitude self-ascriptions display *enough* of the characteristic marks of authority (non-inferential warrant, asymmetry, immunity to certain challenges) that any adequate theory must account for them. Moran's theory doesn't. That's not equivocation—it's showing his explanatory target is narrower than the phenomenon requires.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails.

The attempted rescue—positing a "third, unified understanding" of authority as "non-evidential warrant plus asymmetry"—is too thin to do the required work. Here's why:

First, the claim that implicit bias self-ascriptions involve "non-evidential warrant" is precisely what's in dispute. When I say "I realize I have an implicit bias against X," this typically *follows from* evidence: I noticed patterns in my behavior, I took an IAT, I reflected on my reactions. The objector is right that this looks like an *inference* from behavioral evidence, not the non-inferential access that characterizes genuine authority. Calling it "not in the same way I infer facts about external objects" doesn't establish that it's non-inferential in the relevant sense—it might just be a different kind of inference.

Second, the "distinctive normative force" and "self-constituting acknowledgment" move actually concedes the game. If what makes implicit bias avowals special is their *normative* dimension—that acknowledging the bias changes my commitments—then we're back in Moran's territory. The authority attaches to my *deliberative stance* toward the bias (my acknowledgment, my commitment to address it), not to the implicit attitude itself. This is exactly what Moran would say: you have authority over your *judgment* that you have the bias, which is a deliberatively formed attitude.

Third, and most fatally, the objection's "two phenomena" point stands. The defence tries to flip this as supporting the thesis, but it doesn't work. Moran never claimed to explain *every* epistemic asymmetry between first and third person—he claimed to explain a *specific* phenomenon (authority for rational attitudes). Showing he doesn't explain a *different* phenomenon (whatever epistemic privilege attaches to implicit attitude self-ascriptions) isn't a dialectical victory. It's like objecting to a theory of perception because it doesn't explain proprioception.

The equivocation remains: the thesis needs implicit attitudes to have authority *in Moran's sense* (to create a genuine problem), but they plausibly don't. They have *some* first-person privilege, but of a different kind that Moran never claimed to explain. The thesis treats this as fragmentation when it's really just scope limitation—and legitimate scope limitation at that.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as "first-person authority." The weak point is the phrase "reveals that authority is not a metaphysical feature...but a normative achievement"—this formulation makes the thesis unfalsifiable by allowing any apparent counterexample to be reinterpreted.

Consider potential counterexamples: (1) Cases where implicit attitudes seem to lack authority (I'm wrong about my implicit bias)—these get counted as supporting the thesis ("see, authority is restricted"). (2) Cases where implicit attitudes seem to have authority (my sincere avowal of implicit bias carries special weight)—these also support the thesis ("see, Moran can't explain this, so authority must fragment"). (3) Cases where Moran explicitly limits his scope—this supports the thesis ("he's retreating to a subset"). (4) Cases where other theorists distinguish types of self-knowledge—this supports the thesis ("see, fragmentation").

Every possible observation confirms the thesis because "normative achievement restricted to deliberatively endorsed attitudes" is elastic enough to accommodate anything. If implicit attitudes have authority, that shows Moran's account is too narrow. If they lack authority, that shows authority is "restricted" rather than "metaphysical." The thesis cannot specify in advance what would count against it—what observation would show that authority IS a unified metaphysical feature after all, or that Moran's restriction is principled rather than revealing fragmentation.

The protective maneuver is treating "reveals a commitment" as unfalsifiable: whatever Moran says becomes evidence that his view "commits him" to something problematic, because the thesis can always reframe explicit theoretical choices as "revealing" hidden costs.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — There's a genuine problem here. The thesis does seem structured so that both authority and non-authority for implicit attitudes confirm it, and "normative achievement" is vague enough to flex around counterexamples. However, the thesis could potentially be tested by asking: do implicit attitude self-ascriptions display the specific markers of authority (asymmetry, non-inferential warrant, immunity to error) that Moran's account was designed to explain? If they clearly don't, the thesis loses force. So it's not completely unfalsifiable, just heavily protected.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misunderstands what makes a philosophical thesis falsifiable. The thesis isn't empirically unfalsifiable—it makes a specific conceptual claim that *can* be defeated by showing that first-person authority is genuinely unified despite surface appearances.

Here's what would falsify the thesis:

**Falsifier 1**: Show that implicit attitude self-ascriptions *don't actually display* the marks of first-person authority at all—that they're purely third-personal hypotheses with no epistemic asymmetry, no default credibility, no immunity to certain challenges. Then the thesis's crucial premise fails: there's no phenomenon Moran fails to explain, so no fragmentation is revealed.

**Falsifier 2**: Provide a *unified account* that explains both deliberatively accessible attitudes AND implicit attitudes under a single mechanism that preserves what's distinctive about first-person authority. This would show authority isn't fragmented after all—it just has a more complex grounding than Moran recognized.

**Falsifier 3**: Demonstrate that the distinction between deliberative and implicit attitudes maps onto a *principled metaphysical difference* in the attitudes themselves (not just our epistemic access), such that it's *not* ad hoc for authority to track only the first kind. Then Moran's restriction wouldn't reveal fragmentation but would track a real joint in mental reality.

The objection complains that "both authority and non-authority for implicit attitudes confirm" the thesis, but this misreads the dialectic. The thesis requires a *specific combination*: that implicit attitudes (a) display *enough* authority-markers to demand explanation, but (b) fall outside Moran's account, thus (c) forcing either counterintuitive denial or theoretical fragmentation. This is testable: challenge any of these three conjuncts.

The "normative achievement" language isn't protective vagueness—it's a substantive claim. It means: authority isn't automatic, isn't metaphysically guaranteed by occupying the first-person perspective, but depends on meeting certain conditions (for Moran: deliberative accessibility). What would falsify this? Show that authority attaches to *all* first-person self-ascriptions regardless of the subject's rational capacities or deliberative engagement—that even a subject incapable of deliberation, or one who explicitly refuses the deliberative stance, still has authority. If authority is truly "metaphysical" (built into the first-person perspective itself), it shouldn't depend on what the subject *does* or what stance they take.

The objection's examples actually demonstrate falsifiability: (1) and (2) aren't *both* confirmations—they're competing empirical claims about whether implicit attitudes have authority-markers, and the thesis depends on (2) being true. If (1) is true instead, the thesis fails. Similarly, (3) and (4) only support the thesis *if* the distinctions they draw are theoretically unprincipled or ad hoc—if they're well-motivated, the thesis loses force.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though it makes some valid points about what could falsify the thesis.

The fundamental problem is that the defence's proposed falsifiers don't actually address the objection's core charge: that the thesis is *structured* to make any outcome look like confirmation through strategic redefinition.

Take Falsifier 1: "Show implicit attitudes don't display authority-marks." The defence says this would defeat the thesis. But the objection already showed this is precisely the dispute—whether implicit attitudes have authority *in the relevant sense*. The thesis needs to have *already established* which sense is relevant and that implicit attitudes have authority in that sense. Instead, it leaves "authority" flexible enough that when challenged, it can retreat to "well, *some* first-person privilege" (as the previous defence did), but when arguing against Moran, it needs the strong sense. The unfalsifiability lies in this flexibility: the thesis can adjust what counts as "authority-marks" based on dialectical need.

Falsifier 2 actually reveals the problem. The defence says a "unified account" would defeat the thesis. But Moran *could* claim to offer this—he could say: "Authority always comes from the deliberative stance; implicit attitudes aren't attitudes we have authority about because they're not deliberatively accessible; this is unified and principled." The thesis would respond: "But that's just stipulative restriction!" Yet what makes it stipulative rather than principled? The thesis seems to assume any restriction is problematic, which is precisely the unfalsifiability worry.

Most tellingly, look at how the defence handles the objection's examples (1)-(4). It claims they're not *all* confirmations because (1) and (2) are "competing empirical claims." But this misses the point: the thesis is structured so that whichever of (1) or (2) turns out true, it can declare victory. If implicit attitudes lack authority-markers (1), that's fine—Moran's account is "restricted." If they have them (2), that's also fine—Moran's account "fragments." The thesis wins either way by reframing the question from "Is Moran's account correct?" to "Does Moran's account reveal something about authority?" This reframing is what makes it unfalsifiable.

The objection stands: the thesis protects itself by treating any theoretical boundary-drawing as "revealing fragmentation" and any scope limitation as "revealing that authority is normative not metaphysical." These aren't discoveries that could be falsified—they're interpretive frames that can be imposed on any position.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis presents itself as revealing a problematic commitment of Moran's agentialist view, but this position is actually **already established and defended by neo-Rylean dispositionalists** like Eric Schwitzgebel. Schwitzgebel explicitly argues that "belief" doesn't name a unified phenomenon—that we should be **eliminativists** about belief as a natural kind precisely because different putative beliefs (dispositional beliefs, occurrent judgments, implicit attitudes) have radically different functional profiles and epistemic accessibility conditions. The weak point is the claim that showing fragmentation "reveals" something problematic: Schwitzgebel has already argued this fragmentation is real and that we should accept **belief pluralism** or eliminativism rather than seeking unified accounts.

Moreover, interpretivists like Carruthers have explicitly argued that implicit attitudes require third-person interpretive access while explicit attitudes don't, and that this is perfectly compatible with a naturalistic account of mind—no unified "authority" phenomenon needs explaining. The thesis treats as a discovery what's already a well-established position in philosophy of mind: that "belief" or "attitude" may be a folk-psychological category that cross-cuts genuinely different cognitive kinds.
