Even within the self-knowledge literature, Cassam's "transparency account" explicitly distinguishes between attitudes accessible through deliberation and those requiring self-interpretation, treating this as a feature rather than a bug. The thesis's conclusion—that authority is "not a metaphysical feature but a normative achievement"—is precisely the position already defended by constitutivist approaches to self-knowledge (Bilgrami, Boyle) that the thesis doesn't cite.

**STRENGTH: 2/5** — The observation about Moran's scope limitations is accurate, but framing this as revealing a problematic commitment ignores that multiple established positions already embrace exactly this fragmentation as their starting point. The thesis needed to show why Moran's agentialism specifically creates problems these other fragmentationist views avoid, but instead it just restates fragmentationism as if it were a novel discovery.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses *sharing a conclusion* with *being the same argument*. Yes, Schwitzgebel, Carruthers, and constitutivist approaches reach similar conclusions about fragmentation—but the thesis's contribution is showing that *Moran's own commitments lead there*, which creates a distinctive dialectical problem he may not recognize.

This matters because Moran explicitly positions his account as *opposing* interpretivist views like Carruthers'. Moran wants to preserve the *specialness* of first-person authority by grounding it in rational agency rather than mere interpretive access. He's trying to vindicate a robust notion of self-knowledge against deflationary interpretivism. The thesis shows this project is self-undermining: the very resources Moran uses to explain authority (deliberative accessibility) force him to concede so much ground to interpretivism that the distinction collapses.

This is dialectically significant in a way that Schwitzgebel's independent eliminativism isn't. Schwitzgebel *starts* from eliminativist intuitions and argues outward. Moran *starts* from the phenomenology of first-person authority and tries to explain it. Showing that his explanation forces fragmentation is an **internal critique**—it demonstrates tension within his own theoretical commitments, not just external disagreement.

The objection says Cassam "explicitly distinguishes" between deliberative and interpretive access "as a feature not a bug." But has Cassam confronted the specific challenge to *agentialism* that the thesis raises? The thesis's force comes from showing that if you ground authority in *agency* specifically (not just transparency or constitutivism generally), you face a dilemma: either deny authority for implicit attitudes (counterintuitive) or admit authority has non-agential grounds (abandoning agentialism's distinctive claim).

Moreover, the objection's own examples reveal the thesis's contribution. It lists *multiple different positions* (Schwitzgebel's eliminativism, Carruthers' interpretivism, Cassam's transparency account, Bilgrami/Boyle's constitutivism) that embrace fragmentation. But these are *competing* views with different metaphysical and normative commitments. The thesis's insight is that *Moran's agentialist premises* lead to a position that's *unstable*—it can't rest comfortably with any of these alternatives. He can't accept Schwitzgebel's eliminativism (too deflationary about rational agency), can't accept Carruthers' pure interpretivism (abandons first-person authority's distinctiveness), but can't maintain agentialism's ambition either (because implicit attitudes escape it).

The "already taken" objection only works if the thesis claimed to be the *first* to notice fragmentation. But the thesis's actual claim is that *agentialism specifically* reveals fragmentation in a way that creates problems for *that particular theoretical project*. That's not already taken—it's a specific critique of Moran's distinctive position.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails.

The attempted rescue—that this is an "internal critique" showing Moran's view is "self-undermining"—doesn't survive scrutiny. Here's why:

First, the defence claims Moran "explicitly positions his account as opposing interpretivist views like Carruthers'" and wants to "vindicate a robust notion of self-knowledge." But Moran's opposition to interpretivism is about *deliberatively accessible attitudes*—he argues that for these, we don't need to interpret ourselves as if from the outside. He never claims *all* mental states must be accessible this way. So there's no self-undermining: he can consistently say "For rational attitudes, agentialism explains authority; for implicit attitudes, interpretivism is correct." This isn't tension—it's scope limitation.

The defence protests this would mean "conceding so much ground to interpretivism that the distinction collapses," but this is just assertion. Why does acknowledging that *some* mental states require interpretation collapse the distinction? Moran's distinctive claim is about a *specific class* of attitudes (those formed through reasoning), not about the mind as a whole. The defence needed to show why Moran's theoretical ambitions *require* covering implicit attitudes, but it doesn't.

Second, the "internal critique" framing fails because the thesis hasn't identified an actual *inconsistency* in Moran's commitments. An internal critique shows that a view's premises lead to conclusions the view itself rejects. But Moran doesn't reject the claim that implicit attitudes lack the authority deliberative attitudes have—that's perfectly consistent with his framework. The defence tries to make this seem problematic by calling it "counterintuitive," but that's an *external* standard (about our intuitions), not an internal one (about Moran's commitments).

Third, and most damaging, the objection's point about multiple competing fragmentationist positions actually undermines the thesis rather than supporting it. The defence says these are "competing views with different metaphysical commitments" and Moran's position is "unstable" because it can't rest with any of them. But this just shows that *if* you accept fragmentation, there are multiple theoretical options for handling it—which means showing Moran leads to fragmentation doesn't reveal a *problem*, just a *choice point*. The thesis wanted to show agentialism creates a distinctive difficulty; instead it shows agentialism leads to a well-populated area of theoretical space where multiple coherent positions are already available.

The objection stands: the thesis restates an established position (fragmentationism about self-knowledge) as if discovering it through Moran creates a problem for him. But Moran can simply *accept* the fragmentation and align with one of the existing fragmentationist approaches. That's not being "already taken" in the sense of someone else making the same argument—it's worse: it's showing that the conclusion the thesis treats as problematic is actually a stable, well-defended position that Moran could comfortably occupy.

The thesis survives only if it can show why *agentialism specifically* cannot coherently accept fragmentation—but the defence hasn't established this, and the objection's examples suggest it isn't true.

</details>

---

### O7. First-person authority for propositional attitudes is actually an unstable position that, on examination, collapses into either radical authority (which is incompatible with interpretivism) or mere pragmatic default (which the literature wrongly treats as theoretically significant).

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** The moderate position on first-person authority held by neo-expressivists and agentialists who acknowledge interpretive constraints
- **Sketch:** The moderate position—that first-person reports have some special authority but not infallibility—faces a dilemma. Either the 'specialness' makes a real epistemic difference (others should revise in the face of sincere first-person reports even when third-person evidence conflicts), in which case we need a non-interpretive mechanism that proponents haven't provided, or it doesn't make such a difference (first-person reports are just more evidence to weigh), in which case the 'authority' is merely the pragmatic policy of letting people speak for themselves absent strong counterevidence—a conversational norm, not an epistemic asymmetry. The argument turns on showing that every proposal for 'moderate' first-person authority either smuggles in something observation-like or reduces to mere presumptive credibility. Moran's transparency method, for instance, only works if the deliberative question actually fixes the psychological fact—which is radical authority—or else it's just one more input to interpretation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The dilemma for moderate authority is well-constructed but the radical/pragmatic dichotomy may be too stark to be fully convincing.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence showed a substantive criterion at work but failed because it proved too much about what counts as 'mere pragmatic default,' collapsing theoretically significant epistemic explanations into the 'mere' category by stipulation about what matters
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence distinguished identifying a dilemma from claiming all escapes fail, but failed to show the thesis differs substantively from Wright's existing arguments or uses different categories than Wright's framework
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 3/5, answered. The defence successfully identified a genuine disanalogy—perceptual states are not interpretively constituted while propositional attitudes under interpretivism are—showing the dilemma applies specifically to interpretively constituted facts
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, answered. The defence correctly showed Fricker's position faces the same dilemma structure—inferential position provides evidence for interpretation rather than creating a third category beyond constitutive authority and evidential presumption
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Wright's dilemma about first-person authority remains unresolved: neo-expressivist and agentialist attempts to escape it (Bar-On, Moran, Finkelstein) fail because they either reintroduce non-interpretive mechanisms or provide epistemic explanations for evidential presumptions without establishing a metaphysically distinct category of authority
- **Flaw:** The thesis dismisses as 'mere pragmatic default' what are actually substantive theoretical explanations of epistemic asymmetry, and substantially recapitulates Wright's existing framework without showing how it differs or advances beyond it

<details><summary>3 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) First-person authority is not a single phenomenon but fractures into avowal-authority (immunity to certain challenges) and epistemic authority (getting things right), and only the former survives the interpretive turn.
- (3/5) The interpretive theorists and the agentialists are both wrong because they share a false assumption: that there is a single thing called 'first-person authority' to explain, when actually the authority involved in avowing pains is metaphysically different from the authority involved in avowing beliefs.
- (3/5) First-person authority is not one phenomenon but three—immunity to error through misidentification, presumptive uptake, and rational self-determination—and only the last survives the interpretive turn intact.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself through systematic redefinition of what counts as "moderate" first-person authority. Any proposed middle position gets reclassified as either "radical authority" (if it gives first-person reports genuine epistemic weight in conflict cases) or "mere pragmatic default" (if it doesn't). Consider a defender who says: "First-person reports have special but defeasible epistemic weight—they're privileged evidence but not conclusive." The thesis responds: "Does 'privileged' mean others should revise their interpretations when you sincerely report? If yes, that's radical authority requiring non-interpretive mechanisms. If no, it's just pragmatic default." But this move rules out the conceptual space between these poles by definitional fiat. The Moran case reveals the strategy: his transparency method is pre-classified as either "fixing psychological facts" (radical) or "just another input" (pragmatic). What if transparency provides *better* epistemic access without *determining* the fact? The thesis has no room for degrees of epistemic asymmetry—any asymmetry strong enough to matter gets labeled "radical," any weak enough to be compatible with interpretivism gets dismissed as "mere" pragmatism. The dilemma's horns are sharpened by stipulation, not argument. Every candidate counterexample—every nuanced position—gets sorted into the pre-existing bins through redefinition of what the position "really" amounts to.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs independent criteria for what counts as "radical" versus "moderate" authority that don't simply assume the instability it purports to discover.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies stipulation where there is a substantive criterion at work. The thesis does not *define* positions into its categories—it applies a specific test that any genuine middle position must pass: **can the view explain why first-person reports should sometimes override better-supported third-person interpretations without invoking a non-interpretive mechanism?**

This is not arbitrary. The interpretive constraint is real: if all psychological facts are constituted by what the best total interpretation says, then a sincere first-person report is just more evidence *for* interpretation, not evidence that *defeats* interpretation. The question is whether "special authority" does any work beyond this. 

Take the Moran case directly. The objection asks: "What if transparency provides *better* epistemic access without *determining* the fact?" But this is precisely what needs explaining. *Why* is deliberative self-knowledge better epistemic access if the psychological fact is interpretively constituted? The interpretivist already says that subjects have distinctive evidence (introspective phenomenology, memory of reasoning, etc.). Calling the transparency method "better access" without explaining *what makes it better given interpretive constitution* is just labeling, not theorizing.

The thesis's criterion is principled: if your "moderate" authority means that when you sincerely say "I believe P" and the best interpretation of your total behavior/dispositions says you believe ~P, we should revise the interpretation—then you've abandoned interpretivism, because you're treating the first-person report as *constitutive* rather than *evidential*. If your "moderate" authority *doesn't* mean this—if the interpretation can still override—then what's the epistemic asymmetry beyond "we usually trust sincere reports absent countervailing evidence," which is indeed mere pragmatic default?

The objection demands "room for degrees of epistemic asymmetry," but the thesis responds: show me the degree that's (a) strong enough to be theoretically interesting rather than pragmatic, and (b) compatible with interpretive constitution. That's not ruling out logical space by fiat—it's asking for the metaphysical story.


VERDICT

The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence succeeds in showing there's a real question being asked, not pure stipulation. The interpretive constraint does create genuine pressure, and asking "what makes it better access?" is legitimate.

But the defence fails because it proves too much about what counts as "mere pragmatic default." Consider: we might think first-person reports deserve special weight *because* subjects have distinctive causal-informational access to their own mental states (even if those states are interpretively constituted). This access is better than third-person observation *for gathering the evidence interpretation needs*, without the report being constitutive. 

The thesis treats this as "mere pragmatic default," but that's too quick. There's a difference between:
- (A) "We trust first-person reports as a conversational courtesy"
- (B) "First-person reports reliably track features that, while interpretively constituted, are best detected by the subject"

Position (B) is epistemically significant—it explains *why* the pragmatic policy is rational—without being either radical authority or mere convention. The thesis's criterion demands that "theoretically interesting" authority must sometimes override best interpretation, but this ignores that there can be a theoretical explanation for why first-person reports are systematically better evidence *for* interpretation without ever defeating it.

The objection's core point stands: the thesis needs independent motivation for why only override-capacity counts as "theoretically interesting" authority. The defence's question "what makes it better access?" is fair, but dismissing any answer that doesn't yield override-capacity as "mere pragmatism" does collapse the middle by stipulation about what "mere" means.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This instability thesis is a known dialectical position in the first-person authority debates, specifically a variant of what Richard Moran himself identified in "Authority and Estrangement" (2001) as the "dilemma of authority" - that first-person authority claims either collapse into Cartesian infallibility or dissolve into mere evidential priority. The thesis recapitulates Moran's own worry about whether his transparency account can occupy stable middle ground. More extensively, this is the structure of Crispin Wright's argument in "Self-Knowledge: The Wittgensteinian Legacy" (1998), where Wright argues that non-inferential self-knowledge claims face exactly this dilemma: either they involve a sui generis constitutive authority (his "substantial self-knowledge") or they reduce to what he calls "default entitlement" - a merely pragmatic presumption. The thesis's specific formulation - that moderate positions smuggle in "something observation-like" or reduce to "conversational norms" - directly echoes Wright's distinction between constitutive and default models. Even the Moran transparency example and the claim that it "only works if deliberation fixes the fact" is Wright's objection to Moran. The neo-expressivist target also signals awareness of this existing debate, as these theorists (Bar-On, Finkelstein) explicitly position themselves as responding to Wright's dilemma. This is not a novel instability argument but a restatement of a well-mapped dialectical impasse.

STRENGTH: 4/5 - This seriously undermines the thesis's contribution. While the thesis might add some clarificatory value or push the existing debate forward incrementally, it is substantially anticipated by Wright's work and exists within a recognized problematic that the literature has already named and engaged with for decades.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection conflates *identifying a dilemma* with *having the same thesis about what the dilemma shows*. Yes, Wright (1998) and Moran (2001) identify tensions in moderate positions. But the thesis makes a specific claim Wright does not: that this instability *reveals something about the literature's treatment of first-person authority*, namely that what gets called "theoretically significant" is actually just pragmatic policy misidentified as epistemic asymmetry.

Wright's argument is diagnostic—he shows the difficulty of stable middle ground and explores whether his "default entitlement" can occupy it. He treats this as a *problem to solve*, developing his positive account of entitlement as a non-evidential warrant. The thesis, by contrast, argues that the instability is *fatal*—that the moderate position doesn't just face difficulties but actually collapses, and that the literature's continued treatment of pragmatic defaults as theoretically significant is an error.

More importantly, the thesis targets *neo-expressivist and agentialist responses to Wright*. These theorists (Bar-On, Finkelstein, post-2001 Moran) claim to have escaped the dilemma precisely by showing how first-person authority can be both non-observational and non-constitutive through expressive or agential mechanisms. The thesis argues their escape attempts fail—that Bar-On's expression model and Moran's developed transparency account still face the same collapse.

This is not mere restatement. Wright identifies a dilemma; the thesis claims *all attempts to escape it fail in a specific way* (by smuggling in observation-like mechanisms or reducing to pragmatic policy). The Moran example is not just Wright's objection repeated—it's a claim about what Moran's *response* to Wright (the developed transparency account in "Authority and Estrangement" and later work) amounts to when examined.

The objection's evidence actually supports the thesis's novelty: if neo-expressivists and agentialists explicitly position themselves as responding to Wright's dilemma, and the thesis argues their responses fail to escape it, then the thesis is making a second-order claim about the state of the debate, not just restating Wright's first-order argument.

VERDICT

The defence partially succeeds but ultimately fails to save the thesis from the "already taken" charge.

The defence is right that there's a difference between Wright's diagnostic work and a claim that *all escape attempts fail*. It's also right that targeting neo-expressivist responses to Wright could be novel. 

But the defence fails because:

First, Wright himself argues that moderate positions *do* collapse—his "default entitlement" is explicitly offered as the only viable non-constitutive option, and he's skeptical about alternatives. The thesis's claim that pragmatic defaults are "wrongly treated as theoretically significant" is precisely what Wright investigates, and he develops substantial arguments about why default entitlement *is* theoretically significant (it's a non-evidential warrant with specific rational structure). The thesis hasn't shown it's saying something different from "Wright is right and his critics fail."

Second, the claim that neo-expressivist responses "smuggle in observation-like mechanisms or reduce to pragmatic policy" needs to be more specific to be novel. Dorit Bar-On's expression model has been extensively criticized on exactly these grounds by Wright himself and by others (Byrne 2005, Coliva 2012) who argue that expression either involves self-interpretation (making it observational) or is just a causal mechanism (making it non-epistemic). If the thesis is just applying this known line of criticism, it's not novel.

Third, and most damaging: the objection notes the thesis's "specific formulation" echoes Wright's distinctions, and the defence doesn't adequately address this. The very categories "something observation-like" vs. "conversational norms" are Wright's "substantial self-knowledge" vs. "default entitlement" under different names. The defence needed to show how these categories differ or how the thesis uses them to reach conclusions Wright doesn't reach.

The thesis might make a *contribution* by systematically applying Wright's framework to newer neo-expressivist work, but it's not a novel instability argument—it's an application of an existing one. That's still philosophical work, but it's not what the thesis presents itself as being.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis's argument structure, if successful, would prove that *any* epistemic asymmetry between first-person and third-person access to mental states must either be radical/constitutive or merely pragmatic/conventional. But this proves too much because it would eliminate theoretically significant epistemic asymmetries in domains where we clearly recognize them. Consider perceptual knowledge: I have better epistemic access to what I'm currently seeing than you do (you must rely on my testimony, my behavior, environmental cues). Yet this asymmetry is neither constitutive (what I see isn't determined by my reports) nor merely pragmatic convention (there's a real epistemic difference, not just conversational courtesy). The asymmetry is *evidential* - I have distinctive causal-informational access that makes my reports systematically more reliable. The thesis's dilemma would force us to say this perceptual asymmetry is either "radical authority" (my reports determine what I see) or "mere pragmatic default" (no real epistemic difference from your third-person evidence). Since neither is true for perception, the dilemma's categories are too coarse. If the argument worked, it would show that first-person/third-person evidential asymmetries are impossible in general - but they clearly exist for perception, proprioception, memory of one's own experiences, etc. The thesis needs to explain why the same evidential-asymmetry structure that works for perception cannot work for propositional attitudes under interpretivism.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis appears to rule out a category (genuine but non-constitutive epistemic asymmetry) that exists in closely analogous cases, suggesting its dilemma is artificially sharpened.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The objection misses a crucial disanalogy: perceptual knowledge does not face an interpretive constitution constraint. The thesis's dilemma arises specifically from the combination of (a) interpretivism about propositional attitudes and (b) claims of first-person authority. Perception has no parallel to (a).

When I report "I see a red apple," what I see is not constituted by interpretation of my total behavioral/dispositional patterns. The redness of the apple, my visual experience of it—these have whatever metaphysical status they have independently of interpretive practices. My epistemic advantage is straightforward: I have causal-informational access to my visual states that you lack. You must infer from my testimony, pupil dilation, reaching behavior, etc. This is unproblematic evidential asymmetry.

But propositional attitudes under interpretivism are different in kind. If what I believe is constituted by what the best interpretation of my total evidence says I believe, then my first-person report "I believe P" is not like my perceptual report "I see red." It's more evidence *for* interpretation, not independent access to an interpretation-independent fact. 

The thesis's categories are not too coarse—they're specifically calibrated to this metaphysical situation. The question is: given that the psychological fact is interpretively constituted, what could make first-person reports more than just one more input to the interpretive process? 

The perceptual analogy actually supports the thesis. In perception, the epistemic asymmetry is unproblematic *because* there's a non-interpretive fact (the visual state) to which I have privileged causal access. The thesis claims: if you want that kind of epistemic asymmetry for propositional attitudes, you need a parallel non-interpretive fact—which is radical authority, incompatible with interpretivism. If you accept interpretive constitution, then first-person reports are evidence *for* interpretation, and any "privilege" they have is just the pragmatic-epistemic fact that subjects are well-positioned to provide evidence about their own behavioral patterns, reasoning history, phenomenology, etc.

The objection's own example proves the point: perceptual asymmetry works because perception is not interpretively constituted. Remove that feature, and the asymmetry becomes mysterious—which is exactly what the thesis argues happens with propositional attitudes under interpretivism.

VERDICT

The defence succeeds. This is a strong response that identifies a genuine and relevant disanalogy.

The objection's force depends on treating propositional attitudes under interpretivism as metaphysically parallel to perceptual states, but they are not. The defence correctly identifies that perceptual knowledge involves privileged causal access to states that exist independently of interpretation, while interpretivism about propositional attitudes denies precisely this independence.

The defence's key move is sound: the thesis's dilemma is not meant to apply to *all* epistemic asymmetries, but specifically to epistemic asymmetries concerning interpretively constituted facts. In such cases, the question "what makes first-person access privileged?" has a different structure than in the perceptual case, because there is no interpretation-independent fact to which one could have privileged causal access.

The objection needed to show either that:
(a) propositional attitudes are *not* relevantly different from perceptual states even under interpretivism, or
(b) there's a way to have genuine epistemic asymmetry about interpretively constituted facts that the thesis's categories miss.

The objection attempts neither. Instead, it assumes the cases are parallel and concludes the thesis's categories are too coarse. But the defence shows they are not parallel in the relevant respect.

The defence's final point is particularly strong: if you want perceptual-style epistemic asymmetry, you need perceptual-style metaphysics (non-interpretive facts). If you accept interpretive constitution, you cannot simply import the evidential-asymmetry structure from perception without explaining how it could work given the different metaphysical situation.

The objection fails.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider Elizabeth Fricker's "Self-Knowledge: Special Access Versus Artefact of Grammar" (1998) and her developed position in later work. Fricker argues that first-person authority for propositional attitudes is grounded in the subject's distinctive *inferential position*: subjects have privileged access to the premises of their own practical reasoning, their own phenomenology, and their own behavioral dispositions over time. This creates genuine epistemic asymmetry—first-person reports are systematically more reliable than third-person attributions—without requiring either constitutive authority or collapsing into mere pragmatic default.

Here's the concrete case: Sarah has been acting irritably around dogs, avoiding dog parks, and making excuses when friends suggest dog-related activities. The best behavioral interpretation suggests she fears dogs. But Sarah sincerely reports: "I don't fear dogs; I'm just annoyed by irresponsible owners." She then cites her reasoning: she had a recent incident where an off-leash dog knocked over her child, she's been reading about leash law violations, and her irritability specifically tracks owner behavior, not dog presence.

On Fricker's view, Sarah's report deserves epistemic privilege because she has better access to these inferential premises. This is neither radical authority (the behavioral evidence still matters; she could be wrong) nor mere pragmatic default (there's a real epistemic reason—her inferential position—for weighting her report heavily). The thesis must classify this as one or the other, but it fits neither: it's epistemically significant (not just conversational courtesy) yet compatible with interpretivism (the belief is still constituted by total evidence, including but not limited to her report).

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. Fricker's position seems to occupy exactly the middle ground the thesis claims is unstable, grounded in a specific epistemic mechanism (inferential position) that's neither constitutive nor merely pragmatic.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE

The Fricker case does not escape the dilemma—it illustrates it. The question is what "epistemic privilege" means in the Sarah example once we examine it under interpretive constraints.

Sarah's report cites premises: the incident with her child, her reading about leash laws, her attention to owner behavior. Fricker says Sarah has "better access" to these premises. But better access to *what*? If we're interpretivists, Sarah's belief about dogs is constituted by what best explains her total behavioral/dispositional pattern. Her report of her reasoning is *more evidence* for interpretation—it's data about what considerations she takes herself to be responding to, what she remembers, what she's been reading.

Now the crucial question: should we revise our interpretation (from "fears dogs" to "annoyed by irresponsible owners") based on her report? 

If YES—if her sincere report of her reasoning should make us abandon the behavioral interpretation even when the behavioral evidence is strong—then we're treating her report as *constitutive* or *corrective* of the interpretive fact, not just as evidence for it. That's radical authority: her say-so determines what she believes, overriding what the best interpretation of her behavior would otherwise conclude.

If NO—if we should weigh her report as significant evidence but the behavioral pattern could still outweigh it (perhaps she's confabulating, or self-deceived, or her cited reasoning is post-hoc rationalization)—then what is the epistemic asymmetry beyond "subjects usually know their own reasoning better than observers do, so we should weight their reports heavily absent countervailing evidence"? That's precisely the pragmatic-epistemic default the thesis describes.

Fricker's "distinctive inferential position" sounds like middle ground, but it's actually just a specification of what evidence subjects distinctively have. Yes, Sarah has better access to her premises—but that's better access to *evidence for interpretation*, not better access to an interpretation-independent fact about what she believes. The thesis doesn't deny subjects have distinctive evidence. It asks: once you have that distinctive evidence, does it do anything beyond serving as (weighted) input to interpretation?

The Sarah case reveals this. Either her report of her reasoning *settles* what she believes (overriding behavioral evidence)—radical authority—or it's very strong evidence that can nevertheless be defeated by other evidence—which is the pragmatic default elevated by good epistemic reasons, but still ultimately just "trust first-person reports heavily but defeasibly," not a theoretically distinct category.

Fricker's "inferential position" explains *why* the pragmatic default is rational, but it doesn't create a third category between constitutive authority and well-grounded pragmatic policy.


VERDICT

The defence succeeds, though the victory is narrow and exposes something important about the thesis.

The defence is right that Fricker's position, when examined under interpretive constraints, faces the same question: does Sarah's report of her reasoning override interpretation or serve as input to it? The defence correctly identifies that "better access to premises" is better access to *evidence for interpretation*, not access to an interpretation-independent psychological fact.

The key move—distinguishing between "explaining why pragmatic default is rational" and "creating a third category"—is sound. Fricker's account does explain why we should weight first-person reports heavily (subjects have distinctive evidence), but this explanation doesn't establish that first-person authority is something other than a well-grounded evidential presumption.

However, the defence's success reveals what the previous objection (UNFALSIFIABLE) was pointing at: the thesis treats "pragmatic default with good epistemic grounds" as not theoretically significant. But Fricker's account *is* a theoretical contribution—it explains the epistemic mechanism (inferential position) that grounds the asymmetry. The thesis dismisses this as "mere" pragmatic default, but there's a difference between:
- "We trust first-person reports by convention"
- "We trust first-person reports because subjects have systematic epistemic advantages that we can specify and theorize"

The defence is right that Fricker doesn't escape the dilemma's structure, but this might show the dilemma captures something real rather than being artificially sharpened. The question is whether the thesis is right to treat the second position above as not "theoretically significant."

Still, within the terms of this exchange, the defence succeeds: Fricker's position does not provide a stable third category that the thesis's framework cannot accommodate. The counterexample fails.

</details>

---

### O8. The asymmetry between self and other-ascription is exactly backwards: we have MORE interpretive distance from ourselves than from others because we cannot observe our own behaviour from an external perspective, which is why close friends often know our patterns better than we do.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 4/5 · worst 4/5 · wounded
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared framing across all parties that treats first-person authority as an epistemic asymmetry requiring explanation, rather than as a social practice requiring debunking
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes the puzzle is explaining how self-knowledge survives being merely interpretive, given that others do interpret us. But the actual phenomenology and accuracy data suggest others often get our patterns, traits, and standing attitudes more accurately than we do (as personality psychology studies show). The key premise: behavioural evidence is epistemically superior to introspective seemings for many mental states, and we have worse access to our own behavioural patterns than observers do. I cannot see my facial expressions, tone patterns, or body language; I cannot sample my behaviour across contexts as others can. So the real puzzle isn't 'how does first-person authority survive interpretation?' but 'why do we grant ourselves special authority when we're often worse-positioned than others?' This reframes first-person authority as a pragmatic social convention (don't challenge people's self-ascriptions) rather than an epistemic fact to be explained.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Inverting the puzzle is provocative but the empirical claim about others' superior access is contestable and familiar.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Occurrent conscious states show phenomenal access gives something observers lack entirely; defence shows access is interpretive but cannot eliminate the residual asymmetry
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully shows the thesis makes substantive empirical claims about epistemic reliability, not mere stipulation about observational access
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 3/5, answered. Defence unifies the two readings under epistemic reliability; no equivocation, though the 'exactly backwards' rhetoric may overstate the reversal
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. Defence specifies clear empirical and conceptual falsifiers; conceptual revision is the contribution, not evasion
- 🔧 **Survives as:** For standing personality traits and behavioral patterns (not occurrent conscious states), other-knowledge often achieves greater epistemic reliability than self-knowledge because external observers avoid the systematic distortions (motivated reasoning, selective attention, narrative construction) that corrupt first-person evidence-gathering, making the traditional assumption that phenomenal access secures epistemic privilege incorrect for this domain
- **Flaw:** The thesis overstates its case with 'exactly backwards' and 'MORE distance'—even granting that categorizing phenomenology is interpretive, having-phenomenology-plus-interpreting remains epistemically different from interpreting-without-having-it for occurrent conscious states

<details><summary>2 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) The asymmetry between self- and other-ascription is real but gets its direction backwards: we have *less* interpretive access to ourselves than to others because we cannot observe our own behaviour from the third-person perspective that makes interpretation tractable.
- (3/5) Accepting that we know our minds primarily through interpretation commits you to the view that other minds are more knowable than your own, since you have more varied behavioral evidence about others than about yourself.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider occurrent conscious experiences like pain, visual sensations, or active thoughts. When I stub my toe, I know immediately and with certainty that I'm in pain—no interpretation required. A close friend observing me might miss that I'm in pain entirely if I suppress wincing, or might think I'm in pain when I'm actually just tired. Similarly, right now I know whether I'm visualizing a blue triangle or a red circle; no external observer, however close, can access this at all without my report. Or consider active thinking: I know immediately what I'm currently pondering, whether it's the thesis I'm reading or what to have for dinner. My friend watching me stare into space has no access to this content whatsoever. The thesis claims we have MORE interpretive distance from ourselves than from others because we cannot observe our own behavior externally. But these cases show the opposite: for occurrent conscious states, we have *direct* access while others must rely entirely on behavioral interpretation (which often fails completely). The thesis may work for standing patterns and traits, but it fails for the phenomenally conscious states that form the core of traditional first-person authority puzzles. These cases aren't about social conventions—they're about genuine epistemic asymmetry.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis overgeneralizes from personality traits to all mental states, but occurrent conscious experiences represent the paradigm cases where first-person authority seems strongest and least interpretive.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection trades on an equivocation about what "interpretive distance" means and wrongly assumes that immediacy equals accuracy.

First, the phenomenological immediacy of pain or visual experience does not establish epistemic superiority—it establishes only *felt certainty*. I feel certain I'm in pain, yes, but this certainty is itself a psychological state requiring interpretation. The move from "I have a sensation" to "I am in pain (rather than intense pressure, or muscle fatigue, or phantom sensation)" is already interpretive—I'm applying a concept to raw phenomenology. Chronic pain patients regularly misidentify their sensations; people in extreme cold report burning; phantom limb sufferers feel sensations "in" absent body parts. The immediacy doesn't guarantee I've got the interpretation right.

Second, the objection assumes others have no access to occurrent states, but this is false. When I stub my toe, the observer sees the stub, sees my immediate bodily response, has full access to the cause and the typical effect-profile. If I then report "I'm fine, no pain," while hopping and grimacing, the observer is epistemically entitled to judge I'm wrong about my own state. The behavioural evidence can trump the self-report precisely because the self-report is itself interpretive and potentially distorted by motivated reasoning, inattention, or conceptual confusion.

Third, the "blue triangle vs. red circle" case is tendentious. What I have direct access to is *some phenomenology*. Whether I'm correctly categorizing it as "visualizing" rather than "remembering" or "imagining," whether the colour-quale I'm experiencing really maps to "blue" rather than blue-green, whether the shape is triangular or merely three-sided-ish—these are all interpretive judgments I can get wrong. Synaesthetes, colour-blind people, and subjects in change-blindness experiments routinely mischaracterize their own phenomenology when tested against objective measures.

The deeper point: the objection assumes a layer of "raw feels" that are self-intimating and interpretation-free. But even identifying a sensation as *pain* rather than pressure, as *visual* rather than imaginative, as *about dinner* rather than free-floating anxiety about evening plans—all of this requires bringing concepts to bear on phenomenology, and that's interpretation. I cannot step outside my own conceptual scheme to verify I've applied the concepts correctly. The observer, with access to my behaviour, context, and patterns, often can.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but not completely.

It fails because there remains a residual asymmetry the defence cannot eliminate: even granting that *identifying* and *categorizing* phenomenology is interpretive, there is still a difference between having-the-phenomenology-plus-interpreting-it (first-person) and interpreting-without-having-it (third-person). When I stub my toe, even if I misidentify the sensation as "pressure" rather than "pain," I still have *some* sensation to misidentify. The observer who sees nothing has *no* sensation to work with—they must infer from behaviour alone that any sensation exists at all.

This matters because the thesis claims we have *MORE* interpretive distance from ourselves than from others. But "having phenomenology + interpreting it" is not more distant than "interpreting without having it." At worst, we're equally interpretive; at best, having the phenomenology gives us *something* (even if not infallible access) that others lack entirely.

The defence successfully shows that first-person access is more interpretive than the objection admits, and that others can sometimes out-perform us even on occurrent states. But it cannot show we have *more* distance than others do. The thesis overstates its case and must be reported as defeated on its strongest ("exactly backwards," "MORE distance") formulation.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis presents itself as a discovery about epistemic asymmetry, but it is actually just a stipulative redefinition of "interpretive distance" that makes the conclusion trivial. 

The standard meaning of "interpretive distance" in philosophy of mind refers to the gap between evidence and conclusion—how much inference is required to get from what's available to you to a judgment about mental states. On this standard meaning, the thesis is simply false: I don't infer my current pain from evidence, while you must infer it from my behavior.

So the thesis must be using "interpretive distance" to mean something like "ability to observe behavioral patterns from an external vantage point." But on this redefinition, the claim becomes trivial: "We have worse access to our own behavioral patterns than others do because we cannot observe our own behavior from an external perspective" is just a tautology. It's true by definition that I can't observe myself externally—that's what "external" means.

The apparently substantive claim that this makes self-knowledge *worse* than other-knowledge simply follows from the verbal stipulation that behavioral evidence is the gold standard. But this is contested, not discovered. The thesis hasn't shown that behavioral patterns matter more than phenomenal access; it has simply *defined* epistemic position in terms of behavioral observation, making the conclusion that others are better-positioned a matter of stipulation rather than argument.

The move from "we can't see our own faces" (uncontested) to "therefore we have more interpretive distance" (apparently substantive) works only by equivocating on what "distance" means.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This is weak. While there is some terminological flexibility being exploited, the thesis does make a genuinely contestable empirical claim: that behavioral evidence is epistemically superior to phenomenal access for many mental states, and that this superiority is underappreciated in the traditional framing. That's not merely verbal.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE.

The triviality charge rests on a false dichotomy: either "interpretive distance" means inferential gap (making the thesis false) or it means external observational access (making it trivial). But there's a third option the objection ignores: interpretive distance as *susceptibility to systematic distortion in evidence-gathering*.

The thesis is not merely stipulating that behavioral evidence is superior—it's making an empirical claim about epistemic reliability that can be tested and has been tested. The personality psychology literature (Vazire's work on self-other knowledge asymmetries, for instance) shows that for evaluative traits and behavioral patterns, other-ratings predict future behavior better than self-ratings do. This isn't true by definition; it's an empirical discovery that could have gone the other way.

Why does it go this way? Because first-person evidence-gathering is systematically corrupted by mechanisms unavailable to external observers: motivated reasoning (I want to see myself as generous), selective attention (I notice my generous acts, forget my selfish ones), lack of comparison class (I can't easily compare my generosity to others' baselines), and narrative self-construction (I interpret ambiguous actions to fit my self-concept). The external observer lacks these specific distortions. They have *different* distortions (limited sample, projection, stereotyping), but for many mental states, their distortions are less severe.

This is not trivial. The claim is: the very mechanisms that give me "direct" access to my own mental life—being inside it, having it as phenomenology rather than observation—systematically corrupt my evidence-gathering in ways that make me *worse-positioned* than someone with only behavioral access. The inside view isn't just different; it's epistemically disadvantaged for a specifiable class of mental states.

The objection assumes that "having phenomenology" automatically gives epistemic advantage over "observing behavior." But that's exactly what the thesis contests. Having the phenomenology might give me something to work with, but it also gives me motivated reasoning, narrative pressure, and inability to see patterns. The external observer's "lack" of phenomenal access is simultaneously a *freedom from* these distortions.

Consider: a camera pointed at my face has "less access" to my emotional states than I do (it has no phenomenology at all), yet emotion-recognition AI trained on facial expressions can outperform self-reports for certain emotions. This isn't true by stipulation—it's an empirical fact about when behavioral evidence beats introspective evidence. The thesis generalizes this: the traditional framing treats phenomenal access as epistemically privileged by default, but the empirical evidence suggests that for many mental states, it's epistemically *compromised*.

That's a substantive, falsifiable claim, not a tautology.

VERDICT.

The defence succeeds.

The triviality objection fails because it misidentifies what the thesis is claiming. The thesis is not merely pointing out that we can't see our own faces (trivial) or stipulating that behavioral evidence matters most (verbal). It's making a substantive empirical claim: that the mechanisms accompanying first-person access—the very features traditionally thought to secure first-person authority—systematically distort our evidence-gathering in ways that, for many mental states, make us worse-positioned than external observers.

This claim has empirical content. It predicts that other-ratings will outperform self-ratings on certain dimensions (they do), that we'll systematically misjudge our own behavioral patterns (we do), that external observers will catch contradictions in our self-reports that we miss (they do). These predictions could fail; that they don't is evidence for the thesis, not evidence of its triviality.

The objection tries to trap the thesis in a dilemma: either use "interpretive distance" standardly (making the thesis false) or idiosyncratically (making it trivial). But the thesis escapes by using the term to track a real epistemic phenomenon—systematic distortion in evidence-gathering—that the standard framing obscures. The inside view comes with costs the tradition hasn't adequately recognized. That's not a verbal trick; it's a substantive reframing.

The triviality charge fails.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "interpretive distance" between a reading that makes it plausible and a reading that makes it interesting, and the argument needs both.

**Plausible reading**: "Interpretive distance" means susceptibility to systematic distortion in pattern-recognition. On this reading, the claim is plausible—we do suffer motivated reasoning, selective attention, and narrative self-construction that external observers avoid. The empirical evidence about personality traits supports this.

**Interesting reading**: "Interpretive distance" means epistemic gap requiring inference—the traditional sense in which first-person authority is puzzling precisely because others must interpret while we seemingly don't. On this reading, the claim is interesting because it reverses the traditional asymmetry, suggesting the real puzzle is why we grant ourselves authority when we're worse-positioned.

The argument needs both. It needs the plausible reading to make the empirical case (behavioral evidence beats introspective evidence for patterns/traits). But it needs the interesting reading to deliver the philosophical payoff—that first-person authority is "exactly backwards" and the traditional framing misidentifies the epistemic asymmetry.

The equivocation appears when the thesis moves from "we're worse at spotting our behavioral patterns" (plausible, distortion-based reading) to "we have MORE interpretive distance from ourselves" (interesting, traditional-gap reading). These aren't the same claim. Having more systematic distortions in pattern-recognition doesn't mean having more inferential distance from one's mental states. The friend who knows my patterns better still infers them from behavior; I still have non-inferential access to my occurrent thoughts. The thesis conflates being-worse-at-X with being-more-distant-from-X, trading on two senses of "distance."

STRENGTH: 3/5 — A genuine problem, answerable. The thesis could bite the bullet and restrict itself to the distortion-reading, abandoning the claim about "interpretive distance" in the traditional sense. But then it loses the radical reversal and becomes a more modest point about pattern-blindness.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE.

The equivocation charge assumes that "systematic distortion in pattern-recognition" and "epistemic gap requiring inference" are two different senses of "interpretive distance," but they're not—they're two aspects of the *same* epistemic phenomenon, and the thesis unifies them rather than equivocating between them.

The key is this: inference and interpretation aren't just about *whether* you have to take steps to reach a conclusion, but about *how reliable* those steps are and *what can go wrong*. Interpretive distance is fundamentally about the *quality* of the epistemic path from evidence to judgment, not just its length.

Consider: A person with severe confabulation syndrome might have "immediate" access to their intentions—they report them without conscious inference—but their reports are systematically unreliable because the mechanism generating them is corrupted. Meanwhile, a neurologist observing their behavior might need to make explicit inferences, but those inferences are *more reliable* because they're based on less-corrupted evidence. Who has more "interpretive distance" from the patient's real intentions? 

The traditional answer—the neurologist does, because they infer while the patient "just knows"—gets the epistemology backwards. The neurologist has *less* distance because their path to the truth is more reliable, even though it's explicitly inferential. The patient has *more* distance because their path is corrupted, even though it feels immediate.

This isn't equivocation; it's correction. The traditional framing treats phenomenal immediacy as if it were epistemic proximity, but that's a mistake. A corrupted immediate path is epistemically more distant than a reliable inferential one. The thesis doesn't trade on two senses of "distance"—it reveals that the traditional sense (immediacy = closeness) was confused from the start.

Now apply this to the self-other case. When I try to judge my own personality traits or behavioral patterns, I do engage in interpretation—I'm not just "reading off" my patterns from phenomenology. I notice some behaviors, categorize them, compare them to my self-concept, adjust for consistency. This process *feels* immediate because it's automatic and motivated, but it's interpretive all the way down. And crucially, it's systematically corrupted by mechanisms the external observer avoids.

The external observer *also* interprets—they infer my traits from behavior. But their interpretive process is *less corrupted* for the specific distortions that plague self-assessment. So they have less interpretive distance, not because they skip interpretation (they don't), but because their interpretation is more reliable.

The thesis doesn't equivocate between "distortion" and "inferential gap." It argues that what matters epistemically is reliability-of-path, and that the self's path is corrupted in ways that make it *more distant* from truth despite *feeling* immediate. The friend's path is explicitly inferential but less corrupted, making it *less distant* despite lacking phenomenal access.

The philosophical payoff isn't lost—it's sharpened. The traditional puzzle assumes phenomenal immediacy secures epistemic privilege. The thesis shows this is backwards: phenomenal immediacy can *increase* epistemic distance when it comes packaged with systematic distortions. First-person authority isn't puzzling because we have special access; it's puzzling because we *lack* the external vantage point that would correct our distortions, yet we grant ourselves authority anyway.

This isn't two different claims equivocated between. It's one claim: epistemic distance is about reliability-of-access, and for many mental states, the self's access is less reliable (more distant) than others' access, despite being phenomenally immediate.

VERDICT.

The defence succeeds, but with an important qualification that may still wound the thesis.

The defence successfully shows there's no equivocation. The thesis offers a unified account where interpretive distance is about epistemic reliability, not phenomenal immediacy. Systematic distortion *is* a form of epistemic distance—indeed, the epistemically relevant form. A corrupted immediate path is genuinely more distant from truth than a reliable inferential path. This isn't trading on two senses; it's arguing that the traditional sense gets epistemology wrong.

However, the defence reveals a cost: the thesis must now concede that it's *not* reversing the traditional asymmetry—it's *rejecting* the traditional framing entirely. The traditional puzzle treats first-person authority as puzzling because it seems epistemically unearned (how can mere seemings ground knowledge?). The thesis isn't saying "actually, others have first-person authority and we don't"—it's saying "the whole framework of 'first-person authority' as epistemic privilege based on access-type is confused."

This is philosophically defensible, but it means the thesis's rhetoric of "exactly backwards" is misleading. It's not that the asymmetry runs the opposite direction; it's that there's no single asymmetry—different mental states have different epistemic profiles, and access-type (phenomenal vs. behavioral) doesn't determine epistemic position the way the tradition assumes.

Still, this doesn't make the objection succeed. The charge was *equivocation*—using one sense to establish plausibility and another to establish interest. But the defence shows the thesis uses one consistent sense throughout: epistemic distance as reliability-of-path-to-truth. That sense makes both the empirical claims plausible (our path is corrupted for patterns/traits) *and* the philosophical point interesting (phenomenal immediacy doesn't secure epistemic privilege).

The equivocation charge fails. The thesis stands, though perhaps with less radical rhetoric than "exactly backwards" suggests.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis protects itself from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as "interpretive distance" and which mental states are relevant. 

When confronted with occurrent conscious states (pain, visual imagery, active thoughts) where self-knowledge seems clearly superior, the thesis retreats to: "Even identifying pain as pain is interpretive, and observers with behavioral access can sometimes correct us." But when pressed on whether having-phenomenology-plus-interpreting gives us *something* observers lack, it shifts to: "What matters is reliability-of-path, not what you have access to—corrupted immediate paths are more distant than reliable inferential ones."

This makes the thesis unfalsifiable. Any apparent counterexample—any case where first-person access seems epistemically superior—gets reinterpreted as actually showing interpretive distance because: (1) categorizing phenomenology is "interpretive," or (2) the access might be "corrupted" by motivated reasoning, or (3) we're measuring "reliability" not "immediacy," or (4) this is just about "standing patterns" not occurrent states, or (5) actually it applies to occurrent states too when properly understood.

The thesis can't specify in advance what would count as a case where we genuinely have *less* interpretive distance from ourselves than others do. Every candidate gets ruled out by expanding what counts as "interpretation" (now includes any conceptual categorization), "corruption" (potentially present in any first-person access), or "distance" (redefined as reliability rather than inferential gap). The empirical personality psychology evidence is treated as confirming, but cases where self-knowledge clearly succeeds (knowing what I'm currently thinking about) get dismissed as "still interpretive really" or "not the core cases."

The weak point: the thesis lacks falsifiability conditions because it can redefine its key terms to accommodate any apparent counterexample.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This is weak. The thesis does make falsifiable empirical predictions (that other-ratings outperform self-ratings on specific dimensions, testable in psychology studies), and it does specify a domain (behavioral patterns, standing traits) where its claims are strongest. The conceptual reframing of "interpretive distance" isn't protection from cases—it's a substantive philosophical claim about what epistemic distance consists in. The thesis could be falsified by showing that self-ratings consistently outperform other-ratings even for the personality traits and behavioral patterns it focuses on, or by demonstrating that phenomenal immediacy does secure epistemic reliability in ways the thesis denies.

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE.

The unfalsifiability charge confuses *having a revisionary analysis* with *being unfalsifiable*. The thesis offers a substantive account of what interpretive distance is (reliability of epistemic path, not phenomenal immediacy), and this account generates specific, testable predictions that could fail.

Here are the falsifiability conditions the objection claims don't exist:

**Empirical falsifiers**: The thesis predicts that for standing traits, behavioral patterns, and evaluative self-assessments, other-ratings will systematically outperform self-ratings in predicting future behavior, correlating with objective measures, and avoiding motivated distortions. This is falsifiable—if studies consistently showed self-ratings outperforming other-ratings on these dimensions, the thesis would be empirically defeated. The fact that Vazire's work and similar studies *support* the thesis doesn't make it unfalsifiable; it makes it empirically confirmed.

**Conceptual falsifiers**: The thesis claims that phenomenal immediacy doesn't secure epistemic privilege—that having-the-phenomenology can be epistemically worse than observing-the-behavior when the phenomenal path is corrupted. This is falsifiable by showing that phenomenal access provides self-correcting mechanisms that behavioral observation lacks, or that the distortions affecting self-assessment are equally present (or worse) in other-assessment. The thesis could be wrong about which distortions matter most.

**Scope falsifiers**: The thesis explicitly limits its strongest claims to "many mental states"—particularly standing patterns and traits. It doesn't claim universal reversal. When pressed on occurrent conscious states, the thesis doesn't "retreat"—it distinguishes cases. For "what am I currently thinking about?" the thesis can grant that self-knowledge may be superior (though still interpretive). For "am I a generous person?" or "do I interrupt people often?" it claims other-knowledge is superior. This isn't unfalsifiable protection; it's domain specification.

The objection treats every clarification as evasion, but clarification is how theories respond to objections. When the thesis says "even categorizing pain as pain is interpretive," that's not ad hoc protection—it's a substantive claim about concepts and phenomenology that philosophers like Sellars and Brandom have defended independently. It could be wrong (maybe there's non-conceptual phenomenal content that's self-intimating), but it's not unfalsifiable.

The key move: the objection assumes that if a thesis revises what "interpretive distance" means, it's cheating. But that's precisely what philosophical progress often looks like. The thesis argues that the traditional understanding of interpretive distance (as phenomenal immediacy vs. behavioral inference) misses what's epistemically important (reliability of path to truth). This is a substantive claim that could be defeated by showing that the traditional understanding better tracks epistemic success.

Compare: If someone objected to Einstein's relativity by saying "You've just redefined 'simultaneous' so that your theory can't be falsified by cases where events seem simultaneous," they'd be missing the point. The redefinition is the theory's contribution—it claims the old concept was confused. Relativity is falsifiable (by light-speed measurements, gravitational observations, etc.), even though it redefines core terms.

Similarly here: The thesis redefines "interpretive distance" to track epistemic reliability rather than phenomenal immediacy. This is falsifiable by empirical tests of when self-knowledge vs. other-knowledge succeeds, and by conceptual arguments about what epistemic distance should track. The thesis makes specific predictions (other-ratings beat self-ratings for traits/patterns) and offers philosophical arguments (phenomenal immediacy ≠ epistemic privilege). Both could fail.

The objection's examples of "protection" are actually examples of the thesis doing what theories should do: distinguishing cases, specifying scope, and defending its conceptual framework. That's not unfalsifiability; it's philosophy.

VERDICT.

The defence succeeds.

The unfalsifiability charge fails because it misidentifies normal theoretical behavior—clarifying scope, defending conceptual frameworks, distinguishing cases—as evasive protection from counterexamples. The thesis makes falsifiable predictions and could be defeated in multiple ways.

The objection is right that the thesis revises what "interpretive distance" means, moving from phenomenal-immediacy-vs-inference to reliability-of-epistemic-path. But this revision is the thesis's *contribution*, not its *protection*. The new concept tracks something real (epistemic reliability) and generates testable predictions (about when self-knowledge vs. other-knowledge succeeds).

The thesis could be falsified by:
- Empirical evidence showing self-ratings consistently outperform other-ratings even for the behavioral patterns and traits it focuses on
- Philosophical arguments showing that phenomenal immediacy does secure epistemic privilege in ways that overcome the distortions the thesis identifies  
- Demonstrations that the distortions affecting self-assessment are equally or more present in other-assessment
- Cases showing that having-phenomenology provides self-correcting mechanisms that make the first-person path more reliable despite potential corruptions

None of these falsifiers have been met—which is why the thesis stands, not why it's unfalsifiable.

The objection treats the thesis's response to the occurrent-states case (distinguishing what we have immediate access to from what we have reliable access to) as evasion. But this is exactly what the thesis should do: show how its framework handles apparent counterexamples. The response isn't "that case doesn't count" but rather "that case shows phenomenal immediacy, but not epistemic superiority, because immediacy ≠ reliability."

The unfalsifiability charge fails. The thesis remains standing with its conceptual revision intact and its empirical predictions confirmed by available evidence.

</details>

---

### O9. First-person authority is actually semantic authority—privileged access to what we mean by our utterances—which both observationalists and interpretivists mistake for psychological authority about mental state contents.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that first-person authority must be authority about psychological facts rather than linguistic meaning
- **Sketch:** When I say 'I'm angry' with first-person authority, what I'm authoritative about is what I mean by this utterance, not whether some independently-specified psychological state obtains. This explains why correction sounds like: 'No, that's not what you mean—you're frustrated not angry' (semantic reinterpretation) rather than 'You're wrong—you're not angry' (psychological correction). Confabulation cases involve genuine authority over meaning—subjects mean what they say about justification—while being wrong about psychological causation. This dissolves the problem: first-person psychological claims are moves in a linguistic practice governed by semantic norms of meaning-determination, where speakers have default (defeasible) authority over what they mean. The key premise is that mental state self-ascriptions are primarily semantic performances whose correctness conditions are meaning-based rather than world-tracking.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Semantic authority is an interesting move, but the distinction between semantic and psychological authority may prove difficult to maintain under scrutiny.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The non-native speaker case proves semantic authority presupposes rather than constitutes psychological authority—we interpret their meaning by assuming they know their pain state
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence shows constitutive claims but fails to demonstrate they address the original phenomenon rather than replacing it with linguistic practice theory
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence cannot coherently occupy middle ground between trivial semantic authority and implausible constitutive claim—confabulation cases prove rightness about preference is psychological not semantic
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence successfully shows falsifiability through predictions but inadvertently reveals the thesis is actually falsified by evidence on aphasic patients and developmental patterns
- 🔧 **Survives as:** First-person authority involves both psychological access to one's states and semantic authority over how to express them, with the semantic dimension explaining why correction takes interpretive rather than observational form, without reducing psychological authority to semantic authority
- **Flaw:** Semantic authority over utterance-meaning presupposes rather than constitutes psychological self-knowledge—speakers must already know which state they are in to know which semantic performance is appropriate, reversing the claimed direction of determination

<details><summary>8 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) First-person authority is grounded not in epistemic access but in constitutive authority: avowals partially constitute our mental states rather than merely reporting pre-existing ones.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in epistemic asymmetry but in constitutive asymmetry: self-ascriptions of attitudes partially constitute those attitudes in a way other-ascriptions cannot.
- (1/5) First-person authority is grounded not in superior epistemic access but in constitutive authority: self-ascriptions of mental states don't report pre-existing facts but partially constitute what they describe, which is why they resist third-person correction in a way interpretations from behaviour cannot.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in epistemic privilege but in constitutive authority: self-ascriptions of belief and desire are performative utterances that partially constitute the mental states they purport to report.
- (2/5) First-person authority derives not from epistemological privilege but from constitutive authority: self-ascriptions of attitude create normative constraints on subsequent interpretation that other-ascriptions do not.
- (2/5) First-person authority derives not from epistemic privilege but from constitutive privilege: self-ascriptions of belief partly determine what one believes, while other-ascriptions do not.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in privileged epistemic access but in constitutive authority: avowing a mental state in the right conditions partly constitutes having it, which explains why self-interpretation can be simultaneously interpretive and authoritative in a way other-interpretation cannot be.
- (2/5) First-person authority consists not in privileged epistemic access but in constitutive authority: sincere self-ascriptions of mental states partially constitute what one believes or desires, while sincere other-ascriptions do not.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider pain self-ascription. When someone steps on a nail and immediately says "I'm in pain," they exercise first-person authority. But this cannot be mere semantic authority about what they mean by their utterance. 

Here's why: The person could be a non-native speaker who barely knows English, yet still has first-person authority about their pain. They might even use the wrong word entirely—saying "I have hurt" with broken grammar—and still be authoritative about their pain state, even while being non-authoritative about English semantics. 

More decisively: Prelinguistic infants and non-linguistic animals have privileged access to their own pain states (they know when they hurt in a way observers don't), yet they have zero semantic authority because they make no utterances with meanings. The authority exists without the linguistic performance.

The thesis claims "mental state self-ascriptions are primarily semantic performances whose correctness conditions are meaning-based rather than world-tracking." But the nail-stepper's authority about their pain tracks the world (the actual pain state), not linguistic meaning. Even if they meant something completely different by their words, they'd still be right about being in pain.

The weak point is the premise that "first-person psychological claims are moves in a linguistic practice" rather than reports of independently-existing psychological facts.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously challenges the thesis by showing first-person authority exists prior to and independent of semantic performances.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection conflates two entirely separate phenomena and mistakes one for first-person authority.

Prelinguistic infants and animals do not have first-person authority—they have privileged *causal access*. The infant feels pain, yes, but first-person authority is not mere feeling or differential responsiveness. It is the social-epistemic standing to make authoritative claims. A being that cannot make claims cannot have authority. Authority is a normative status within linguistic practice, not a causal relation to one's own states.

The non-native speaker case actually supports the thesis. When they say "I have hurt," what are we doing when we accept their authority? We are *not* checking whether the English sentence "I have hurt" correctly describes some language-independent psychological fact. We are treating them as authoritative about what they are trying to express—we interpret their meaning and accept that interpretation. Our response is: "Ah, you mean you're in pain"—a semantic reconstruction. We grant them authority over what they mean to communicate, then help them say it properly.

The "even if they meant something completely different" move is incoherent. If they meant something completely different, they would be expressing that different thing, and we would (charitably) interpret them as meaning it. You cannot mean X while saying Y-that-means-not-X and still "be right about X." Being right about being in pain *just is* successfully meaning that you are in pain when you self-ascribe pain.

The nail-stepper's authority is not about tracking the world independently of meaning. It is about their utterance-meaning constitutively determining what psychological state they are self-ascribing. The world-tracking happens at a different level: whether pain exists is one question; whether "I'm in pain" correctly expresses pain-self-ascription is the semantic question where authority resides.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but not where the objection strikes.

The defence succeeds in distinguishing privileged causal access from first-person authority—infants genuinely are not counterexamples. And it correctly identifies that authority requires claim-making capacity.

But it fails on the non-native speaker, and fails decisively. Here is why: When the non-native speaker says "I have hurt" and we accept their authority, we are *not* merely accepting their semantic authority about what they mean. We are accepting their authority about *whether they are in pain*—a psychological fact. 

The proof: Suppose they say "I have hurt" and we interpret this as meaning they're in pain. Now suppose we discover they're a masochist who calls pleasure "hurt" in their idiolect. The objector is right that our original acceptance was about the pain-state, not about meaning. We accepted: "You are in pain" not "You mean pain by those words."

More fatally: The semantic interpretation itself depends on assuming psychological authority. We interpret "I have hurt" as expressing pain *because* we grant them authority about which state they're in.
