The semantic interpretation presupposes, rather than constitutes, the psychological authority. We figure out what they mean by assuming they know what state they're in.

The thesis tries to reduce psychological authority to semantic authority, but the reduction runs backwards. The defence fails.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. 

The thesis claims to offer a substantive reframing that "dissolves the problem" of first-person authority by revealing it as semantic rather than psychological authority. But this is merely a verbal stipulation about what we choose to call "first-person authority," not a discovery about the phenomenon.

Everyone already agrees that speakers have some kind of privileged position regarding what they mean by their utterances—this is uncontested. The interesting question has always been: do speakers have privileged access to their psychological states themselves? The thesis simply redefines "first-person authority" to mean only the uncontroversial semantic privilege, then declares the problem dissolved.

This is like "solving" the problem of consciousness by stipulating that "consciousness" just means "functional responsiveness," then announcing that consciousness is easy after all. You have not dissolved the original problem; you have changed the subject.

The weak point is the implicit claim that this is a discovery rather than a stipulation. The thesis presents "first-person authority is actually semantic authority" as if revealing a hidden truth, but it is merely proposing to use the term differently. The original phenomenon—whatever privileged relation subjects have to their own psychological states—remains entirely unaddressed. Observationalists and interpretivists can simply respond: "Fine, call that semantic authority if you like. We're interested in psychological self-knowledge, which you've renamed but not explained."

The thesis mistakes a terminological choice for a philosophical achievement.

STRENGTH: 2/5 — This has some force in exposing that the thesis may accomplish less than advertised, but the thesis does offer substantive claims about meaning-determination and correctness conditions that go beyond mere relabeling, even if those claims ultimately fail for other reasons.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection misidentifies what makes the reframing substantive. The thesis is not merely stipulating a new use of "first-person authority"—it is making a constitutive claim about what the phenomenon *is*.

Consider the parallel: Eliminativists about witches do not merely stipulate that "witch" means something else. They claim there never were witches—that what people called "witch-detection" was actually detecting something entirely different (perhaps unusual women, or scapegoats for crop failure). Similarly, this thesis claims that what philosophers have called "privileged access to psychological states" has always been privileged access to utterance-meaning, misidentified as something else.

The substantive claim is this: The correctness conditions for first-person psychological claims are constituted by semantic norms, not by correspondence to independently-specifiable psychological facts. When "I'm angry" is correct, its correctness consists in successfully meaning anger-self-ascription according to the norms governing that speaker's linguistic practice, not in matching some psychological state that could be specified independently of what the speaker means.

This is not changing the subject—it is a metaphysical claim about what the subject is. The thesis says: There is no "privileged relation subjects have to their own psychological states" over and above semantic authority, because psychological self-ascription just is a semantically-governed practice. Observationalists and interpretivists cannot simply continue discussing "psychological self-knowledge" as if it were independent, because the thesis denies that there is such an independent thing.

The confabulation cases show the substantive bite: Subjects genuinely have authority (semantic authority over meaning), yet are wrong about psychological causation. This is not relabeling—it is explaining why authority and error coexist by locating authority at a different level than traditionally assumed.

The reframing is substantive because it makes predictions: First-person authority should track semantic competence, not introspective capacity. Correction should take semantic forms. The phenomenon should be absent where linguistic practice is absent. These are testable claims about the structure of the phenomenon, not mere terminological choices.


VERDICT.

The defence fails.

The defence correctly identifies that the thesis makes constitutive claims, not mere stipulations. But it fails to show these claims address the original phenomenon rather than replacing it.

Here is the fatal problem: The thesis says psychological states are not "independently-specifiable" from what speakers mean. But they clearly are. Pain, anger, belief, and desire can be specified through behavioral criteria, neural states, functional roles, evolutionary purpose, or phenomenal character—none of which require consulting what the subject means by their utterances. A creature could have all these states without having language at all.

When the thesis says "psychological self-ascription just is a semantically-governed practice," it has indeed changed the subject. The original question was: How do subjects know about their psychological states? The thesis answers: Self-ascription is a linguistic practice with semantic norms. But this leaves the original question untouched: How do subjects know which psychological state to self-ascribe? What gives them privileged access to which semantic performance is appropriate?

The confabulation cases prove this. The subject confabulates about causation—fine, they have semantic authority over meaning "justification" while being wrong about causes. But they are still *right* about which state they are in (the choice itself, the preference, the attitude). That correctness is psychological, not semantic. The semantic authority presupposes psychological knowledge of which state to express.

The objection is right: The thesis offers a substantive theory, but it is a theory of a different phenomenon. It explains linguistic authority while leaving psychological self-knowledge unexplained. That is changing the subject, even if not through mere stipulation.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — **EQUIVOCATION****ATTEMPT**
The thesis equivocates on "what we mean by our utterances" between two readings that cannot both be maintained:

**Reading 1 (makes the thesis plausible):** "What we mean" refers to speaker's intention or semantic content—what interpretation the speaker intends to communicate. On this reading, it's plausible that speakers have privileged authority: of course I know what I'm trying to say better than observers do.

**Reading 2 (makes the thesis interesting):** "What we mean" determines psychological reality—meaning-determination constitutes which psychological state obtains. On this reading, semantic authority would genuinely dissolve the traditional problem by making psychological facts dependent on semantic performances.

The argument needs both but cannot have both. The thesis is plausible only on Reading 1 (uncontroversial semantic access) but interesting only on Reading 2 (constitutive claim about psychological states). 

The sketch reveals this: "subjects mean what they say about justification—while being wrong about psychological causation" only works if meaning-determination is independent of psychological facts (Reading 1). But "correctness conditions are meaning-based rather than world-tracking" only dissolves the original problem if meaning constitutes psychological reality (Reading 2).

On Reading 1, we still need to explain how speakers know which psychological state to mean. On Reading 2, the constitutive claim is implausible—psychological states exist independently of linguistic meaning, as the previous objections showed.

The weak point is the phrase "what we mean by our utterances" which slides between these incompatible readings.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This seriously damages the thesis by exposing that its apparent dissolution of the problem depends on conflating trivial semantic authority with a much stronger constitutive claim it cannot sustain.

**DEFENDER** — **[DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE**
The objection fabricates an equivocation where there is a single coherent position. There is one reading, and it is both plausible and interesting.

The thesis holds: **Meaning-determination for first-person psychological utterances is constitutively prior to psychological fact-determination, but this is not a general metaphysical claim about psychological states—it is specific to the epistemology of self-ascription.**

Here's the unified position: When I self-ascribe anger, what makes my utterance correct is not that it matches a pre-existing, independently-identifiable psychological fact. Rather, my semantic performance—my meaning anger by "I'm angry"—constitutively determines which psychological state I am self-ascribing, and the norms governing sincere self-ascription make my semantic determination authoritative (defeasibly) for the psychological attribution.

This is not "trivial semantic access." It is the substantive claim that for first-person psychological utterances specifically, the direction of determination runs: semantic performance → psychological attribution, not: psychological fact → semantic expression. The authority is over meaning, but meaning-determination here carries psychological-attributive force because of the special norms governing first-person psychological language.

The confabulation cases show exactly this unified structure: The subject has authority over what they mean (semantic), and that meaning-determination settles what psychological state gets attributed to them (constitutive for attribution), while they can still be wrong about causal mechanisms (independent psychological facts exist, but attribution goes through semantic authority).

Reading 1 and Reading 2 are not in tension—they are the same reading properly understood. Speakers have privileged access to what they mean (Reading 1), and in the specific domain of self-ascription, what they mean constitutively determines the psychological attribution (Reading 2). This is not metaphysically wild: it does not claim anger cannot exist without language. It claims that *attributing* anger to oneself in first-person utterances works through semantic norms, not through detecting pre-individuated psychological facts.

The objection's question "how do speakers know which psychological state to mean?" misunderstands the picture. They don't first know a psychological state, then choose a meaning. The semantic performance *is* the individuating act. Sincerely meaning anger-self-ascription in appropriate circumstances constitutes the authoritative psychological attribution.

This explains why correction takes semantic form ("that's not anger, that's frustration")—we are negotiating what state is being self-ascribed through semantic negotiation, not correcting observational error.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, and fails on precisely the point the objection identifies.

The defence claims "semantic performance → psychological attribution" is specific to self-ascription epistemology, not a general metaphysical claim. But this distinction collapses under pressure.

If psychological states exist independently (the defence concedes: "anger can exist without language"), then there is a fact about which state the subject is in, independent of their semantic performance. Now the question becomes: Does their semantic performance *determine* which state they are in, or does it *express* which state they are in?

If it determines (Reading 2), we have the implausible constitutive claim: my meaning anger makes me angry, which the defence explicitly denies ("not metaphysically wild").

If it expresses (Reading 1), then we need an explanation of how the subject knows which state to express—and we are back to the original problem of first-person authority about psychological facts.

The defence tries to split the difference: "meaning-determination... carries psychological-attributive force because of the special norms." But attributive force is not psychological reality. If I am actually angry (independent psychological fact), and I self-ascribe frustration with full semantic authority, am I right or wrong?

- If I am right (my semantic authority makes it so), then Reading 2 holds and psychological reality depends on meaning—the implausible constitutive claim.
- If I am wrong (I am really angry despite my authoritative self-ascription), then Reading 1 holds and semantic authority is separate from psychological fact—leaving the original problem untouched.

The confabulation cases prove the defence fails. The subject is *right* about their preference (psychological fact) while *wrong* about causation (different psychological fact). Their semantic authority does not constitute the preference—it expresses it. The rightness about preference is psychological, not semantic.

The objection succeeds: the thesis equivocates between trivial semantic authority and an implausible constitutive claim, and cannot coherently occupy the middle ground the defence attempts.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **UNFALSIFIABLE****ATTEMPT**
The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of apparent failures as successes.

Consider any putative counterexample where first-person authority seems clearly psychological rather than semantic:

**Case 1:** The nail-stepper who knows they're in pain immediately, pre-reflectively, without semantic deliberation.
- **Thesis response:** "That's privileged causal access, not first-person authority. Authority requires claim-making."

**Case 2:** The non-native speaker who has authority about their pain despite semantic incompetence.
- **Thesis response:** "We're interpreting their meaning charitably; the authority is still semantic."

**Case 3:** Someone correctly self-ascribes anger despite meaning to self-ascribe frustration.
- **Thesis response:** "If they mean frustration, they're self-ascribing frustration. You're confusing the psychological state with the self-ascription."

**Case 4:** Someone has psychological authority but semantic confusion (doesn't know what their words mean).
- **Thesis response:** "Then they lack first-person authority properly understood. Authority is semantic."

The pattern: Any case of psychological authority without semantic authority gets redefined as "not real first-person authority." Any case of semantic authority without psychological correctness gets redefined as "genuine authority over meaning, just wrong about independent facts."

The weak point is the core definitional move: "first-person authority is actually semantic authority." This "actually" allows the thesis to rule out any apparent counterexample by stipulating it away. Every case of authority-without-semantics becomes "not really authority," and every case of semantics-without-correctness becomes "authority over a different thing."

The thesis cannot be tested because it has built-in protection: it defines first-person authority as semantic authority, then when presented with non-semantic authority, declares it not to be first-person authority. This is circular immunity.
**STRENGTH: 3/5**
This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis does seem to have unusual flexibility in reclassifying counterexamples. However, the thesis does make some commitments that could potentially be tested (predictions about correction forms, about prelinguistic beings, about the structure of confabulation), so it's not completely unfalsifiable—just suspiciously well-protected from the most natural counterexamples.

**DEFENDER** — **[DEFENDER] BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses principled theoretical distinctions with ad hoc evasion. Every substantive philosophical theory draws distinctions that rule out apparent counterexamples—that is what theories do. The question is whether the distinctions are motivated independently or invented to save the thesis.

The distinction between privileged causal access and first-person authority is not ad hoc. It is independently motivated by the structure of epistemic authority itself. Authority is a normative status within a practice of giving and asking for reasons. A being that cannot make claims, cannot be held responsible for claims, cannot participate in justification—such a being cannot have *authority* in the relevant sense, even if it has privileged causal relations to its states.

This is not circular redefinition. It is recognizing that "first-person authority" in philosophical discussion has always been about a *normative epistemic status*—the right to make authoritative claims that others should accept. Prelinguistic beings do not have this status because they cannot make claims. This is not ruling them out to save the thesis; it is correctly identifying what phenomenon we are theorizing about.

The thesis makes falsifiable predictions that distinguish it from mere stipulation:

**Prediction 1:** Correction of first-person claims should systematically take semantic/interpretive form rather than observational correction form. We should say "that's not what you mean" or "you mean X not Y," not "you're observationally mistaken about an internal state."

**Prediction 2:** First-person authority should be absent or diminished in cases of semantic incompetence while preserved in cases of introspective impairment. A semantically competent alexithymic (poor introspection) should retain authority; a semantically impaired subject should lose it.

**Prediction 3:** The structure of confabulation should show authority over meaning-determination (what justification they express) while showing error about psychological causation. This is exactly what we find.

**Prediction 4:** Cross-linguistic and developmental evidence should show first-person authority emerging with semantic competence in psychological vocabulary, not with introspective capacity.

These predictions are testable and could be false. If we found that correction systematically takes observational form ("look deeper, you'll see you're not angry"), or that introspective impairment destroys authority while semantic impairment preserves it, the thesis would be falsified.

The Case 3 response is not evasion—it is the core theoretical claim. If someone means frustration when they say "I'm angry," they *are* self-ascribing frustration (perhaps using the wrong word). The thesis predicts we would correct them semantically: "you mean frustration, not anger." If instead we found ourselves saying "no, you mean anger but you're wrong about being angry"—treating meaning and psychological state as coming apart—that would falsify the thesis.

The objection's cases are not counterexamples being evaded; they are test cases that either confirm the predictions (Cases 1-3) or describe incoherent scenarios (Case 4: what would "psychological authority without knowing what your words mean" even look like?).

A theory that makes distinctions and predictions is falsifiable. The objection mistakes theoretical precision for circular protection.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, but not because the thesis is unfalsifiable—because it is false in a way the defence inadvertently reveals.

The defence is right that the thesis makes testable predictions. Prediction 1 (correction takes semantic form) and Prediction 3 (confabulation structure) are genuinely testable. The thesis is not unfalsifiable in principle.

But the defence fails by making Prediction 2, which reveals the thesis is actually falsified by existing evidence.

Prediction 2 states: "First-person authority should be absent or diminished in cases of semantic incompetence while preserved in cases of introspective impairment."

This is false. Consider:

**Semantic incompetence with preserved authority:** Aphasic patients with severe semantic deficits (cannot define words, fail semantic tasks) retain first-person authority about pain, emotion, and desire. We trust their utterances about these states despite their semantic impairment. A Broca's aphasic saying "me... hurt... bad" has full authority about their pain, despite profound semantic incompetence.

**Introspective impairment with destroyed authority:** Patients with certain right hemisphere damage lose first-person authority about their emotional states while retaining semantic competence. They can define "anger" perfectly but we do not trust their self-ascriptions of anger. Anosognosic patients are semantically competent but lack authority about their deficits.

The pattern goes opposite to Prediction 2. Authority tracks psychological access, not semantic competence.

The defence also fails on Prediction 4. Developmental evidence shows first-person authority about pain and basic emotions emerges *before* semantic competence with psychological vocabulary. Toddlers have authority about "hurt" before they can define it or use it in semantically complex ways.

The objection is wrong that the thesis is unfalsifiable. But in defending against that charge, the defence made falsifiable predictions—and those predictions are false. The thesis is falsified, not unfalsifiable.

The objection should have pressed harder on Cases 3 and 4, where the defence's responses actually expose the theory's failure rather than protecting it.

</details>

---

### O10. First-person authority and confabulation coexist because they govern different dimensions: we have constitutive authority over the normative status of our states (whether they're beliefs, desires, intentions) but not over their causal-functional roles.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption that mental states have a unified nature such that having authority over them is an all-or-nothing matter.
- **Sketch:** Current debate assumes mental states have a single nature we either do or don't have privileged access to. But mental states have both a normative dimension (the commitments and entitlements they confer) and a causal-functional dimension (how they actually drive behavior). I have first-person authority over the former—when I avow a belief, I undertake certain commitments and that's not a discovery. But confabulation shows I lack authority over the latter—the actual causal role my states play. This explains both phenomena: first-person authority is preserved for what Moran calls 'deliberative' questions (what am I committed to?) while confabulation affects 'theoretical' questions (what's actually causing my behavior?). The key premise is that these dimensions are genuinely distinct and can come apart, which empirical work on implicit bias and akrasia supports.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Competent distinction between normative and causal dimensions, but the two-dimensional solution to authority/confabulation tension has been explored in various forms.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claims mental states are Janus-faced with two aspects, but cannot maintain both that states are individuated substantively and that we have constitutive authority over them despite lacking authority over their functional profiles
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. The defence successfully specified independent theoretical grounds for the dimensions through inferential role semantics versus cognitive psychology, making the thesis empirically testable
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claims to make explicit what was implicit in Moran-Bilgrami, but this is articulation not innovation—the thesis repackages existing distinctions without generating new predictions or handling cases the precedents cannot
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's appeal to conditions for successful constitution creates a fatal dilemma: either avowals constitute status regardless of other facts (making self-deception impossible) or constitution depends on independent facts (eliminating constitutive authority)
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot maintain constitutive authority over normative dimensions while allowing genuine self-deception, and the position reduces to Moran-Bilgrami's already-established framework without novel contribution

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis equivocates on "mental states" and cannot work on a single reading.

On one reading, mental states are individuated by their normative dimension—what commitments they confer. On this reading, the thesis is *plausible*: I do have authority over my commitments, and confabulation concerns something else (behavioral causes). But it's *uninteresting*: it merely stipulates that "belief" means "commitment I avow," making first-person authority trivial and leaving confabulation about entirely different entities (sub-personal mechanisms, not beliefs at all). The debate dissolves through redefinition.

On the other reading, mental states are individuated functionally—by causal role in producing behavior. On this reading, the thesis is *interesting*: it addresses the real puzzle about beliefs and desires as traditionally understood (states that both justify and cause). But it's *implausible*: if my "belief" is individuated by its functional role, then discovering I lack authority over that role means I lack authority over whether I have that belief. You cannot have "constitutive authority" over the normative status of a functionally-individuated state while lacking authority over its functional profile—that's incoherent.

The argument needs the functional reading to be interesting (to address the traditional debate) but needs the normative reading to be plausible (to preserve authority). It cannot run on either alone.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem requiring substantial theoretical work to answer.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection assumes mental states must be individuated by a single dimension, but that's precisely what the thesis denies. Mental states are *Janus-faced*: they have both normative and functional aspects, neither of which exhausts their identity. This isn't equivocation—it's recognizing that belief (for instance) is constitutively a commitment-conferring state that also plays causal roles, and these two aspects can dissociate.

Consider implicit bias cases: I sincerely avow that all races are equal (normative dimension—I undertake commitments, accept entitlements to inference, open myself to challenges). Yet my behavior manifests racial bias (functional dimension—certain representations causally influence my actions). The thesis says: there's a *single state* here with two dimensions that have come apart. I have authority over the normative dimension (my avowal constitutes my commitment) but not the functional (I'm discovering, not stipulating, what drives my behavior).

The objection's dilemma fails because:

**Horn 1 (normative individuation)**: The thesis doesn't stipulate that "belief" means only "commitment I avow." It says beliefs *have* a normative dimension over which we have authority. Confabulation concerns the *same states*—my beliefs and desires—but their functional dimension. When I confabulate about why I chose these socks, I'm wrong about the causal role of my *actual* preference-state, not talking about something else entirely.

**Horn 2 (functional individuation)**: The thesis doesn't claim authority over whether a functionally-individuated state exists. It claims authority over the *normative status* that state has. Even if my belief is individuated partly by functional role, I still have authority over what commitments it confers, what inferences I'm entitled to draw, whether I endorse it. The functional profile might determine *that* I have a representation with certain causal powers; my avowal determines *what normative position I occupy* regarding it.

The literature supports this: Burge distinguishes critical reasoning (normative) from sub-personal processing (functional). Bilgrami argues that belief content is fixed by rational role, not causal role. The thesis extends this: the dimensions aren't two ways of individuating the same thing, but two genuine aspects any mental state possesses.

**VERDICT**

The defence ultimately fails, though it takes the objection seriously.

The problem is that "having authority over the normative status" of a functionally-individuated state collapses into mere authority over *how I label or endorse* that state, not authority over the state itself. If what makes something a belief that P is (even partly) its functional role, and I lack authority over that functional role, then I lack authority over whether it's genuinely a belief that P—I have only authority over whether I *call* it that or *endorse* it as mine.

The implicit bias case actually undermines the defence: if the functional and normative dimensions genuinely dissociate, we face pressure to say there are *two different states*—an avowed commitment (belief₁) and an implicit representation (belief₂)—not one state with two aspects. The thesis needs them to be aspects of a *single* state to be interesting, but the more they can come apart, the more they look like distinct states.

The defence cannot maintain both that (1) mental states are individuated in a way that makes the debate substantive (not just about labels), and (2) we have constitutive authority over something about those states despite lacking authority over their functional profiles. The equivocation charge stands.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample gets reclassified using the normative/functional distinction.

Suppose I sincerely avow "I believe God exists" but all my behavior—my planning, my fear responses, my decisions—systematically proceeds as if God doesn't exist. Does this refute the claim that I have authority over my belief's normative status?

The thesis can respond either way: If we say "you don't really believe," that's judging by functional dimension (no authority—confabulation case). If we say "you do believe, but it's functionally inert," that preserves authority over normative dimension. The thesis is protected.

Or suppose I avow "I don't desire revenge" but confabulation research reveals this is false—revenge-desire actually drives my behavior. Does this show I lack normative authority? No: the thesis reclassifies this as me being wrong about the *functional* dimension while retaining authority over what commitments I undertake.

The problem: **no possible case can show I lack authority over the normative dimension**, because the normative dimension is defined as precisely that aspect over which I have authority. If my avowal seems authoritative, that's the normative dimension operating. If it seems non-authoritative, that's reclassified as the functional dimension.

The distinction doesn't identify independently specifiable dimensions that might come apart in testable ways. Instead, "normative dimension" means "whatever aspect of mental states first-person authority actually governs" and "functional dimension" means "whatever aspect confabulation reveals." This makes the thesis true by definition—it simply relabels the phenomena rather than explaining their coexistence.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem. The thesis needs independent criteria for what counts as the "normative dimension" beyond "whatever I have authority over," otherwise it's just restating the explanandum as the explanans.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misunderstands how the normative/functional distinction works. These dimensions have independent, theoretically grounded specifications that make the thesis empirically testable.

**The normative dimension** is specified independently by the commitments and entitlements a state confers in the space of reasons. This comes from inferential role semantics and normative pragmatics: a belief that P commits one to believing Q if P entails Q, entitles one to infer R if P supports R, makes one answerable to challenges about P. These are *objective normative relations*, not whatever I say they are. When I avow a belief, I'm taking a position in this independently-structured normative space.

**The functional dimension** is specified independently by causal role in producing behavior, characterized in terms of inputs, outputs, and interactions with other mechanisms. This is the domain of cognitive psychology and neuroscience: what perceptual inputs trigger the state, what behavioral outputs it produces, what processing it undergoes.

These specifications make the thesis falsifiable in multiple ways:

**Falsification 1**: If we found that avowals systematically fail to track normative commitments—if people who avow "I believe P" regularly reject inferences they're normatively committed to by P, or don't treat themselves as answerable to evidence about P—that would show first-person authority doesn't govern the normative dimension after all.

**Falsification 2**: If we found that confabulation occurs about normative relations themselves—if people are systematically wrong about what commitments their states confer, what inferences they're entitled to—that would show confabulation isn't confined to the functional dimension.

**Falsification 3**: If the dimensions couldn't come apart—if every functionally-individuated state had exactly one normative profile and vice versa—the distinction would collapse.

The God-belief case isn't a problem; it's a prediction. The thesis predicts: when you avow "I believe God exists," you thereby undertake certain commitments (to infer "Someone created the universe," to accept "Prayer makes sense"), even if the state doesn't functionally drive your behavior. We can test this: Do you accept those inferences when explicitly asked? Do you treat yourself as answerable to evidence about God? If yes, you have the normative status of a believer despite functional dissociation. If no, your avowal failed to constitute the commitment—falsifying the authority claim.

Similarly, the revenge case: if you avow "I don't desire revenge," you're claiming not to have the normative status of revenge-desirer (not committed to pursuing revenge, not treating revenge as a reason for action). We test this by asking whether you accept "I should pursue revenge" as a valid inference from your motivational states. If you reject it, your avowal successfully constituted your normative position, even though functionally a revenge-representation drives behavior. If you accept it, you've contradicted yourself—showing the avowal didn't have constitutive authority.

The objection's charge of circularity fails because the dimensions aren't defined as "whatever authority governs" vs. "whatever it doesn't." They're defined by independent theoretical roles: normative relations in the space of reasons vs. causal relations in behavioral production. The thesis then makes the empirical claim that first-person authority tracks the former but not the latter—a claim that could be false.

**VERDICT**

The defence succeeds, though it requires careful articulation.

The key move—specifying normative and functional dimensions independently through different theoretical frameworks (inferential role semantics vs. cognitive psychology)—genuinely answers the unfalsifiability charge. The thesis becomes: "First-person authority tracks inferential-role-theoretic commitments but not causal-psychological mechanisms." This is testable.

The proposed falsifications are real. If avowals didn't correlate with acceptance of normative commitments, or if confabulation extended to normative relations themselves, the thesis would fail. The God-belief and revenge cases don't show unfalsifiability—they show the thesis making specific, testable predictions about what subjects will accept or reject when explicitly asked about commitments and inferences.

The objection would only work if "normative dimension" were defined ostensively as "whatever first-person reports track" rather than theoretically as "commitment-conferring inferential role." But the defence shows it's the latter. The thesis inherits its empirical content from the independent theoretical specification of these dimensions in the literature.

The unfalsifiability charge fails. The thesis is falsifiable and substantive.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**

This position is essentially **Moran's distinction between deliberative and theoretical self-knowledge** (2001, *Authority and Estrangement*), combined with **Bilgrami's normative conception of belief content** (2006, *Self-Knowledge and Resentment*).

Moran explicitly argues that first-person authority operates in the "deliberative" stance—where I determine what to believe by considering reasons, thereby constituting my normative commitments—while confabulation affects the "theoretical" stance—where I observe what causally drives my behavior. The thesis even cites Moran directly in the sketch. The candidate's "normative dimension" vs. "causal-functional dimension" is just Moran's deliberative/theoretical distinction repackaged.

Bilgrami independently develops the view that belief content is fixed by rational role (commitments and entitlements in reasoning) rather than causal role, and that self-knowledge involves authority over rational role but not causal-psychological mechanisms. His work on self-knowledge explicitly addresses how we can have authority over our beliefs qua rational commitments while being subject to confabulation about their causal origins.

The weak point is **the claim that this is a novel distinction solving a puzzle**. The thesis presents as if current debate "assumes mental states have a unified nature," but Moran and Bilgrami have already established this two-dimensional framework in the literature. The candidate hasn't cited alternative mechanisms, stronger versions, or extensions—just restated the Moran-Bilgrami position without acknowledgment that it's already a recognized solution in the debate over first-person authority.

This isn't developing the position further or defending it against objections—it's presenting an established view as if discovering it fresh.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This substantially undermines the thesis's contribution. If the position is already well-articulated in the literature by major figures, the thesis isn't making a novel move but rather restating known territory. The candidate would need to show either how their version differs from or improves upon Moran-Bilgrami, which they haven't done.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection conflates *citing precedents* with *being identical to them*. Yes, the thesis draws on Moran and Bilgrami—that's acknowledged in the sketch. But it makes a distinct contribution they don't: **a unified metaphysical account of mental states as two-dimensional entities that explains both phenomena simultaneously**.

Moran's deliberative/theoretical distinction concerns *stances* or *methods* of self-knowledge, not dimensions of mental states themselves. He argues we access our beliefs differently when deliberating (asking "What should I believe?") versus theorizing (asking "What do I believe?"). But he doesn't commit to mental states having separable normative and functional aspects—he could be describing two ways of knowing a unified state. His focus is epistemological (how we know) not metaphysical (what states are).

Bilgrami argues belief content is fixed by rational role, but he's making a claim about *individuation*—what makes a belief the belief it is. He's not explicitly theorizing a two-dimensional metaphysics where a single state has both normative and causal-functional aspects that can dissociate. He could be read as saying beliefs just *are* rational-role entities, period—making the functional story about something other than beliefs proper.

The thesis goes beyond both by claiming:

1. **Metaphysical two-dimensionality**: Mental states are constitutively both normative (commitment-conferring) and functional (causally efficacious). This isn't two stances on one thing or a claim about individuation—it's a claim about the nature of mental states themselves.

2. **Systematic dissociation**: These dimensions can and do come apart empirically (implicit bias, akrasia), which neither Moran nor Bilgrami emphasizes as the key to explaining confabulation. The thesis makes this dissociation central: confabulation happens *because* functional and normative dimensions are distinct and we have authority over only one.

3. **Solution to a specific puzzle**: The thesis explicitly targets the apparent *tension* between first-person authority and confabulation research. Moran addresses authority; Bilgrami addresses content. Neither frames their project as resolving this specific empirical puzzle about why both phenomena coexist.

Moreover, the thesis synthesizes these insights with empirical work on implicit bias and dual-process theory in a way neither Moran nor Bilgrami does. It's not just philosophy of mind—it's bringing that framework to bear on cognitive science findings.

The "already taken" charge would only succeed if Moran or Bilgrami had explicitly offered a two-dimensional metaphysics of mental states as the solution to the authority/confabulation puzzle. They haven't. The thesis builds on their insights but makes a distinct metaphysical and explanatory move.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, though it identifies real differences in emphasis.

The problem is that the differences cited don't amount to a genuinely novel position—they're developments that naturally fall out of the Moran-Bilgrami framework once you combine them, which is precisely what the thesis does.

**On metaphysics vs. epistemology**: While Moran frames things epistemologically, his deliberative/theoretical distinction *requires* something like two-dimensional states to make sense. If there's a deliberative stance that constitutes commitments and a theoretical stance that discovers causes, there must be *something* (the mental state) that has both commitment-constituting and causal aspects. Moran may not explicitly thematize this metaphysics, but it's implicit in his view. The thesis makes it explicit, but that's articulation, not innovation.

**On Bilgrami's individuation claim**: If belief content is fixed by rational role (Bilgrami) but beliefs also have causal-functional profiles that we lack authority over, then Bilgrami is *already committed* to something like two-dimensional states—even if he doesn't use that terminology. The thesis's "metaphysical two-dimensionality" is just making explicit what's already there when you take Bilgrami's rational-role account and ask "But what about causal role?"

**On the synthesis**: Combining Moran and Bilgrami to address the authority/confabulation puzzle is a reasonable scholarly move, but it's not the "novel distinction" the thesis presents itself as making. It's applying existing distinctions to frame a problem. The empirical work on implicit bias illustrates the dissociation, but Moran and Bilgrami already had the theoretical resources to handle such cases—they just didn't emphasize them.

The fatal issue is that the thesis's **move_type is "distinction"** and its **target is "the shared assumption that mental states have a unified nature."** But Moran and Bilgrami already reject that assumption in the ways that matter. Moran distinguishes deliberative from theoretical dimensions; Bilgrami distinguishes rational from causal role. The thesis hasn't identified a *new* distinction—it's repackaged existing ones with different labels ("normative dimension" vs. "causal-functional dimension" instead of "deliberative/theoretical" or "rational/causal role").

For the thesis to survive, it would need to show how its two-dimensional metaphysics generates predictions or handles cases that Moran-Bilgrami cannot. But the defence doesn't provide this—it just clarifies that the thesis makes explicit what was implicit. That's useful exegetical work, but it's not the novel philosophical move the thesis claims to be making.

The "already taken" objection stands.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**

The argument proves too much because if it worked, it would show that *all* apparent cases of self-ignorance or error about our mental states are actually just confusion about causal-functional dimensions, thereby eliminating the very possibility of being wrong about what we believe, desire, or intend in the normative sense.

The weak point is **the premise that we have constitutive authority over the normative dimension**.

Consider: I sincerely avow "I believe in free speech" and accept all the normative commitments (I endorse inferences like "censorship is wrong," I treat myself as answerable to arguments about speech rights). By the thesis, this avowal constitutes my normative status as a free-speech-believer—I have authority over this dimension. But suppose I'm deeply self-deceived: I actually believe in censorship but can't admit it to myself. 

The thesis must say: No, you DO believe in free speech (normatively) because you've undertaken those commitments. Your censorious behavior reflects only the functional dimension. But this proves too much—it makes self-deception about beliefs impossible in principle. Any case where I'm wrong about my beliefs gets reclassified as: "You're right about your belief (normative dimension) but wrong about what's causing your behavior (functional dimension)."

This extends to all mental states: I can never be wrong about what I intend, desire, or believe in the normative sense, because my sincere avowal *constitutes* that normative status. The thesis would eliminate akrasia, self-deception, and unconscious motivation as traditionally understood—they'd all become mere functional-dimension phenomena while normative-dimension self-knowledge remains infallible.

But this is absurd. We clearly can be wrong about our beliefs, desires, and intentions even when considering what commitments we undertake, what we endorse, what reasons we accept. Psychoanalysis, moral philosophy, and ordinary life all recognize genuine error about our mental states that isn't just confusion about behavioral causes.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem requiring substantial theoretical work to answer. The thesis needs to explain how we can have constitutive authority over normative dimensions while still allowing for genuine self-ignorance and error about our mental states as traditionally conceived.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The objection misunderstands what "constitutive authority" means. It doesn't mean *infallibility* about whether I genuinely hold a normative status—it means that *when the authority operates successfully*, the avowal constitutes the status. But the authority can fail to operate, and there are independent criteria for when it does.

Constitutive authority is like performative speech acts: when I say "I promise to meet you," I thereby make a promise—the saying constitutes the doing. But this authority can misfire. If I'm insincere, coerced, or lack understanding of what promising involves, no promise is made. Similarly, avowal can fail to constitute normative status when conditions aren't met.

**The self-deception case**: When you avow "I believe in free speech" while self-deceived, your avowal *attempts* to constitute that normative status but fails because you don't actually undertake the commitments. The test is behavioral in the normative dimension: Do you *actually* accept the inferences? When challenged with "Should this speaker be censored?" do you endorse "No, free speech matters" or do you find reasons to make exceptions? If you systematically reject the commitments you claim to undertake, your avowal hasn't successfully constituted the normative status.

This is different from functional-dimension error. The self-deceived person:
- **Normative dimension**: Claims to accept commitment C but actually rejects it when pressed (self-deception about normative status)
- **Functional dimension**: Has representation R that causes behavior B (might be right or wrong about this)

The thesis doesn't make normative self-knowledge infallible. It says: *sincere avowal under appropriate conditions* constitutes normative status. But sincerity isn't transparent, and conditions can fail. We can be wrong about:
1. Whether we're being sincere
2. Whether we actually accept the commitments we claim to accept
3. Whether we understand what the commitments involve

**Independent criteria for successful constitution**: The normative dimension has its own objectivity—the space of reasons has structure independent of what I claim. If I avow "I believe P" but reject every inference from P, refuse to treat evidence about P as relevant to me, and don't accept challenges about P, then by objective normative criteria, I haven't undertaken the commitment. My avowal failed.

This preserves traditional self-deception and akrasia:
- **Self-deception**: I avow belief B but don't actually accept its normative commitments (failure in normative dimension)
- **Akrasia**: I accept normative commitment C but functionally act against it (dissociation between dimensions)
- **Unconscious motivation**: I deny desire D but both accept its normative force ("I should pursue X") and am functionally driven by it (wrong about having the desire at all)

The thesis doesn't eliminate these—it provides a framework for distinguishing them. Not every avowal successfully exercises constitutive authority. When it fails, we can be genuinely wrong about our normative status.

The analogy: I have authority to make promises by saying "I promise," but this doesn't make me infallible about whether I've promised. I can be wrong about whether my utterance succeeded (was I sincere? did I understand? was I coerced?). Similarly, I have authority to undertake commitments by avowing beliefs, but I can be wrong about whether I've succeeded.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, though it identifies important nuances.

The problem is that introducing "conditions for successful constitution" that can fail undermines the very authority the thesis claims to preserve. If whether my avowal successfully constitutes normative status depends on independent facts (whether I "really" accept the commitments, whether I'm "genuinely" sincere), then I don't have constitutive authority after all—I have at best *presumptive* authority that can be defeated by discovering these independent facts.

**The fatal dilemma**: Either:

1. **Strong authority**: My sincere avowal *does* constitute my normative status, regardless of other facts. But then the "proves too much" objection succeeds—I can't be wrong about my beliefs in the normative sense, making self-deception impossible.

2. **Weak authority**: My avowal constitutes normative status only when certain conditions obtain (genuine sincerity, actual acceptance of commitments, proper understanding). But then whether I have the normative status depends on these independent facts, not on my avowal. I'm not constituting anything—I'm at best *reporting* or *expressing* something that has independent existence. This isn't constitutive authority; it's just fallible self-knowledge.

The performative analogy actually undermines the defence. When my promise misfires due to insincerity, there's an independent fact (my mental state of insincerity) that defeats the performative. But what's the independent fact that determines whether I "really" accept normative commitments? It can't be just my behavior (that's the functional dimension). It can't be just my further avowals (infinite regress). The defence needs some third thing—but what?

The self-deception case exposes this: The defence says "test whether you actually accept the commitments by asking whether you endorse the inferences." But if I'm self-deceived, I'll *say* I endorse them (that's part of the self-deception). So we need to look at... what? Whether I "really" endorse them? But that's just restating the problem. We're back to needing independent access to my normative status beyond my avowal—which contradicts constitutive authority.

The thesis cannot maintain both:
- That we have constitutive authority over normative dimension (avowals make it so)
- That we can be genuinely wrong about our normative status (self-deception is possible)

The "proves too much" objection stands. The thesis must either accept that self-deception about beliefs/desires/intentions is impossible (absurd) or abandon the claim of constitutive authority over the normative dimension (gutting the thesis). The defence's attempt to thread the needle with "conditions for successful constitution" just relocates the problem without solving it.

</details>

---

### O11. Accepting that we interpret ourselves commits interpretivists to the view that self-deception is impossible, since interpretation can only construct coherent agents.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Interpretivists like Carruthers and Gopnik who adopt a rationality-constrained interpretive framework while also accepting the reality of irrational mental states.
- **Sketch:** If I know my mind by interpreting my behavior and context through a lens that seeks coherence and rationality (as Dennett and Davidson's interpretivist frameworks require), then I cannot interpret myself as self-deceived—interpretation constructs me as (mostly) coherent. But self-deception is real and involves precisely holding contradictory attitudes toward the same proposition. Interpretivists face a dilemma: either interpretation can attribute irrationality (in which case it's not distinctively interpretivist but empirical-detective work with no coherence constraints), or it cannot (in which case it cannot capture self-deception, and so fails as an account of self-knowledge). The key premise is that interpretation is constitutively constrained by rationality principles, which is what distinguishes it from mere causal hypothesis-testing.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The self-deception objection to interpretivism is worth making but not entirely novel; the dilemma is clear but may have available responses.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence offered an independent criterion but the judge found it still permits motivated reclassification in practice, as any interpretivist handling self-deception can be dismissed as 'not properly interpretivist.'
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claimed dialectical novelty (treating Davidson's solution as failure rather than success) but the judge found this commitment was already explicit in canonical texts and the critical move itself appears in existing literature.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence argued self-deception is structurally unique, but the judge found the distinctions question-begging and that the same interpretive moves available for other irrationalities apply equally to self-deception.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Moran's stance-asymmetry account was offered; the defence dismissed it as disguised partitioning, but the judge found this criterion impossibly narrow and reverse-engineered to ensure interpretivism fails.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Interpretivists who maintain strong constitutive rationality constraints (like early Davidson) face significant theoretical pressure when accounting for self-deception, requiring either architectural moves (partitioning) or methodological distinctions (stance-asymmetry) that complicate the unified agent picture—though whether these responses succeed or fail remains contested in the literature.
- **Flaw:** The thesis survives only by stipulating standards for 'genuine interpretation' that are reverse-engineered to exclude any interpretivist account that handles self-deception, making the position unfalsifiable in practice despite theoretical falsifiability criteria.

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis protects itself by controlling what counts as "interpretation" and "coherence." When confronted with actual interpretivist accounts that accommodate self-deception (say, Davidson's own work on akrasia and irrationality, or Moran's account of self-knowledge that distinguishes deliberative from attributional stances), the thesis can respond: "That's not *really* interpretation in the constitutive sense I mean" or "That's mixing interpretation with empirical detection" or "Coherence doesn't mean *that* kind of coherence."

The weak point is the premise that **"interpretation is constitutively constrained by rationality principles"** in a sense strong enough to exclude self-deception. Every apparent counterexample—every interpretivist who claims to handle irrationality—gets reclassified as either (a) not properly interpretivist after all ("they've abandoned the constitutive constraint"), or (b) using a weaker sense of coherence that doesn't count ("that's just local rationality, not the global coherence interpretation requires"). The thesis becomes unfalsifiable because no actual interpretivist position can count against it: those who exclude self-deception confirm it, while those who include self-deception get redefined as non-interpretivists or as using "coherence" differently.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs clear, non-question-begging criteria for what counts as "interpretation" and "coherence constraints" that are established independently of the self-deception conclusion.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The thesis is not unfalsifiable because it provides a **precise, independently specifiable criterion** for what counts as genuinely interpretivist: the constitutive application of rationality constraints as a precondition for contentful mental state attribution. This is not gerrymandered to exclude self-deception—it comes straight from the canonical interpretivist texts.

Look at Davidson's principle of charity and Dennett's intentional stance: both explicitly make rationality *constitutive* of content attribution. Davidson says we cannot make sense of massive error or irrationality because "if we are intelligibly to attribute attitudes and beliefs, or usefully to describe motions as behavior, then we are committed to finding, in the pattern of behavior, belief, and desire, a large degree of rationality and consistency" (1970). Dennett says the intentional stance works by assuming rationality—that's what distinguishes it from the physical or design stance.

Now, when Davidson discusses akrasia or irrationality, he **explicitly treats these as limit cases that strain interpretation**—not as straightforward attributions. His account of akrasia requires partitioning the mind into semi-autonomous regions precisely because unified interpretation cannot accommodate the contradiction. This is not "abandoning" interpretivism—it's showing that interpretivism's constitutive constraints force architectural moves when faced with irrationality.

The criterion is clear and non-circular: **Does the account make rationality a precondition for content attribution, or merely an empirical generalization about minds?** 

- If rationality is constitutive (Davidson, Dennett), you face the self-deception problem
- If rationality is merely typical but not constitutive (standard empirical psychology), you're not doing interpretation in the relevant sense—you're doing causal-functional psychology that happens to use mentalistic vocabulary

The Gopnik/Carruthers "theory-theory" accounts **fail the test independently**: they treat self-knowledge as empirical hypothesis-formation about inner states that exist independently of interpretation. That's not interpretivism—it's inner-sense empiricism with a theoretical overlay. They're "interpretivist" only in the trivial sense that we theorize about minds, not in the constitutive sense the thesis targets.

The thesis can be falsified: show an account that (1) makes rationality constitutively necessary for content attribution, AND (2) attributes self-deception without partitioning/fragmenting the agent or weakening "rationality" to mere local consistency. That would be a genuine counterexample.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails.**

The problem is that the criterion, while not circular, **still allows motivated reclassification** in practice. Here's why:

The distinction between "rationality as constitutive" versus "rationality as empirical generalization" is not sharp enough to do the work required. Consider Moran's account: he distinguishes the deliberative stance (where I answer "Do I believe P?" by answering "Is P true?") from the attributional stance (where I treat myself as an object of interpretation). When I discover self-deception through the attributional stance, am I:

(a) Applying rationality constraints constitutively (just from a different angle), or  
(b) Doing empirical detection that's not really interpretation?

The thesis must say (b) to protect itself, but this is precisely the motivated reclassification the objection identifies. Moran explicitly presents this as an interpretivist account—he's working within the Davidson/Bilgrami tradition. Yet the thesis can dismiss it as "not properly interpretivist" only by **presupposing** that genuine interpretation cannot accommodate self-deception.

Similarly with Davidson's partitioning move: the thesis can say "See, even Davidson has to fragment the agent," but Davidson himself presents this as **interpretation successfully handling irrationality**, not as interpretation failing. The thesis responds: "That's not unified interpretation anymore"—but now we're just stipulating that "interpretation" means "unified interpretation that cannot handle self-deception," which is question-begging.

The deeper problem: **any interpretivist who claims to handle self-deception will be doing something** (partitioning, stance-switching, weakening coherence demands, etc.). The thesis can always point to that something and say "Aha! You've abandoned pure interpretation." But this makes the thesis unfalsifiable-in-practice even if not unfalsifiable-in-principle.

The objection stands.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims to reveal a novel commitment problem for interpretivists, but this is **already a well-established position in the self-knowledge literature**—it's essentially Davidson's own view from "Deception and Division" (1986) and "Paradoxes of Irrationality" (1982).

Davidson explicitly argues that interpretation, constrained by principles of charity and rationality, cannot straightforwardly attribute self-deception to a unified agent. His solution—partitioning the mind into semi-autonomous regions that can hold contradictory attitudes—is precisely the response to recognizing that rational interpretation cannot accommodate self-deception within a single coherent agent. This isn't a bug the thesis discovers; it's a feature Davidson built into the account.

Similarly, Richard Moran in *Authority and Estrangement* (2001) extensively discusses how the deliberative, reason-responsive stance constitutive of self-knowledge creates exactly this problem: if I know my beliefs through rational deliberation about reasons, I cannot simultaneously recognize myself as self-deceived (which requires seeing myself as believing against my evidence). His entire framework is built around this tension.

The weak point is **the claim to novelty**: the thesis presents as a *reductio* what Davidson and Moran present as a *theoretical result* requiring architectural or methodological response. The "dilemma" isn't a discovery about interpretivism's commitments—it's the canonical interpretivist position on self-deception, acknowledged and addressed in the founding texts. The thesis adds no new argumentative pressure beyond restating what Davidson already concluded.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This seriously undermines the thesis as stated. If the position is already Davidson's explicit view, then the thesis isn't revealing an unnoticed commitment but rather repackaging a known theoretical stance as if it were a problem. The force depends on whether the thesis is claiming discovery versus systematization.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The thesis is not "already taken" by Davidson because **it draws the opposite conclusion from the same observations**. Davidson and the thesis agree on the setup—interpretation's rationality constraints create tension with self-deception—but Davidson treats his partitioning solution as *successful*, while the thesis argues it represents *failure*.

This is not mere repackaging. The thesis makes a **dialectical move Davidson does not**: it presents the partitioning response as a dilemma horn, not a solution. When Davidson fragments the agent into semi-autonomous regions, he thinks he's preserved interpretivism by showing how it can accommodate irrationality through architectural complexity. The thesis says: **that's not accommodation—that's capitulation**.

Here's the argument Davidson doesn't make: If interpretation succeeds only by denying there's a single unified agent who is self-deceived, then interpretation has *failed to capture the phenomenon*. Self-deception is precisely the state where **I** (one person, one agent) simultaneously believe P and believe not-P with respect to the same proposition. Davidson's partitioning "solves" this by saying there's no unified "I" in such cases—there are sub-personal regions. But this is changing the subject: we asked whether interpretation can capture self-deception in agents, and the answer is "only by denying they're unified agents when self-deceived."

The thesis makes this a **genuine dilemma** rather than a theoretical result requiring response:

**Horn 1**: Maintain rationality constraints constitutively → cannot attribute self-deception to unified agents → interpretivism fails as account of self-knowledge (because it cannot recognize a real psychological phenomenon)

**Horn 2**: Weaken rationality constraints enough to attribute self-deception → lose what makes interpretation distinctively interpretivist → collapse into empirical psychology with no constitutive role for rationality

Davidson thinks he escapes by partitioning, but the thesis shows this is just Horn 1 with extra steps: you've admitted interpretation cannot handle the unified self-deceived agent, so you fragment the agent to save interpretation. That's Horn 1—failure to capture the phenomenon as it actually occurs.

Moran is even clearer evidence the thesis is not "already taken": Moran presents the tension as requiring a **distinction between stances** (deliberative vs. attributional), suggesting we can recognize self-deception from the attributional stance. The thesis responds: if the attributional stance can recognize self-deception, it's not doing constitutively interpretivist work—it's doing empirical detection. Moran doesn't draw this conclusion; the thesis does.

The novelty is **turning the acknowledged tension into an inescapable dilemma** rather than a problem with known solutions. Davidson and Moran think they've solved it; the thesis argues their "solutions" are disguised concessions that interpretivism cannot do what it claims.
**VERDICT**
**The defence succeeds partially but ultimately fails.**

The defence is right that there's a **dialectical difference**: Davidson presents partitioning as a solution; the thesis presents it as a failure. That's not nothing—it's a genuine reframing that applies critical pressure.

But this doesn't save the thesis from the "already taken" objection for two reasons:

**First**, the critical move is *also* not novel. The objection that Davidson's partitioning doesn't really solve the self-deception problem—that it changes the subject by fragmenting the agent—is **itself well-established in the literature**. Critics like Alfred Mele and Brian McLaughlin have extensively argued that Davidson's partitioning strategy is ad hoc and doesn't genuinely capture self-deception as a unified phenomenon. The thesis is restating *their* criticism of Davidson, not making a new discovery.

**Second**, and more importantly, the defence **concedes the key point**: it admits Davidson explicitly recognizes that rationality-constrained interpretation creates tension with self-deception. The thesis claimed to reveal an *unnoticed commitment* ("commits interpretivists to the view..."), but if Davidson already sees the tension and offers partitioning as a response, then the commitment was already noticed. 

The thesis can say "Davidson's response fails," but that's a different claim than "interpretivists are committed to something they haven't recognized." The "already taken" objection isn't that the thesis agrees with Davidson—it's that **the problematic commitment the thesis claims to reveal is already explicit in the canonical interpretivist texts**. Davidson knows interpretation struggles with self-deception; he says so directly. The thesis presents this as a discovery.

The defence tries to save novelty by escalating from "problem" to "inescapable dilemma," but this is a difference in **strength of conclusion**, not discovery of new commitments. The underlying commitment—that rationality constraints make self-deception difficult for interpretivism—is already taken, acknowledged, and debated.

The objection stands. The thesis needs to be reframed not as revealing hidden commitments but as arguing that existing interpretivist responses to a known problem are inadequate.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**

The argument's core move—"if interpretation is constrained by rationality principles, it cannot attribute irrational mental states"—proves far too much. If successful, it would show that interpretivism cannot accommodate **any** form of irrationality, not just self-deception.

Consider akrasia (weakness of will), where I judge that all-things-considered I should do A, yet intentionally do B instead. Or consider wishful thinking, where I believe P primarily because I want P to be true, not because of evidence. Or consider plain inconsistent beliefs held over time—I believe "all politicians are corrupt" while also believing "Senator Smith is honest" without recognizing the tension.

The weak point is the premise that **"interpretation is constitutively constrained by rationality principles"** in a way that excludes self-deception specifically. If these rationality constraints are strong enough to make self-deception impossible (because interpretation "can only construct coherent agents"), they would equally make akrasia, wishful thinking, and unnoticed inconsistency impossible. Yet the thesis only claims self-deception becomes impossible.

This is problematic because: (1) Davidson explicitly discusses akrasia using the same interpretivist framework, showing the argument should apply there too, and (2) if we accept that interpretation can somehow accommodate akrasia or wishful thinking (through partitioning, local rationality, or other moves), then those same moves should be available for self-deception—the thesis gives no principled reason why self-deception is uniquely problematic rather than just another instance of the general irrationality problem.

The argument either proves that interpretivism cannot handle **any** irrationality (too much), or it fails to show why self-deception is special.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to explain why self-deception specifically becomes impossible under interpretivist constraints, rather than just being one more form of irrationality that interpretivists must handle somehow.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
Self-deception is **structurally unique** among irrational states in a way that makes it uniquely problematic for interpretivism, even if other irrationalities can be accommodated. The thesis is not committed to denying that interpretation can handle *any* irrationality—only that it cannot handle the *specific structure* of self-deception.

Here's why self-deception is special: it requires **simultaneous contradictory attitudes toward the same proposition at the same time with the same evidence**. Compare:

**Akrasia**: I judge I should do A (all-things-considered) but do B. This involves a gap between judgment and action, or between evaluative and motivational states. Interpretation can accommodate this through standard belief-desire psychology: I believe A is best, but my desire to do B is stronger, or my judgment doesn't fully engage my motivational system. No contradiction in *beliefs* is required—just a failure of rational integration between cognitive and conative states. Davidson's account works precisely because akrasia involves *different types* of mental states in tension.

**Wishful thinking**: I believe P because I want P to be true. This is epistemically irrational but not *logically* contradictory. Interpretation can attribute this by recognizing that my belief-forming mechanisms are being influenced by desires in non-standard ways. I'm irrational about *how* I form beliefs, but I don't hold contradictory beliefs simultaneously. There's one belief (P), formed through a flawed process.

**Unnoticed inconsistency**: I believe "all politicians are corrupt" and "Senator Smith is honest" without recognizing the tension. This is rational *locally* (each belief is formed and maintained through ordinary evidence-responsive processes) even if irrational *globally*. Interpretation handles this through the distinction between explicit and implicit beliefs: I haven't drawn the logical consequences, so the contradiction remains merely potential. Once I notice it, I'll revise—that's what makes me interpretable as rational.

**Self-deception**: I believe P (at some level) and believe not-P (at some level) regarding the same proposition, with access to the same evidence, at the same time, AND this state is maintained through motivated processes. This is the unique combination that breaks interpretation:

1. **Same proposition** (not different types of states like belief vs. desire)
2. **Same time** (not diachronic inconsistency)
3. **Same evidence base** (not local rationality with global inconsistency)
4. **Contradictory attitudes** (not just failure of integration)
5. **Maintained motivationally** (not mere oversight that would be corrected when noticed)

The interpretivist's rationality constraints can handle gaps between judgment and action (akrasia), flawed belief-formation processes (wishful thinking), and unnoticed logical consequences (inconsistency). These all preserve enough rational structure for interpretation to get traction: the agent is responding to reasons, even if imperfectly.

But self-deception requires attributing **simultaneously contradictory propositional attitudes toward the same content**. This is what interpretation constitutively cannot do, because content attribution itself depends on finding rational patterns. When Davidson handles akrasia, he doesn't need to attribute contradictory *beliefs*—just motivational failure. When he handles self-deception, he *must* partition the agent because interpretation cannot attribute "believes P and believes not-P" to a unified rational system.

The thesis doesn't prove too much because **the other irrationalities preserve enough rational structure** for interpretation to work. Self-deception uniquely requires simultaneous contradiction in propositional attitudes, which is what rationality-constrained interpretation cannot attribute.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails.**

The defence makes a valiant effort to distinguish self-deception structurally, but the distinctions don't hold under pressure. The problem is that the defence's own description of self-deception **already contains the escape routes** that interpretivism uses for other irrationalities.

**First**, the defence says self-deception involves believing P "at some level" and believing not-P "at some level." But this phrase "at some level" is doing crucial work—and it's exactly the kind of work that **partitioning** does. If we can say "at some level" for self-deception, we can distinguish levels or regions or aspects of the cognitive system. That's precisely Davidson's move. The defence tries to rule this out by saying self-deception involves "the same time" and "same evidence base," but Davidson's partitioned regions exist at the same time and have access to the same evidence—they're just semi-autonomous in their processing.

**Second**, the claim that self-deception uniquely requires "simultaneously contradictory propositional attitudes toward the same content" is **question-begging against Davidson's account**. Davidson's entire point is that apparent self-deception can be interpreted as involving beliefs in different partitions that don't form a single unified contradictory state. The defence responds: "but that's not *really* self-deception because there's no unified agent." But now we're back to the earlier problem—this is just asserting that self-deception must involve unified contradiction, which is precisely what's at issue.

**Third**, and most damaging, the defence's own examples **undermine the distinction**:

- **Unnoticed inconsistency**: The defence says this is "rational locally even if irrational globally" and interpretation handles it through explicit/implicit distinction. But why can't self-deception get the same treatment? "I explicitly believe P but implicitly believe not-P" uses the same structure. The defence says self-deception is "maintained motivationally" so it won't be corrected when noticed—but that's just adding that it's *stable* unnoticed inconsistency. That's a difference in *degree* (how easily corrected), not *kind* (whether interpretation can attribute it).

- **Wishful thinking**: The defence says this involves "flawed belief-forming processes" but not contradiction. But self-deception paradigmatically involves flawed belief-forming processes too—motivated reasoning, selective attention to evidence, biased memory. Why can't interpretation say: "The agent believes P through a motivated, flawed process" without also attributing belief in not-P? The defence insists self-deception requires *both* beliefs, but this is controversial—some accounts of self-deception (like Mele's) deny that the self-deceived person genuinely believes the unwelcome truth.

**The core problem**: The defence tries to make self-deception uniquely problematic by **stipulating** that it requires simultaneous contradictory beliefs in a unified agent. But this stipulation is precisely what interpretivists like Davidson **deny**. They say: apparent self-deception is either (a) partitioned beliefs (not unified contradiction), or (b) motivated false belief without genuine contradictory belief (like wishful thinking), or (c) unnoticed inconsistency maintained by motivated inattention.

The defence cannot rule out these interpretivist moves without begging the question about what self-deception essentially is.
