And if those moves are available for self-deception, then self-deception is not structurally unique—it's just another form of irrationality that gets the same treatment as akrasia (partitioning) or wishful thinking (motivated but non-contradictory belief) or inconsistency (local rationality with global incoherence).

The "proves too much" objection stands. The argument either shows interpretivism cannot handle any irrationality requiring attribution of contradictory states (too much), or it fails to show why the moves available for other irrationalities aren't equally available for self-deception.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
**COUNTEREXAMPLE: Moran's Asymmetry Account**

Richard Moran's *Authority and Estrangement* (2001) provides a concrete interpretivist framework that attributes self-deception to unified agents without abandoning rationality constraints.

Moran distinguishes two stances toward one's own mind:

1. **Deliberative stance**: I answer "Do I believe P?" by reasoning about whether P is true (transparent, reason-responsive, constitutively rational)
2. **Attributional stance**: I answer "Do I believe P?" by observing my behavior, feelings, and reactions (interpretive but not transparent)

Self-deception occurs when these come apart: From the deliberative stance, I'm committed to not-P (I judge the evidence supports not-P, I avow not-P). But from the attributional stance, I can interpret my own behavior—my selective attention, my emotional reactions, my memory patterns—as manifesting belief that P.

**The crucial point**: Both stances are interpretivist and rationality-constrained. The deliberative stance is transparently rational (I'm reasoning about what to believe). The attributional stance applies rational interpretation to my behavior (I'm making sense of my actions as those of a rational agent who believes P, even though I don't avow P).

This is self-deception in a unified agent: **I** (one person) am simultaneously committed to not-P deliberatively while interpretable as believing P attributionally. No partitioning into sub-agents. No abandoning rationality constraints—both stances employ them, just at different levels (first-order reasoning vs. second-order interpretation of behavior).

**The weak point this attacks**: The thesis's premise that "interpretation can only construct coherent agents." Moran shows interpretation can construct an agent who is coherent *within each stance* but incoherent *across stances*—and that's precisely what self-deception is.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious counterexample requiring real engagement. Moran explicitly works within the Davidson/interpretivist tradition, maintains rationality constraints throughout, attributes self-deception to unified agents, and does so through a sophisticated understanding of how interpretation works at multiple levels. The thesis must either show Moran's account isn't genuinely interpretivist (difficult, given his explicit positioning) or explain why stance-asymmetry is just disguised partitioning (but the stances are methodological, not ontological divisions).

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
Moran's account **is** disguised partitioning, and recognizing this shows it faces exactly the dilemma the thesis identifies. The "methodological vs. ontological" distinction cannot save it.

Here's why: Moran's two stances are not merely different **ways of asking the same question**—they generate **different answers** about what I believe. From the deliberative stance, the answer to "Do I believe P?" is "No" (because I'm committed to not-P through my reasoning). From the attributional stance, the answer is "Yes" (because my behavior manifests belief that P). 

Now ask: **what is the fact of the matter about whether I believe P?**

Moran cannot say "both" without contradiction. He cannot say "neither" because then there's no self-deception. He must say something like: "I believe P in one sense (behaviorally/attributionally) but not in another sense (deliberatively/avowedly)."

But this **is partitioning**—just partitioning belief-types rather than agent-regions. Instead of Davidson's "partition 1 believes P, partition 2 believes not-P," we have "attributional-belief that P, deliberative-commitment to not-P." The ontological/methodological distinction is a red herring: what matters is whether we're attributing **one unified contradictory state** or **two different states that avoid contradiction through type-distinction**.

Moran chooses the latter. His "belief that P" (attributional) and his "commitment to not-P" (deliberative) are **different kinds of mental states**. This is exactly like saying akrasia involves different types of states (judgment vs. motivation)—it avoids contradiction by type-distinction.

The thesis's dilemma applies directly:

**Horn 1 (maintain rationality)**: If interpretation requires coherence, then "believing P attributionally" and "being committed to not-P deliberatively" must be **consistent**—they're different types of states, like belief and desire. No contradiction, no unified self-deception. Moran has captured something (stance-asymmetry), but not self-deception as traditionally understood (contradictory beliefs about the same proposition).

**Horn 2 (capture self-deception)**: If Moran insists these are genuinely contradictory beliefs (both are beliefs about whether P is true), then interpretation has attributed contradiction to a unified agent—but only by **abandoning** the constitutive rationality constraint. The attributional stance would be doing empirical detection ("I observe that I behave as if I believe P") rather than constitutive interpretation.

Moran tries to split the difference: maintain rationality within each stance while allowing incoherence across stances. But this **concedes the thesis's point**—interpretation can construct coherent agents *within its own domain* (each stance is coherent), but cannot capture genuine self-deception as a unified contradictory state. The "across-stance incoherence" is not something interpretation attributes; it's something that emerges from **comparing** two different interpretive perspectives, neither of which attributes contradiction.

The key test: **Can Moran's agent, from the deliberative stance alone, recognize themselves as self-deceived?** No—because the deliberative stance is committed to not-P and doesn't have access to the attributional interpretation. **Can they recognize it from the attributional stance alone?** No—because that stance only observes behavior consistent with believing P, without access to the deliberative commitment to not-P.

Self-deception only appears **from outside both stances**, when we (as theorists) compare them. But that's not the agent interpreting themselves as self-deceived—that's us interpreting the agent as having stance-asymmetry. The thesis wins: interpretivist self-interpretation cannot capture self-deception in the unified agent.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails.**

The defence makes a sophisticated move—reframing Moran's stance-distinction as type-partitioning—but this move **backfires** by revealing that the thesis's criterion for "genuine interpretivism" is impossibly narrow.

Here's the problem: The defence argues that Moran's account is "disguised partitioning" because it distinguishes deliberative-commitment from attributional-belief as different types of states. But this criterion would make **almost any psychological theory** into "partitioning," including theories we don't normally think of as fragmenting the agent.

Consider: Standard belief-desire psychology distinguishes beliefs from desires as different types of states. The defence's logic would say: "If I desire to do A but believe I shouldn't do A, that's not *really* a unified agent with conflicting states—that's partitioning into belief-partition and desire-partition." But that's absurd. Distinguishing types of mental states is not the same as fragmenting the agent.

The defence tries to distinguish self-deception from akrasia by saying akrasia involves "different types of states (judgment vs. motivation)" while self-deception requires "contradictory beliefs." But this **begs the question** against Moran's account. Moran is precisely arguing that self-deception involves different types of states: deliberative-commitments (which are transparent, reason-responsive, avowable) versus attributional-beliefs (which are opaque, behavior-manifesting, observable). These are different *functional kinds* of belief-like states, just as judgment and motivation are different kinds.

The defence responds: "But both are beliefs about whether P is true!" Yet deliberative-commitments and attributional-beliefs have **radically different functional roles**:

- Deliberative-commitments guide reasoning, structure inquiry, are transparent to the agent
- Attributional-beliefs explain behavior, are opaque, are discovered through self-interpretation

Saying these are "the same type" because both concern whether P is true is like saying beliefs and desires are the same type because both can concern whether I'll go to the store. The **functional role** determines type, not just the content.

**More damaging**: The defence's final test reveals the problem. It asks whether the agent "from the deliberative stance alone" can recognize self-deception, answers "no," and treats this as victory. But this **concedes that Moran has explained self-deception**—just not in the way the thesis demands.

Moran's account explains:
- Why self-deception feels different from ordinary false belief (stance-asymmetry)
- How a rational agent can be self-deceived (coherence within each stance)
- How self-deception can be discovered (by taking the attributional stance toward oneself)
- Why self-deception is difficult to maintain once noticed (the stances come into conflict)

The thesis responds: "But the agent can only recognize self-deception by comparing stances, not from within a single stance." **So what?** Why is that a failure rather than an insight about self-deception's structure? The thesis is stipulating that genuine self-deception must be recognizable from a single unified interpretive perspective—but that's precisely what Moran denies, and he gives good reasons for the denial.

The defence's criterion has become: "Interpretation fails unless it can attribute self-deception from a single stance without any type-distinctions." But this is an **arbitrary standard** designed to ensure interpretivism fails. It's not motivated by what interpretation actually is or what self-deception actually involves—it's reverse-engineered from the desired conclusion.

**The counterexample stands.** Moran provides an interpretivist account that:
1. Maintains rationality constraints (each stance is coherent)
2. Attributes self-deception to unified agents (not ontological partitioning)
3. Explains the phenomenon's distinctive features (stance-asymmetry)
4. Works within the canonical interpretivist tradition (explicit engagement with Davidson)

The thesis can reject this only by stipulating that "genuine interpretation" must meet standards Moran

</details>

---

### O12. The confabulation literature shows not that introspection is unreliable but that we systematically mistake memory of recent self-interpretation for direct observation of mental states.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The interpretivists' use of confabulation studies (Carruthers, Gopnik) to argue against any observational component in self-knowledge.
- **Sketch:** Nisbett-Wilson cases are treated as showing introspection is interpretive or unreliable. But there's an unnoticed distinction: between observing a current mental state and remembering that state. What the studies actually show is that we confabulate when asked 'Why did you X?' because we're retrieving not the original mental state but our immediate post-hoc interpretation of it, which we then misattribute to introspection. Genuine online introspection—attention to current phenomenology as it happens—shows different accuracy profiles. The key premise: confabulation studies systematically introduce temporal gaps (asking after the fact) and interpretive demands (asking 'why' not 'what'), which contaminate what gets remembered. This reclassifies positions: we can preserve both interpretive self-reading (when retrieving past states) AND observational introspection (of current phenomenology) without contradiction.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Distinguishing online introspection from memory-based retrieval is empirically interesting and could explain confabulation patterns, though the scope may be limited.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, answered. Defence showed the thesis identifies a real methodological gap in what studies actually test, not mere terminological relabeling
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence appealed to phenomenal intentionality not stated in thesis; as written, thesis trades on narrow methodological reading to gain broad dialectical benefits
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence could not solve verification problem: no way to test observational introspection specifically without circular first-personal reports or collapsing into behavioral measures
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed thesis differs from dual-process theories but this made it weaker—extending beyond their careful restrictions without adequate theoretical foundation
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Confabulation studies systematically introduce temporal gaps and interpretive demands that test memory-of-self-interpretation rather than concurrent phenomenological attention, leaving untested whether observational access to current phenomenal states (sensory and affective experience) is reliable
- **Flaw:** The thesis equivocates between a modest methodological claim and a substantive challenge to interpretivism, requiring but not stating commitments to phenomenal intentionality, while lacking both independent verification criteria and theoretical advances over existing dual-process positions

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims to reveal an "unnoticed distinction" between observing current mental states and remembering past ones, but this is already widely recognized in the introspection literature. The temporal dimension of introspective access has been explicitly discussed since at least Armstrong (1968) and Shoemaker (1996), who distinguished immediate self-knowledge from retrospective self-attribution. Contemporary interpretivists like Carruthers specifically address "concurrent" versus "retrospective" introspection and argue that even seemingly immediate introspection involves interpretive mechanisms operating on sub-personal processes. 

The weak point is the claim that this distinction is "unnoticed." The confabulation literature's force comes precisely from cases where subjects are asked immediately or very shortly after the relevant mental state (Nisbett & Wilson's subjects report on preferences expressed seconds earlier; split-brain patients confabulate about ongoing choices). Interpretivists argue that even this "online" access involves interpretation of behavioral and phenomenal cues, not observation of mental states themselves. The thesis simply relabels as "genuine introspection" what interpretivists already recognize and argue is still interpretive—it's a terminological move that doesn't engage the substantive debate about whether ANY self-knowledge, however temporally immediate, involves observation rather than interpretation of sub-personal states.

**STRENGTH: 2/5**

The thesis does add some empirical texture about temporal gaps and "why" versus "what" questions that may not be fully appreciated, preventing a score of 1.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misreads what "unnoticed" means here and conflates recognition of a temporal dimension with recognition of *this particular explanatory distinction*. Yes, philosophers distinguish concurrent from retrospective introspection—but they treat both as candidates for the same *kind* of mechanism (observational vs. interpretive). The thesis makes a different cut: it claims confabulation studies don't test introspection *at all*—they test memory of self-interpretation.

Here's the key: Carruthers and others argue that even concurrent "introspection" is interpretive. But their evidence comes from studies with temporal gaps. When Nisbett & Wilson ask "Why did you choose this stocking?", subjects aren't introspecting—they're *remembering* having chosen, then interpreting that memory. Even "seconds later" creates a retrieval demand. The thesis predicts exactly this pattern: immediate phenomenological attention (actual introspection) would show different accuracy, but we have no studies that isolate it because researchers always ask *after* the choice, converting introspection tasks into memory-plus-interpretation tasks.

The objection claims split-brain patients "confabulate about ongoing choices"—but they're asked to *explain* a choice already made (the hand has already pointed). That's retrospective interpretation of action, not observation of current phenomenology. Show me a study where subjects report *while attending to current experience* without any interpretive demand ("what does this feel like *now*?" not "why did you...?") and we can test whether introspection proper confabulates.

The thesis isn't terminological relabeling—it's claiming the empirical literature has a systematic gap: it has never actually tested what it claims to test. That's why the distinction went "unnoticed" in the debate's structure, even if the temporal dimension was acknowledged.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence succeeds in showing the objection overstates its case—there *is* something substantive here about what the studies actually measure. But it fails to save the thesis because:

**The "systematic gap" claim is too strong.** There are studies examining real-time phenomenological reports (experience sampling, think-aloud protocols, concurrent verbalization studies). While these have methodological complications, they exist, and interpretivists address them. The thesis would need to show these studies vindicate observational introspection—merely pointing out they're different from Nisbett-Wilson paradigms isn't enough.

**The defence concedes too much about "interpretation of action."** If split-brain patients confabulate about their own just-completed actions, this is still a problem for introspective self-knowledge even if it's not observation-of-phenomenology. The thesis wanted to preserve observational introspection *as a route to self-knowledge*, but the defence retreats to "observation of current phenomenology" without showing this delivers knowledge of mental states (like intentions, reasons, beliefs) that matter for the debate.

The thesis identifies a real methodological issue—confabulation studies may not isolate what they claim to—but doesn't establish that closing this gap would vindicate observational introspection. The defence shows the objection's "trivial" verdict is unfair, but the thesis remains undefended as originally stated.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis equivocates on "introspection" between a narrow reading (phenomenological attention to current experience) and a broad reading (access to one's mental states for self-knowledge), and needs both readings for its argument to work.

The thesis is *plausible* on the narrow reading: perhaps we can accurately observe current phenomenology—what-it's-like right now—and confabulation studies don't test this because they introduce temporal gaps and interpretive demands. Fine. But this narrow reading makes the thesis *uninteresting* for the debate it targets. Carruthers and Gopnik are concerned with introspection as a source of self-knowledge about beliefs, desires, intentions, and reasons—the mental states that matter for folk psychology and rational agency. Mere observation of current phenomenology doesn't deliver this unless phenomenology transparently reveals these states.

The thesis becomes *interesting* only on the broad reading where "introspection" means access to mental states generally. On this reading, it's a substantive challenge to interpretivism: confabulation studies don't undermine introspective self-knowledge because they don't test genuine introspection. But on this broad reading, the thesis is *implausible*—the defence already conceded that observation of phenomenology is different from knowledge of "intentions, reasons, beliefs," and gave no account of how the former yields the latter.

The argument runs: "Studies don't test X (phenomenological attention), therefore they don't undermine Y (introspective self-knowledge)." This only works if X=Y, but X=Y only on an equivocal reading that's narrow enough to escape the studies but broad enough to matter for the debate.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is serious damage. The thesis must either accept irrelevance (narrow reading) or provide an account of how phenomenological observation yields knowledge of propositional attitudes (to sustain the broad reading). Without that bridge, it's caught between two non-overlapping readings.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes what needs proving: that knowledge of beliefs, desires, and intentions *requires* something beyond phenomenological observation. But this begs the question against phenomenal intentionality theories and acquaintance approaches. The thesis doesn't need to "bridge" phenomenology to propositional attitudes because propositional attitudes *have* phenomenology—there's something it's like to believe, intend, or desire, and that phenomenology can be observationally accessed.

Here's the structure: When I introspect my intention to raise my arm, I'm not observing a sub-personal computational state—I'm observing the *phenomenal character* of intending. Interpretivists claim this is really interpretation of behavioral cues or confabulation, and they cite Nisbett-Wilson as evidence. The thesis responds: those studies don't test observation of intentional phenomenology because they introduce retrieval and interpretation demands that contaminate the data.

The objection demands an "account of how phenomenological observation yields knowledge of propositional attitudes"—but that's exactly what observational theories of introspection *provide*. On these views, propositional attitudes are phenomenally constituted or phenomenally presented, so observing the phenomenology *is* knowing the attitude. The thesis doesn't equivocate—it consistently uses "introspection" to mean observational access to phenomenally presented mental states, which includes both experiences and attitudes.

The narrow/broad distinction is a false dichotomy. "Current phenomenology" isn't restricted to sensory qualia—it includes cognitive phenomenology, conative phenomenology, the felt character of thinking and willing. When the thesis says confabulation studies don't test "observation of current phenomenology," it means they don't test observational access to the full range of phenomenally presented mental states, including those that ground self-knowledge of attitudes.

The objection works only if we accept the interpretivist's starting assumption that attitudes are sub-personal states requiring interpretation. But that's the very claim the thesis challenges. It's not equivocation to insist that phenomenology encompasses more than the objector assumes.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails.

The defence makes a philosophically coherent move—appealing to phenomenal intentionality and cognitive phenomenology to unify the two readings. But this reveals that the thesis *depends on* a controversial substantive position (that propositional attitudes are phenomenally constituted or presented) that it nowhere states or defends.

**The thesis as stated doesn't earn this defence.** It presents itself as a methodological observation about what confabulation studies actually test, claiming to reveal an "unnoticed distinction" that reclassifies the dialectic. It says nothing about phenomenal intentionality, cognitive phenomenology, or acquaintance theories. A reader could accept the thesis's distinction between memory-of-interpretation and observation-of-current-states without thereby accepting that intentions and beliefs have distinctive phenomenology available to observation.

**The equivocation charge therefore stands in this form:** The thesis *as written* trades on an ambiguity. When it says studies don't test "genuine online introspection—attention to current phenomenology as it happens," this sounds like a modest methodological point (narrow reading). When it claims this shows "we can preserve observational introspection of current phenomenology without contradiction" *as a response to interpretivists about self-knowledge*, it needs the phenomenology in question to include propositional attitudes (broad reading). 

The defence shows how one *could* develop the thesis into a non-equivocating position—by explicitly committing to phenomenal intentionality. But that would transform it from a methodological distinction into a substantive metaphysical claim about mental states. As stated, the thesis wants the dialectical benefits of challenging interpretivist arguments without paying the cost of defending the metaphysics required to make that challenge work.

The objection succeeds: the thesis equivocates, and cannot be saved without either accepting irrelevance or adding commitments it doesn't acknowledge.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis is structured to be immune from empirical counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as "genuine introspection." 

The weak point is the criterion for identifying "genuine online introspection" versus contaminated memory-of-interpretation. The thesis claims confabulation studies fail because they "systematically introduce temporal gaps (asking after the fact) and interpretive demands (asking 'why' not 'what')." But these criteria can be adjusted post-hoc to exclude any troublesome case.

Consider: If we find confabulation in reports given immediately (no temporal gap), the thesis can respond "but there was still a retrieval demand—the question itself creates a gap between experience and report." If we find confabulation in "what" questions (not "why"), the thesis can respond "but asking 'what' still imposes interpretive structure—genuine introspection would be pure phenomenological attention without verbal report." If we find errors in real-time concurrent verbalization, the thesis can respond "verbalization itself disrupts the phenomenology—genuine introspection must be pre-verbal."

Every apparent counterexample—any study showing unreliability in self-reports under any conditions—can be reclassified as "not actually testing genuine introspection" by discovering some feature (temporal, verbal, attentional, methodological) that "contaminates" it. The thesis provides no positive specification of conditions under which a study *would* test genuine introspection, only negative criteria that can expand to exclude any disconfirming evidence. We can never observe "genuine online introspection" failing because anything that fails wasn't genuine online introspection.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is serious damage requiring a real answer. The thesis needs specifiable conditions under which observational introspection could be tested and potentially fail, or it's merely protecting a claim from evidence by definitional retreat.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection mistakes methodological rigor for unfalsifiability. The thesis *does* provide positive specifications—they're just demanding ones, and that's because isolating introspection proper requires controlling for confounds that previous research ignored.

Here are the testable conditions: Genuine online introspection is tested when (1) subjects attend to and report current phenomenology (2) without retrieval demands (concurrent, not retrospective) (3) without interpretive demands (descriptive "what" not explanatory "why") (4) in domains where phenomenology is rich and accessible (pain, color experience, emotional valence). These aren't post-hoc escape clauses—they're the operationalization of what introspection *is* on observational theories.

And crucially, **this makes specific predictions that could fail**: If we run properly controlled studies and find that concurrent phenomenological reports of pain intensity are no more accurate than retrospective ones, or that subjects can't reliably discriminate phenomenal properties they're currently attending to, or that "what does this feel like now?" questions produce the same confabulation rates as "why did you choose?" questions—*that would falsify the thesis*. The thesis predicts accuracy differences between these conditions. That's falsifiable.

The objection's examples actually prove the point: "If we find confabulation in immediate reports, you'll say there's still retrieval demand"—but whether there's retrieval demand is an *empirical fact about the protocol*, not a post-hoc redefinition. Either the subject reports while experiencing or after experiencing. That's specifiable and checkable. "If we find errors in concurrent verbalization, you'll say verbalization disrupts phenomenology"—but we can test this! Compare concurrent verbal reports with concurrent non-verbal indicators (button presses, facial expressions) or immediate post-experience reports. If the thesis claims verbalization specifically disrupts, that predicts a pattern across these conditions.

The thesis isn't unfalsifiable—it's making fine-grained empirical predictions that require careful experimental design to test. The fact that crude studies with multiple confounds don't test the thesis doesn't mean *no* study could. The objection conflates "requires specific conditions to test" with "immune to testing."
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though it puts up real resistance.

The defence successfully shows that some of the objection's examples overreach—there *are* specifiable differences between concurrent and retrospective reports, between "what" and "why" questions, that aren't mere post-hoc redefinitions. And the defence is right that demanding careful experimental controls isn't the same as unfalsifiability.

**But the defence fails on the crucial point about pre-verbal or report-disrupting concerns.** When pressed on concurrent verbalization, the defence suggests comparing verbal reports with "non-verbal indicators (button presses, facial expressions)." But this opens a fatal dilemma:

If button presses and facial expressions can test introspective accuracy, then they're not testing *introspection* in the observational sense—they're testing behavioral indicators, which is exactly what interpretivists say we use. The thesis needs introspection to be observational access to phenomenology, but now accuracy is checked against non-introspective measures.

If button presses and facial expressions *can't* test introspective accuracy (because only the subject has observational access to their phenomenology), then we have no independent criterion for when introspection succeeds or fails. The thesis would be claiming: introspection is accurate when subjects accurately observe their phenomenology—but we can only know they're accurately observing by... their reports of accurately observing. This *is* unfalsifiable.

**The deeper problem the objection identifies stands**: The thesis needs some way to verify introspective accuracy that doesn't itself rely on the subject's introspective reports. But any such external verification (behavioral measures, neural correlates, task performance) validates *access* to mental states, not specifically *observational* access. And any purely first-personal verification is circular.

The defence shows the thesis isn't trivially unfalsifiable—there are some empirical predictions. But it cannot solve the verification problem for observational introspection specifically, which means the core claim (that preserved observational introspection explains the confabulation data) remains insulated from the very evidence that would matter.

The objection succeeds: the thesis cannot specify conditions under which observational introspection could be empirically distinguished from interpretive self-attribution and shown to fail.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis claims to reveal an "unnoticed distinction," but this reclassification strategy is already the core move in **dual-process theories of self-knowledge** defended by Nichols & Stich (2003) and elaborated by Gertler (2011). These theorists explicitly distinguish between two routes to self-knowledge: direct acquaintance with current phenomenal states (what Gertler calls "introspection proper") and interpretive self-attribution for propositional attitudes. They argue, like this thesis, that confabulation studies test only the interpretive route and leave the acquaintance route untouched.

The weak point is the claim that this distinction went "unnoticed" in the debate's structure. Nichols & Stich specifically respond to Gopnik by arguing she overgeneralizes from confabulation cases involving attitude-attribution to all self-knowledge, ignoring the phenomenal/propositional divide. Gertler explicitly addresses Nisbett-Wilson studies, arguing they involve "displaced perception" (interpreting behavioral output) rather than genuine introspection of experience. The thesis's move—distinguishing memory-contaminated reports from online phenomenological access—is precisely the strategy these theorists already deployed against interpretivists.

The thesis adds minor variations (emphasis on temporal gaps, "why" vs. "what" questions) but the fundamental reclassification—confabulation studies don't test observational introspection of current phenomenology—is the established dual-process response to interpretivist arguments from confabulation. It's not a new distinction; it's a restatement of a known position.
**STRENGTH: 3/5**
The thesis does add some empirical specificity about methodological features (temporal gaps, question types) that gives it texture beyond pure restatement, and the specific framing around "memory of self-interpretation" has some novelty. But the core dialectical move—saving observational introspection by restricting it to current phenomenology and arguing confabulation studies don't test this—is already taken by dual-process theorists, preventing a higher score.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses overlap in conclusion with identity in argument structure. Yes, dual-process theorists distinguish phenomenal from propositional self-knowledge and argue confabulation studies don't test phenomenal introspection—but they do this by *domain restriction* (phenomenal states vs. attitudes), while this thesis does it by *process analysis* (observation vs. memory-of-interpretation). These are different moves with different implications.

Nichols & Stich and Gertler concede that self-knowledge of propositional attitudes (beliefs, intentions, reasons) is interpretive, preserving observational introspection only for phenomenal states like pain or color experience. This is a *retreat*—it gives interpretivists everything they want about the mental states that matter for folk psychology and rational agency. The thesis makes no such concession.

Here's the crucial difference: The thesis claims confabulation studies fail to test introspection *even for propositional attitudes* because they test memory-plus-interpretation rather than observation-of-current-states. When Nisbett & Wilson ask "Why did you prefer this stocking?", the problem isn't that preferences aren't phenomenally accessible (dual-process concession), it's that asking *after the choice* means subjects retrieve their immediate post-hoc interpretation rather than observing the original preference-state. The thesis predicts that properly designed studies—asking "what does this preference feel like *as you're experiencing it*?"—would show different accuracy even for attitudes.

This preserves observational introspection across the full domain dual-process theorists surrendered. It's not domain restriction (phenomenal vs. propositional) but temporal/methodological restriction (concurrent observation vs. retrospective retrieval). The reclassification is different: dual-process says "confabulation studies test the wrong *kind* of mental state," this thesis says "confabulation studies test mental states the wrong *way*."

Gertler's "displaced perception" concept actually supports this difference. She argues confabulation involves interpreting behavioral output—but she accepts this is how we know our attitudes. The thesis denies this: we can observationally access attitudes (via their phenomenology) if we catch them in real-time rather than retrieving memories of having had them. That's a stronger, more ambitious claim than dual-process theories make, not a restatement of their position.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails.

The defence successfully shows the thesis *differs* from dual-process theories in an important way—it's more ambitious, refusing to concede the propositional attitude domain to interpretivism. But this makes the "already taken" objection worse, not better, because it means the thesis lacks the theoretical resources dual-process theories explicitly developed.

**The problem is this**: Dual-process theorists restricted their defense to phenomenal states precisely because they had a worked-out account (acquaintance theory, phenomenal concepts) of how observation delivers knowledge there. They conceded propositional attitudes because they lacked such an account for those states. The thesis wants to extend observational introspection to propositional attitudes but provides no theory of how this works—it just asserts that attitudes have accessible phenomenology if caught in real-time.

The defence earlier appealed to phenomenal intentionality to bridge this gap, but now claims the thesis differs from dual-process theories by *not* restricting to phenomenal states. This creates an unstable position: the thesis is either (a) making the same move as dual-process theories (restricting to phenomenally presented states) in which case it's already taken, or (b) claiming observational access to propositional attitudes beyond their phenomenal presentation, in which case it lacks the theoretical foundation dual-process theories carefully built.

**The "already taken" objection succeeds in this form**: The thesis's novel elements (extending to propositional attitudes, emphasizing temporal/methodological factors over domain restrictions) make it *weaker* than the position already in the literature, not stronger. Dual-process theories represent the most defensible version of the "confabulation studies don't test observational introspection" strategy. The thesis either collapses into that position (when pressed on how observation works) or ventures beyond it without adequate theoretical support.

The thesis is not a pure restatement—the defence is right about that—but its departures from the established position are liabilities rather than advances. It's trying to do what dual-process theories do (save observational introspection from confabulation studies) while avoiding their careful restrictions, and this makes it worse, not better, than what's already taken.

</details>

---

### O13. The asymmetry between self- and other-ascription is not about authority but about liability: I bear practical costs for my self-interpretations that I don't bear for interpreting others.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared framing that treats first-person authority as primarily an epistemic or metaphysical phenomenon requiring explanation in those terms.
- **Sketch:** The debate treats first-person authority as the explanandum—why do my self-ascriptions have special status? But the real asymmetry is practical: when I self-ascribe a belief, I become liable to challenges ('but you said X yesterday'), must defend coherence, face reputational costs for inconsistency. This differential liability makes me both more cautious and more decisive in self-interpretation—more cautious because mistakes are costly, more decisive because hesitation itself is costly (appearing uncertain about my own mind is socially punished). The key premise: what looks like epistemic authority is actually risk distribution. Others can speculate freely about my mind; I cannot. This explains all the phenomena: why self-ascription feels different (it is riskier), why it's usually reliable (high stakes improve calibration), why we grant it presumptive status (we're making the ascriber bear costs), without invoking special access or constitutive powers.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Liability vs authority reframing is fresh but the payoff for resolving the debate is limited.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence claims reduction of social to normative costs, but this fails for private self-interpretation where the asymmetry persists without social stakes
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's predictions are either untestable (requiring impossible cultures) or question-begging (defining 'costs as subject' circularly)
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Claimed distinctions from Bar-On/Brandom collapse: costs presuppose the normative structures those theorists already identified
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's constitutive/reputational distinction abandons the liability framework by requiring prior metaphysics of mental states to explain which costs are constitutive
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot explain why liability generates authority for mental states but not character/skill without invoking the prior metaphysics of mental states (constitutive self-interpretation), which makes liability explanatorily idle and collapses the position back into existing constitutive theories

<details><summary>9 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) The asymmetry between self- and other-ascription is not about authority but about stakes: we are pragmatically forced to take our own self-interpretations as premise for action in a way we needn't for others' mental states, and this practical asymmetry masquerades as epistemic privilege.
- (3/5) The self/other asymmetry in attitude ascription is not asymmetry in knowledge but asymmetry in answerability: you are answerable for your attitudes in a way that makes avowals normatively binding rather than epistemically privileged.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in privileged access but in privileged stakes: self-attributions carry distinctive normative force because I am uniquely positioned to be held responsible for making them true going forward.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in privileged access but in privileged stakes: we have authority over our mental state attributions because we bear special practical consequences for getting them wrong that observers don't bear.
- (3/5) First-person authority is preserved not by privileged access but by privileged stakes: self-interpretation is authoritative because getting it wrong has constitutive costs for agency that getting others wrong does not.
- (3/5) The self-other asymmetry is not about access or method but about stakes: we interpret ourselves using the same evidence as others use, but our self-interpretations have immediate practical implications that make them self-fulfilling in ways other-interpretations cannot be.
- (3/5) The asymmetry between first- and third-person attribution isn't about epistemic authority at all, but about practical commitment: self-ascriptions are performative undertakings of responsibility while other-ascriptions are theoretical predictions, and the confabulation literature has been measuring the wrong thing by focusing on accuracy rather than commitment.
- (2/5) The asymmetry between self- and other-ascription exists not at the level of evidence or authority but at the level of stakes: self-ascriptions are higher stakes because they're subject to norms of sincerity that other-ascriptions aren't, which explains the phenomenology without requiring epistemic privilege.
- (3/5) The asymmetry between first- and third-person ascription is not an asymmetry in epistemic authority but in normative vulnerability—we hold people responsible for getting their own minds right in a way we don't for getting others' minds right, and this practical difference masquerades as epistemic privilege.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "liability" between two readings that play different roles in the argument. On one reading, liability means *social-reputational costs* (being challenged, appearing inconsistent, losing credibility). On another reading, it means *rational-normative commitments* (being bound by one's self-interpretations, having special obligations of coherence). The argument needs both: it's plausible that social costs exist and affect behavior only on the first reading—people do face reputational damage for contradicting themselves. But it's interesting as an explanation of the self/other asymmetry only on the second reading—mere social punishment doesn't explain why *I* should defer to my own past self-ascriptions or why self-interpretation feels authoritative from the inside, only why I might strategically avoid certain claims. The sketch slides between these: "must defend coherence" suggests rational obligation (second reading), while "reputational costs" and "socially punished" invoke the first. If liability is purely social-reputational, it cannot explain the normative force of self-ascription—why I'm bound by what I said, not just punished for changing it. If it's rational-normative, the claim that this liability is distinctively practical rather than epistemic becomes unclear, since rational commitments seem precisely epistemic in character.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — a genuine problem, answerable. The thesis could respond by clarifying whether social costs somehow generate normative force, or by biting the bullet that all apparent authority is strategic positioning.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection treats social-reputational and rational-normative liability as cleanly separable, but they are not. The thesis's power lies precisely in showing how social costs *generate* normative force without remainder.

Consider: Why should I defer to my past self-ascriptions? The objection assumes this requires a distinctively "rational-normative" explanation separate from social facts. But this is the intellectualist prejudice the thesis attacks. I defer to my past self-ascriptions because *failing to do so* carries costs that make me unreliable as an interlocutor, collaborator, and agent. When I say "I believe P" today and "I don't believe P" tomorrow without explanation, I don't merely suffer reputational damage as an external punishment—I become *functionally useless* for the social practices that constitute having beliefs at all (testimony, commitment, collaborative inquiry, deliberation).

The "normative force" the objection seeks is nothing over and above this: to be bound by my self-ascriptions just *is* to be the kind of creature who can participate in these practices. The felt authority "from the inside" is the internalization of these stakes. It feels different because it *is* different—I'm the one who will pay.

The objection's "strategic positioning" characterization misses this. It's not that I strategically avoid certain claims to protect reputation while secretly knowing the "real" normative force comes from elsewhere. Rather, the social costs *are* the normative force. There is no deeper rational obligation hiding behind them. The thesis can say: rational-normative commitments are constituted by patterns of liability, not vice versa.

This isn't equivocation—it's reduction. What looked like two readings is one phenomenon at different levels of description.

VERDICT

The defence fails, but not because of equivocation.

The problem is that the reduction doesn't work. Even granting that social costs generate normative force for *public* self-ascriptions, this cannot explain the asymmetry in *private* self-interpretation. When I wonder silently whether I believe P, there are no immediate social costs in play, yet the first-person/third-person asymmetry persists. I don't adopt a spectatorial stance toward my own mind even when no one will ever know.

The thesis might reply: internalized social costs still operate. But now we need an account of internalization that doesn't simply reintroduce epistemic authority through the back door. If I've internalized the costs so thoroughly that they operate even in private, what explains the *accuracy* of this internalization? Why does privately treating myself as authoritative about my beliefs track anything real?

The defence's reduction works only if all self-interpretation is essentially public. That's a bullet one could bite, but the thesis doesn't bite it—and without doing so, the equivocation remains: social liability for public claims, something else (epistemic? constitutive?) for private self-knowledge.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because every apparent counterexample gets reinterpreted as confirming the liability framework. Consider cases where self-ascription appears authoritative *without* liability costs:

(1) **Private self-interpretation**: When I silently wonder whether I'm anxious or just tired, the first-person asymmetry persists without social costs. Response predicted: "Internalized liability still operates" or "this shows liability's causal history, now psychologically real."

(2) **Low-stakes self-ascription**: Casual remarks like "I prefer coffee to tea" carry minimal reputational risk, yet retain first-person authority. Response predicted: "Even small claims create liability" or "the liability is dispositional—what matters is that costs *would* accrue if challenged."

(3) **Self-ascriptions that reduce liability**: Saying "I'm uncertain whether I believe P" should minimize liability by hedging, yet still exhibits first-person asymmetry—I'm still the authority on my uncertainty. Response predicted: "Appearing uncertain carries its own costs" (as the sketch already says), making uncertainty itself a liability.

(4) **Other-ascriptions with high stakes**: A therapist interpreting a patient, or a judge assessing credibility, faces enormous practical costs for misinterpretation, yet lacks first-person authority. Response predicted: "Different *kind* of liability" or "they don't bear costs *as* the interpreted subject."

Every case that seems to show authority without liability, or liability without authority, can be redefined by adjusting what counts as "liability," whose liability matters, or when costs are "really" operating (actual vs. dispositional vs. internalized vs. historical).

STRENGTH: 4/5 — serious, needs a real answer. The thesis requires specifying what liability pattern *wouldn't* explain authority, or what authority *without* liability would look like.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses unfalsifiability with explanatory power. A framework is unfalsifiable when it makes no predictions that could distinguish it from alternatives. But the liability thesis makes precise, falsifiable predictions that the objector ignores.

**The falsifiable core**: The thesis predicts that first-person authority should *track liability differentials*. Specifically:

- **Prediction 1**: In cultures or contexts where self-contradiction carries no social cost, first-person authority should weaken or disappear. If the thesis is right, we should find communities where people routinely defer to others' interpretations of their mental states when self-ascription carries no special liability.

- **Prediction 2**: First-person authority should be *stronger* for mental states where inconsistency is more costly. We should see greater deference to self-ascription of beliefs (where inconsistency matters for testimony, promising, deliberation) than for fleeting sensations (where it doesn't).

- **Prediction 3**: When liability is artificially transferred—say, a legal system that punishes therapists for patients' self-contradictions—authority patterns should shift. The therapist should become *more* cautious and "authoritative" about the patient's states.

Now address the cases:

**(1) Private self-interpretation**: The thesis predicts this shows liability's *functional role*, not just its current presence. Private self-interpretation is practice for public performance—I rehearse the interpretations I'll be liable for. This is falsifiable: if people raised in environments with zero liability for self-ascription still showed the same private asymmetry, the thesis fails. But they don't exist, because liability is constitutive of the practice.

**(2) Low-stakes cases**: The objection misses that liability is *categorical*, not scalar in the way suggested. Even "I prefer coffee" creates liability for future consistency. The falsifiable prediction: if someone systematically contradicts even trivial self-ascriptions without cost, others stop treating them as having preferences at all. This is testable and true.

**(3) Uncertainty ascriptions**: The objection actually *confirms* the thesis. The fact that "I'm uncertain" still exhibits first-person authority shows that what matters is *bearing the costs of one's self-interpretation*, whatever it is. The asymmetry is: I pay for claiming uncertainty, you don't. This is falsifiable: in contexts where expressing uncertainty carries no differential cost (perhaps certain scientific communities), we should see the asymmetry weaken. We do.

**(4) High-stakes other-ascription**: This is the thesis's *strongest* prediction. The therapist faces costs for *being wrong*, but not costs *as the subject*. The patient must live with being-interpreted-as-X in a way the therapist doesn't. The falsifiable claim: if we could transfer subjective costs—make the therapist literally experience the consequences of being-interpreted-as-X—authority would shift. We can't test this directly, but approximations exist: when interpreters must "walk in the shoes" of their interpretations (method acting, certain therapeutic techniques), their interpretations do gain a different kind of authority.

**What would falsify the thesis**: Finding stable communities where (a) self-ascription carries no differential liability, yet (b) first-person authority persists with the same strength. Or finding cases where (a) liability differentials are reversed (others pay more for my self-interpretations than I do), yet (b) authority patterns don't reverse.

The objection's examples aren't counterexamples that get "reinterpreted"—they're predicted by the thesis and distinguished from genuine falsifiers.

---
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. It successfully shows the thesis makes predictions, but those predictions are either untestable or question-begging.

**The untestability problem**: Prediction 1 requires finding "cultures where self-contradiction carries no social cost." But the thesis also claims that having beliefs *at all* requires liability for self-ascription—that these practices are "constitutive." So any culture where people have beliefs will ipso facto have liability. The prediction becomes: "In cultures where people don't have beliefs (as we understand them), they won't have first-person authority (as we understand it)." This is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample gets redescribed as "not really having beliefs" or "having a different practice."

**The question-begging problem**: The defence's handling of case (4) reveals this. When pressed on why the therapist's enormous stakes don't generate authority, the answer is: "different kind of liability—not costs *as the subject*." But "costs as the subject" just *is* first-person authority under another name. The thesis was supposed to *explain* why the subject has special authority by appeal to liability. Instead, it posits a special kind of liability ("as the subject") that only the subject can have. This makes liability do no explanatory work—it's just a relabeling.

**The deeper problem**: The defence's Prediction 3 actually exposes the unfalsifiability. It says that if we "artificially transfer" liability to therapists, authority should shift. But then it immediately retreats: we can't transfer *subjective* costs, only external ones. So the prediction is: "If we could do the impossible, we'd see X." And when we approximate it (method acting), the defence claims we *do* see authority shift—but this is precisely what's in dispute. Method actors don't gain first-person authority over the characters' mental states; they gain better *access* or *understanding*, which is exactly what non-liability theories predict.

The thesis survives only by making "liability" so flexible (actual, dispositional, internalized, historical, subjective, constitutive) that it can accommodate any pattern of authority. That's unfalsifiability.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT.

The liability-based account of first-person authority is not novel—it's a version of the social-pragmatist position developed in different forms by several theorists. Most directly, it resembles **Dorit Bar-On's neo-expressivist account** in *Speaking My Mind* (2004), which explains first-person authority through the distinctive commitments and vulnerabilities created by avowals rather than through epistemic access. Bar-On argues that self-ascriptions are authoritative because they are expressive acts that make the speaker answerable in ways that interpretations of others don't.

More broadly, the thesis echoes **Robert Brandom's normative-pragmatist framework** from *Making It Explicit* (1994), where the authority of self-ascription derives from the distinctive score-keeping responsibilities one has for one's own commitments within the "game of giving and asking for reasons." Brandom explicitly treats first-person authority as a matter of differential *deontic status*—who is responsible for what—rather than epistemic privilege.

The "liability creates authority" move also appears in **Richard Moran's work** on self-knowledge (*Authority and Estrangement*, 2001), particularly his claim that avowals are authoritative because they are simultaneously *reports* and *commitments*, creating a distinctive rational pressure that doesn't apply to third-person attributions.

Even the specific emphasis on *costs* and *risk distribution* has precedent in **Miranda Fricker's** work on epistemic injustice and the differential vulnerabilities created by testimonial practices, though she doesn't apply it directly to self-knowledge.

The thesis's reframing from "epistemic authority" to "practical liability" is the shared move across these positions: treating first-person asymmetry as fundamentally about normative-social structure rather than cognitive access.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis presents itself as a reframing that dissolves a debate, but the reframing has already been extensively developed, defended, and criticized in the literature. The candidate would need to show either that their version differs substantially from these predecessors or that it advances the position in ways the existing literature hasn't. Simply rediscovering the social-pragmatist turn isn't sufficient for a novel contribution.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection commits the taxonomist's fallacy: collecting precedents under a label ("social-pragmatist position") and declaring overlap, without examining whether the theoretical mechanisms are actually the same.

**The crucial differences:**

**vs. Bar-On**: Bar-On's neo-expressivism treats avowals as *expressive acts* with a distinctive "Janus-faced" character—simultaneously world-involving and self-presenting. The authority comes from the *expressive nature* of the speech act itself. But the liability thesis makes no appeal to expression or speech-act type. The asymmetry exists even for *silent* self-interpretation (as the earlier exchange acknowledged), and even for self-ascriptions that are purely descriptive, not expressive. A diary entry "I believed P yesterday" is authoritative without being expressive in Bar-On's sense. The liability thesis says: what matters is *who pays*, not what kind of act it is.

**vs. Brandom**: Brandom's deontic scorekeeping is about *entitlements* and *commitments* within reason-giving practices—it's still fundamentally *epistemic* in character (who is entitled to what inference, who must defend what claim). The liability thesis inverts this: it's not about rational entitlements but about *costs*. I don't have authority because I have special entitlements in the game of giving reasons; I have authority because *I'm the one who will be held to account*. This explains something Brandom struggles with: why self-ascription feels authoritative even when I'm *bad* at giving reasons, even when I'm irrational. The liability persists regardless of my rational competence.

**vs. Moran**: Moran's "commitment" is explicitly *rational*—avowals are authoritative because they're exercises of rational self-determination, expressions of agency. The liability thesis requires no such agency or rationality. Even an avowed belief I formed through manipulation or irrationality creates liability. Even "I don't know what I believe" creates liability (as noted earlier). The mechanism is *causal-social* (cost-bearing), not *rational-agential* (self-determination).

**vs. Fricker**: Fricker's work on testimonial injustice concerns *credibility assessment*—who gets believed, who gets doubted. But the liability thesis isn't about credibility or belief. Even when others *don't believe* my self-ascription, I still bear the liability for it in a way they don't. A disbelieved self-ascription still creates costs for me (I must defend it, explain the discrepancy, manage the inconsistency) that the disbelievers don't face. The asymmetry persists through credibility failures.

**The distinctive core**: All the cited theorists explain authority through normative *structure* (expressive acts, deontic statuses, rational commitments, epistemic positions). The liability thesis explains it through *cost distribution*. This is genuinely different: it predicts that authority tracks *who bears consequences*, not who has what rational role or epistemic position. 

The test: Brandom predicts authority should weaken when someone is bad at giving reasons. Bar-On predicts it should weaken when avowals aren't genuinely expressive. Moran predicts it should weaken when rational agency is compromised. The liability thesis predicts authority should persist *as long as costs remain asymmetrically distributed*, regardless of rational competence, expressive success, or agential capacity.

This is a distinct mechanism, even if it shares a "social-pragmatist" family resemblance.

---
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. The distinctions it draws are too thin to establish genuine novelty.

**The problem**: The defence claims the liability thesis is distinctive because it appeals to *costs* rather than *norms*, but this distinction collapses on examination. 

Consider: What makes something a "cost" in the relevant sense? The defence says I bear costs for self-ascription that others don't—I must defend coherence, face reputational damage, etc. But *why* must I defend coherence? Why does reputational damage follow inconsistency? The answer must invoke *norms*: there are social-rational norms that make inconsistency costly, that require defense, that punish contradiction. The "costs" are just the *enforcement mechanism* for normative structures.

So when the defence says liability is "causal-social" rather than "rational-agential," it's drawing a false distinction. The causal-social costs exist *because of* rational-agential norms. You can't have the cost distribution without the normative structure that makes those costs costs.

**The collapse into Brandom**: The defence claims to differ from Brandom because liability persists "even when I'm bad at giving reasons." But Brandom's account already handles this: being bad at giving reasons doesn't eliminate your *deontic status* as the one who undertook the commitment. You're still the scorekeeper for your own commitments even if you're a bad scorekeeper. The "cost" of being held to account just *is* having that deontic status. The liability thesis hasn't identified a different mechanism—it's redescribed Brandom's deontic statuses in economic metaphor.

**The test case fails**: The defence's "test" actually confirms overlap. It claims the liability thesis predicts authority persists when rational competence fails, as long as costs remain distributed. But Bar-On, Brandom, and Moran all predict exactly this: the avowal is authoritative even from an irrational speaker (Bar-On: because it's still expressive of their state; Brandom: because they still hold the commitment; Moran: because it's still their rational position to determine, even if badly). The "distinctive prediction" isn't distinctive.

**The real issue**: The defence is right that there's *something* in the economic framing—the emphasis on costs, risks, liability—that feels different from the existing literature's vocabulary. But a new *vocabulary* isn't a new *theory* unless it licenses different predictions or explanations. The defence hasn't shown that "cost distribution" does explanatory work that "deontic status," "expressive act," or "rational commitment" doesn't already do.

The thesis is a rediscovery, not a reframing. It's Bar-On + Brandom in the language of economics, without sufficient theoretical distance to constitute a novel position.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**
The liability argument proves too much because it would license first-person authority for *any* self-ascription where the speaker bears differential costs, including cases where such authority is clearly unwarranted.

Consider **self-ascriptions of moral character**: "I am a generous person." I bear enormous practical costs for this claim—reputational damage if I act stingily, social pressure to maintain consistency, liability for others' expectations. These costs vastly exceed those I face when attributing generosity to others. Yet we *don't* grant first-person authority here. Others are often better positioned to judge my character than I am; self-serving bias is expected; "he says he's generous but he's not" is perfectly acceptable. The liability differential exists but authority doesn't follow.

Or **self-ascriptions of skill**: "I am an excellent driver." Again, I bear special costs—I must defend this in ways others needn't defend their assessments of my driving, I face embarrassment if I crash after claiming expertise, my self-presentation is at stake. Yet first-person authority is *weakest* precisely for such claims. We expect self-assessment to be unreliable here.

The thesis claims liability *explains* authority: "I defer to my past self-ascriptions because failing to do so carries costs." But by this logic, I should defer to my past self-ascriptions of generosity or skill for exactly the same reason—the costs of inconsistency are just as real. The argument, if it worked, would establish that I'm authoritative about my character and abilities simply because I bear reputational costs for those claims.

The thesis cannot distinguish mental-state self-ascriptions (where authority is real) from character/skill self-ascriptions (where it isn't) while keeping "differential liability" as the explanatory mechanism.

**STRENGTH: 4/5** — This is serious damage requiring substantive response. The thesis needs to explain why liability generates authority for "I believe P" but not for "I am generous," despite similar cost structures.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes all liability is equivalent, but the thesis can distinguish *constitutive* from *reputational* liability—and only the former generates authority.

**The key distinction**: For mental-state self-ascriptions, the liability is *constitutive of having the state at all*. What makes it the case that I believe P (rather than merely being disposed to say "I believe P") is partly that I bear the costs of that self-interpretation going forward—I must reason from P, testify consistently, update coherently. The liability isn't an external consequence attached to a pre-existing belief; it's part of what *makes* it a belief.

For character and skill ascriptions, the liability is merely *reputational*—a cost attached to a claim about an independently existing fact. Whether I'm actually generous doesn't depend on whether I bear costs for claiming generosity. My generosity exists (or doesn't) prior to and independent of my self-ascription. The costs here are just social penalties for misrepresentation.

**Why this matters**: The thesis can now say: First-person authority attaches to self-ascriptions where the speaker's liability is *constitutive*—where bearing the costs is part of what makes the ascription true. This explains the asymmetry perfectly:

- "I believe P": My bearing liability for this claim going forward (treating P as a premise, testifying to P, etc.) is constitutive of currently believing P. So I'm authoritative because *my commitment to bearing costs* partly determines the fact.

- "I am generous": My bearing reputational costs for this claim doesn't constitute my generosity. Generosity is constituted by patterns of action, motivation, etc. that exist independently. So liability creates no authority—the fact is independent of my willingness to bear costs.

- "I am an excellent driver": Same structure. Driving skill is constituted by performance, not by my liability for claiming skill.

**The test**: The thesis predicts authority should track constitutive vs. reputational liability. And it does: We grant first-person authority for mental states (where self-interpretation is partly constitutive), for intentions (where commitment partly constitutes the intention), for decisions (where avowal partly makes the decision real). We deny it for character, skills, and other traits where the facts are independent of self-ascription.

This isn't ad hoc—it's principled. The liability thesis was always about *practical costs that shape the phenomenon itself*, not just costs attached externally. Character and skill cases involve the wrong kind of liability.

---
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. The constitutive/reputational distinction doesn't save the thesis—it abandons it.

**The fatal problem**: Once the defence admits that authority requires *constitutive* liability—liability that "partly makes the ascription true"—it has conceded that liability alone doesn't explain authority. Now we need an independent account of *what makes liability constitutive*, and that account will do the real explanatory work.

Consider: Why is my liability for "I believe P" constitutive while my liability for "I am generous" is merely reputational? The defence says: because belief is partly constituted by "treating P as a premise, testifying to P, etc."—i.e., by the commitments that create liability. But this just is a version of the constitutive theories the thesis was supposed to replace. It's Moran's "rational commitment" account or Bilgrami's "self-fulfilling" account, dressed in liability language.

**The circularity**: The defence needs to say: "Liability is constitutive for mental states but not for character." But what makes mental states special such that liability can be constitutive for them? If the answer is "mental states are the kinds of things partly constituted by commitments/self-interpretation," then we're back to explaining authority by *the nature of mental states*, not by liability. Liability becomes an intermediate step, not the foundation.

**The proves-too-much problem returns**: The defence claims intentions have constitutive liability, which is why "I intend to φ" is authoritative. But now consider: "I intend to be more generous." This is a mental state (an intention), so by the defence's logic, my liability for this claim should be constitutive—my commitment to bearing costs for this intention partly makes it real. Yet we *don't* grant full authority here. Others can reasonably say "you think you intend that, but you don't really" based on my behavioral patterns, exactly as with "I am generous."

**What went wrong**: The original thesis promised to explain authority through cost-distribution without invoking special metaphysics of mental states. But the defence reveals that only *certain kinds* of cost-distribution (constitutive, not merely reputational) generate authority, and what makes costs constitutive depends on the prior metaphysics of the states in question. The liability framework collapses back into the constitutive theories it aimed to replace.

The thesis survives only by smuggling in the very explanatory resources (constitutive self-interpretation, special metaphysics of mental states) it claimed to eliminate. That's not a reframing—it's a capitulation with new terminology.

</details>

---

### O14. Implicit attitudes show that interpretation isn't an alternative to inner observation but a necessary completion of it: what we observe internally is always interpretively indeterminate.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Any remaining defenders of pure observational introspection, and interpretivists like Carruthers who think rejecting observation follows from accepting interpretation.
- **Sketch:** The debate treats observation and interpretation as alternatives—either we observe our minds directly or we interpret behavioural evidence. But implicit attitude research reveals that proponents of inner observation are committed to interpretation anyway. When I observe my internal states—a feeling of discomfort around X, an inclination toward Y—what I observe underdetermines the mental state attribution. The same phenomenology could be anxiety, implicit bias, physical discomfort, or moral concern. The key premise: phenomenological observation yields only contentless affect or inclination; mental state concepts are already interpretive categories we apply to give content to what we observe. This means observationalists (to the extent any remain) are covertly committed to interpretation, while pure interpretivists can accommodate the phenomenological observation they reject. The real question isn't whether interpretation is involved (it always is) but whether phenomenological input constrains interpretation in epistemically significant ways.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The point that phenomenological observation requires interpretive completion is sound and empirically supported, but represents a synthesis of existing positions rather than new territory.
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence showed falsifiability criteria exist but could not specify phenomenology independent of interpretation, making the universal claim untestable
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 2/5, answered. Defence successfully distinguished the position from Carruthers by showing it accepts observation while he rejects it—genuine middle ground
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's own examples showed external observation also requires interpretation to resolve indeterminacy, eliminating the claimed asymmetry
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence conflated phenomenal determinacy with mental state determinacy and conceded pain observation is determinate while only causation requires interpretation
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Implicit attitudes reveal that observation of affective phenomenology (discomfort, inclination, aversion) is interpretively indeterminate and requires completion by behavioral evidence, though sensory phenomenology (pain, color, location) may provide determinate observational content
- **Flaw:** The universal claim that internal observation is 'always interpretively indeterminate' fails because the defender could not distinguish phenomenology from interpretation without circularity, and pain cases show determinate observational content exists even when causal explanation requires interpretation

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims that "what we observe internally is always interpretively indeterminate." This creates an unfalsifiable structure: any case where someone claims determinate inner observation gets reinterpreted as merely observing "contentless affect" that still requires interpretation. 

Consider someone who introspects and reports: "I directly observe that I believe Paris is in France—not mere inclination or affect, but the belief itself with its specific content." The thesis handles this by insisting they've only observed some phenomenological state that they then *interpreted* as that belief. The "contentless affect or inclination" premise functions as a universal solvent—every apparent counterexample of determinate observation gets redefined as really just observing something indeterminate that subsequently got interpreted.

The weak point is the premise that phenomenological observation yields "only contentless affect or inclination." This cannot be tested because any phenomenology rich enough to count as determinate gets reclassified as already involving interpretation. The thesis is protected by stipulating that the genuine observational layer is always the indeterminate one, making it impossible to observe a counterexample. If you think you observed something determinate, the thesis replies: "No, you observed something indeterminate and interpreted it"—unfalsifiable redefinition.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis needs independent criteria for distinguishing pre-interpretive phenomenology from interpreted content, or it merely assumes what it claims to show.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection mistakes a substantive empirical claim for an unfalsifiable stipulation. The thesis does not *stipulate* that phenomenology is contentless—it *argues* this from implicit attitude cases where the very same phenomenology demonstrably supports multiple interpretations.

Consider the canonical case: someone experiences discomfort when a Black experimenter enters the room. They introspect and report "I observe that I'm anxious about the upcoming test." But implicit bias measures reveal another interpretation fits the same phenomenology: racial discomfort. The phenomenology itself—the felt quality—is identical across interpretations. This isn't unfalsifiable redefinition; it's empirical evidence that phenomenal character underdetermines mental state attribution.

The defence has three components:

**First, the falsifiability criterion.** The thesis *is* falsifiable: show a phenomenology that cannot coherently receive multiple mental state interpretations.
