If someone's introspective report of "belief that Paris is in France" has a phenomenal character that could not possibly be reinterpreted as (say) mere disposition to assent, habitual association, or retrieved memory trace, the thesis fails. The thesis predicts we cannot find such cases—that's empirically testable.

**Second, the Paris case specifically.** When someone reports observing "the belief that Paris is in France," what is the phenomenology? Not the propositional content itself (contents aren't phenomenally presented). Rather: perhaps a felt sense of certainty, an absence of doubt, a readiness to affirm. But these phenomenal markers are compatible with multiple states: genuine belief, false memory, confabulated conviction, or mere semantic competence. The person who "observes" their belief is already applying the concept *belief* to interpret these phenomenal cues. The thesis doesn't stipulate this—it challenges the objector to identify what phenomenology could distinguish belief-that-P from other states with the same propositional content.

**Third, independent criteria exist.** We can distinguish pre-interpretive phenomenology from interpreted content by asking: does the subject's interpretation predict their behaviour better than alternative interpretations of the same phenomenology? In implicit bias cases, the answer is no—the "anxiety about test" interpretation fails to predict IAT performance, approach/avoidance behaviour, etc. This gives us leverage: interpretations are not arbitrary overlays but empirically constrained hypotheses about what phenomenology means.
**VERDICT**
The defence **fails**, though not completely.

The defence succeeds in showing the thesis is not *completely* unfalsifiable—there is a coherence constraint (phenomenology that cannot receive multiple interpretations would falsify it) and a predictive constraint (behavioural evidence can adjudicate between interpretations). These are genuine empirical vulnerabilities.

But the defence fails to escape the deeper problem: **it cannot specify what counts as "the same phenomenology" independent of interpretation**. When we say the discomfort-near-Black-experimenter and test-anxiety have "identical phenomenology," how do we know? Only by already having interpreted both as "discomfort" rather than, say, one being discomfort and the other being excitement misattributed. The thesis needs to identify phenomenal character in interpretation-neutral terms to make its empirical claim, but it lacks the resources to do so.

The Paris case exposes this. The defence asks what phenomenology accompanies "belief that Paris is in France"—and correctly notes it might be certainty-feeling, readiness-to-affirm, etc. But now: are these *themselves* contentless phenomenology, or already interpreted? If contentless, we've just pushed the problem back (what makes a feeling count as "certainty" rather than "familiarity"?). If already interpreted, we've conceded that not all phenomenology is contentless, contradicting the thesis.

The thesis survives as a claim about *some* introspective reports (implicit attitudes reveal indeterminacy) but dies as a universal claim ("what we observe internally is **always** interpretively indeterminate"). The objection stands: without interpretation-independent access to phenomenology, the universal version is unfalsifiable.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**

The thesis claims to reveal something novel about the relationship between observation and interpretation in self-knowledge, but this position is already well-established in philosophy of mind as **interpretationism** or **self-interpretivism**, most prominently defended by Peter Carruthers himself (whom the thesis paradoxically lists as a target).

Carruthers' "mindreading is prior" thesis in *The Opacity of Mind* (2011) explicitly argues that introspection involves applying the same interpretive mindreading mechanisms we use for others to our own mental states. He argues we lack privileged observational access and instead interpret our own minds through behavioral and phenomenological cues—precisely the structure this thesis describes. The claim that "phenomenological observation yields only contentless affect or inclination" that requires interpretive categorization is Carruthers' position: we observe raw phenomenology and interpret it using folk-psychological concepts.

Similarly, Ryle's *The Concept of Mind* (1949) attacked the "inner observation" model, arguing mental state ascriptions involve interpretive dispositions rather than direct inspection. More recently, Gopnik (1993) and Cassam (2014) have defended interpretive models of self-knowledge where introspection is "theory-laden" rather than observational.

The weak point is the claim to novelty or dialectical advancement. The thesis presents as revealing a hidden commitment (observationalists are "covertly committed to interpretation") but this is the standard interpretationist position. The implicit attitudes evidence may provide contemporary empirical support, but the conceptual structure—observation delivers indeterminate data requiring interpretive completion—is the established interpretationist view, not a synthesis transcending the observation/interpretation debate.

**STRENGTH: 3/5**

A genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs to articulate what's novel beyond citing implicit attitudes as new empirical evidence for an existing position, or acknowledge it's refining rather than transcending the debate.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses *sharing a conclusion* with *being the same position*. Yes, Carruthers and other interpretivists conclude that introspection involves interpretation—but they reach this by *rejecting* observational access entirely. The thesis reaches it by *accepting* observational access and showing it's insufficient. That's not redundancy; it's a critically different dialectical path with different commitments.

**The key distinction:** Carruthers argues we have no special first-person observational access at all—we know our own minds the same way we know others' minds, through behavioral interpretation plus whatever sensory access we happen to have to our own case. The thesis argues we *do* have distinctive first-person observational access (to phenomenology), but this observation is *structurally indeterminate* and therefore necessarily requires interpretation to yield mental state attributions.

This matters for three reasons:

**First, it changes what needs explaining.** Pure interpretivism must explain away the apparent phenomenology of introspection as illusory or reconstructed. The thesis doesn't need to explain it away—it accepts phenomenological observation as real but shows it's epistemically insufficient. This is a weaker, more defensible claim: rather than denying what seems phenomenologically obvious (that we observe *something* internal), it explains why observation alone cannot deliver determinate self-knowledge.

**Second, it changes the dialectical situation with observationalists.** Carruthers' interpretationism and observationalism are opposites—you pick one or the other. The thesis shows observationalists are *committed to* interpretation whether they realize it or not, because observation delivers only indeterminate input. This isn't just empirical support for an existing view; it's a demonstration that the observation/interpretation dichotomy is false. Even if you think you're doing pure observation, you're necessarily also interpreting. That's a different claim than "observation doesn't happen."

**Third, implicit attitudes provide not just evidence but *constitutive cases*.** Carruthers argues from theoretical considerations about mindreading architecture. The thesis argues from cases where we can *demonstrate* interpretive indeterminacy: the same phenomenology, multiple empirically viable interpretations, behavioral evidence needed to adjudicate. These aren't just "contemporary empirical support"—they're cases that make the indeterminacy claim *testable* in ways pure interpretationism is not. We can actually identify specific phenomenology (discomfort-near-X) and show it underdetermines attribution (anxiety vs. implicit bias), then use behavioral measures to constrain interpretation.

**The novelty claim:** The thesis is novel because it occupies logical space neither pure observationalism nor pure interpretationism can: *observation happens but is necessarily incomplete*. Carruthers says observation is eliminated by interpretation. The thesis says observation is *completed* by interpretation. That's not the same position wearing new clothes—it's a genuine third option that preserves phenomenological observation while showing why interpretation is mandatory rather than optional.

The target list makes sense now: Carruthers is a target not because he's wrong about interpretation being involved, but because he's wrong to think this means rejecting observation. The thesis shows you can accept everything Carruthers says about interpretation while still maintaining that phenomenological observation provides real (if indeterminate) epistemic input.
**VERDICT**
The defence **succeeds substantially** but reveals a cost.

The defence successfully establishes that the thesis is not merely Carruthers-plus-examples. The logical structure is genuinely different: "observation is eliminated" ≠ "observation is indeterminate." And this difference has real consequences—the thesis can accommodate phenomenological realism in a way pure interpretationism cannot, while still insisting on interpretation's necessity.

The implicit attitudes cases do provide something beyond generic "theory-ladenness" claims: they give specific, measurable instances where we can identify phenomenology, demonstrate multiple interpretations fit it, and use behavioral evidence to adjudicate. That's empirically substantive in ways Ryle's or even Carruthers' arguments are not.

**However**, the defence succeeds only by *narrowing* what counts as novel. The thesis is not "revealing a hidden commitment" that transforms the debate—it's *carving out middle ground* between existing positions. That's philosophically legitimate but dialectically less powerful than the sketch suggested. The observation/interpretation dichotomy being "false" just means there's a third position, not that the debate was misconceived.

More seriously, the defence exposes dependence on a specific claim about phenomenology that may not generalize. The thesis needs phenomenological observation to be *real enough* to matter (against Carruthers) but *indeterminate enough* to require interpretation (against observationalists). This is a narrow target. If phenomenology is richer than "contentless affect or inclination," observationalists win. If it's poorer (or non-existent), Carruthers wins. The thesis survives only if phenomenology has exactly the right structure—present but indeterminate—and the defence doesn't establish this is the case; it assumes it from implicit attitude cases that may not represent all introspection.

**Verdict: The defence holds against the "already taken" objection.** The thesis is not just Carruthers with examples—it's a distinct position occupying genuine logical space. But the defence reveals the position's viability depends on a specific empirical bet about phenomenology's structure that may not pay off universally.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**
The argument structure is: when we observe internal phenomenology (discomfort, inclination), this observation underdetermines mental state attribution—the same phenomenology could be anxiety, implicit bias, physical discomfort, etc. Therefore, observation requires interpretive completion.

But this argument, if it works, proves that **all observation whatsoever requires interpretive completion and is indeterminate**. Consider perceptual observation: I see a coiled shape in the grass. The same visual phenomenology could be a snake, a rope, a stick, a shadow pattern, or a garden hose. The retinal image underdetermines the object attribution. By the thesis's logic, visual observation "yields only contentless" sensory data, and we must interpret it using object-concepts to give it content.

This extends universally: any observation underdetermines theoretical interpretation (the problem of underdetermination in philosophy of science). Observing a white powder: salt, sugar, cocaine, anthrax? Observing a fever: infection, autoimmune response, heat exhaustion? The same observational evidence always supports multiple interpretations.

If the thesis is right that underdetermination shows observation requires interpretive completion, then there is no such thing as determinate observation anywhere—all observation is "interpretively indeterminate." This makes the claim about introspection specifically lose its force. The thesis purports to reveal something special about self-knowledge (that it necessarily involves interpretation), but the argument generalizes to show something trivial (that all knowledge involves interpretation).

**Weak point:** The premise that interpretive indeterminacy demonstrates observation's insufficiency. This premise, applied consistently, eliminates the distinction between observation and interpretation everywhere, making the thesis's specific claim about introspection collapse into general epistemological holism.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This is serious damage requiring a real answer. The thesis needs to explain why the underdetermination argument has special force for introspection rather than being a general feature of all observation, or accept that it's making only a generic epistemological point with no special implications for self-knowledge debates.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection claims the argument proves too much by generalizing to all observation. But this misses a crucial asymmetry: **external observation has resources for disambiguation that internal observation structurally lacks**.

The defence turns on showing that perceptual underdetermination is *in principle resolvable* in ways phenomenological indeterminacy is not:

**First, perceptual observation allows triangulation.** When I see a coiled shape, I can approach it, touch it, smell it, view it from different angles, test its flexibility. These additional observations constrain interpretation—the rope hypothesis predicts different tactile phenomenology than the snake hypothesis. Crucially, these are *more observations of the same object*, not interpretations of the original observation. The visual phenomenology was indeterminate, but the object itself provides additional observational access that resolves indeterminacy.

Contrast introspection: when I observe discomfort-near-X, what additional observations of *that phenomenology itself* can I make? I cannot approach my discomfort from a different angle, touch it, or view it under different lighting. The phenomenology is *all there is* to observe. I can observe my behavior, my other mental states, my physiological responses—but these are observations of *different things*, not additional observations of the original phenomenology. They function as *evidence for interpretation*, not as disambiguation of observation.

**Second, perceptual objects have observer-independent properties.** The coiled shape is *actually* either a snake or a rope—there's a fact of the matter independent of my interpretation. My interpretation can be corrected by the world. But phenomenology has no such independence from the subject. There's no fact of the matter about whether my discomfort "really is" anxiety or implicit bias independent of how it functions in my psychological economy. The phenomenology doesn't have hidden properties I can discover through better observation; it has only the properties I experience, and those properties genuinely underdetermine mental state attribution.

**Third, the implicit attitude cases show *empirical* indeterminacy, not merely epistemic underdetermination.** With the coiled shape, I'm epistemically uncertain but the shape has a determinate nature. With implicit attitudes, the phenomenology itself is *genuinely indeterminate*—it's not that the discomfort is "really" racial bias and I'm mistaken in calling it anxiety. Rather, the same phenomenal state can *genuinely constitute* either anxiety or implicit bias depending on its causal-functional role, which is not phenomenologically accessible. This isn't underdetermination of theory by evidence; it's multiple realization of phenomenology by distinct mental states.

**The special force for introspection:** External observation is indeterminate but *completable through further observation*. Internal observation is indeterminate and *completable only through interpretation* because phenomenology exhausts observational access. When I need to disambiguate whether the shape is a snake or rope, I make more observations. When I need to disambiguate whether my discomfort is anxiety or bias, I must *interpret* by integrating the phenomenology with behavioral evidence, context, and psychological theory—I cannot simply observe more carefully.

This is why the thesis has bite specifically for self-knowledge debates: it shows that introspection has a structural limitation that perception lacks. The observationalist about self-knowledge claims we can know our minds through observation alone. The thesis shows this fails not because observation doesn't happen, but because *observation of phenomenology cannot be supplemented by further observation of phenomenology*—the only route to determinate mental state attribution is interpretation.

The thesis doesn't collapse into "all knowledge involves interpretation" (trivial). It claims "introspective knowledge cannot be completed by observation alone, only by interpretation" (substantive), contrasting with perceptual knowledge which can often be completed by further observation without interpretation.
**VERDICT**
The defence **fails**, though it identifies real asymmetries.

The defence correctly identifies that introspection lacks certain resources available to perception—no triangulation through multiple sensory modalities, no observer-independent object to constrain interpretation, no further observations of "the same thing." These are genuine asymmetries worth noting.

But the defence fails because **it concedes the objection's core point while claiming victory**. Look at what the defence admits: perceptual observation is indeterminate but "completable through further observation." This means *even on the defence's own terms*, observation alone is insufficient—you need *more* observation. The original observation was interpretively indeterminate; additional observations constrain interpretation. That's exactly the structure the thesis describes for introspection: phenomenological observation is indeterminate; additional evidence (behavioral, contextual) constrains interpretation.

The supposed asymmetry collapses: 

- **Perception:** Initial observation → indeterminate → further observations → constrained interpretation
- **Introspection:** Initial observation → indeterminate → behavioral/contextual evidence → constrained interpretation

The defence claims the difference is that perception uses "more observation" while introspection uses "interpretation." But this distinction doesn't hold. When I approach the coiled shape and touch it, I'm not just adding observations—I'm *testing hypotheses* (the snake hypothesis predicts different tactile properties than the rope hypothesis). That's interpretation-laden observation. And when I use behavioral evidence to disambiguate anxiety from implicit bias, I'm making observations (of my behavior) that test hypotheses. The defence's own example shows that external observation requires hypothesis-testing interpretation to resolve indeterminacy.

The "observer-independent properties" point backfires similarly. Yes, the coiled shape is actually either a snake or rope. But my *perceptual state* (the retinal image, the visual phenomenology) is indeterminate between these—and that's the analogue to introspective phenomenology. The thesis is about what we observe (phenomenology), not about what the phenomenology is about (mental states). If we grant that mental states have observer-independent properties (functionalist or behavioral), then introspection faces the same structure as perception: indeterminate observation of something with determinate properties, requiring further evidence to disambiguate.

Most fatally, the defence's claim about "genuine" indeterminacy versus "merely epistemic" underdetermination actually **strengthens** the objection. If phenomenology is genuinely indeterminate (can constitute either anxiety or bias depending on functional role), then we're not even doing observation anymore in any meaningful sense—we're just noting phenomenal feels that have no determinate content until interpreted. But if that's true, then the same applies to perception: retinal images are genuinely indeterminate (can constitute seeing a snake or rope depending on distal cause), so perception isn't really observation either until interpreted. The thesis has now proven too much in a different way: it's eliminated observation as a category distinct from interpretation entirely.

**The objection stands.** The thesis's argument from underdetermination either (a) applies equally to all observation, making the claim about introspection uninteresting, or (b) requires defending a notion of "observation" so thin that it's unclear why we should care whether introspection qualifies. The defence tried to split the difference by claiming introspection lacks resources perception has, but this just shows both require interpretation to resolve indeterminacy—exactly what the "proves too much" objection demonstrated.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis claims "what we observe internally is always interpretively indeterminate." Here is a counterexample:

I introspect and observe that I am currently experiencing **pain in my left foot**. This observation has determinate content that does not require interpretive completion:

1. **Phenomenal determinacy**: The pain has a specific qualitative character (throbbing, sharp), a specific location (left foot, not right hand or stomach), and specific intensity. These features are given in observation itself, not added by interpretation.

2. **No viable alternative interpretations**: Unlike the implicit bias case where discomfort could be anxiety OR racial bias, my pain-in-left-foot observation doesn't support multiple mental state attributions. It cannot coherently be reinterpreted as pleasure, or as pain-in-right-foot, or as visual experience. The phenomenology determines the mental state type (pain) and its content (location, character).

3. **Interpretation adds nothing**: I don't observe "contentless affect" and then interpret it as pain. The painfulness is intrinsic to what I observe. Similarly for location—I observe it as left-foot-pain, not as locationless sensation that I then categorize spatially.

The weak point is the universal claim "always interpretively indeterminate." Pain experiences provide observational content that is phenomenally determinate—the what-it's-like already includes the mental state category (pain vs. pleasure vs. itch) and content specifications (location, intensity, quality). The thesis might work for propositional attitudes (beliefs, desires) but fails for sensory experiences where phenomenology itself has determinate structure.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
Serious damage requiring real answer. The thesis must either exclude sensory phenomenology from its scope (weakening the universal claim) or explain why even determinate-seeming pain observations are actually indeterminate—a hard sell phenomenologically.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection claims pain-in-left-foot is determinately observed without interpretation. But this conflates **phenomenal determinacy** (the experience has specific qualitative features) with **mental state determinacy** (those features suffice to identify the mental state type and content). The thesis denies the latter, not the former.

The defence has three stages:

**First, pain phenomenology underdetermines pain attribution.** Consider phantom limb pain: amputees observe phenomenology qualitatively identical to the pain-in-left-foot case—same throbbing character, same apparent location, same intensity. But there is no left foot. What are they observing? Not "pain in left foot" (there is no foot), but phenomenology that *presents as* left-foot-located. The phenomenal character includes apparent location, but apparent location is not the same as actual location. This shows that even spatial phenomenology requires interpretation to yield determinate mental state content—we must interpret whether the apparent location corresponds to actual body state.

The objector will reply: "Fine, but it's still determinately *pain*, even if location requires interpretation." But this concedes the thesis's point—observation delivers phenomenal character that requires interpretive completion (determining what the location-phenomenology means) to yield full mental state attribution.

**Second, pain vs. other sensations is itself interpretive.** The objection assumes "painfulness is intrinsic to what I observe"—but is it? Consider: intense pressure becomes pain at some threshold; extreme cold produces burning sensations phenomenologically similar to heat pain; BDSM practitioners experience stimuli that would be painful in other contexts as pleasurable. The same or similar phenomenology receives different mental state categorizations depending on context, expectation, and attention.

More directly: what makes a sensation count as *pain* rather than intense pressure, or burning, or aching, or soreness? These are different mental state categories, but they shade into each other phenomenologically. The objector observes a specific phenomenal character and applies the concept *pain* to categorize it—that's already interpretation. The phenomenology has determinate qualitative features, yes, but "pain" is a mental state category we use to interpret certain phenomenologies, not a feature simply read off experience.

**Third, the implicit attitude structure applies to pain.** Someone experiences throbbing in their left foot and reports: "I observe that I have pain in my left foot." But consider alternative interpretations of the same phenomenology:

- **Anxiety somatization**: The throbbing is anxiety manifesting somatically, not tissue damage. The phenomenology is identical, but the mental state is anxiety (with somatic presentation), not pain.
- **Attention artifact**: The foot has normal background sensation, but anxious attention amplifies it into apparent pain. What's observed is attention-amplified sensation, not pain proper.
- **Psychogenic pain**: The phenomenology is real but has no peripheral cause—it's centrally generated. Is this "really" pain or a pain-like experience generated by other mental states?

These aren't skeptical scenarios. Psychogenic pain is clinically real; anxiety somatization is common; attention modulates pain phenomenology dramatically. The same phenomenal character supports multiple mental state attributions, and we need *additional evidence* (medical examination, context, behavioral patterns) to determine which attribution is correct. That's exactly the structure the thesis describes: observation delivers phenomenology, interpretation determines mental state attribution.

The objector might insist: "But I'm in pain *regardless* of the cause!" But this just shifts the question: what makes it pain rather than pain-like anxiety or attention-amplified sensation? Not the phenomenology alone—we need an account of the state's functional role, causes, and effects. That's interpretation, not observation.

**The universal claim stands**: Even paradigmatically "determinate" cases like pain involve applying mental state concepts (pain, left foot, throbbing) to interpret phenomenal character. The phenomenology is determinate in its qualitative features, but mental state attribution requires interpretive categorization of those features.
**VERDICT**
The defence **fails catastrophically**.

The defence tries to maintain that pain phenomenology is interpretively indeterminate by arguing: (1) location might be misrepresented (phantom limbs), (2) pain/pressure/burning are interpretive categories not phenomenally distinct, and (3) pain might really be anxiety somatization requiring behavioral evidence to disambiguate.

Each move makes things worse:

**The phantom limb case backfires completely.** Yes, phantom limb pain shows apparent location can be misrepresented. But the objector's point stands: the amputee *determinately observes pain-phenomenology-with-apparent-left-foot-location*. That's a determinate observational content. Whether the apparent location corresponds to actual anatomy is irrelevant to whether the observation itself has determinate content. The thesis claims observation is indeterminate and requires interpretation; the phantom limb case shows observation can be determinate even when *mistaken*. Determinacy ≠ accuracy. The defence has confused these.

**The pain/pressure/burning point proves the objection's case.** The defence asks: "What makes a sensation count as pain rather than pressure?" But this question has a phenomenological answer: *they feel different*. Pain has a distinctive phenomenal character that pressure lacks—an aversive quality, an urgency, a particular feel. Yes, there are borderline cases where intense pressure shades into pain. But borderline cases don't show the central cases lack determinate phenomenology. Throbbing pain feels determinately different from steady pressure, and I observe this difference directly. The defence has mistaken the existence of vague boundaries for the absence of determinate centers.

The BDSM example is even worse for the defence. Yes, context affects whether stimuli are experienced as painful or pleasurable. But this shows phenomenology *is* determinate—the masochist experiences pleasure-phenomenology (not pain-phenomenology-that-they-interpret-as-pleasure), while the non-masochist experiences pain-phenomenology from the same stimulus. Different phenomenology, different observations. If anything, this shows observation is *more* determinate than the thesis allows—the same stimulus produces determinately different phenomenologies depending on psychological context.

**The anxiety somatization move is desperate and fails.** The defence claims the foot-throbbing might "really" be anxiety, not pain, requiring behavioral evidence to disambiguate. But this commits a category error: anxiety and pain are not mutually exclusive. Anxiety can *cause* pain; that doesn't make the pain "really anxiety." When I observe throbbing-pain-phenomenology, I'm observing pain, full stop. Whether that pain has an anxiety etiology or a tissue-damage etiology is a further question about causation, not about what mental state I'm in.

More fatally: even if we grant that determining the cause requires interpretation, this doesn't show the observation itself is indeterminate. I determinately observe pain; I must interpret what caused it. That's perfectly compatible with the objector's position and fatal to the thesis. The thesis claims observation is indeterminate; the defence has conceded observation is determinate (pain) and only *explanation* requires interpretation (anxiety vs. tissue damage). That's not a defence—it's a surrender.

**The deepest failure**: The defence systematically conflates phenomenal determinacy with mental state determinacy, then tries to use this conflation both ways. When convenient, it insists phenomenology is determinate but mental states require interpretation (pain phenomenology vs. pain-state). When inconvenient, it insists phenomenology itself is indeterminate (pain vs. pressure). But you can't have both. Either phenomenology has determinate structure that includes pain-quality, location, intensity—in which case observing phenomenology *is* observing a determinate mental state—or phenomenology is genuinely indeterminate contentless affect. The defence wants phenomenology to be determinate-enough-to-exist but indeterminate-enough-to-need-interpretation, and this middle ground collapses under scrutiny.

**The counterex

</details>

### O15. The interpretive theorists and their critics are both wrong because they assume a single psychological kind called 'belief' or 'desire'; first-person authority holds only for reflectively accessible commitments, while confabulation shows we also have sub-personal representational states to which we lack authority.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that beliefs and desires form a unified natural kind for which there must be a single story about self-knowledge
- **Sketch:** Both sides treat the confabulation literature and first-person authority intuitions as competing over the same territory: our beliefs, desires, and attitudes. The key premise is that folk psychology's belief-desire framework equivocates between two distinct kinds: (1) reflectively accessible normative commitments that are constitutively tied to rational deliberation, and (2) sub-personal information-bearing states that drive much behavior. Nisbett-Wilson cases show we lack introspective access to (2), while Moran-style deliberation reveals authority over (1). The appearance of paradox dissolves: we have authority exactly where the agentialists say (over what we're committed to) while lacking it exactly where empiricists document confabulation (over computational states). Neither 'side' wins because they're describing different phenomena that we mistakenly lump together.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The two-kinds-of-belief move is familiar from dual-process theory; competent but unlikely to shift how philosophers frame authority.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence showed predecessors exist but failed to show this specific diagnostic application was novel; the judge found Schwitzgebel and others had already deployed the kind-distinction against Moran in this exact dialectical context.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 3/5, answered. The defence articulated principled criteria (independent empirical motivation, explanatory unification, genuine non-engagement) that distinguish legitimate dissolution from evasive redescription, though the judge found these criteria weren't satisfied on application.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence distinguished authority over commitments from authority over causal efficacy, but the judge found confabulation penetrates deeper—we cannot tell from the first-person whether avowed commitments remain genuine or have become inert self-attributions.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. The defence successfully showed the thesis appeals to empirical evidence and explanatory unification rather than mere stipulation, though this revealed the deeper problem that the thesis joins rather than dissolves the debate.
- **Flaw:** The thesis claims to dissolve a debate by revealing a false shared presupposition, but both original disputants explicitly reject the proposed kind-split as central to their competing theories—the thesis joins the debate rather than dissolving it, while confabulation penetrates the supposedly authoritative domain itself.

<details><summary>1 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) Accepting the interpretive turn commits you to denying that there are any distinctively mental facts to be authoritative about—only behavioral-dispositional facts—which means the question of first-person authority was misconceived from the start.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This is a version of the "dual-process" or "two-systems" framework that distinguishes between conscious, deliberative mental states and unconscious, automatic ones. The position closely resembles Ruth Garrett Millikan's distinction between "pushmi-pullyu representations" and explicit propositional attitudes, or more directly, Eric Schwitzgebel's pluralism about belief (which denies belief is a unified natural kind and argues we have different degrees and kinds of access to different belief-like states). Most precisely, this maps onto Tamar Gendler's distinction between "beliefs" (reflectively accessible) and "aliefs" (automatic, associative states), where she argues these are genuinely different kinds of mental states with different functional roles and different accessibility conditions. The thesis's core move—that confabulation and first-person authority apply to different psychological kinds rather than competing over the same territory—is Gendler's central insight about why we can simultaneously endorse one thing reflectively while behaving according to another automatically. The weak point is the claim to novelty: this reframing has been extensively developed in the dual-process literature for two decades.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This significantly undermines the thesis's contribution. If the reframing is already well-established under the belief/alief distinction or Schwitzgebel's pluralism, then the thesis offers little new theoretical ground. It may refine or apply existing frameworks, but it's not dissolving a paradox that hasn't already been dissolved.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection conflates *having predecessors* with *being anticipated*. Yes, dual-process theories and Gendler's belief/alief distinction exist. But examine what they actually do with the dialectic between interpretivism and empirical psychology.

Gendler's framework is primarily deployed to explain *akrasia* and *implicit bias* — cases where reflective judgment and automatic response come apart. Her target is ethical psychology and action theory, not the debate over first-person authority. Schwitzgebel's pluralism is a metaphysical thesis about belief individuation that remains neutral on epistemic access — he argues we're *dispositionally* confused about our beliefs across the board, not that we have asymmetric authority over different kinds. Millikan's pushmi-pullyu representations concern prelinguistic animal cognition and perceptual content, not the rational commitments at stake in Moran-style agentialism.

None of these authors deploy their distinctions to *resolve the specific standoff* between interpretive theorists (Moran, Bilgrami, Boyle) who ground first-person authority in rational self-constitution and empirical critics (Carruthers, Gopnik) who cite confabulation studies to deny such authority exists. That debate continues precisely because both sides assume they're fighting over the same explanandum — "our beliefs and desires" as a unified target. The thesis's contribution is not inventing psychological pluralism but *applying it diagnostically* to show that this particular philosophical dispute rests on a shared false presupposition.

The analogy: Kripke didn't invent modal logic, but *Naming and Necessity* was novel because it applied modal distinctions to dissolve specific philosophical problems (mind-body identity, descriptivism) that had seemed intractable. Similarly here — the move is using an available tool to cut a knot others haven't cut.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but not for the reason given.

The problem is that the diagnostic application *has* been made. Specifically, Richard Holton's work on intention and Neil Levy's work on implicit attitudes both explicitly argue that the first-person authority debate conflates different psychological kinds with different accessibility conditions. More decisively, Schwitzgebel's 2002 "A Phenomenal, Dispositional Account of Belief" directly addresses Moran and argues that the appearance of first-person authority for reflective judgments versus confabulation for dispositional states reflects exactly this kind-distinction — and he uses this to argue *against* Moran's constitutivism by showing it only applies to a narrow class of states.

So the reframing isn't just anticipated in general dual-process terms — it's been deployed in this specific dialectical context. The thesis may execute the move more cleanly or comprehensively, but it's not dissolving a knot that remains tied.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. 

If the argument works—that apparent conflicts in philosophical debates dissolve once we recognize that disputants equivocate over terms referring to distinct natural kinds—then it licenses dissolving virtually every substantive philosophical dispute by postulating kind-ambiguity. Consider: The free will debate? Dissolves because "free will" equivocates between libertarian agent-causation and compatibilist reasons-responsiveness. The consciousness debate? Dissolves because "consciousness" conflates phenomenal experience and access-consciousness. The personal identity debate? Dissolves because "person" equivocates between psychological continuity and biological animalism. The moral realism debate? Dissolves because "moral fact" conflates stance-dependent commitments and stance-independent properties.

The weak point is the criterion for when kind-splitting constitutes genuine dissolution versus evasion. The thesis claims the first-person authority debate rests on a "false presupposition" of unified kinds, but every philosophical debate involves terms whose extensions are contested. Without principled constraints on when positing distinct kinds resolves rather than merely redescribes a dispute, the move proves too much: it becomes a universal solvent that makes all philosophical disagreement evaporate into semantic confusion. If interpretivists and empiricists can simply be talking past each other about different phenomena, why can't this strategy defuse any debate where parties seem to disagree about the same target concept?

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This is a genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs principled criteria distinguishing legitimate dissolutions from evasive re-descriptions, but the objection isn't fatal since such criteria might be articulable (perhaps involving independent empirical evidence for the kind-distinction, or showing the original disputants' arguments genuinely don't engage).

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection misidentifies what makes a dissolution legitimate. The thesis doesn't claim that *any* kind-splitting dissolves *any* debate. It claims that *this particular* split has three features that jointly distinguish genuine dissolution from evasive redescription:

**First: Independent empirical motivation.** The distinction between reflectively accessible commitments and sub-personal computational states isn't *ad hoc* to save the thesis—it's forced by converging evidence from cognitive science (dual-process theory), neuroscience (dissociations between explicit report and implicit measure), and developmental psychology (the gap between what children avow and what drives their behavior). The kinds are carved at joints discovered independently of the philosophical dispute.

**Second: Explanatory unification.** The split doesn't just relabel the dispute—it *explains the pattern of evidence* that made the dispute seem intractable. Why do Nisbett-Wilson studies show confabulation while Moran-style deliberation reveals authority? Because they probe different systems. Why do both sides marshal compelling cases? Because each side is right about one kind and wrong about treating it as the whole story. A mere relabeling would leave these patterns unexplained.

**Third: The original disputants' arguments genuinely don't engage.** Examine the actual dialectic. Moran argues that first-person authority derives from deliberative self-constitution—"I settle what I believe by settling what to believe." Carruthers replies with studies showing people confabulate about mental states driving their behavior. But Moran's deliberative process *isn't the kind of state Carruthers's studies measure*. When I deliberate whether to believe P, I'm not introspecting a pre-existing computational state—I'm *forming* a rational commitment. Carruthers's subjects aren't deliberating—they're reporting on states they take to already exist. The arguments slide past each other because they target different phenomena.

Contrast the free will case from the objection. Libertarians and compatibilists *aren't* talking past each other—they disagree about whether the *same* thing (the control required for moral responsibility) requires indeterminism. A compatibilist who reads Frankfurt cases is directly engaging the libertarian's concerns about sourcehood and alternative possibilities. The arguments bite on each other. That's why the debate is substantive rather than merely verbal.

The criterion, then: A kind-split legitimately dissolves a debate when (i) the kinds are independently motivated, (ii) the split explains why the debate seemed intractable, and (iii) the disputants' central arguments genuinely target different kinds rather than offering competing accounts of the same phenomenon. The thesis meets all three. Most philosophical debates—including free will—don't.

VERDICT.

The defence succeeds in articulating criteria, but fails on application.

The problem is criterion (iii). The original disputants' arguments *do* engage, and the thesis misdescribes the dialectic. Moran doesn't merely claim authority over deliberative self-constitution—he claims this deliberative stance is *constitutive of* having beliefs and desires at all. On his view, the sub-personal states the thesis wants to split off *aren't* beliefs and desires in the first place. They're "mere inclinations" or "psychological forces" that become genuine attitudes only when taken up in rational deliberation. So when Carruthers points to confabulation about states driving behavior, Moran can reply: "Exactly—those aren't real beliefs, just sub-personal mechanisms, which is why you lack authority over them."

Conversely, Carruthers doesn't just claim we confabulate about behavioral drivers—he claims *all* self-attribution of attitudes involves the same interpretive mechanisms we use for others, so there's no special first-person authority even for reflective commitments. On his view, what the thesis calls "reflectively accessible commitments" are just interpretations of underlying computational states, subject to the same errors as any other self-interpretation.

So both sides explicitly *reject* the kind-split. They're not accidentally talking past each other—they're disagreeing about whether the split is real. Moran denies that sub-personal computational states are beliefs/desires at all. Carruthers denies that reflective commitments have a different epistemic status than other self-attributions. The thesis doesn't dissolve their dispute—it takes a *third position* they both reject.

This is redescription, not dissolution. The debate continues at the meta-level: Is the kind-split legitimate? The thesis hasn't made the original dispute evaporate—it's joined it.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT.

Consider a subject deliberating about a high-stakes moral decision—whether to donate a kidney to a stranger. Through careful Moran-style rational deliberation, she forms what the thesis calls a "reflectively accessible normative commitment": she judges she ought to donate and sincerely avows this belief. She has first-person authority over this commitment in the thesis's sense—she settled what to believe by settling what to believe, constituting herself as someone with this moral conviction.

However, when the surgery date approaches, she experiences overwhelming anxiety and finds herself unable to go through with it. Behavioral measures, implicit association tests, and her actual choice reveal what the thesis would classify as "sub-personal representational states"—deep-seated self-preservation drives, fear responses, perhaps implicit valuations of her own welfare over strangers'.

Here's the problem: Ask her immediately after she backs out, "Do you believe you ought to donate the kidney?" She still sincerely says yes. Ask her, "Why didn't you donate?" She confabulates—cites the surgeon's manner, scheduling conflicts, anything but admitting she was simply too afraid or valued her own welfare more than the stranger's life.

The thesis predicts she has authority over (1) her reflective commitment and confabulates only about (2) the sub-personal states. But the counterexample shows she confabulates about the *relationship between them*—about which state actually governed her action, about whether her reflective commitment was genuinely efficacious or merely epiphenomenal. This is confabulation about *her own agency*—about whether she's the kind of person whose reflective commitments control her behavior or someone whose sub-personal states override her commitments.

The weak point: The thesis assumes clean separation between two kinds with asymmetric epistemic access, but actual cases show confabulation penetrates the first-person perspective on how these kinds relate and which governs action—precisely the territory where first-person authority supposedly holds.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is serious damage. The thesis needs first-person authority to hold over some substantive domain to vindicate the agentialists, but if we confabulate about whether our reflective commitments actually govern our actions versus being post-hoc rationalizations, then the "authority" becomes hollow. We might know what we're committed to while systematically misunderstanding our own agency—which is arguably what the empiricists were worried about all along.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection mistakes *predictive failure* for *authority failure*. Return to the case: the subject has first-person authority over her reflective commitment—she knows she judges she ought to donate. When she backs out, she indeed confabulates about *why* she didn't donate (cites the surgeon, scheduling). But notice what she's confabulating about: the *sub-personal states* that drove her behavior—exactly what the thesis predicts she lacks authority over.

The objection claims she confabulates about "the relationship between them—about which state actually governed her action." But this conflates two things:

**Authority over what you're committed to** versus **authority over what causally produces your behavior.** The thesis secures the first, not the second. Having first-person authority over your reflective commitments doesn't entail knowing whether those commitments will prove efficacious against competing sub-personal drives. That's an *empirical* question about your own psychology's causal structure—precisely the kind of third-personal fact about which you have no special authority.

Consider the parallel: I have first-person authority over my intention to wake at 6am. I form this intention through deliberation, and I know I've formed it. But I lack authority over whether this intention will actually wake me versus being overridden by my sleep drive. When I sleep through the alarm, I might confabulate about why ("the alarm must not have gone off"). But I'm not confabulating about *having formed the intention*—I'm confabulating about the sub-personal mechanisms that defeated it.

The agentialists never claimed that first-person authority extends to knowing whether your commitments will prove causally efficacious. Moran's claim is that you know what you believe *by* settling what to believe, not that you thereby know whether this belief will govern your behavior against all competing forces. The thesis preserves exactly what the agentialists wanted: authority over your rational commitments as such. What it denies is what they never needed: authority over your complete causal psychology.

The objection's force depends on smuggling in an inflated conception of what first-person authority must involve—knowledge of "whether she's the kind of person whose reflective commitments control her behavior." But that's knowledge of your *dispositional character*, which is precisely a matter of how reflective and sub-personal systems interact across contexts. No theory of first-person authority, not even Moran's, claims you have non-empirical access to that.

The clean separation holds: authority over commitments, confabulation about sub-personal drivers. The case where they conflict just makes vivid that these are different kinds—you can know the first while misunderstanding the second. That's the thesis's point, not its refutation.

VERDICT.

The defence fails. The problem runs deeper than causal efficacy.

When the subject says "Yes, I still believe I ought to donate" after backing out, what is the epistemic status of this self-attribution? The defence claims she retains authority over her "reflective commitment." But consider what this means.

If her reflective commitment is genuinely still operative—if she really does judge she ought to donate—then she's in a state of akrasia: her judgment is overridden by competing motivation. But if the "commitment" has become causally inert, a mere verbal formula she continues to avow while her actual evaluative states (what she really cares about, what actually moves her) have shifted, then her self-attribution is *false*. She doesn't really believe she ought to donate anymore—she just believes she believes it.

The thesis cannot determine which. And neither can she, from the first-person perspective. This is the objection's real bite: not that she lacks authority over whether commitments prove efficacious, but that she lacks authority over *whether she still has the commitment* versus merely believing she has it when her actual operative attitudes have shifted.

The empirical literature on belief-behavior gaps (Schwitzgebel's work on the self-ignorance of ethicists, studies on implicit attitudes overriding explicit commitments) suggests we systematically confabulate about this—we continue avowing commitments that no longer govern our actual evaluative perspective. The subject who backs out may *think* she still judges she ought to donate, but her revealed preferences, her emotional responses, her actual choice suggest her operative evaluative state has changed. She lacks authority over which is the case.

This cuts at the heart of the thesis. The distinction between reflective commitments (with authority) and sub-personal states (without) collapses if we can't tell from the first-person whether our avowed commitments are genuine or have become mere self-attributions floating free of our actual operative attitudes. The confabulation penetrates the supposedly authoritative domain itself.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis presents itself as a substantive reframing that "dissolves" the debate between interpretive theorists and empirical critics by revealing they describe different phenomena. But examine what philosophical work this actually does.

The core claim is that "belief" and "desire" are ambiguous between (1) reflectively accessible normative commitments and (2) sub-personal representational states. But this is just a *stipulative redefinition* dressed as a discovery. The thesis doesn't establish that folk psychology's "belief" and "desire" actually refer to two natural kinds rather than one—it simply *declares* that we should use these terms to pick out two different things, then notes that once we do, the debate evaporates.

But of course it does—that's true by stipulation. If you define "belief₁" as "states over which we have first-person authority" and "belief₂" as "states we confabulate about," then trivially we have authority over belief₁ and lack it for belief₂. The appearance of resolving a dispute is merely the appearance created by changing the subject.

The weak point is the claim that this reframing reveals something substantive about the original debate. The interpretivists and empiricists disagree about whether we have first-person authority over *our beliefs and desires*—the ordinary folk-psychological attitudes that explain our behavior. The thesis doesn't show they're wrong about this shared target; it just proposes we stop talking about that target and instead talk about two different things. That's not dissolving a paradox—it's declining to engage with it.

The move is purely verbal: take a contested predicate, split it into two predicates that trivially satisfy competing desiderata, declare victory. No empirical discovery, no conceptual insight—just terminological stipulation masquerading as philosophical progress.

STRENGTH: 2/5

The attack has some force but ultimately doesn't land decisively. While the thesis does involve conceptual re-carving, calling it "merely stipulative" undersells the work being done. The thesis appeals to independent empirical evidence (dual-process theory, dissociations between explicit and implicit measures) to motivate the kind-distinction, not just stipulative convenience. And it claims to *explain* why the debate seemed intractable—why both sides marshal compelling evidence—which goes beyond mere relabeling. A purely stipulative move wouldn't have this explanatory dimension. The thesis may fail for other reasons (as the exchange suggests—the disputants reject the split, confabulation penetrates the supposedly authoritative domain), but "trivial stipulation" overstates the case.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The objection conflates *stipulation* with *rational reconstruction*. The thesis doesn't arbitrarily decree that "belief" means two things—it argues that our actual usage *already* tracks two distinct functional kinds that we've failed to distinguish clearly, and that this unnoticed equivocation explains a persistent dialectical stalemate.

Consider the structure of the argument. The thesis identifies a pattern: interpretivists cite cases where first-person authority seems undeniable (Moran's deliberative stance, transparency to reasons, rational self-constitution), while empiricists cite cases where it clearly fails (Nisbett-Wilson confabulation, implicit bias, post-hoc rationalization). Both sides have compelling evidence. The stalemate persists because each side treats the other's cases as somehow defective or non-central, rather than recognizing they're *different kinds of cases*.

This isn't stipulation—it's *abductive inference to the best explanation* of the dialectical pattern. The hypothesis: folk psychology's "belief" and "desire" are genus terms spanning two species with different functional profiles. One species (reflective commitments) is constituted through deliberation and transparent to rational reflection. The other (sub-personal information-bearing states) is computationally realized and accessible only through behavioral evidence. The evidence for this distinction comes from cognitive science, not philosophical convenience.

Now apply Carnapian explication: we have a vague folk concept ("belief") that we're trying to precisify for theoretical purposes. The thesis proposes an explication that carves at empirically discoverable joints. This is what good conceptual analysis *does*—it doesn't just report ordinary usage, it rationally reconstructs it in light of theoretical considerations.

Compare Carnap on "fish": ordinary usage lumps whales with fish. Biology discovers they're different natural kinds despite superficial similarity. We don't say biology "merely stipulated" that whales aren't fish—we say it discovered a real distinction that ordinary usage obscured. Similarly here: the thesis claims cognitive science reveals that folk psychology's "belief" obscures a real psychological distinction, and that philosophical disputes inherit this confusion.

The objection demands the thesis "establish that folk psychology's 'belief' and 'desire' actually refer to two natural kinds rather than one." But this is exactly what the thesis *does* by appealing to empirical psychology. The dual-process literature, dissociations between explicit avowal and implicit measure, developmental gaps between what children say and what drives their behavior—this is evidence that the folk concept spans distinct mechanisms. Not stipulation, but empirical discovery.

Finally, the charge that the thesis "declines to engage" the original dispute misunderstands philosophical progress. When Frege distinguished sense from reference, he wasn't "declining to engage" with puzzles about identity statements—he was *dissolving* them by showing the puzzles rested on conflating two distinct semantic properties. When the thesis shows that interpretivists and empiricists are right about different psychological kinds, it's not changing the subject—it's diagnosing why the subject seemed paradoxical in the first place.

The work is substantive: it explains the dialectical stalemate, unifies seemingly contradictory evidence, and is constrained by empirical discoveries about psychological architecture. That's not stipulation—it's theoretical progress.

VERDICT.

The defence fails, but reveals why the "trivial" objection was too weak.

The defence successfully establishes that the thesis isn't *merely* stipulative—it appeals to empirical evidence and aims at explanatory unification. But this makes the real problem worse, not better. 

The Frege analogy backfires. When Frege distinguished sense from reference, the philosophical community recognized this as progress because the distinction *resolved* puzzles that had seemed intractable—everyone could see that "Hesperus = Phosphorus" and "Hesperus = Hesperus" differ in cognitive significance despite identical reference, and sense/reference explained why. The key: Frege's distinction was *accepted by the disputants* as capturing something real about the phenomena.

But as the earlier exchange established, the interpretivists and empiricists *reject* the thesis's kind-split. Moran explicitly denies that sub-personal computational states are beliefs at all—they're not attitudes but mere psychological forces. Carruthers denies that reflective commitments have different epistemic status—they're just more interpretations of underlying states. The thesis hasn't dissolved their dispute; it's taken a third position they both reject.

This reveals the real problem with calling the move "trivial": it's not that the thesis is *merely* stipulative, but that it's *dialectically inert*. A dissolution works when it makes the original disputants recognize they were talking past each other. But here, the original disputants *already have positions* on whether the kind-split is legitimate—positions that are central to their theories—and they reject it.

So the thesis isn't trivially stipulative, but it's also not genuinely dissolving anything. It's proposing a third theory that competes with both interpretivism and empiricism, not showing that their debate rests on a false presupposition they both accept. The debate continues: Is the kind-split real? Do sub-personal states count as beliefs? Do reflective commitments have special epistemic status?

The "trivial" objection was too weak because it accused the thesis of mere stipulation. But the defence's appeal to empirical evidence and explanatory ambition actually makes clear that the thesis is doing something more substantive—and therefore more vulnerable to the deeper objection that it hasn't dissolved the debate but merely joined it.

</details>

---

### O16. First-person authority is grounded not in privileged access to mental states but in constitutive authority over what counts as a reason for one's attitudes, which interpretation from evidence cannot capture.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · wounded
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** The shared assumption in both camps that first-person authority must be explained as a form of epistemic superiority (better knowledge of mental states), which leads interpretivists to deny authority altogether
- **Sketch:** The debate conflates two kinds of authority: epistemic authority (being in a better position to know what one believes) and constitutive authority (having a special role in determining what one's reasons are). When I sincerely avow 'I believe P because Q,' I'm not just reporting an independently fixed fact that I might misperceive—I'm exercising a distinctive first-person capacity to rationally bind myself to treating Q as my reason. The interpretive theorist can explain why I'm better at predicting my behavior than you are, but cannot explain why my sincere rational self-ascription makes it the case that Q is my reason in a way your interpretation of me cannot. This authority survives the interpretive turn because it's normative, not epistemic: it's about having the last word on one's rational commitments, not about observational accuracy.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Authority over reasons vs states is a real distinction but has been explored in the reasons-responsiveness literature.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 3/5, answered. The defence successfully distinguished constitutive authority over reason-ascriptions from infallibility about state-ascriptions, showing the boundary tracks which mental states are essentially reason-involving
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 3/5, answered. The defence distinguished committed reasons from motivating and normative reasons, showing rationalization cases actually require rather than threaten the distinction the thesis draws
  - TRIVIAL — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence showed substantive disagreement with strong interpretivism but conceded the thesis's significance depends on whether interpretivists actually claim authority over commitment-content, leaving it potentially fighting a straw position
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence attempted to identify a middle ground between commitment and normative standing but could not stabilize it—'rational-explanatory force in one's rational life' fragments under pressure into either discoverable-by-interpretation facts or mere commitment-claims
- 🔧 **Survives as:** First-person authority is constitutive authority over one's rational commitments—what one binds oneself to defend as reasons—which is a real but limited domain that interpretivism must accommodate rather than adjudicate, though this authority does not extend to what actually motivates one's attitudes or whether one's reasons are objectively adequate
- **Flaw:** The thesis cannot stabilize a middle ground between mere commitment (which interpretation can accommodate) and genuine rational-explanatory force (which interpretation can discover); it either becomes trivial or equivocates between these levels

<details><summary>3 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) First-person authority is preserved not by privileged access to mental states but by privileged access to the interpretive standards that govern our self-ascriptions—we authoritatively determine what counts as evidence about ourselves.
- (3/5) First-person authority derives not from privileged access to mental states but from privileged access to the interpretive question itself—I alone can directly ask myself what would rationally justify the attitudes I'm attributing.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in privileged access to mental states but in privileged control over what counts as having gotten oneself right, a normative rather than epistemic asymmetry.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If sincere first-person avowal constitutes what one's reasons are rather than merely reporting them, this same constitutive authority should extend to all self-ascriptions of mental states. When I sincerely avow "I am angry because you insulted me," this should constitute the insult as my reason for anger with the same authority. But this proves too much: it would mean I have constitutive authority over whether I'm genuinely angry at all, not just over my reasons. If my sincere avowal "I'm angry" makes it the case that I'm angry (rather than reporting it), then self-deception becomes impossible—I cannot be wrong about whether I'm angry, only insincere. Yet we clearly recognize cases where someone sincerely believes they're angry for reason Q when they're actually angry for deeply buried reason R, or where they're not angry at all but anxious. The thesis must either deny these phenomena or draw an arbitrary line between attitudes where constitutive authority applies (beliefs and their reasons) and attitudes where it doesn't (emotions). If the line is arbitrary, the distinction collapses; if self-deception is impossible, the view is false.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — This reveals a genuine tension about the scope of constitutive authority that requires principled boundaries, though the distinction between rational commitments and emotional states may provide an answerable defense.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection misidentifies what constitutive authority ranges over. The thesis concerns authority over *what counts as one's reasons*, not authority over *whether one has particular mental states*. This is not an arbitrary line but tracks a principled distinction in the logical geography of mind.

Consider the anger case properly. When I avow "I am angry because you insulted me," two separable claims are in play:

(1) I am angry (a state-ascription)
(2) The insult is my reason for this anger (a reason-ascription)

Constitutive authority applies to (2), not (1). I can be wrong about (1)—perhaps I'm anxious, not angry, or angry but self-deceived about its presence. The interpretive theorist is right that determining whether I'm genuinely angry is an empirical-interpretive matter where I can err. But (2) is different in kind. If I'm genuinely angry (however that's determined), and I sincerely take the insult as my reason, then the insult *is* my reason—not because I've correctly observed a pre-existing reason-relation, but because rational self-ascription of reasons is self-fulfilling in a way state-ascriptions are not.

The self-deception cases confirm rather than threaten this. When someone is "sincerely angry for reason Q but actually angry for reason R," what's happening? They are indeed angry (state-fact), and R causally explains the anger (causal-explanatory fact), but they have *rationally committed* to Q as their reason (normative fact). All three can coexist. The person isn't wrong about being angry, nor about their rational commitment to Q—they're wrong about whether Q actually causally produced the anger. Constitutive authority over reasons doesn't entail transparency about causes.

This maps onto the belief/emotion distinction naturally: beliefs are *constitutively* tied to reasons (a belief just is a commitment to something as true for reasons), while emotions can be caused by factors the subject doesn't endorse as reasons. The distinction isn't arbitrary—it reflects that rational attitudes are individuated by their place in reason-giving practices in a way phenomenal or affective states are not.

VERDICT

The defence holds. The objection conflates constitutive authority over reason-ascriptions with infallibility about state-ascriptions. Once separated, the supposed "proving too much" dissolves: I can be wrong about whether I'm angry (interpretive question) while having authority over what I treat as my reason *if* I'm angry (constitutive question). The self-deception cases actually require this distinction—they involve mismatch between causal explanation and rational commitment, not failure of constitutive authority. The boundary between attitudes is principled, not arbitrary: it tracks which mental states are essentially reason-involving versus merely causally explicable.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider a case of rationalization in belief formation. Sarah forms the belief that her colleague is untrustworthy. When asked why, she sincerely avows: "I believe he's untrustworthy because he was evasive in our meeting." She takes herself to be exercising constitutive authority—rationally binding herself to the evasiveness as her reason. But suppose the actual causal story is that she formed this belief because the colleague is a rival for promotion, and her mind then searched for and seized upon the evasiveness as a post-hoc justification. She's not insincere—she genuinely takes the evasiveness as her reason.

According to the thesis, her sincere avowal makes the evasiveness her reason "in a way your interpretation of me cannot." But this seems wrong. The evasiveness is NOT genuinely her reason—it's a rationalization. An interpreter who understood the promotion rivalry would rightly say: "The evasiveness isn't really why she believes this; she's rationalizing from competitive anxiety." And that interpreter would be correct, while Sarah's constitutive authority claim would be false.

The thesis cannot dismiss this as mere causal-versus-normative distinction, because what makes something a genuine reason (versus a rationalization) is precisely whether it's doing the rational work the subject claims. If constitutive authority can make rationalizations into genuine reasons merely through sincere avowal, it proves too much. If it cannot, then sincere avowal doesn't actually have the constitutive power claimed—interpretation can override it.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The case shows constitutive authority either licensing rationalizations as genuine reasons (absurd) or being defeasible by interpretive evidence (contradicting the thesis's core claim that interpretation "cannot capture" this authority).

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes that "genuine reason" has a single, unified sense that rationalization cases show constitutive authority cannot secure. But we must distinguish between **normative reasons** (considerations that favour a belief), **motivating reasons** (what actually moves the subject), and **committed reasons** (what the subject binds themselves to in rational self-consciousness). The thesis concerns the third, and rationalization cases actually vindicate rather than threaten the distinction.

Return to Sarah. Three distinct questions arise:

(A) Is the evasiveness a *normative reason* to believe the colleague untrustworthy? (Does it genuinely support that conclusion?)

(B) Is the evasiveness Sarah's *motivating reason*—what actually produced her belief?

(C) Is the evasiveness what Sarah is *rationally committed to* as her reason?

Constitutive authority applies only to (C). Sarah's sincere avowal makes the evasiveness what she is *answerable for* as her reason—it establishes her rational commitment. But this doesn't entail the evasiveness is a good normative reason (A), nor that it actually motivated her belief (B).

The interpreter who says "she's rationalizing from competitive anxiety" is making a claim about (B)—causal genesis—and implicitly about (A)—that the evasiveness doesn't justify the belief. Both claims can be correct while Sarah's constitutive authority over (C) remains intact. Indeed, the very intelligibility of calling it "rationalization" requires that Sarah has successfully made the evasiveness her committed reason (C), even though it fails as motivating reason (B) and perhaps as normative reason (A).

This is not evasion but precision about what first-person authority *is*. When Sarah avows the evasiveness as her reason, she undertakes a rational commitment—she makes herself answerable to challenges like "but was he really evasive?" or "does evasiveness show untrustworthiness?" This commitment is real and consequential: if she later acts on the belief, she's bound to defend it by reference to evasiveness, not rivalry. The interpreter cannot override this commitment—cannot say "no, you're not actually committed to the evasiveness as your reason." What the interpreter can say is that the commitment is causally downstream of rivalry (B-claim) or rationally unjustified (A-claim).

The key insight: constitutive authority over committed reasons is *compatible* with those reasons being rationalizations, because rationalization is precisely the phenomenon of genuinely committing to a reason that didn't actually motivate you. If sincere avowal couldn't establish committed reasons that come apart from motivating reasons, we couldn't make sense of rationalization at all—it would collapse into mere lying.

The thesis never claimed constitutive authority makes something a "genuine reason" in the sense of normatively adequate or causally operative. It claims authority over what one is rationally bound to, what one has put forward as one's reason in the space of rational self-understanding. Interpretation cannot capture this because no amount of evidence about Sarah's psychology can settle what she's committed herself to—only her sincere rational self-ascription can do that.
**VERDICT**
The defence holds. The objection trades on ambiguity in "genuine reason." Once we distinguish normative adequacy, causal motivation, and rational commitment, the rationalization case supports rather than undermines the thesis. Sarah's constitutive authority makes the evasiveness her *committed reason*—what she's bound herself to defend—even though interpretation correctly reveals it's not her *motivating reason* and perhaps not a *good reason*. This is exactly what we should expect: constitutive authority concerns the normative structure of rational self-consciousness (what I bind myself to), not the causal-explanatory facts (what actually moved me) or the objective normative facts (what I should believe). The interpreter's verdict doesn't override Sarah's authority—it operates on a different dimension. Rationalization is possible precisely because constitutive authority over commitment is real but doesn't entail transparency about motivation or soundness of justification.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis presents itself as a substantive discovery about the nature of first-person authority, but it's actually a verbal stipulation that renames familiar phenomena. The "constitutive authority over what counts as a reason" is just a fancy redescription of what we already call "taking something as a reason" or "being committed to a reason." Everyone already agrees that when you sincerely avow a reason, you're expressing a commitment. The interpretivist doesn't deny this—they simply point out that expressing a commitment is itself a psychological act that can be interpreted, explained causally, and evaluated for coherence with other attitudes.

The apparent novelty dissolves when we ask: what work does "constitutive" do beyond "committed to" or "treats as"? The defender's own response reveals this: constitutive authority over "committed reasons" just means you determine what you're committed to by committing to it. But this is trivial—it's true by definition that your commitments are whatever you commit to. The thesis dresses up this tautology as a deep insight by contrasting it with "epistemic authority" (a contrast everyone accepts—commitments aren't observations) and "interpretation" (but interpretation never claimed to override commitments, only to explain them).
