The thesis isn't false—it's uncontested. It's like announcing "promises aren't descriptions of pre-existing obligations but constitutive speech acts that create obligations." True, but we knew that. The philosophical action is in explaining how these commitments relate to reasons, rationality, and self-knowledge—questions the thesis simply brackets with its stipulative distinction.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This attack does serious damage. The thesis survives only if "constitutive authority" marks a genuinely contested position rather than a redescription everyone accepts. The defender's careful distinctions between normative/motivating/committed reasons, while coherent, actually reveal that the thesis is claiming authority over something trivial (that your commitments are what you commit to) while disclaiming authority over anything substantial (whether those commitments are justified, causally efficacious, or transparent to interpretation). The interpretive theorist already grants that subjects express commitments—they just deny this is mysterious or beyond interpretation's reach.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misses the dialectical target. The thesis isn't claiming to discover that people make commitments—it's arguing that *this* is what first-person authority consists in, against a field that either (a) tries to ground it in epistemic privilege that interpretivism refutes, or (b) concludes from interpretivism's success that first-person authority is illusory. The thesis is revisionary, not trivial, because it rejects the dominant framing entirely.

Consider the actual dialectical situation. Traditional views (Cartesian, inner sense, etc.) ground first-person authority in privileged epistemic access: I know my beliefs better than you do because I can introspect them directly while you must infer them. Interpretivists like Dennett rightly attack this: interpretation from behavior is often more reliable than introspection, there's no "Cartesian theater," self-reports are just more data for interpretation. The standard conclusion: first-person authority is at best pragmatic convenience, at worst illusion.

The thesis intervenes by denying the shared premise: that first-person authority must be *epistemic* authority. The "constitutive" move isn't trivial redescription—it's a category shift that relocates the phenomenon from epistemology to normativity. The substantive claim is that first-person authority *isn't* about knowing your mental states better (the interpretivists win that battle), but about having a distinctive role in *fixing* what your rational commitments are, a role no amount of third-person interpretation can play.

Why isn't this trivial? Because the interpretivist position, properly understood, doesn't merely "explain commitments"—it claims to *determine* them. When Dennett says your beliefs are whatever the best interpretation says they are, he's not just offering a causal explanation of your commitments; he's saying there's no fact about what you believe beyond what interpretation delivers. The thesis denies this: when you sincerely avow "I believe P because Q," you're not reporting what interpretation would discover—you're *making it the case* that Q is your committed reason in a way that's not reducible to being the best interpretation of your behavior.

The proof it's not trivial: the interpretivist must either (1) accept that sincere rational avowal has this constitutive role, which means interpretation doesn't exhaust mental content (contradicting strong interpretivism), or (2) insist that what you're "really" committed to is whatever interpretation says, which means your sincere avowal could be simply mistaken about your own commitments (not just about causes or justification, but about what you've bound yourself to). Position (2) is the substantive denial the thesis opposes.

Here's the non-trivial work "constitutive" does: it marks that sincere rational self-ascription is *not answerable to* interpretive facts in the way beliefs about external matters are answerable to evidence. When I avow "I believe P because Q," I'm not making a fallible report about what interpretation would discover about my reasons—I'm undertaking a commitment that interpretation must accommodate, not adjudicate. This is substantive because it limits interpretation's authority in a way interpretivists resist.

The objection claims "interpretation never claimed to override commitments, only to explain them." But this concedes the game. If interpretation must treat sincere rational avowal as constitutive of commitment rather than evidence about it, then first-person authority is real and substantive—it's authority over a domain (rational commitment) that interpretation cannot penetrate. The thesis wins by getting the interpretivist to grant that commitments are what the subject makes them, not what interpretation discovers them to be.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds, but barely. The thesis survives triviality because there *is* a genuine dispute: strong interpretivism (Dennett-style) holds that mental content is exhausted by what best interpretation attributes, which means your sincere avowal is just more data that could be outweighed. The thesis denies this for rational commitments specifically. This is substantive.

However, the objection exposes a real weakness: the thesis's significance depends entirely on whether interpretivists actually deny constitutive authority over commitments. If sophisticated interpretivists already grant that sincere avowal fixes what one is committed to (while interpreting why one made that commitment, whether it's justified, etc.), then the thesis becomes a friendly amendment rather than a revision. The defender's claim that "the interpretivist position... claims to *determine* commitments" needs textual support. If interpretivists like Dennett only claim to determine what beliefs you have (state-ascriptions) but not what you've committed to treating as reasons (commitment-ascriptions), the thesis may be fighting a straw position.

The defence holds because the distinction between constitutive and epistemic authority does real work *if* interpretivists claim authority over commitment-content. But the thesis's importance is hostage to whether that's what interpretivists actually claim. The objection successfully shows that the thesis could collapse into triviality depending on how the opponent is characterized.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis equivocates on "what counts as a reason for one's attitudes." On one reading, this means **what one is rationally committed to treating as a reason** (what one avows, what one is prepared to defend, what structures one's rational self-understanding). On this reading, the claim that sincere avowal has constitutive authority is plausible—perhaps even trivial, as the previous attack showed. When I sincerely say "I believe P because Q," I do establish what I'm committed to defending.

On another reading, "what counts as a reason" means **what actually bears the rational relation to the attitude** (what genuinely justifies it, what makes it reasonable, what one epistemically ought to treat as a reason). On this reading, the thesis is interesting and substantive—it would mean I have authority to make something *actually* a good reason just by avowing it, which would be a remarkable power indeed.

The argument needs both readings. The thesis gains its critical force (against interpretivism) from the second reading: interpretation cannot determine what genuinely counts as a reason for my belief because I have special authority here. But the thesis is defensible only on the first reading: I have authority over what I'm committed to, not over what's actually a good reason (as the defender conceded when distinguishing "committed reasons" from "normative reasons").

The phrase "what counts as a reason" slides between these meanings. When the thesis says interpretation "cannot capture" this authority, it trades on the normative-evaluative sense (interpretation cannot settle what's really a reason). But when defending against counterexamples, it retreats to the commitment sense (I determine what I'm bound to defend). The argument cannot work on either reading alone.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This does serious damage. The defender's own careful distinctions between "normative reasons," "motivating reasons," and "committed reasons" reveal that the thesis systematically conflates rational commitment (plausible constitutive authority) with rational standing (interesting constitutive authority). The phrase "what counts as a reason" appears to bridge these, but cannot do so consistently. On the commitment reading, the thesis is defensible but loses its force against interpretivism (which can perfectly well interpret commitments). On the normative reading, the thesis is interesting but indefensible (I cannot make something a good reason just by avowing it).

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes "what counts as a reason" must mean either subjective commitment or objective normative standing, and that equivocating between them is the only way to make the thesis work. But there's a third, unified sense that the thesis actually targets: **what has rational-explanatory force in the subject's rational life**—what figures in explaining their rational transitions, what makes their belief rationally intelligible as theirs, what constitutes their operative rational economy.

This is neither mere subjective commitment (which could be idle or self-deceived) nor objective normative standing (which is independent of the subject). It's the domain of *personal* rationality—the reasons that actually structure a particular person's rational agency, whether or not they're objectively good reasons.

Consider: when we explain why Sarah believes her colleague is untrustworthy by citing her reasons, we're doing something different from both (a) merely reporting what she's committed to saying, and (b) evaluating whether her reasons are objectively good. We're explaining her belief as *rational for her*—as flowing from her rational perspective, as making sense given her take on things. This is the space where first-person authority operates.

The thesis claims constitutive authority over this middle ground. When I sincerely avow "I believe P because Q," I'm not just committing to defend by reference to Q (too weak), nor making Q objectively normatively adequate (too strong). I'm establishing that Q has rational-explanatory force *in my rational economy*—that my belief stands in a rational relation to Q within my perspective, that Q is playing the reason-role in my rational life.

This isn't equivocation but precision about a distinct normative domain. The interpretive theorist who tries to explain my belief must accommodate that Q plays this role—not because Q is objectively a good reason (it might not be), and not just because I'm committed to citing Q (I might cite things that don't actually function as reasons in my psychology), but because my sincere rational avowal makes Q the operative reason in my rational system.

The proof this is unified: rational explanation requires both dimensions. To explain Sarah's belief rationally, we need to know both (a) what she takes as her reason (commitment), and (b) that this actually structures her rational transitions (not just idle commitment). Sincere avowal establishes both together—it's a rational act that simultaneously commits and configures one's rational economy. This is why it's constitutive: the avowal doesn't report a pre-existing configuration, it *enacts* one.

The objection to interpretivism now has consistent force: interpretation from the outside can discover what would rationalize the belief, and what the subject is committed to defending, but cannot determine what actually has rational-explanatory force in the subject's operative rational economy. Only the subject's sincere rational self-ascription can do that, because rational economies are partly self-constituting—they're configured by the subject's own rational acts, including avowal.

The distinction from "normative reasons" remains: I cannot make Q objectively adequate just by avowing it. But I can make Q the reason that actually operates in my rational life, the one that explains my belief as rational-for-me. This is substantial authority that interpretation cannot capture, because no pattern in my behavior settles what's playing the reason-role in my rational self-understanding versus merely causing my behavior or being what I'm disposed to cite.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though it fights hard.

The problem is that "rational-explanatory force in the subject's rational life" still splits under pressure. Either this means:

(1) **What actually explains the subject's rational transitions psychologically**—the real operative reasons in their cognitive economy. But then sincere avowal doesn't have constitutive authority: Sarah might sincerely avow evasiveness while rivalry actually structures her transitions. Interpretation can discover this better than avowal.

(2) **What the subject takes to structure their rational transitions**—their rational self-understanding. But this collapses back into commitment: it's just what the subject is committed to treating as operative, which might be wrong about what's actually operative.

The defender tries to fuse these: sincere avowal both commits and configures. But this only works if rational economies are purely self-constituting—if there's no fact about what actually structures your rational transitions beyond what you take to structure them. And that's implausible: people are wrong about what reasons actually move them, what considerations they actually weigh, what patterns actually govern their rational transitions.

The deeper problem: the defender concedes that constitutive authority doesn't extend to whether Q is a good reason (objective normative standing), but wants to claim authority over whether Q has "rational-explanatory force" in one's life. But rational-explanatory force is itself a normative-cum-descriptive hybrid that fragments. The descriptive component (what actually explains) is discoverable by interpretation. The normative component (what ought to explain, or what one is committed to letting explain) either reduces to commitment (trivial) or to objective rational standing (which was already conceded as beyond first-person authority).

The thesis survives only if there's a stable middle ground between "what I'm committed to" and "what actually operates," a domain where sincere avowal genuinely constitutes facts that interpretation cannot access. The defence hasn't shown this middle ground exists—it's asserted that "rational economies are partly self-constituting," but this is the thesis restated, not defended. Without independent argument that sincere rational avowal literally configures (not just expresses commitment about) one's operative rational economy, the equivocation stands: the thesis needs normative force but can only defend commitment-claims.

</details>

---

### O17. First-person authority and confabulation are compatible because authority attaches to different mental state types than those the confabulation literature targets: we have authority over occurrent judgments but not over the dispositional states or processes that generate them.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both Carruthers (who treats all self-knowledge as equally interpretive) and traditional perceptualists (who treat all mental states as equally transparent)
- **Sketch:** The debate treats 'mental states' as a unified category, but the confabulation literature (Nisbett & Wilson) studies causal processes, trait attributions, and dispositional attitudes, while first-person authority traditionally concerns occurrent judgments, decisions, and phenomenal states. When I sincerely judge that p, there's conceptual pressure to treat me as believing p (this is what Bar-On's expressivism captures), but this doesn't extend to why I judge it, what dispositions I have, or what implicit attitudes govern my behavior. The interpretive theorists overreach by generalizing from cases where we lack authority (causal explanations, implicit bias) to all mental self-ascription. The perceptualists overreach by assuming authority about occurrent states extends to the psychological background. Both camps are partly right but about different domains that need to be carved apart.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The occurrent/dispositional distinction is sensible and underexplored in this debate, though the reconciliation feels more like peaceful coexistence than deep insight.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Occurrent self-deception cases (jealousy mischaracterized as professional concern) do damage; defence splits judgment from concurrent state but this makes authority too narrow to matter
  - TRIVIAL — landed 3/5, **UNANSWERED**. The distinction risks circularity; defence shows pre-existing taxonomy exists but fails to prove it tracks the authority/confabulation divide as needed
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Thesis needs narrow reading for plausibility, broad for significance; defence's 'constitutive authority' changes subject from epistemology to metaphysics, abandoning the debate's actual question
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's late-arriving criteria are tailored to guarantee the thesis; 'occurrent judgment' defined precisely as what we don't confabulate about, making refutation impossible
- 🔧 **Survives as:** First-person authority and confabulation target different questions: authority concerns the constitutive role of avowal in fixing one's rational commitments, while confabulation concerns epistemic access to the causes and full content of one's mental states—both can be right because constitutive authority is compatible with systematic epistemic limitation
- **Flaw:** The distinction collapses into either triviality (authority over what we're authoritative about) or unfalsifiability (criteria constructed to exclude counterexamples), while the constitutive authority move changes the subject from epistemic privilege to metaphysical constitution

<details><summary>6 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) The empirical evidence on confabulation reveals not a failure of introspection but that 'belief' systematically ambiguates between standing commitments (which we do have observational access to) and in-the-moment motivating states (which we infer)—first-person authority attaches only to the former.
- (2/5) The interpretive model and first-person authority are compatible because authority attaches to different mental states than the confabulation literature examines: we have interpretive access to occurrent judgments but authority over standing commitments, and the literature systematically conflates these.
- (2/5) We have first-person authority about our occurrent phenomenology but not our standing attitudes, and the entire debate misfires by treating 'self-knowledge' as univocal—the observational model was right about experience, the interpretive model right about belief, but neither camp's explanation of authority generalizes.
- (3/5) First-person authority survives interpretation only for dispositional states (beliefs, desires) but not for occurrent episodes (thoughts, experiences): the asymmetry between self and other-ascription tracks not access but the difference between constituting a disposition and reporting an episode.
- (3/5) The interpretive and observational models both fail because they assume a single faculty called 'introspection,' when actually we have parallel systems: a reliable but narrow perceptual-access mechanism for phenomenal states and an interpretive mechanism for propositional attitudes, neither reducing to the other.
- (3/5) Interpretivism undermines first-person authority for beliefs about standing states but preserves it for occurrent thoughts, revealing that 'first-person authority' names two unrelated phenomena mistakenly treated as one.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT: Consider the phenomenon of **occurrent self-deception**. A person in the grip of jealousy forms the occurrent judgment "I'm just concerned about professional standards" while actively experiencing jealous feelings toward a colleague's success. This is not a dispositional state or background process—it's a live, conscious, occurrent judgment happening right now. Yet the person systematically mischaracterizes their own current mental state. They confabulate about what they're experiencing *in the moment*.

The thesis claims we have authority over occurrent judgments, but here the subject sincerely makes an occurrent judgment ("I judge that my concern is professional") that misrepresents their concurrent occurrent state (the jealous phenomenology they're experiencing). The confabulation isn't about causal history or dispositions—it's about *what is currently going on in consciousness*. If first-person authority attached to occurrent judgments as the thesis claims, self-deception about current experiences would be impossible. But such cases are common: occurrent racist thoughts rationalized as "pattern recognition," occurrent attraction misidentified as "aesthetic appreciation," occurrent anger labeled as "principled disagreement."

The weak point is **the premise that authority attaches to occurrent judgments**—these cases show confabulation operates at that level too.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious challenge requiring substantial response, as it directly targets the distinction's load-bearing boundary with common phenomena.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection conflates **having an occurrent judgment** with **accurately characterizing a different concurrent mental state**. When the jealous person judges "I'm concerned about professional standards," they DO have that occurrent judgment—that's precisely what they're expressing, and we must take them as authoritative about having it. The authority attaches to the judgment itself, not to whether that judgment correctly represents some other state (the jealousy).

Here's the crucial move: **the jealousy and the professional-concern-judgment are two different occurrent states**. The subject has authority that they're making the professional-concern judgment (they are!), but they lack authority about whether this judgment exhausts their current motivational field. The confabulation consists in treating one occurrent state (the judgment) as if it were the only relevant state, not in lacking authority about whether they hold that judgment.

This parallels vision: I have authority that things look square to me right now (an occurrent perceptual state), but not about whether this exhausts my current visual phenomenology—perhaps I'm simultaneously experiencing peripheral motion I'm not attending to. The authority is **state-specific**, not **field-exhaustive**.

The objector's examples actually support the distinction: when someone says "I'm just noticing patterns" while having racist thoughts, they DO have an occurrent pattern-recognition judgment (authority holds there), but they're wrong about whether other states (racial animus) are also present. The confabulation is about **what else is going on**, not about whether the cited state is present. Self-deception typically involves **selective attention to one's occurrent states**, not authority-failure about the states one is attending to.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though not completely.

The defence succeeds in showing that some self-deception cases preserve a narrow authority: the subject is authoritative that they have the judgment they report. But this victory is Pyrrhic—it renders "authority over occurrent judgments" almost trivially true while abandoning the explanatory work the thesis needed it to do.

The fatal problem: **what we care about in first-person authority is authority about what we're thinking/feeling, not merely that we're tokening certain sentences**. When the jealous person says "I'm concerned about professional standards," the interesting question is whether they're authoritative about their concern being professional rather than jealous. The defence says "they're authoritative that they have a professional-concern-judgment, just wrong about whether jealousy is also present." But this splits the atom too finely.

In the self-deception cases, the subject ISN'T merely failing to notice additional states—they're mischaracterizing the very state they're attending to. The jealous person isn't experiencing jealousy PLUS professional concern; the phenomenology is jealousy that they're experiencing AS professional concern. The occurrent judgment misrepresents the occurrent experience it's supposed to express. That's confabulation at the occurrent level, and it breaks the thesis's boundary.

The defence retreats to: "you're authoritative that you're making this judgment." But that's too weak to do the work—it makes authority unfalsifiable rather than substantial.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — **TRIVIAL****ATTEMPT**
The thesis claims to resolve a substantive debate by distinguishing occurrent judgments (where we have authority) from dispositional states and processes (where we don't). But this "distinction" merely **relabels the explanandum as the explanans**.

The weak point is the **entire distinction itself** — it's just a terminological repackaging of "we're authoritative about the things we're authoritative about."

Here's why it's trivial: Everyone in the debate already knows that first-person authority, if it exists, doesn't extend to causal processes or unconscious dispositions. When Nisbett & Wilson show people confabulating about *why* they chose the rightmost stockings, no one thought subjects had broken some authority over *causal explanations*. The interesting question was always: does this undermine authority over the *beliefs/preferences themselves*?

The thesis "resolves" this by saying: "Authority holds for occurrent judgments!" But what's an occurrent judgment? It's whatever mental state the subject is authoritative about. We've gone in a circle. The thesis reduces to: "We have first-person authority over the domain of first-person authority (which I'll call 'occurrent judgments'), and we lack it elsewhere (which I'll call 'dispositions and processes')."

This is like "resolving" the free will debate by distinguishing "free actions" (those we're responsible for) from "determined actions" (those we're not). You've just named the two sides of the problem, not solved it.

The thesis offers no independent criterion for what counts as "occurrent" versus "dispositional" beyond "whatever preserves the authority/confabulation split I want."
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This seriously damages the thesis. The distinction appears to do explanatory work but actually just stipulates the boundary it needs. Without independent criteria for "occurrent judgment" beyond "the mental states we have authority about," the thesis becomes unfalsifiable verbal maneuvering rather than a substantive philosophical claim.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misreads the dialectical situation. The thesis doesn't *stipulate* what counts as occurrent versus dispositional and then declare victory—it *identifies an existing fault line in mental state taxonomy* that both sides of the debate have run together, then shows this explains the conflicting intuitions.

There ARE independent criteria for the distinction that predate this debate:

**Occurrent states** are episode-individuated, temporally bounded, and constitutively involve the subject's current perspective: the judgment I'm making *right now*, the experience I'm having *at this moment*, the decision I'm currently forming. These are the states picked out by present-tense, present-progressive attributions: "I am thinking that...", "I am deciding to...", "it seems to me now that..."

**Dispositional states** are standing states that persist across episodes and can be present while unmanifested: my belief that Nairobi is in Kenya (which I've held for years, including while asleep), my implicit bias (which structures behavior across contexts), my trait of being jealous (which explains patterns but isn't a datable event).

This distinction is *not* drawn to save the thesis—it's a standard distinction in philosophy of mind (cf. Audi on occurrent vs. dispositional belief, Peacocke on conscious vs. standing states). The thesis's contribution is recognizing that **the confabulation literature and the authority literature have been talking past each other because they focus on different sides of this pre-existing divide**.

Nisbett & Wilson's subjects confabulate about: (1) causal explanations (processes), (2) standing preferences (dispositions), (3) trait self-attributions (dispositions). The traditional authority cases concern: (1) what I'm currently judging, (2) how things seem to me now, (3) what intention I'm currently acting on. These map onto the occurrent/dispositional distinction using criteria independent of the authority question.

The thesis isn't "we have authority where we have authority"—it's "we have authority over the episodic, perspective-involving states, and the confabulation literature studies standing states and sub-personal processes, so both sides are right about their respective domains." The distinction does explanatory work: it predicts that confabulation studies focusing on occurrent states (if any exist) would threaten authority, while authority intuitions about dispositional states (if any exist) would be weak. And this is what we find: the jealousy case (previous objection) was powerful precisely because it threatened to be an occurrent confabulation.

The independent criterion is: **Can this state be present while the subject is dreamlessly asleep?** If yes, dispositional; if no, occurrent. This isn't defined by authority—it's defined by temporal profile. But it tracks the authority distinction, which is the thesis's explanatory payoff.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though it lands some hits.

The defence is right that there's a pre-existing occurrent/dispositional distinction in philosophy of mind with independent criteria. That's a real point against the triviality charge. But the defence fails because **the confabulation literature doesn't neatly target only dispositional states, and the authority literature doesn't neatly concern only occurrent ones**.

The critical problem: **Many confabulation cases concern what subjects are occurrently experiencing or judging**. When Nisbett & Wilson's subjects say "I chose these stockings because of quality," they're making an occurrent judgment about their occurrent reasoning. When people in choice blindness experiments immediately justify choices they didn't make, they're forming occurrent judgments about occurrent preferences. These aren't standing dispositions—they're live, present-tense self-interpretations happening in the moment.

Conversely, first-person authority claims often concern standing states. "I believe God exists," "I prefer coffee to tea," "I intend to finish my dissertation"—these are paradigm authority claims, but they're about dispositional states that persist when I'm not thinking about them. The authority literature doesn't restrict itself to "what I'm judging right this second."

The "asleep test" doesn't save the thesis because the interesting cases fall on both sides: My occurrent thought "I'm choosing these stockings for quality" wouldn't persist through sleep (it's occurrent), yet I confabulate about it. My standing belief "I believe in God" would persist through sleep (it's dispositional), yet I seem authoritative about it.

The thesis tries to resolve the debate by sorting cases into pre-existing bins, but the debate exists precisely because **both authority and confabulation cross-cut the occurrent/dispositional distinction**. The defence shows the distinction isn't *trivial*, but it doesn't show the distinction does the work the thesis needs. The fault line is real, but it's not the fault line that explains the authority/confabulation tension.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — **EQUIVOCATION****ATTEMPT**
The thesis equivocates on **"occurrent judgment"** — it needs two different readings to do its work, one to sound plausible and another to sound interesting.

**Reading 1 (Narrow)**: An occurrent judgment is merely the tokening of a sentence in inner speech, the bare fact that I'm entertaining a proposition right now, independent of whether that proposition correctly characterizes anything. On this reading, "I have authority over occurrent judgments" is plausible — when I think the words "I'm concerned about professional standards," I'm authoritative that I'm thinking those words.

**Reading 2 (Broad)**: An occurrent judgment is a substantive characterization of my current mental life — what I'm really feeling, what's actually motivating me, what I'm genuinely experiencing right now. On this reading, "confabulation doesn't threaten authority over occurrent judgments" is interesting — it would mean something philosophically significant that we're authoritative about our current mental reality even if we confabulate about background dispositions.

**The equivocation**: The thesis defends itself using Reading 1 (as we saw with jealousy: "they DO have that judgment") but claims philosophical significance using Reading 2 (resolving the debate by protecting authority over our current mental lives). The argument cannot work on either reading alone:

- On Reading 1 alone: The claim is plausible but philosophically uninteresting — trivially true that we know what sentences we're tokening, but this doesn't address what first-person authority debates are actually about.

- On Reading 2 alone: The claim is interesting but implausible — the confabulation literature shows we systematically mischaracterize our current motivations, current reasons, current experiences, not just background dispositions.

The thesis needs both: plausibility from the narrow reading, significance from the broad reading.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This does serious damage. The thesis's defence against the jealousy case revealed the equivocation clearly: retreating to "authoritative that they're making this judgment" (narrow) while claiming to resolve debates about authority over "what we're thinking/feeling" (broad). The distinction can't simultaneously be substantive enough to resolve the debate and narrow enough to survive counterexamples.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies an equivocation where there's actually a **principled intermediate position**. The thesis doesn't oscillate between "mere sentence-tokening" and "infallible self-knowledge"—it identifies a specific grade of authority that's neither trivial nor implausibly strong.

Here's the position: An occurrent judgment is **a committal taking-to-be-the-case that constitutively involves the subject's rational perspective**. When I judge "I'm concerned about professional standards," I'm not merely tokening words (Reading 1), nor am I infallibly characterizing some independent mental reality (Reading 2). I'm **taking a stand** that constitutively makes it the case that this is how things are *from my perspective*.

This is what Bar-On's expressivism captures: the judgment doesn't *report* a pre-existing concern—it *expresses* my current evaluative stance. The authority isn't infallibility about an independent fact; it's the **constitutive role of avowal in fixing what counts as my perspective**. When I sincerely judge "I'm professionally concerned," this judgment partially *constitutes* my current rational orientation, not merely reports it.

The jealousy case doesn't break this. The subject IS professionally concerned (they have that occurrent judgment), AND they're jealous (they have that occurrent emotion). The confabulation consists in **taking their explicit judgment to exhaust their motivational field**—a failure of comprehensiveness, not authority about the judgment itself. They're authoritative that professional concern is part of their current perspective; they're wrong that it's the *whole* story.

This isn't equivocation—it's recognizing that **authority comes in grades**:
- **Trivial authority** (Reading 1): knowing what sentences you're tokening
- **Constitutive authority** (the thesis): your avowals partially constitute your rational perspective
- **Infallible transparency** (Reading 2): complete and accurate access to all current mental states

The thesis occupies the middle ground. It's more than trivial because constitutive authority has normative force—if you judge that p, you're committed to p in a way that constrains rational interpretation. It's less than Reading 2 because this authority is **state-specific, not field-exhaustive**: you're authoritative about the judgments you make, not about whether other states (emotions, implicit attitudes) are also present.

The confabulation literature threatens Reading 2 (we lack comprehensive transparency), but not the constitutive authority of Reading 1.5. The thesis resolves the debate by showing both sides were right: interpretivists correctly note we lack comprehensive transparency; perceptualists correctly note our avowals have constitutive authority. Different grades of authority for different questions.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, but it's the best available and reveals why the thesis ultimately cannot succeed.

The defence's "intermediate position" is genuinely more sophisticated than simple equivocation—the idea of constitutive authority is philosophically substantive. But this sophistication actually **exposes the deeper problem**: the thesis cannot simultaneously resolve the first-person authority debate AND accommodate the confabulation literature, because these literatures are asking incompatible questions about the same territory.

The fatal issue: **Constitutive authority doesn't address what the first-person authority debate is about**. When philosophers debate whether we have first-person authority, they're asking whether subjects have a privileged epistemic position regarding their mental states—whether they can *know* what they think and feel in a way others cannot. The constitutive move—"your avowal makes it so"—changes the subject from epistemology to metaphysics. It doesn't answer "do you know your mind better than others?" but rather "do your avowals partly create what's true about your mind?"

Even granting constitutive authority, the confabulation literature still threatens the epistemic claim. If the jealous person's judgment "I'm professionally concerned" constitutively makes professional concern part of their perspective, but they're *unaware* of the jealousy that's also part of their perspective, they still lack the epistemic authority the debate is about. They don't know their own mind comprehensively, even if their avowals partly shape it.

The defence says: "authority is state-specific, not field-exhaustive." But this concedes too much. The first-person authority tradition claims something stronger than "you know about the specific states you're attending to"—it claims a **general epistemic privilege** about your mental life. Even Ryle's skeptics grant that you know what you're currently attending to; the debate is about whether this extends to a broader privilege. By retreating to state-specific authority, the thesis abandons the territory it claimed to defend.

Moreover, the confabulation cases show failures even at the state-specific level. When subjects confabulate about *why* they chose the rightmost stockings, they're wrong about their occurrent reasoning process—not just about background dispositions. The constitutive move doesn't help: even if their avowal "I chose it for quality" constitutively makes quality-concern part of their perspective, they're still wrong about what actually motivated the choice, and that's an occurrent state.

The thesis promised to reconcile authority and confabulation by separating their domains. But the defence reveals they're fighting over the same domain—occurrent mental life—with the thesis trying to redefine "authority" to avoid the conflict rather than resolving it. That's not equivocation exactly, but it's not resolution either.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — **UNFALSIFIABLE****ATTEMPT**
The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of "occurrent judgment" and "authority." Whenever a case threatens the boundary, the thesis can reclassify either what counts as "occurrent" or what "authority" means.

**The weak point is the premise that "authority attaches to occurrent judgments"** — this is unfalsifiable because both terms are flexible enough to exclude any apparent counterexample.

Watch the pattern: When self-deception cases showed confabulation about current mental states, the defence split "having a judgment" from "characterizing concurrent states" — suddenly authority meant only "authoritative that you're making this judgment," not "authoritative about what you're experiencing." When this seemed too trivial, the defence shifted to "constitutive authority" — your avowal partly creates the mental fact. When confabulation about occurrent reasoning processes threatened this, the defence could say "that's about causal processes (dispositional), not the judgment itself (occurrent)."

Every apparent counterexample gets handled the same way: "That's not really an occurrent judgment" or "That's not the kind of authority we mean." 

- Confabulation about current motivations? "Motivation is dispositional, not occurrent."
- Confabulation about current reasoning? "Reasoning processes aren't judgments."
- Self-deception about current feelings? "The judgment and the feeling are different states; authority attaches only to the judgment."
- Wrong about what you're currently thinking? "You're right about the judgment you're making, wrong about other concurrent states."

The thesis has no **fixed, independent criteria** for what counts as an "occurrent judgment" that could make it vulnerable to cases. Instead, "occurrent judgment" means "whatever mental state type preserves the authority claim" — making the thesis true by definition, not by capturing a real distinction that could turn out to be false.
**STRENGTH: 5/5**
The thesis cannot survive this. The exchange has demonstrated that every counterexample gets absorbed by redefining the boundaries. The defence's sophistication (constitutive authority, state-specific vs field-exhaustive, etc.) is actually the problem — these aren't refinements that make the thesis more precise and thus more vulnerable to refutation, but rather escape hatches that make it infinitely flexible. A thesis that can mean something narrow when defending against counterexamples and something broad when claiming significance, that can reclassify any problematic case as "not really occurrent" or "not the relevant kind of authority," is not a substantive philosophical claim — it's an unfalsifiable framework that relabels the debate rather than resolving it.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection conflates **dialectical refinement** with **ad hoc maneuver**. Yes, the defence has specified what "occurrent judgment" and "authority" mean—but that's not unfalsifiability, that's *doing philosophy*. Any serious thesis must clarify its terms when challenged, and clarification that narrows scope isn't automatically evasion.

Here are **fixed, independent criteria** that were implicit throughout and can now be made explicit:

**For "occurrent judgment":**
- Must be a propositional attitude (belief, judgment, intention) with conceptual content
- Must be episodic: datable to a specific time, not a standing state
- Must be accessible to consciousness: the subject can, in principle, avow it
- Must involve the subject's rational perspective: it's the kind of thing that can be a reason for action

**For "authority":**
- The subject's sincere avowal creates a **presumption** that must be defeated by special evidence
- This presumption is **stronger than third-person evidence** from behavior or circumstances
- It's **defeasible** but the burden of proof falls on the challenger
- It's **not infallibility**: the subject can be wrong, but they can't be systematically wrong in the way third-person observers can

These criteria make the thesis **falsifiable**. Here are cases that would refute it:

1. **Systematic occurrent confabulation**: If subjects regularly and systematically misreport their current, conscious, propositional judgments (not feelings, not processes, not dispositions—actual judgments they're making right now), the thesis fails.

2. **Symmetric evidence**: If third-person behavioral evidence about someone's current judgment were *routinely* as reliable as first-person avowal, the authority claim fails.

3. **Unconscious judgment formation**: If it turned out that most judgments are formed unconsciously and only post-hoc rationalized, with no privileged access even at the moment of avowal, the thesis fails.

Now, has the defence actually reclassified counterexamples illegitimately? Let's check:

**Self-deception about jealousy**: The defence didn't arbitrarily exclude this—it correctly noted that jealousy is an *emotion*, not a judgment. The subject's judgment "I'm professionally concerned" is distinct from their jealous emotion. This isn't gerrymandering; it's recognizing that emotions and judgments are different mental state types with different access conditions. The thesis claims authority over judgments, not emotions. That's a substantive, falsifiable claim: if subjects proved non-authoritative about their judgments (not just their emotions), it would fail.

**Confabulation about reasoning**: The defence distinguished reasoning *processes* from reasoning *conclusions* (judgments). This isn't ad hoc—it's the same distinction as between "how you computed 234 + 567" (process) and "what answer you got" (judgment). You have authority about your answer, not your computational process. Falsifiable prediction: subjects should be more reliable about their conclusions than their procedures.

**"That's not the relevant authority"**: The defence specified *constitutive* authority, but this isn't a retreat—it's a clarification that was needed from the start. The thesis never claimed *infallible transparency*. Saying "you're authoritative about the judgment you make, not about whether other states are present" isn't moving goalposts—it's specifying what authority means in a way that's been consistent since Bar-On's expressivism was invoked in the sketch.

The objection demands that the thesis be vulnerable to *any* case of mental state misattribution. But that would make it indefensible from the start—obviously we lack authority about *some* mental states (unconscious processes, implicit biases). The thesis's contribution is *drawing a principled line*. That line can be challenged by showing:
- The criteria for "occurrent judgment" are themselves circular or undefined (but I've now defined them)
- Cases that meet these criteria but lack authority (the objection hasn't provided one that survives scrutiny)
- The line doesn't track any real psychological or phenomenological distinction (but it does: judgments vs. emotions vs. processes are real categories)

The thesis makes **predictions**: 
- Confabulation studies targeting occurrent propositional judgments should be rare/controversial (they are—most target processes, dispositions, or implicit attitudes)
- Authority intuitions should be strongest for occurrent judgments, weaker for emotions, weakest for processes (they are)
- When subjects are wrong about their occurrent judgments, there should be special explanation (self-deception, insincerity, conceptual confusion)—not routine error

These predictions could be false. That's falsifiability.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails decisively.

The defence's "fixed criteria" arrive too late and don't actually fix anything. More damningly, **the criteria themselves reveal the thesis's emptiness**.

Look at what "occurrent judgment" now requires: propositional, episodic, consciously accessible, involving rational perspective. And what's "authority"? A defeasible presumption stronger than third-person evidence. These aren't independent criteria—they're **jointly tailored to guarantee the thesis's truth**. 

The fatal circularity: Why do occurrent judgments require conscious accessibility? Because if they didn't, we might lack authority over them. Why does authority mean "defeasible presumption" rather than "reliable access"? Because if it meant reliable access, confabulation cases would defeat it. The criteria are **constructed to ensure that whatever has the first property has the second**.

The defence's "falsification conditions" confirm this. Condition 1: "systematic occurrent confabulation" would refute the thesis. But what counts as "occurrent" is defined by the very criteria that exclude confabulation cases! When Nisbett & Wilson's subjects confabulate about their reasoning, the defence says "that's about processes, not judgments." When self-deceivers confabulate about their motivations, the defence says "motivations are dispositional" or "that's about emotions, not judgments." The thesis has defined "occurrent judgment" precisely as *the category of mental states we don't confabulate about*—making "systematic occurrent confabulation" impossible by definition.

The defence claims jealousy is an emotion, not a judgment, so it's legitimately excluded. But this misses the point: **the jealous person makes an occurrent judgment about their occurrent motivation** ("I'm motivated by professional standards"). That judgment is propositional, episodic, conscious, and involves their rational perspective—it meets all the criteria. Yet they're wrong about it. The defence's move—"they're right about having that judgment, wrong about whether other states are present"—is precisely the kind of flexible redefinition the objection identified.

The "predictions" are equally circular. "Confabulation studies targeting occurrent propositional judgments should be rare"—but whether a study targets "occurrent propositional judgments" is determined by whether it threatens the thesis. Choice blindness studies, where subjects immediately form judgments about preferences they didn't have, seem like occurrent judgment confabulation. But the thesis can reclassify: "those are about standing preferences, not occurrent judgments" or "those are about choices, not judgments about choices."

The deepest problem: **The defence has made "occurrent judgment" so narrow that the thesis becomes trivial again**. If occurrent judgments are just "consciously accessible propositional attitudes that we have privileged access to," then "we have authority over occurrent judgments" reduces to "we have privileged access to the mental states we have privileged access to." The criteria don't independently identify a category that *then* turns out to have the authority property—they identify the category *by* the authority property.

The thesis promised to resolve the debate by showing both sides were right about different domains. But it resolves nothing—it just stipulates that authority holds

</details>

### O18. The asymmetry between self and other-ascription that motivates anti-interpretivist views is actually an artifact of differential conversational stakes: self-ascriptions are typically avowal-like speech acts that alter the dialectical score, while other-ascriptions are descriptive reports, and this pragmatic difference (not epistemic asymmetry) explains why challenges to self-ascriptions are treated as inappropriate.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Bar-On's neo-expressivism and the shared assumption that asymmetry reflects epistemic or metaphysical differences rather than pragmatic ones
- **Sketch:** Bar-On and Moran point to the asymmetry: 'You don't believe P' is a legitimate challenge but 'No you don't' in response to 'I believe P' seems inappropriate. They take this to reveal an epistemic or metaphysical asymmetry. But the key premise is that self-ascriptions function as commitments or stances in conversation (closer to promises than reports), while other-ascriptions function as descriptions. Just as 'I promise' creates a commitment rather than reports a mental state, 'I believe P' stakes a claim in conversational space. The inappropriateness of challenging it is pragmatic—you can't tell someone they haven't undertaken the commitment they're undertaking. This explains the asymmetry without privileged access: we have authority not because we observe better but because self-ascription is a different speech act. Confabulation shows we're wrong about the descriptive-psychological facts; authority shows we're right about what commitment we're undertaking.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Speech-act approaches to avowal exist; this conversational-stakes angle is competent but incremental.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The dual-level defence concedes descriptive challenges ARE appropriate when properly framed, contradicting the thesis's claim that challenge-inappropriateness is explained pragmatically
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 2/5, answered. The defence provided genuinely falsifiable predictions distinguishing pragmatic from epistemic explanations, though it revealed the thesis is empirically untested
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence misrepresented Bar-On as denying descriptive content; once corrected, Bar-On's actual dual-aspect expressivism IS the thesis's dual-level account under different vocabulary
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence retreated to epistemic considerations (availability of third-person evidence) to distinguish applicable cases, conceding epistemic asymmetry does the real explanatory work
- **Flaw:** The thesis is already taken by its stated target (Bar-On's neo-expressivism with dual descriptive-expressive dimensions), and when pressed to distinguish applicable cases, it retreats to epistemic rather than pragmatic criteria

<details><summary>4 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) First-person authority and symmetric interpretive access are compatible because the asymmetry lies not in evidence or method but in dialectical role: self-ascriptions are moves in the space of reasons that terminate challenges in a way other-ascriptions cannot, even when both rest on equally interpretive grounds.
- (2/5) First-person authority comes apart into two phenomena—exemption from challenge and exemption from evidence—and the interpretive turn undermines only the second.
- (3/5) The asymmetry between self and other-ascription doesn't locate first-person authority in a special epistemic or metaphysical relation, but in the pragmatic immunity of first-person claims to a particular type of defeat—testimonial override—that generates different rational consequences rather than different truth-conditions.
- (3/5) First-person authority emerges not from a distinct epistemic method but from the normative structure of assertion: self-ascriptions are authoritative because they are subject to different felicity conditions than other-ascriptions, making them more like avowals than reports.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis equivocates on "self-ascription" between two incompatible readings, and the argument requires both. On one reading, self-ascriptions are genuine belief-reports with descriptive content (making claims about one's mental states that can be true or false). On this reading, the thesis is interesting because it addresses the actual Bar-On/Moran debate about belief-ascriptions like "I believe P." On the other reading, self-ascriptions are pure commitment-undertakings or stance-takings with no descriptive content about mental states at all (like "I promise"). On this reading, the thesis is plausible—of course you can't challenge someone's undertaking of a commitment—but it's also trivial and doesn't engage the target, since Bar-On and Moran are specifically concerned with ascriptions that DO purport to describe mental states.

The argument needs the descriptive reading to be relevant to the debate (the whole point is explaining asymmetry in belief-ascriptions), but needs the non-descriptive reading to make the pragmatic explanation work (pure commitment-undertakings can't be challenged). The thesis cannot run on either reading alone: purely descriptive self-ascriptions face the original asymmetry problem; purely non-descriptive self-ascriptions aren't what Bar-On and Moran are discussing.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis must clarify whether "I believe P" has descriptive content about mental states or not, and explain the asymmetry on whichever answer it gives.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misses that speech acts can be BOTH commitment-undertaking AND descriptive simultaneously—this is dual-level content, not equivocation. Consider "I promise to come tomorrow." This has descriptive content (it can be true or false whether I actually promise), yet its primary function is performative. Similarly, "I believe P" has two levels:

**Descriptive level**: It purports to describe my mental state (can be assessed for accuracy via confabulation studies, neuroimaging, etc.)

**Performative level**: It stakes a commitment in conversational space to defend P, treat P as a premise, etc.

The key move: Bar-On and Moran's asymmetry targets the PERFORMATIVE level, not the descriptive level. When you challenge "I believe P" with "No you don't," you're not merely disputing a descriptive claim—you're attempting to BLOCK the commitment-undertaking itself. This is pragmatically inappropriate in the same way interrupting "I promise" with "No you don't promise" is inappropriate, even though promises can fail descriptively (insincere promises).

Crucially, the descriptive content CAN be challenged—just differently. "You believe P? But you just said Q, which contradicts P!" or "Brain scans suggest you don't actually believe that" are perfectly appropriate. These challenge the descriptive accuracy without blocking the performative force.

The thesis thus engages Bar-On/Moran directly: their asymmetry exists because they conflate levels. Other-ascriptions are PURELY descriptive. Self-ascriptions are DUAL-LEVEL, and the asymmetry tracks which level is being engaged. This explains why confabulation (descriptive failure) coexists with authority (performative success).
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails.**

The dual-level move cannot rescue the thesis because the Bar-On/Moran asymmetry specifically concerns the DESCRIPTIVE level, and the defence implicitly concedes this cannot be explained pragmatically.

Notice what the defence admits: descriptive challenges ARE appropriate when properly framed ("Brain scans suggest you don't believe that"). But this contradicts the original thesis, which claimed "challenges to self-ascriptions are treated as inappropriate" and explained this via pragmatics. If descriptive challenges are appropriate, then the asymmetry Bar-On/Moran identify—the special inappropriateness of "No you don't" as a descriptive challenge—remains unexplained by pragmatics alone.

The performative-blocking explanation only works for a different phenomenon (blocking commitment-undertakings). But "No you don't" in response to "I believe P" is naturally read as a descriptive disagreement about mental states, not an attempt to block performative force. The defence's own examples prove this: "You just said Q" challenges the belief-ascription by providing EVIDENCE about the mental state, treating it as a descriptive claim throughout.

The thesis therefore fails on both readings after all: either self-ascriptions are descriptive (and the pragmatic explanation doesn't cover descriptive challenges), or they're performative (and we're no longer explaining the asymmetry in the actual debate about belief-reports).

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT. The thesis is unfalsifiable because any apparent counterexample—any case where the asymmetry seems genuinely epistemic rather than pragmatic—can be redefined as involving "differential conversational stakes" or reinterpreted through the performative/descriptive distinction. 

Consider: If someone sincerely self-ascribes "I believe God exists" but all behavioral and neurological evidence suggests otherwise, and we find "No you don't" inappropriate here, this SHOULD count against the pragmatic explanation (suggesting genuine epistemic privilege). But the thesis can simply respond: "The inappropriateness shows the performative level is engaged; the evidence shows descriptive failure—both are accommodated." 

Conversely, if we find a self-ascription where "No you don't" IS appropriate (say, in psychotherapy: "I believe I'm worthless"/"No you don't, that's depression talking"), this SHOULD support the thesis. But it can be dismissed: "Here the stakes are different" or "therapeutic contexts alter the speech-act norms."

The problem: EVERY case gets sorted post-hoc into whichever level (performative/descriptive) or stakes-configuration makes it fit. Cases where challenges seem inappropriate? Performative level engaged. Cases where they're appropriate? Descriptive level engaged. The dual-level apparatus makes the thesis immune to counterexamples by ensuring any asymmetry pattern can be "explained" through flexible reinterpretation of which level or stakes are operative.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is a serious problem. The thesis needs to specify IN ADVANCE what observable patterns would count against the pragmatic explanation versus supporting an epistemic one.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The unfalsifiability charge fails because the thesis makes specific, testable predictions that distinguish it from epistemic explanations—predictions that could straightforwardly be falsified by linguistic evidence.

**Prediction 1: Cross-linguistic variation in asymmetry patterns.** If the asymmetry is pragmatic (tied to conversational norms about commitment-undertaking), it should vary across languages and cultures with different discourse practices. If it's epistemic (tied to privileged access), it should be universal wherever belief-ascriptions exist. Evidence: If languages with radically different politeness systems or evidential-marking requirements show the SAME asymmetry pattern in belief-ascriptions, that falsifies the pragmatic explanation.

**Prediction 2: Context-sensitivity within a single language.** The pragmatic explanation predicts the asymmetry should weaken or disappear in contexts where self-ascriptions explicitly function as descriptive reports rather than commitment-undertakings. Test case: "Let me describe my mental states for the psychological study—I believe P" versus ordinary conversational "I believe P." If "No you don't" remains equally inappropriate in the explicitly-descriptive-report context, that falsifies the pragmatic explanation, since the conversational stakes have been explicitly neutralized.

**Prediction 3: Developmental trajectory.** If pragmatic, the asymmetry should emerge when children master the relevant speech-act norms (relatively late, tied to understanding commitments). If epistemic, it should appear as soon as children grasp belief-ascription (earlier). Falsifiable: If 3-year-olds who can barely understand promising already show the full asymmetry in belief-ascriptions, that's evidence against the pragmatic view.

**Prediction 4: The "alienated self-ascription" test.** Consider: "The psychological evidence suggests I believe P, though I don't feel I do." Here the speaker explicitly separates descriptive from performative levels. The pragmatic explanation predicts "No you don't" remains appropriate here (purely descriptive framing). If it still feels inappropriate, that falsifies the pragmatic explanation.

The psychotherapy example actually SUPPORTS rather than threatens the thesis: therapeutic contexts are precisely where we've socially negotiated that self-ascriptions should be treated as descriptive reports open to challenge rather than commitment-undertakings. This isn't post-hoc—it's predicted by the view that pragmatic norms are context-dependent.

The thesis isn't unfalsifiable; it's empirically committal in ways epistemic explanations are not.
**VERDICT**
**The defence succeeds partially but reveals a deeper problem.**

The predictions ARE genuinely falsifiable and do distinguish the pragmatic explanation from epistemic alternatives. Prediction 4 especially is sharp: if "The evidence suggests I believe P, though I don't feel I do"/"No you don't" still feels inappropriate despite explicit descriptive framing, that would be clear evidence against the thesis. These aren't post-hoc accommodations—they're advance commitments.

However, the defence exposes that the thesis hasn't actually CHECKED these predictions. The argument was presented as if the pragmatic explanation were established, but the defence reveals it's actually a hypothesis awaiting empirical test. We don't know if the asymmetry varies cross-linguistically as predicted, or appears late developmentally, or disappears in alienated self-ascriptions.

More seriously, the defence's own examples undercut confidence in the thesis. The psychotherapy case was supposed to be dismissible, but the defence instead concedes it's a context where the asymmetry genuinely disappears—which means the "inappropriateness" Bar-On and Moran identify ISN'T a stable feature of self-ascriptions after all, but something that comes and goes with context. This makes the original explanatory target wobblier than presented.

The unfalsifiability charge fails—the thesis makes testable predictions. But the defence inadvertently reveals the thesis is empirically premature: it's a research program, not an established reframing. The strength of the predictions as falsifiers is also the weakness of the thesis as a current explanation.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis's core move—that self-ascriptions function as commitment-undertakings or stance-takings in conversational space rather than purely as descriptive reports—is already the central claim of **expressivist** and **neo-expressivist** positions in philosophy of mind, particularly those developed by **Dorit Bar-On herself** (the very target named in the thesis).

Bar-On's neo-expressivism in *Speaking My Mind* (2004) argues that self-ascriptions are "expressive" speech acts that manifest or give voice to mental states rather than describing them based on inner observation. Her view explicitly contrasts the expressive function of first-person avowals with the descriptive function of third-person ascriptions. This is precisely the pragmatic/speech-act asymmetry the thesis claims to offer as a novel reframing.

Similarly, **Richard Moran's** work on self-knowledge emphasizes the deliberative, commitment-involving character of first-person present-tense psychological claims—that "I believe P" is answered by deliberating about P, not by introspecting belief-states. This is the stance-taking or commitment dimension the thesis presents as its key insight.

The thesis claims to target "Bar-On's neo-expressivism" by offering a pragmatic alternative, but it actually IS a version of neo-expressivism. The only difference is terminological: where Bar-On uses "expression" and "avowal," the thesis uses "commitment-undertaking" and "conversational stakes." The explanatory structure is identical: asymmetry explained by differential speech-act function rather than epistemic asymmetry.

The position is already taken by the very theorist it claims to oppose.
**STRENGTH: 5/5**
The thesis cannot survive this. It presents itself as an alternative to Bar-On's neo-expressivism, but it simply IS neo-expressivism under different terminology. This isn't a case of overlapping with existing work—it's a case of unwittingly restating the target position while claiming to oppose it. The thesis has no independent content.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misreads both Bar-On and the thesis by conflating **expressivist semantics** with **pragmatic score-keeping**. These are fundamentally different explanatory frameworks, even when applied to the same phenomenon.

**Bar-On's neo-expressivism** is a view about the **semantic content and epistemic grounds** of self-ascriptions. Her central claim is that avowals EXPRESS mental states rather than DESCRIBE them based on evidence—they are "groundless" in the sense of not requiring self-directed epistemic access. The asymmetry arises because self-ascriptions have a different **semantic function** (expressing vs. describing) rooted in a different **psychological capacity** (transparency of mind vs. observation). This remains a story about the **nature of self-knowledge** and the **metaphysics of first-person authority**.

**The thesis's pragmatic explanation** is entirely different. It claims self-ascriptions and other-ascriptions have the **same semantic content** (both are descriptive reports about mental states that can be true or false), but they perform different **conversational moves** that alter **dialectical obligations**. The asymmetry arises not from what self-ascriptions mean or how they relate to mental states, but from what **commitments they create in conversational space**—a Brandomian or Stalnakerian story about score-keeping, not a story about expression or transparency.

**The crucial difference**: On Bar-On's view, "I believe P" doesn't describe a mental state at all (it expresses it), so there's no descriptive content to get wrong—confabulation is impossible because there's nothing to confabulate about. On the thesis's view, "I believe P" DOES describe a mental state (hence confabulation is genuine error about psychological facts), but it ALSO stakes a conversational commitment. Bar-On explains away the descriptive dimension; the thesis preserves it while adding a pragmatic layer.

**Evidence this is a real distinction**: Bar-On must explain why self-ascriptions seem to have truth-conditions and descriptive content (why "I believe P" can be false). The thesis doesn't face this problem—it accepts the descriptive content straightforwardly and explains the asymmetry elsewhere. Conversely, the thesis must explain what makes self-ascriptions authoritative if they're just descriptions (answer: performative force in conversation). Bar-On doesn't face this problem—expression is inherently authoritative.

**Moran is not the same either**: Moran's deliberative account is about the **epistemic method** for self-knowledge (transparency procedure: answer "Do I believe P?" by answering "Is P true?"). The thesis makes no claim about epistemic methods—you might use introspection, deliberation, or confabulation to form self-ascriptions. The point is that WHATEVER method you use, the resulting speech act functions as commitment-undertaking. Moran is about **how we know**; the thesis is about **what we do** when we self-ascribe.

The thesis is not terminological variant of neo-expressivism. It's a **pragmatic alternative** that preserves descriptive content while explaining asymmetry through conversational role rather than semantic function or epistemic access.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails, though not quite for the reasons the objection states.**

The defence is right that there's a genuine difference between expressivist semantics and pragmatic score-keeping. But it fatally misrepresents Bar-On's actual position in ways that undermine the thesis.

**Critical error**: Bar-On's neo-expressivism does NOT claim self-ascriptions lack descriptive content or truth-conditions. This is a caricature of expressivism. Bar-On explicitly argues that avowals have both expressive and descriptive dimensions—they can be true or false AND they express mental states. Her view is precisely that self-ascriptions are **dual-aspect**: semantically descriptive but pragmatically/epistemically expressive. She writes extensively about how avowals can be mistaken (allowing confabulation) while retaining their expressive character.

**The fatal problem**: Once we correct this misrepresentation, Bar-On's actual view IS the dual-level account the defender articulated earlier (descriptive content + performative/expressive force). The thesis claimed to offer this dual-level structure as an alternative to Bar-On, but Bar-On already has it. The defence tries to distinguish them by saying Bar-On "explains away" the descriptive dimension, but she doesn't—she explicitly preserves it.

**What about the pragmatic vs. semantic distinction?** The defence claims Bar-On's story is about semantic function while the thesis is about conversational role. But Bar-On's notion of "expression" is itself already pragmatic in the relevant sense—it's about what speech act you're performing, what you're doing in making the utterance. The expressivist slogan "expressing not describing" is precisely a claim about speech-act function, not just semantics.

**The Brandomian score-keeping language** doesn't create real distance. Bar-On could (and arguably does) accept that avowals alter dialectical commitments—that's compatible with their being expressions. The thesis hasn't identified a genuine alternative; it's restated Bar-On's dual-aspect expressivism using pragmatist vocabulary.

The objection's core charge stands: the thesis is already taken by its stated target, despite genuine differences in terminology and theoretical framing. It's neo-expressivism presented as an alternative to neo-expressivism.

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — **ATTEMPT**
If the pragmatic explanation works for belief self-ascriptions, it proves too much by licensing the same explanation for paradigm cases of genuine epistemic asymmetry where we clearly DO have privileged access. Consider pain: "I'm in pain" also functions as a commitment-undertaking in conversational space (committing to seeking relief, accepting sympathy, etc.), and "No you're not" in response is pragmatically inappropriate. By the thesis's logic, this asymmetry is also just pragmatic—we have authority about pain not because we feel it directly but because pain-ascriptions are performative speech acts.

But this is absurd. Pain is the central case where we DO have privileged epistemic access—I know I'm in pain through direct sensation, not through confabulation or inference. If the pragmatic explanation "works" for pain-ascriptions too, it proves too much by eliminating the very distinction between genuine epistemic privilege (pain, sensation) and merely apparent privilege (beliefs, attitudes). 

The thesis cannot block this extension. Every feature it cites for beliefs applies to pain: (1) self-ascriptions alter conversational score, (2) challenges seem inappropriate, (3) other-ascriptions are purely descriptive, (4) we have "authority" over self-ascriptions. If these features suffice to explain away epistemic asymmetry for beliefs, they equally explain it away for sensations—collapsing a distinction we need to preserve.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This is a serious problem requiring a real answer. The thesis must either explain why the pragmatic account doesn't extend to pain (identifying some relevant difference), or accept that it eliminates epistemic asymmetry even for paradigm cases of privileged access—which would make it not an explanation of apparent asymmetry but a radical eliminativism about all first-person authority.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection fails because pain-ascriptions and belief-ascriptions differ in precisely the dimension that matters: **the availability of third-person evidence that can underwrite descriptive challenges**.

**The key asymmetry within asymmetries**: For beliefs, there IS robust third-person evidence available—behavioral patterns, neuroimaging, implicit association tests, testimony about related commitments, etc. This evidence can and does ground appropriate descriptive challenges: "Brain scans suggest you don't actually believe that," "But you just acted as if not-P," "Your implicit associations contradict that belief." These challenges work because belief-ascriptions have a substantial descriptive dimension that's accessible to third-person investigation.

For pain, there is NO comparable third-person evidence. We have correlates (grimacing, neural activity in pain-processing regions), but these don't constitute independent evidence of pain in the way behavior constitutes evidence of belief. You can't say "Brain scans suggest you're not really in pain" in the same way you can say "Brain scans suggest you don't really believe that"—the neural activity IS the pain (or constitutes it), rather than evidencing it.

**Why this saves the thesis**: The pragmatic explanation works SPECIFICALLY for cases where:
1. Self-ascriptions function as commitment-undertakings (true of both pain and belief)
2. BUT there exists substantial third-person descriptive evidence (true of belief, false of pain)

For beliefs, the tension between (1) and (2) creates the puzzle: why does the performative inappropriateness of "No you don't" persist despite the availability of descriptive evidence? Answer: because we're conflating performative and descriptive levels. The pragmatic explanation resolves this specific tension.

For pain, there's no tension to resolve. The inappropriateness of "No you're not" isn't puzzling because there's no third-person descriptive evidence that would make the challenge appropriate. The pragmatic explanation doesn't apply because it's not needed—the epistemic explanation works fine. Pain involves genuine privileged access precisely because third-person evidence is unavailable.

**The thesis thus preserves the distinction**: Pain has epistemic asymmetry (privileged access through sensation) + pragmatic asymmetry (commitment-undertaking). Belief has only pragmatic asymmetry, which we mistook for epistemic asymmetry because we didn't notice the dual-level structure. The "proves too much" worry assumes the pragmatic explanation must apply wherever its surface features appear, but it actually applies only where those features create a puzzle that needs resolving—which requires the availability of third-person evidence.

**Supporting consideration**: Notice that for intermediate cases (emotions, intentions), the thesis predicts a gradient. Emotions have more third-person evidence than sensations but less than beliefs—and indeed, "You're not really angry" is somewhat more appropriate than "You're not really in pain" but less appropriate than challenges to belief-ascriptions backed by behavioral evidence. The thesis predicts this gradient; pure epistemic privilege cannot.
**VERDICT**
**The defence fails.**

The defence collapses under scrutiny because the criterion it offers—availability of third-person evidence—actually undermines rather than supports the thesis.

**Fatal problem 1**: The defence concedes that for pain, "the epistemic explanation works fine" because third-person evidence is unavailable.
