But this means the inappropriateness of challenges DOES track epistemic asymmetry when present. The defence has just admitted that where we have genuine privileged access (pain), challenges are inappropriate for epistemic reasons. This directly contradicts the thesis's core claim that the inappropriateness is pragmatic rather than epistemic.

**Fatal problem 2**: The availability of third-person evidence should make challenges MORE appropriate, not create a puzzle. If there's behavioral and neurological evidence about beliefs, then "No you don't believe that—look at the evidence" should be straightforwardly appropriate, and there's no asymmetry to explain. The thesis claimed the asymmetry is pragmatic, but the defence now says the asymmetry only needs pragmatic explanation where third-person evidence exists—which is exactly where we'd expect NO asymmetry at all.

**Fatal problem 3**: The defence's own gradient example destroys the thesis. If "You're not really angry" is intermediate in appropriateness between pain and belief challenges, this shows the inappropriateness DOES track epistemic access (emotions involve more introspective access than beliefs but less than sensations). The gradient follows the epistemic dimension, not the pragmatic one. The defence claims the thesis predicts this, but it actually contradicts it—pragmatic commitment-undertaking doesn't come in degrees corresponding to introspective access.

**The deeper failure**: The defence tries to save the thesis by restricting it to cases where third-person evidence is available, but this restriction is fatal. In those very cases, the Bar-On/Moran asymmetry is weakest or absent—as the earlier defence admitted, challenges like "Brain scans suggest you don't believe that" ARE appropriate. The thesis was supposed to explain why challenges seem inappropriate despite evidence being available, but the defence now admits challenges are appropriate when evidence is available (for beliefs) and inappropriate when evidence is unavailable (for pain). This is just the epistemic explanation with extra steps.

The "proves too much" objection stands. The thesis cannot distinguish cases where it should apply from cases where it shouldn't, because the features it cites (commitment-undertaking, challenge-inappropriateness) appear in both. When pressed, it retreats to epistemic considerations (availability of evidence, privileged access) to draw the distinction—thereby conceding that epistemic asymmetry, not pragmatic asymmetry, is doing the real explanatory work.

</details>

---

### O19. First-person authority survives the interpretive turn because interpretation itself comes in two varieties—reconstructive interpretation (third-personal, evidence-based) and constitutive interpretation (first-personal, which doesn't answer to prior facts but settles them)—and only confusing these makes the interpretive model seem threatening.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both Carruthers's pure interpretivism and the assumption shared by all sides that 'interpretation' means evidence-based reconstruction of pre-existing facts
- **Sketch:** The key premise is that interpretation is not univocal. When I interpret you, I'm reconstructing what you already believe/desire from limited evidence. But when I 'interpret' myself in avowing a belief, I'm often constituting what I believe by taking a stand, much as an artwork's meaning is partly constituted by the artist's interpretive choices rather than merely discovered. The confabulation literature shows we're bad at reconstructive self-interpretation, but this is compatible with constitutive self-interpretation grounding first-person authority. This explains the asymmetry: others can only reconstruct me, but I can constitute myself. Moran's agentialism gets at this but doesn't clearly distinguish it from observation; Carruthers's interpretivism wrongly assumes all interpretation is reconstructive.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The reconstructive/constitutive distinction is useful but closely parallels existing work; it clarifies more than it innovates.
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence claimed Moran rejects the interpretive picture while this accepts it, but the judge found this distinction collapses—calling Moran's deliberative stance 'constitutive interpretation' doesn't transform it into something new.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence successfully blocked extension to non-mental facts and self-deception, but only by making constitutive interpretation require rational integration—which the judge found makes it 'not actually interpretation' and reintroduces evidential dependence.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence's 'failed constitution' move required distinguishing successful from failed cases using behavioral evidence, which the judge found collapses the constitutive/reconstructive distinction and eliminates the asymmetry the thesis needed.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. The defence argued both types involve rational constraints as a common genus, but the judge found this equivocates—rational constraints function as epistemic tools in one case and constitutive commitments in the other, making the shared label mere stipulation.
- **Flaw:** The thesis relabels Moran's deliberative/theoretical distinction as 'constitutive/reconstructive interpretation' without the relabeling doing theoretical work—constitutive interpretation either collapses into ordinary deliberation (making it non-interpretive) or requires evidential checking (making it reconstructive after all).

<details><summary>13 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) First-person authority is preserved on the interpretive turn precisely because interpretation is never finished: the asymmetry lies not in our access but in our revisionary power—we can make our self-interpretations true by rational integration in a way we cannot with other-interpretations.
- (2/5) First-person authority survives the interpretive turn not despite interpretation but because self-interpretation operates under constitutive constraints that other-interpretation lacks: interpreting yourself as believing p makes it the case that you believe p in a way that interpreting another never can.
- (2/5) First-person authority survives on the interpretive model because self-interpretation operates under constitutive rather than epistemic norms—I have authority not because I know myself better but because my self-interpretation partly determines what is true of me.
- (2/5) First-person authority is preserved not despite interpretation but because self-interpretation operates under constitutive constraints that other-interpretation lacks: namely, that the act of settling what you believe itself partly determines what you believe.
- (3/5) First-person authority survives interpretation not because self-knowledge avoids interpretation, but because self-interpretation is performatively self-fulfilling in a way other-interpretation cannot be.
- (2/5) Interpretive self-knowledge generates authority when we make our minds conform to our self-interpretations through active self-constitution, collapsing the gap between reading and reality.
- (2/5) First-person authority survives the interpretive turn because interpretation itself comes in two flavors — confabulatory interpretation (manufacturing explanations post-hoc) and constitutive interpretation (where the act of self-ascription partially fixes what attitudes you have) — and only the former undermines authority.
- (2/5) First-person authority survives interpretation not because self-knowledge has privileged inputs but because avowal creates the very mental states it purports to report, collapsing the gap between interpretation and truth-making that exists in the third-person case.
- (3/5) First-person authority survives interpretation not because self-knowledge has special epistemic features, but because self-interpretation has a constitutive rather than merely epistemic relation to mental states in the personal-level domain.
- (2/5) First-person authority is preserved not despite interpretation but through it, because self-interpretation operates under constitutive rational norms that other-interpretation does not—norms that make certain self-ascriptions answerable to what one ought to think rather than merely to evidence about what one does think.
- (3/5) The interpretive turn actually reveals that first-person authority was never primarily epistemic but alethic: self-ascriptions have authority not because they're better known but because they tend to make themselves true through a mechanism of self-shaping that other-ascriptions cannot trigger.
- (2/5) First-person authority survives the interpretive turn because interpretation itself has first-personal authority: when you interpret your own mental states, the act of endorsing that interpretation constitutively settles what you believe, unlike third-personal interpretation which merely aims to track an independent fact.
- (3/5) First-person authority is grounded not in superior evidence or expressive avowals but in the constitutive role self-ascription plays in settling what we believe—interpretation creates rather than discovers psychological facts when performed in the first person.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — ATTEMPT. This distinction between reconstructive and constitutive interpretation is precisely the move made by Richard Moran in *Authority and Estrangement* (2001). Moran distinguishes between the "theoretical stance" (third-personal, evidence-based, observational) and the "deliberative stance" (first-personal, where avowing a belief involves taking a stand on what to believe). The candidate acknowledges Moran but claims he "doesn't clearly distinguish it from observation"—yet Moran explicitly argues that first-person authority rests on the deliberative stance being constitutive rather than observational. The candidate's "constitutive interpretation" is just Moran's deliberative stance relabeled, and the "reconstructive interpretation" is his theoretical stance. Even the artwork analogy appears in Moran's discussion of self-constitution. The only novelty is applying the term "interpretation" to both sides, but this terminological choice adds nothing substantive to Moran's framework. The candidate has redescribed Moran's position using "interpretation" vocabulary without advancing beyond it.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This is essentially Moran's agentialism with different labels. The thesis needs to show what theoretical work the "interpretation" framing does that Moran's deliberative/theoretical distinction doesn't already accomplish. Simply renaming established positions doesn't constitute a new thesis.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE.

The charge misreads what's at stake. Moran's deliberative stance is *not* interpretive at all—it's a stance of practical reasoning about what to believe, explicitly contrasted with any interpretive or observational stance. When I deliberate about whether p, I'm asking a world-directed question (is p true?), not an interpretive question about myself (what do I believe?). That's Moran's core insight: first-person authority comes from *bypassing* self-interpretation entirely.

The thesis here makes a different move: it accepts that self-knowledge *is* interpretive (contra Moran's bypass strategy) but argues that interpretation itself divides into kinds. This matters because the interpretive turn's threat comes precisely from treating all interpretation as evidence-based reconstruction. If interpretation can be constitutive, then we can grant the interpretivist picture while preserving authority—something Moran's framework cannot do, since he rejects the interpretive picture altogether.

The theoretical work is this: Moran leaves us unable to accommodate the interpretive turn's genuine insights (that self-knowledge often involves interpretation, that we lack transparent access to our attitudes). The constitutive/reconstructive distinction lets us accept those insights while blocking the skeptical conclusion. It's a *reconciliation* strategy where Moran's is a *rejection* strategy.

The artwork analogy illustrates something different too: not self-constitution through deliberation (Moran) but self-constitution *through interpretation itself*—the idea that the interpretive act can settle rather than discover facts.

VERDICT.

The defence fails. Even granting that Moran rejects the interpretive framework while this thesis accepts it, the supposed advantage collapses on examination. When the thesis says "I constitute what I believe by taking a stand," this *is* Moran's deliberative stance—the label "constitutive interpretation" doesn't transform it into something else. If taking a stand on what to believe counts as "interpretation," then Moran's view already *is* a view about interpretation being constitutive rather than reconstructive. The thesis hasn't reconciled anything; it's just insisted on calling Moran's non-interpretive stance "interpretive."

Moreover, the claimed advantage is illusory. Moran *can* accommodate that self-knowledge sometimes involves evidence-based interpretation—that's precisely his "theoretical stance," which he grants we sometimes adopt toward ourselves (in estrangement, psychotherapy, etc.). His framework already has both elements. Relabeling them doesn't constitute theoretical progress.

The thesis is Moran with a terminological gloss that obscures rather than clarifies his position. **Strength stands at 4/5.**

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. If constitutive interpretation can settle facts about one's mental states rather than merely discover them, this power should extend far beyond beliefs and desires to any domain where first-person authority is claimed. Consider pain: I have first-person authority about whether I'm in pain, but the thesis would imply that my "interpretation" of my sensation as pain *constitutes* it as pain rather than discovering a prior fact. This seems right—pain is partly constituted by how the subject takes it. But now apply this to delusions and pathological cases: a person with Cotard's delusion who "interpretively constitutes" themselves as dead would thereby *make it true* that they believe they're dead (which is fine) but the same logic suggests their constitutive interpretation could settle other facts—perhaps even whether they're actually dead, if we extend constitutive interpretation beyond mental states. More problematically, it threatens to validate any self-interpretation whatsoever: if my taking a stand that I'm generous constitutes my being generous (or believing I'm generous), then self-deception becomes impossible—whatever I constitute myself as believing, I thereby believe. The distinction collapses the gap between self-conception and reality that makes phenomena like self-deception, rationalization, and bad faith intelligible. Either constitutive interpretation has limits the thesis hasn't specified, or it proves too much by eliminating conceptual space for these phenomena.

STRENGTH: 3/5 — A genuine problem requiring an answer. The thesis needs principled boundaries for constitutive interpretation's scope, but the self-deception worry is answerable (constituting belief-content differs from constituting belief-truth), and extending to non-mental facts seems clearly blocked.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection conflates three distinct questions that constitutive interpretation answers differently:

**First**, constitutive interpretation settles *what attitude I have*, not *whether that attitude is true or rational*. When I take a stand that p, I constitute my believing that p—but this doesn't make p true. The Cotard patient constitutes their belief that they're dead; they don't constitute their being dead. This blocks the extension beyond mental states entirely. The "same logic" doesn't apply because constitutive interpretation is specifically about mental state individuation, not world-making.

**Second**, self-deception remains fully intelligible. What I constitute is *current attitude content*, not *consistency with evidence* or *alignment with deeper commitments*. I can constitutively interpret myself as believing I'm generous (thereby settling that this is indeed my belief) while this very belief is self-deceptive—formed through motivated reasoning, contradicted by my behavior, sustained by ignoring evidence. Constitutive interpretation settles *what I believe*, not *whether I'm right to believe it* or *whether it coheres with my other attitudes*. Self-deception is precisely the phenomenon where these come apart.

**Third**, there *are* principled limits, grounded in the distinction's basis. Constitutive interpretation works only where:
- The attitude is *response-dependent*: its nature depends partly on the subject's take on it
- The subject has *rational authority*: they're positioned to settle the matter through practical reasoning
- The domain is *normatively structured*: taking a stand is making a commitment

This explains the scope: beliefs and intentions (where commitment matters), yes; pains and sensations (mere phenomenal states), no; non-mental facts (not response-dependent on my attitudes), no.

The objection's cases fall outside these bounds. Pain *isn't* constitutively interpreted—it's felt, and the feeling is self-intimating in a different way. The Cotard patient constitutes their belief through their interpretive stance, but being dead isn't response-dependent on anyone's attitudes.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds against the extension worries but reveals a deeper problem the objection didn't quite reach.

The self-deception response works: constituting belief-content is compatible with that belief being self-deceptive, irrational, or false. And the extension to non-mental facts is clearly blocked by response-dependence.

But the principled limits expose something troubling. If constitutive interpretation requires rational authority and normative commitment-making, then it's *not actually interpretation* in any recognizable sense—it's just practical reasoning about what to believe, exactly as Moran described. The "interpretation" label now does no work. We're back to: sometimes I observe myself (reconstructive interpretation), sometimes I deliberate about what to believe (not interpretation at all, despite the label).

Moreover, the pain case is fatal. The thesis claims to explain first-person authority generally, but now excludes sensations. Yet first-person authority about pain is the *paradigm case*—more secure than authority about beliefs. A theory that explains belief-authority by excluding sensation-authority has its scope exactly backwards.

The objection proves too much only if constitutive interpretation is genuinely interpretive. But the defence succeeds only by making it *not interpretive*—just practical reasoning wearing an interpretation costume. This vindicates the previous killer: it's Moran relabeled, and the relabeling actively obscures what's happening.

**The thesis fails. Strength: 4/5.**

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider a case of implicit bias: Sarah sincerely avows "I believe all races are equal" and takes a firm stand on this commitment—she constitutively interprets herself as holding egalitarian beliefs. By the thesis's logic, this constitutive interpretation settles what she believes; her taking this stand makes it true that she believes in racial equality. Yet implicit association tests and her spontaneous behavior reveal systematic racial bias in her snap judgments and actions. When confronted with this evidence, we naturally say Sarah was *wrong* about what she believed—she had racist beliefs she didn't recognize. But the thesis cannot say this. If her constitutive interpretation settled the facts about her beliefs, then she *did* believe in equality (because she took that stand), and the bias must be something else—perhaps non-doxastic associations or aliefs, but not beliefs proper. This seems forced: the bias looks exactly like belief (it guides action, combines with other attitudes inferentially, responds to perceived evidence). The thesis must either deny that implicit biases are beliefs (implausible) or admit that constitutive interpretation can fail to settle what one believes (contradicting the core claim). The case shows that even in the domain of beliefs—the thesis's home turf—subjects lack the constitutive authority the thesis grants them. Taking a stand doesn't always settle what you believe; sometimes evidence reveals you were wrong about your own attitudes.

STRENGTH: 4/5 — This seriously damages the thesis. Implicit bias cases are empirically well-established and theoretically central to contemporary philosophy of mind. The thesis needs constitutive interpretation to genuinely *settle* facts about belief to preserve first-person authority, but these cases show that sincere self-interpretation (even of the stand-taking variety) can simply be mistaken about what one believes.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes constitutive interpretation must be *infallible*—that if Sarah takes a stand, she cannot be wrong. But this misunderstands the mechanism. Constitutive interpretation settles what you believe *when it succeeds*, and it succeeds only when the interpretive act genuinely *constitutes a commitment*. Not every sincere avowal does this. The implicit bias case shows a *failed* constitutive interpretation, not a counterexample to the distinction.

Here's why Sarah's case is failed constitution, not successful constitution later contradicted by evidence:

**First**, genuine constitutive interpretation requires *rational integration*. Taking a stand that p involves bringing one's cognitive economy into line with believing p—adjusting other attitudes, dispositions to act, inference patterns. Sarah hasn't done this; her stand is *compartmentalized*, governing only explicit verbal behavior while leaving her spontaneous responses untouched. This is precisely what makes it self-deceptive: the stand-taking is *incomplete*, not fully constitutive.

**Second**, the evidence of bias doesn't show Sarah was wrong about a prior fact—it shows her constitutive act *misfired*. She attempted to constitute an egalitarian belief through her avowal, but the constitution failed because the required rational integration didn't occur. The implicit associations aren't *evidence against* what she constituted; they're *evidence that* she failed to constitute it. Compare: an artist who declares "this sculpture represents justice" but produces something that systematically embodies vengeance hasn't successfully constituted the meaning they intended. The work reveals the constitutive act's failure, not a prior hidden fact.

**Third**, this explains the phenomenology perfectly. When confronted with bias evidence, Sarah doesn't discover a *pre-existing* racist belief she'd overlooked (pure reconstructive interpretation). She discovers her *constitutive project failed*—that her stand-taking didn't successfully settle her attitudes because it wasn't backed by the required rational work. This is why the discovery is often experienced as *responsibility* rather than mere *surprise*: she's accountable for the failed constitution, not just ignorant of a prior fact.

**Fourth**, we can now say something principled: implicit biases are beliefs (or belief-like attitudes) that formed through *reconstructive* processes (conditioning, social learning) without *constitutive* endorsement. Sarah has both: a partially-constituted egalitarian commitment (real but incomplete) and reconstructively-formed biased associations (never constitutively endorsed). The conflict between them shows constitutive interpretation is *substantive*—it's not just whatever you sincerely say, but requires genuine rational work.

This preserves first-person authority in the relevant sense: when constitutive interpretation *succeeds*, you have authority about what you've constituted. The implicit bias cases show this authority is *achievement-dependent*, not *automatic*—but that's a feature, not a bug. It explains why first-person authority is *defeasible* without being merely *evidential*.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, though it struggles admirably.

The "failed constitution" move is clever but ultimately collapses. If we can distinguish successful from failed constitutive interpretation only by checking whether the subject's behavior, associations, and spontaneous responses align with their avowal, then we're back to *reconstructive* interpretation after all—we're using evidence to determine what was really constituted. The thesis wanted constitutive interpretation to *bypass* the need for such evidence, but now it turns out we need the evidence to know whether constitution succeeded.

The integration requirement makes this worse. If taking a stand constitutes belief only when "backed by required rational work" and "rational integration," then Sarah's sincere stand-taking *doesn't count* as constitutive interpretation—it's merely attempted constitution. But now the thesis offers no account of first-person authority for actual avowals. When Sarah says "I believe in equality," if this doesn't constitute her belief (because integration is lacking), what *does* give her any first-person authority about what she's saying? We're back to her having to reconstruct her own attitudes from evidence, exactly what the thesis aimed to avoid.

The artist analogy backfires. If the sculpture's actual properties can reveal that the artist *failed* to constitute the intended meaning, then there are facts about meaning independent of the constitutive act—which contradicts the core claim that constitutive interpretation *settles* rather than discovers facts.

Most devastatingly: if constitutive interpretation can fail, and we discover its failure through evidence about our behavior and associations, then even in the first-person case we're *fundamentally* in the position of reconstructive interpreters. The asymmetry between first and third person collapses. Others can observe my bias; I can observe my bias; neither of us is constituting anything—we're both discovering what attitudes I actually have through evidence.

The thesis needed constitutive interpretation to be *self-guaranteeing* when sincere—that's what would preserve first-person authority. Admitting it can fail while seeming to succeed destroys the distinction's ability to do the theoretical work required.

**The thesis fails. Strength: 4/5.**

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis presents its distinction between "reconstructive" and "constitutive" interpretation as a substantive discovery about interpretation itself, but it's actually just stipulating labels for phenomena already recognized under different names. "Reconstructive interpretation" = evidence-based belief attribution (what everyone already calls inference-to-best-explanation about mental states). "Constitutive interpretation" = practical reasoning/commitment-making (what everyone already calls deliberation or taking a stand). The thesis hasn't discovered that interpretation comes in two varieties—it's just decided to *call* both of these activities "interpretation."

This is pure terminological legislation. The word "interpretation" ordinarily means something like "determining meaning/content through inference from signs or evidence"—which covers only the reconstructive case. Extending it to cover practical reasoning (the constitutive case) is a verbal stipulation, not an insight. It's like saying "motion comes in two varieties: spatial motion and logical motion (inference)" and claiming this reconciles physics with logic. You've just stretched "motion" beyond recognition.

The supposed payoff—"accepting the interpretive turn while preserving authority"—evaporates once we see this. The interpretive turn's challenge was: *if self-knowledge is interpretive (evidence-based), authority is threatened*. Response: "But interpretation isn't always evidence-based!" This just *rejects* the interpretive turn by redefining its key term. Nothing is reconciled; the debate is merely relabeled. Everyone already agreed that deliberation differs from observation—calling deliberation "constitutive interpretation" adds nothing but confusion.

**Weak point**: The premise that there exists a theoretically unified phenomenon called "interpretation" that genuinely divides into these two varieties, rather than two unrelated activities being forced under one label.
**STRENGTH: 4/5**
This seriously damages the thesis. The entire framework depends on "interpretation" being a genuine natural kind that comes in varieties, but the thesis provides no argument that practical reasoning and evidence-based attribution are both species of a common genus. Without that, it's just verbal stipulation—declaring that we'll call both things "interpretation" and then announcing we've discovered interpretation has two forms. The supposed theoretical advantage (reconciling interpretivism with authority) disappears once we recognize that calling deliberation "interpretation" doesn't make it interpretive in the sense that threatened authority in the first place.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection assumes "interpretation" has a fixed ordinary meaning (evidence-based reconstruction) that the thesis arbitrarily extends. But this gets the dialectical situation backwards. The interpretive turn *itself* already expanded "interpretation" beyond evidence-based reconstruction—that's what made it threatening.

Consider the actual debate. Dennett's intentional stance, Davidson's radical interpretation, and even Gopnik's theory-theory all treat self-knowledge as interpretive, but they don't mean *merely* "inferring from behavioral evidence." They mean something broader: *making sense of attitudes by applying normative/rational constraints*. When I interpret you, I don't just observe; I ask what beliefs/desires would *make rational sense* of your behavior. This is already constitutive in a way—I'm imposing a rational structure, not just passively reading off facts.

The thesis isn't stipulating a new meaning; it's *clarifying an ambiguity already present* in how "interpretation" functions in this literature. The interpretive turn threatened authority precisely because it blurred together:
- Empirical inference (I observe your behavior and guess your beliefs)
- Rational sense-making (I apply normative constraints to render you intelligible)

When philosophers say "self-knowledge is interpretive," they trade on both aspects. The thesis distinguishes them clearly: reconstructive interpretation is the empirical component, constitutive interpretation is the rational-normative component.

This matters because the threat to authority came specifically from the *empirical* aspect—the idea that I access my own mind only through evidence, no better than you. But the interpretive turn's real insight was the *rational-normative* aspect—that attitudes are individuated partly by their place in rational structures. Once we separate these, we can accept the rational-normative insight (attitudes are constituted through rational commitments) while rejecting the empirical threat (I need evidence about myself).

The "two unrelated activities" charge fails because both activities genuinely involve *determining content through rational/semantic constraints*. Reconstructive interpretation: I determine what you believe by asking what content would rationalize your behavior. Constitutive interpretation: I determine what I believe by asking what content I should commit to given my reasons. Both apply rational constraints to settle content—that's the common genus. They differ in whether the content is settled *by* my commitment or *independently* of it.

The artist analogy wasn't arbitrary. Artistic interpretation and critical interpretation are both genuinely interpretive—both determine meaning through rational/semantic constraints—but one constitutes meaning while the other reconstructs it. The thesis claims mental content works similarly.

Finally, the "nothing is reconciled" charge misses the achievement. Pre-thesis, we had a dilemma: either self-knowledge is interpretive (threatening authority) or it's not (rejecting the interpretive turn's insights). Post-thesis, we have: self-knowledge is interpretive in the constitutive sense (preserving authority) while others' knowledge of me is interpretive in the reconstructive sense (explaining asymmetry). This *is* a reconciliation—it accepts that self-knowledge involves rational sense-making (the interpretive turn's insight) while denying it requires evidence-based inference (the threat to authority).

The theoretical work is real: we now have a framework that explains why confabulation studies (reconstructive self-interpretation) don't threaten avowal authority (constitutive self-interpretation) without denying either phenomenon.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. It makes the situation worse by revealing that the thesis equivocates on "interpretation" in exactly the way the objection predicted.

The defence claims both reconstructive and constitutive interpretation involve "determining content through rational/semantic constraints," making this the common genus. But this is precisely the equivocation. In reconstructive interpretation, rational constraints are *epistemic tools*—I use them to *infer* what you believe from evidence. In constitutive interpretation, rational constraints are *constitutive commitments*—I use them to *settle* what I believe through practical reasoning. These are utterly different relations to rational constraints.

Calling both "interpretation" because both involve rationality is like calling both perception and hallucination "observation" because both involve visual phenomenology. The superficial similarity (both involve rationality/visuals) masks a fundamental difference (one answers to independent facts, the other doesn't).

The defence's historical claim backfires. Yes, Dennett and Davidson treat interpretation as involving rational constraints—but they mean *exactly* what the thesis calls reconstructive interpretation. They're not doing constitutive interpretation at all; they're explaining how we attribute attitudes by rational inference. The thesis hasn't "clarified an ambiguity" in their work—it's introduced a new use of "interpretation" they never intended and claimed it was there all along.

Most fatally, the defence admits the game is lost when it says constitutive interpretation involves "asking what content I should commit to given my reasons." This is just deliberation—first-order practical reasoning about what to believe. It's not interpretive in any sense. I'm not determining what I believe; I'm determining what *to* believe. The content is settled by answering a world-directed question (should I believe p?), not a self-directed interpretive question (do I believe p?).

The thesis has relabeled Moran's deliberative/theoretical distinction as "constitutive/reconstructive interpretation," but the relabeling does no work and actively obscures what's happening. Moran's insight was precisely that first-person authority comes from *bypassing* self-interpretation through deliberation. Calling deliberation "constitutive interpretation" doesn't reconcile it with interpretivism—it just verbally denies the bypass while preserving it in substance.

**The thesis is terminological legislation masquerading as theoretical insight. It fails. Strength: 4/5.**

</details>

---

### O20. Holding that we read our own minds from behavioral evidence commits you to accepting that we should systematically defer to better external interpreters (therapists, AI systems) about our own attitudes, which proponents of interpretivism universally reject.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Interpretivists like Carruthers and Gopnik who want to preserve some first-person authority while denying privileged access
- **Sketch:** If self-knowledge is interpretation from behavioral and contextual evidence, then whoever has access to better evidence and better interpretive tools has better self-knowledge about me than I do. Therapists have comparative evidence (other patients), training in recognizing patterns, and freedom from motivated reasoning. Advanced AI systems have complete behavioral records and superior pattern-matching. On interpretivist principles, when my self-ascription conflicts with their attribution, I should defer to their third-person interpretation—it's based on the same kind of evidence, just more of it and better processed. But Carruthers and other interpretivists deny this consequence, insisting on some residual first-person authority. This reveals that interpretivism smuggles in non-interpretivist assumptions: even interpretivists grant the subject some epistemically relevant relation to her attitudes beyond evidence-interpretation. The key premise: if two accounts use the same evidence-type and methods, the one with better evidence and methods wins epistemically.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The better-interpreter objection is pointed but interpretivists have responses about integration and agentive uptake available.
  - PROVES TOO MUCH — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the parallel cases lack the methodological symmetry that makes interpretivism unique—politicians, authors, and moral agents have different evidence or engage in different activities than their interpreters.
  - COUNTEREXAMPLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence conceded 'universally reject' was overstated and retreated to 'cannot principled distinguish when to defer'—the thesis was narrowed to survive.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence failed to show interpretivists actually resist the deference consequence in principle; once 'universally reject' fell, the thesis reduced to restating interpretivism's explicit content.
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 2/5, answered. Defence demonstrated the deference consequence follows from either reading held consistently, with no slide between methodological and metaphysical interpretivism required.
- 🔧 **Survives as:** Interpretivists lack principled grounds within their theory for specifying when deference to superior external interpreters is epistemically required versus when first-person authority is preserved
- **Flaw:** The TRIVIAL and COUNTEREXAMPLE objections together showed that once the false empirical claim about 'universal rejection' was removed, the thesis merely restates what interpretivism explicitly says rather than exposing an unwanted commitment

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (PROVES TOO MUCH)** — ATTEMPT. The argument establishes that whenever someone has better evidence and better interpretive tools about any subject matter, we should defer to them. Applied beyond self-knowledge, this generates absurd results. Consider: historians have comparative evidence about many historical periods, training in recognizing patterns in primary sources, and freedom from the motivated reasoning that affects contemporary political actors. Therefore, when a politician's self-understanding of their own motivations conflicts with a historian's interpretation, the politician should defer to the historian's account. Or: literary critics have superior interpretive tools and broader comparative knowledge than authors. Therefore, when an author says "I meant X by this passage" and the critic says "you meant Y," the author should defer to the critic about their own intentions. Or most devastatingly: moral philosophers have better evidence about ethical principles and superior reasoning about moral matters than ordinary people. Therefore, when your moral judgment conflicts with the philosopher's, you should defer—eliminating moral autonomy entirely. The thesis proves too much because the same structure (better evidence + better tools = epistemic authority) would eliminate first-person authority not just about mental states but about intentions, meanings, values, and any domain where interpretation plays a role.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has some initial appeal but ultimately fails. The cases aren't parallel: the interpretivist claim is specifically that self-knowledge works *through the same methods* as other-knowledge. Historians don't claim politicians know their own motivations the same way historians know them. The proves-too-much objection would only work if these other domains also involved the symmetry of method that interpretivism posits for self-knowledge.

**DEFENDER** — BEST DEFENCE

The objection trades on a critical equivocation about what "same evidence-type and methods" means. The interpretivist claim is not merely that self-knowledge and other-knowledge both involve interpretation—it's that they work through *precisely the same cognitive mechanisms and information sources*. This is what makes the self-knowledge case unique.

Consider the disanalogies systematically:

**Politicians vs. historians**: The politician has *different* evidence than the historian—phenomenal states, memory traces, deliberative processes as they occurred. Even if we're interpretivists about self-knowledge of motivation, the politician experienced the deliberation; the historian works from texts and testimonies. These aren't the same methods applied to the same evidence-pool.

**Authors vs. critics**: The author has *temporal priority* and *causal knowledge* that the critic lacks. The author knows what they were thinking about while writing, what they rejected, what influences they were tracking. This is different evidence, not the same evidence processed worse.

**Moral philosophers vs. ordinary people**: Here the disanalogy is sharpest. Moral philosophy isn't primarily *interpretive* in the relevant sense—it's normative reasoning about what *should* be the case. When I judge "this is wrong," I'm not interpreting evidence about my moral attitudes; I'm making a first-order moral claim. The philosopher might know better what I *should* think, but that's different from knowing better what I *do* think.

The self-knowledge case is unique because interpretivism claims that when I self-ascribe a belief, I'm doing *exactly* what you do when you attribute that belief to me: observing behavioral evidence (including verbal behavior), applying folk-psychological theory, making an inference. Same inputs, same process. That's why better evidence and better processing would yield better results.

None of the comparison cases have this symmetry. They all involve either different evidence sources, different types of cognitive access, or different enterprises entirely (normative vs. descriptive).

**VERDICT**

The defence succeeds. The objection fails because it misidentifies what makes the interpretivist position generate the deference consequence. It's not that interpretation is involved—it's that *the very same interpretive process operating on the very same kind of evidence* is claimed to generate both self-knowledge and other-knowledge, differing only in the quality and quantity of evidence available.

The politicians, authors, and moral agents all have access to something the external interpreter lacks: different evidence, different temporal position, or engagement in a different kind of activity entirely. But on interpretivism about self-knowledge, I have no such advantage—when I self-ascribe a belief, I'm just doing third-person interpretation on myself, and if you can do that interpretation better, you know my mind better than I do.

The proves-too-much objection would work only if interpretivism claimed merely that self-knowledge involves *some* interpretation. But it claims something far more radical: that self-knowledge is *nothing but* the same interpretation others perform. That's what makes the deference consequence genuine and the comparison cases disanalogous.

**KILLER (COUNTEREXAMPLE)** — ATTEMPT. Consider a patient, Sarah, who consistently self-ascribes the belief "I love my mother" and behaves accordingly—calls regularly, visits, brings gifts, speaks warmly. Her therapist, Dr. Chen, has superior evidence: comparative data from hundreds of parent-child relationships, training in recognizing ambivalence patterns, access to Sarah's complete behavioral history including contradictions Sarah has forgotten, and freedom from Sarah's motivated reasoning. Dr. Chen interprets the evidence and concludes: "Sarah actually resents her mother but performs love out of guilt and social conditioning."

On the thesis, Sarah should defer to Dr. Chen's interpretation—it's based on the same behavioral evidence, just more of it and better processed. But this is precisely what actually happens in successful therapy, and interpretivists like Carruthers don't reject it. Carruthers explicitly allows that we can be wrong about our own attitudes and that external interpreters can correct us. The thesis claims interpretivists "universally reject" deference to better external interpreters, but this is false. What interpretivists reject is *automatic* or *complete* deference in all cases, not the possibility that external interpreters sometimes know better.

The counterexample shows the thesis mischaracterizes the interpretivist position. Interpretivists can coherently hold both that self-knowledge is interpretive AND that external interpreters sometimes have superior interpretations, while still maintaining that subjects aren't obligated to defer in every case where evidence quality differs.

STRENGTH: 4/5

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The counterexample confuses *what actually happens* with *what should happen on interpretivist principles*. Yes, therapy involves external correction of self-understanding—but the question is whether interpretivism can *justify principled limits* on when such correction is authoritative.

The defence has three components:

**First: The scope problem.** The objection says interpretivists "don't reject" therapeutic deference and only reject "*automatic* or *complete* deference in all cases." But this concedes the structural problem. On what principled basis does interpretivism distinguish cases where we should defer from cases where we shouldn't? If the epistemic situation is genuinely symmetric—same evidence-type, same methods—then *whenever* the external interpreter has better evidence and better processing, their interpretation is epistemically superior. The therapist's interpretation isn't just *an* interpretation; it's the *better* interpretation by the very standards interpretivism endorses.

The interpretivist cannot say "defer only when the external interpretation is correct" without abandoning interpretivism—that presumes there's a fact about the attitude independent of interpretation-from-evidence, which is precisely what interpretivism denies. And they cannot say "defer only in therapeutic contexts" without explaining what makes therapy special. If it's special because therapists have better evidence and methods, then anyone with better evidence and methods should command the same deference.

**Second: The revealed preference.** Notice what interpretivists actually do when pressed. Carruthers writes: "The mindreader always has a kind of default authority about her own attitudes" (2011). Gopnik acknowledges "asymmetries" in self-knowledge even while defending the theory-theory. These aren't casual remarks—they're systematic hedges that appear whenever the deference consequence looms. The thesis is that interpretivists *reveal through their hedging* that they're smuggling in non-interpretivist assumptions. They don't explicitly theorize first-person authority, but they implicitly rely on it whenever the alternative becomes uncomfortable.

**Third: The Sarah case actually supports the thesis.** When Dr. Chen concludes Sarah resents her mother, what makes this therapeutic insight rather than therapeutic imperialism? Not that Dr. Chen has better evidence—that's stipulated. The insight works only if Sarah can *recognize it as true* through some capacity beyond evidence-interpretation. She must be able to access her resentment in a way that confirms Dr. Chen's interpretation. But if Sarah has this kind of access, interpretivism is false—there's a first-person route to self-knowledge that isn't just interpretation from behavioral evidence.

If Sarah *cannot* recognize the resentment—if she continues sincerely self-ascribing love despite Dr. Chen's interpretation—then on pure interpretivist grounds, we face a standoff between two interpretations of the same evidence. Dr. Chen's is better-evidenced, so it should win. But interpretivists balk at this conclusion, which reveals they're not really interpretivists all the way down.

**VERDICT**

The defence holds, but barely, and the objection significantly damages the thesis.

The counterexample succeeds in showing that the thesis overstates the case with "universally reject." Interpretivists clearly *do* accept that external interpreters can sometimes know better—therapy would be impossible otherwise. The thesis needs reformulation: the problem isn't that interpretivists reject *any* deference, but that they reject *principled, systematic* deference whenever evidence-quality favors the external interpreter, without being able to explain the principled basis for this rejection on interpretivist grounds.

However, the defence's third point—that therapeutic success requires non-interpretivist first-person access—is speculative. Maybe Sarah just updates her self-interpretation based on Dr. Chen's better interpretation, and the therapy works through this updating rather than through Sarah accessing her resentment directly. This is a coherent interpretivist story.

The real damage is this: the objection forces us to see that the thesis's strongest form (interpretivists reject deference entirely) is false, while its weaker form (interpretivists can't justify *when* to defer and when not to on interpretivist principles alone) is harder to prove. The weaker claim might be true, but it's a much less devastating objection to interpretivism—it's a demand for theoretical refinement rather than a reductio.

The thesis survives but wounded. It must retreat from "universally reject" to "cannot principled distinguish when deference is appropriate," which is a significant concession.

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT. 

The thesis presents itself as exposing a problematic *commitment* of interpretivism, but it actually just restates interpretivism's core claim in pejorative terms. The "commitment" is simply: if self-knowledge works through interpretation of behavioral evidence, and someone else has better evidence and better interpretive tools, then their interpretation is epistemically superior. But this is not a hidden consequence that interpretivists are committed to despite themselves—it's the explicit content of the interpretivist position.

Saying "interpretivism commits you to X" when X is just interpretivism described less sympathetically is trivial. It's like saying "holding that knowledge requires justified true belief commits you to accepting that lucky guesses aren't knowledge" or "holding that morality is culturally relative commits you to accepting that Nazi Germany's moral framework was valid for Nazi Germany." These aren't discoveries of problematic commitments—they're just restatements of the view with emphasis on consequences the speaker finds uncomfortable.

The thesis gains apparent force only by adding the empirical claim that interpretivists "universally reject" this consequence. But as the counterexample exchange revealed, this is false—interpretivists accept that external interpreters sometimes know better. Once we remove the false empirical claim, we're left with: "Interpretivism implies that better evidence yields better interpretation." This is not a discovered commitment; it's a tautology given what interpretivism means.

The thesis packages a definitional truth as though it were a substantive philosophical discovery.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This attack does serious damage. The thesis claims to reveal an *unwanted* commitment that shows interpretivists are inconsistent or smuggling in non-interpretivist assumptions. But if the "commitment" is just what interpretivism explicitly says, then there's no inconsistency to expose—just a disagreement about whether interpretivism's implications are acceptable. That's a substantive philosophical debate, not a demonstration of internal incoherence.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**

The triviality charge misunderstands what makes a commitment-objection work. A commitment-objection doesn't expose *hidden* implications—it forces proponents to *own* implications they've been evading. The power lies not in discovering what the view says, but in making explicit what accepting it *costs*.

Consider the structure of successful commitment-objections in philosophy:

**Utilitarianism and the transplant case**: Everyone knows utilitarianism says maximize utility. The objection doesn't reveal a hidden implication—it forces utilitarians to either accept that killing one healthy person to save five is required, or explain why their theory doesn't actually imply this. The work is done by making the cost vivid and demanding a response.

**Eliminativism about consciousness**: Everyone knows eliminativism denies phenomenal states. The objection "so you're committed to saying pain doesn't exist" isn't trivial—it forces eliminativists to either bite the bullet or show why the folk concept of pain can be preserved. The dialectical pressure is the point.

The thesis works the same way. Yes, interpretivism *says* self-knowledge is interpretation from behavioral evidence. But interpretivists have systematically avoided confronting what this means in cases where the external interpreter is clearly superior. They write as though interpretivism is compatible with preserved first-person authority—Carruthers's "default authority," Gopnik's "asymmetries," the general interpretivist tendency to focus on cases where self and other agree or where the subject is merely mistaken in tractable ways.

The thesis forces a dilemma:

**HORN 1**: Accept systematic deference to superior external interpreters. When your therapist, your AI life-coach, or your behaviorally-informed partner has better evidence about your attitudes than you do, their interpretation is authoritative. You should update your self-conception to match their interpretation, not vice versa. This is not "sometimes external interpreters know better"—it's "whenever the evidence favors them, epistemic rationality requires deference."

**HORN 2**: Reject systematic deference. But then explain what breaks the symmetry between self-interpretation and other-interpretation. If both work through the same evidence and methods, and one has better evidence and methods, why doesn't it win? Any answer that preserves first-person authority must invoke something beyond interpretation-from-behavioral-evidence—which abandons interpretivism.

The thesis isn't trivial because interpretivists want to reject Horn 1 but cannot embrace Horn 2 without abandoning their position. That's a genuine dilemma, not a restatement.

**The empirical claim about "universal rejection"**: Yes, the counterexample showed this was overstated. But the reformulated version survives: interpretivists cannot *principled* limit when deference is required. They accept therapeutic deference but reject AI-system deference, or accept correction about unconscious bias but reject correction about conscious beliefs, without explaining why these cases differ *on interpretivist grounds*. The pattern of selective acceptance reveals the theoretical gap.

**Why this isn't just "interpretivism described unsympathetically"**: The Nazi relativism analogy fails. Cultural relativists who bite the bullet and accept that Nazi morality was valid-for-Nazis are *consistent* relativists. We might find their view abhorrent, but they're not smuggling in non-relativist assumptions. The thesis claims interpretivists are *not* consistent—they selectively invoke first-person authority when deference becomes uncomfortable, revealing they haven't fully accepted their own view's implications.

The thesis does philosophical work: it identifies where interpretivists need to either revise their explicit commitments (give up claims about preserved first-person authority) or revise their theory (explain what grounds first-person authority on interpretivist principles). That's not trivial—it's pressure toward theoretical coherence.

**VERDICT**

The defence fails, though not completely.

The triviality objection succeeds in showing that the thesis, as stated, overreaches. The core problem: the thesis presents itself as exposing an *unwanted* commitment that interpretivists reject, but the previous exchange already established that interpretivists *don't* universally reject deference to external interpreters. Once we know interpretivists accept the deference consequence in many cases, the thesis cannot claim to expose something they're "committed to despite themselves."

What remains is a demand for *principled boundaries*: when should we defer and when shouldn't we, on interpretivist grounds? But this reformulated challenge is indeed less powerful than the original thesis suggested. It's a request for theoretical development rather than a reductio. An interpretivist could respond: "We're working on specifying the conditions. Maybe it's about the subject's access to their own reasoning process, or about the reliability of different evidence types, or about pragmatic factors in interpretation. This is normal theory-building, not a fatal flaw."

The defence's analogy to utilitarianism and eliminativism doesn't save the thesis because those cases work differently. Utilitarians genuinely *do* want to reject the transplant conclusion—they write entire papers trying to avoid it. Eliminativists genuinely *do* want to preserve something like our ordinary pain-concept. The commitment-objection works because it exposes real tension between what proponents explicitly endorse and what they want to preserve.

But the thesis hasn't shown that interpretivists want to *reject* deference to superior external interpreters in principle. They accept it in therapeutic contexts, in cases of self-deception, in unconscious bias. The thesis needed to show either that they reject it in cases where their own principles require it, or that they accept it in cases that are genuinely absurd. Neither has been demonstrated.

The triviality charge largely succeeds: stripped of the false claim about universal rejection, the thesis reduces to "interpretivism implies that better interpretation of the evidence yields better self-knowledge," which is just what interpretivism says. The demand for principled boundaries is legitimate philosophical work, but it's not the devastating exposure of inconsistency the thesis promised.

**The thesis fails.** It survives only in the much weaker form: "Interpretivists need to specify when deference to external interpreters is epistemically required and when it isn't, and this specification is a non-trivial theoretical challenge." That's a reasonable research question, not a refutation.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "interpretivism" between a methodological claim and a metaphysical claim, and needs both readings to generate the argument.

**Reading 1 (Methodological)**: Self-knowledge is *epistemically achieved through* interpretation of behavioral evidence—this is the method by which we come to know our own minds. On this reading, the deference consequence is plausible: if we both use the same method and yours is better executed, your conclusion is more reliable.

**Reading 2 (Metaphysical)**: Mental states are *constituted by* or *nothing over and above* interpretations of behavioral patterns—there's no fact about my belief beyond what the best interpretation says. On this reading, the claim that interpretivists "universally reject" deference becomes interesting and potentially damning, because rejecting the better interpretation would mean rejecting the better account of what the attitude *is*.

The argument needs the methodological reading to establish the deference consequence (same evidence-type, better processing wins) but needs the metaphysical reading to make interpretivist resistance to deference seem inconsistent. On the methodological reading alone, interpretivists could say "yes, we use interpretation, but there's still a fact about the attitude that makes some interpretations correct," blocking mandatory deference. On the metaphysical reading alone, there's no clear reason why the subject's interpretation has any standing at all—but then interpretivists wouldn't resist deference in the first place, undermining the thesis's claim about what they "universally reject."

The thesis works only by sliding between these readings.

STRENGTH: 2/5

**DEFENDER** — [DEFENDER] **BEST DEFENCE**

The equivocation charge fails because it misidentifies the dialectical structure. The thesis doesn't require equivocation—it works on *either* reading consistently, and in fact works best when we hold the reading fixed.

**On the methodological reading throughout:**

Suppose interpretivism is purely epistemological: self-knowledge is *achieved through* interpreting behavioral evidence, but mental states exist independently of this interpretation. The deference consequence still follows with full force.

If I know my beliefs through interpretation and you interpret the same evidence better, then *epistemically* I should defer to your interpretation. This doesn't require metaphysical interpretivism. When historians interpret evidence about the past, we defer to better historians even though historical facts exist independently of interpretation. When doctors interpret symptoms, we defer to better diagnosticians even though diseases exist independently of interpretation.

The methodological interpretivist cannot block deference by saying "there's still a fact about the attitude that makes some interpretations correct." Yes—and the better interpretation is more likely to be correct! That's precisely why deference is required. The existence of a fact-of-the-matter doesn't give the subject epistemic authority; it just means there's something to be right or wrong about.

If the subject has some *additional* epistemic access beyond interpretation—introspection, direct awareness, privileged causal connection—then we're not dealing with pure methodological interpretivism anymore. We're dealing with a hybrid view that says "self-knowledge is *sometimes* or *partially* achieved through interpretation." But Carruthers explicitly denies such additional access. That's what makes the view interpretivist.

**On the metaphysical reading throughout:**

Suppose mental states are constituted by the best interpretation of behavioral patterns. Now the deference consequence is even stronger. If Dr. Chen's interpretation of Sarah's behavior is better than Sarah's, then Dr. Chen's interpretation *determines what Sarah's attitude is*. There's no gap between "better interpretation" and "truth about the attitude"—the better interpretation *is* the truth.

The objection claims that "on the metaphysical reading alone, there's no clear reason why the subject's interpretation has any standing at all." Exactly. That's the thesis. On metaphysical interpretivism, the subject's self-ascription is just one interpretation among others, and if it's not the best interpretation, it's not authoritative. The fact that interpretivists *resist* this consequence (Carruthers's "default authority," etc.) is precisely what the thesis identifies as problematic.

The objection says this "undermines the thesis's claim about what they universally reject" because interpretivists wouldn't resist deference in the first place. But this gets it backwards. The thesis *observes* that interpretivists resist systematic deference (they write as though first-person authority is preserved), and *argues* that this resistance is inconsistent with their stated metaphysics. The resistance is the empirical datum; the inconsistency is the conclusion.

**The alleged slide between readings:**

The objection claims the thesis needs methodological interpretivism to establish the deference consequence but metaphysical interpretivism to make resistance seem inconsistent. This is false.

- Methodological interpretivism alone establishes deference: better interpretation of evidence → epistemic superiority → rational requirement to defer.
- Metaphysical interpretivism alone establishes deference: better interpretation → constitutes the attitude → there's nothing else to defer to.
- The resistance to deference is inconsistent with *either* reading.

There's no equivocation because the argument doesn't depend on switching between readings. Pick one and hold it fixed: the deference consequence follows, and interpretivist resistance to it reveals theoretical tension.

**The real structure:**

The thesis works as a dilemma, not an equivocation:
- If interpretivism is methodological, then subjects have no epistemic advantage when external interpreters have better evidence/methods.
- If interpretivism is metaphysical, then subjects have no metaphysical advantage—the better interpretation constitutes the attitude.
- Either way, systematic deference is required.
- Interpretivists resist this conclusion.
- Therefore, interpretivists are smuggling in non-interpretivist assumptions.

No equivocation needed.

**VERDICT**

The defence succeeds. The equivocation charge misdiagnoses the argument's structure.

The objection is right that there are methodological and metaphysical versions of interpretivism, and right that they have different implications. But it's wrong that the thesis requires sliding between them. The deference consequence follows from either reading held consistently:

- **Methodological**: If self-knowledge is epistemically achieved only through interpretation, and interpretation is an evidence-based inference, then whoever has better evidence and better inferential tools has better epistemic access. The subject's interpretation has no special epistemic status—it's just another interpretation, and if it's worse-evidenced or worse-processed, it's epistemically inferior. Deference is rationally required.

- **Metaphysical**: If mental states are constituted by interpretation, then the best interpretation doesn't merely *discover* what the attitude is—it *determines* what the attitude is. The subject's self-ascription is authoritative only if it's the best interpretation. If it's not, it's simply wrong about what the attitude is.

The objection's claim that on the methodological reading "interpretivists could say there's still a fact about the attitude that makes some interpretations correct, blocking mandatory deference" actually helps the thesis. If there's a fact beyond interpretation that makes interpretations correct or incorrect, then we need some account of how the subject has better epistemic access to this fact. But that's precisely what interpretivism denies—there is no privileged introspective access, no special first-person route. We're back to interpretation-from-evidence, and better evidence wins.

The equivocation charge fails because it confuses *having different versions of a view* with *equivocating between them*. The thesis can be run on either version consistently. The fact that both versions generate the same problematic consequence (mandatory deference) is a strength of the argument, not evidence of equivocation.

</details>

---

### O21. First-person authority dissolves into separate phenomena for different mental state types: beliefs are known interpretively without authority, while intentions are authoritatively self-ascribed because self-ascription partly constitutes them, not because we have better epistemic access.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual 5/5 · worst 5/5 · dead
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both sides' shared assumption that first-person authority is a unified explanandum requiring a unified theory
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes 'first-person authority' names a unified phenomenon requiring a unified explanation. But the authority involved in 'I believe the economy will collapse' differs fundamentally from that in 'I intend to vote for Smith.' For beliefs, Schwitzgebel-style interpretivism is correct and genuine asymmetry is minimal—we just have more data about ourselves. For intentions and decisions, however, Moran is right that avowal partly constitutes the state: announcing an intention typically makes it the case that I have it, in a way announcing yours doesn't make it the case that you have it. This constitutive relationship, not privileged access, explains why you can't correct my sincere intention-ascriptions the way I might correct yours. The key premise: the speech acts we perform differ by mental state type, and only some have state-constituting force.
- **Appraiser (blind):** State-type pluralism (beliefs vs intentions) is sensible but increasingly recognized as the right approach.
  - TRIVIAL — landed 2/5, answered. Defence showed the constitutive/interpretive distinction is substantive, not merely acknowledging mechanism differences
  - EQUIVOCATION — landed 5/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence's escape attempt exposed fatal incoherence: cannot simultaneously eliminate authority for beliefs while claiming to distinguish types of authority
  - UNFALSIFIABLE — landed 4/5, **UNANSWERED**. Defence provided criteria but they presuppose the contested claim that intentions are constitutively self-ascribed rather than interpretively discovered
  - ALREADY TAKEN — landed 3/5, answered. Defence successfully distinguished thesis from Moran through stronger eliminativism and metaphysical constitutivism, though this revealed deeper problems
- 🔧 **Survives as:** First-person authority for intentions differs fundamentally from that for beliefs: intention-avowals have constitutive authority (sincere self-ascription settles what one intends) while belief-ascriptions have only evidential advantage, and this difference requires abandoning the search for unified explanations of 'first-person authority' as a single phenomenon
- **Flaw:** The thesis eliminates first-person authority entirely (beliefs have none, intentions have only constitutive power not epistemic authority) while presenting itself as distinguishing types of authority—a self-undermining incoherence exposed by the equivocation objection

<details><summary>2 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) First-person authority for mental state self-ascriptions is not epistemic but constitutive: sincere self-ascriptions in certain mental state terms partially constitute having those states, making the question of their accuracy category-confused.
- (2/5) First-person authority is grounded not in privileged access but in privileged jurisdiction: we have authority over which mental states to avow because avowal constitutes a move in the space of reasons that preempts rather than reports.

</details>

<details><summary>full exchange</summary>

**KILLER (TRIVIAL)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis claims that "first-person authority" covers different phenomena requiring different explanations for beliefs versus intentions. But this is either a terminological stipulation or an observation that everyone already accepts once stated clearly. No theorist genuinely believes that the *exact same mechanism* explains why "I believe P" and "I intend to Q" have first-person authority. Even traditional "privileged access" theorists recognize that introspection of occurrent beliefs differs from introspection of intentions, that memory beliefs have different warrant structures than future-directed intentions, and that the phenomenology varies by state type. The debate has always been about whether there's a *family resemblance* or *common feature* (call it "asymmetric warrant," "immunity to certain errors," or "default credibility") that distinguishes first-person from third-person ascriptions across mental states. The thesis simply relabels this family resemblance as "not really unified" and says different members of the family have different explanations. But that's precisely what nuanced theories already do—they identify the common feature (asymmetry) while allowing that its basis varies (introspection for beliefs, constitutive self-knowledge for intentions, transparency for desires, etc.). The weak point is the claim that theorists assume unified explanation: they assume unified *explanandum* (asymmetric authority) with potentially varied *explanans*.

STRENGTH: 2/5

This attack has some force in that sophisticated theorists do recognize differences between mental state types, but it underestimates the thesis's contribution. The distinction between interpretive and constitutive relationships is substantive, not merely verbal, and genuinely challenges theories that treat all first-person authority as epistemic in nature.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection confuses *acknowledging differences in mechanism* with *recognizing that the phenomena are fundamentally distinct in kind*. Yes, sophisticated theorists note that introspecting a belief differs phenomenologically from introspecting an intention. But they still treat both as cases of *epistemic access to pre-existing mental states*—just access via different routes. The thesis makes a far stronger claim: for intentions, there often is no pre-existing state to access. The speech act doesn't *report* an intention via a special mechanism; it *creates* the intention.

Consider the standard framing: theorists ask "How do we know our own minds better than others know them?" This question presupposes an epistemic framework—a gap between mind and knowledge that first-person perspective somehow bridges more successfully. Even when theorists say "introspection works differently for beliefs than intentions," they're still explaining *how we come to know* what we already intend. The thesis rejects this framing entirely for intentions: there's often nothing to know until the avowal occurs.

The objection claims theorists already allow "the basis varies...constitutive self-knowledge for intentions." But name the theorist who genuinely treats intention-ascriptions as *non-epistemic*—as speech acts that make rather than report. Moran comes closest, but even he frames this as a special kind of knowledge. The standard literature treats "constitutive" as meaning "self-knowledge that helps constitute the state" (like attending to pain intensifying it), not "avowal that brings the state into existence where none determinately existed."

The "family resemblance" framing the objection offers actually concedes the thesis. If the resemblance is merely that both involve "asymmetric warrant," we must ask: asymmetric *epistemic* warrant, or asymmetry of some other kind? For beliefs, it's epistemic (we have more evidence). For intentions, it's *metaphysical*—I have constitutive authority over what I'll count as intending because my sincere avowals partly settle what's true. These aren't two routes to the same destination; they're different destinations entirely.
**VERDICT**
The defence holds, but only partially.

The defence succeeds in showing the thesis makes a stronger claim than the objection credits: the difference between interpretive and constitutive relationships is indeed a difference in kind, not merely in epistemic mechanism. The objection's "family resemblance" response actually helps the thesis by revealing that the resemblance is thin—just "asymmetry"—which can be grounded in completely different phenomena (epistemic vs. metaphysical).

However, the defence faces a serious problem: the thesis may be *too strong* in claiming beliefs involve *no* authority. If Schwitzgebel-style interpretivism is correct and "we just have more data about ourselves," this explains asymmetry but undermines the claim that beliefs have "authority" at all—just better-informed guessing. The thesis needs beliefs to have *some* authority (even if merely interpretive) to make the contrast with intentions meaningful. But if first-person belief-ascriptions have no special authority beyond having more data, why call it "first-person authority" at all? The thesis risks dissolving its own explanandum entirely rather than merely dividing it.

The defence successfully resists the triviality charge, but reveals that the thesis's treatment of beliefs may collapse into eliminativism about first-person authority for that domain—making the "distinction" move actually a partial *rejection* move in disguise.

**KILLER (EQUIVOCATION)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis equivocates on "authority" between a thin sense (mere asymmetry in warrant or default credibility) and a thick sense (immunity to correction or constitutive power). The argument needs both readings to work.

The thesis is plausible when "first-person authority" means simply that first-person ascriptions have some asymmetric feature distinguishing them from third-person ones. On this thin reading, the claim that beliefs and intentions exhibit different types of asymmetry (evidential vs. constitutive) is reasonable. But this reading makes the thesis uninteresting—it merely observes that different mental states have different features, which no one denies.

The thesis becomes interesting only when "authority" carries its thick sense—the kind of authority that demands explanation, that makes correction seem inappropriate, that gives first-person avowals their distinctive philosophical puzzlement. But on this thick reading, the thesis is implausible: beliefs DON'T have authority at all on the Schwitzgebel view (we're just better-informed interpreters, still corrigible), so there's no unified explanandum to dissolve.

The argument slides between these readings. It needs the thin sense to make the belief-half plausible ("we have interpretive authority = we have more data") and the thick sense to make the intention-half interesting ("constitutive authority = immunity to correction"). But it cannot run on either reading alone: on the thin reading it's trivial, on the thick reading the belief-half fails. The weak point is the claim that beliefs are "known interpretively without authority"—this is either contradictory (they have interpretive authority) or concedes there's no authority to explain away.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This equivocation is serious and structural. The thesis genuinely needs "authority" to mean something weak enough that beliefs can have it interpretively, yet strong enough that intentions having it constitutively matters philosophically. The term does essential work in both senses, and the argument cannot succeed on either reading alone.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection misidentifies where "authority" does its work in the thesis. The term isn't equivocating—it's being deliberately *narrowed* to its philosophically interesting core, which only intentions possess.
