The appearance of equivocation comes from the objection's insistence on reading "interpretive authority" as a kind of authority, when the thesis explicitly denies this.

Look at the thesis statement: beliefs are known interpretively "without authority." This isn't contradictory—it's the point. What we call "first-person authority" for beliefs is a misnomer: it's just asymmetric evidence, which doesn't deserve the term "authority" at all. The Schwitzgebel view isn't saying we have *interpretive authority*; it's saying we have *more interpretive resources*, which is completely different. You have more data about your own life than I do, but this doesn't give you *authority* about it—just better information.

The thesis uses "authority" univocally in the thick sense throughout. The claim is:
- Beliefs: no genuine authority exists; the appearance of authority is just evidential asymmetry (eliminativism about authority for beliefs)
- Intentions: genuine authority exists, grounded constitutively (explanation of real authority for intentions)

This isn't equivocation; it's *diagnostic dissolution*. The thesis says the debate has been confused because it lumped together (1) cases where apparent authority is really just information asymmetry, and (2) cases where real authority exists for non-epistemic reasons. The interesting philosophical phenomenon—immunity to correction, the inappropriateness of others contradicting sincere avowals—only exists for intentions and similar states.

The objection claims this makes the belief-half "uninteresting," but that's wrong. It's philosophically significant to show that what everyone thought was a unified puzzle (first-person authority across mental states) is actually two completely different situations: a pseudo-puzzle based on conflating evidential advantage with authority, and a real puzzle about constitutive self-determination. Showing that half the alleged phenomenon doesn't exist is not trivial—it's deflationary progress.

The objection's "thin vs. thick" distinction actually helps the thesis. On the thin reading (mere asymmetry), both beliefs and intentions qualify, but the thesis explains why: beliefs have evidential asymmetry, intentions have constitutive asymmetry. On the thick reading (immunity to correction), only intentions qualify, and the thesis explains why: constitutive relationships create genuine authority, interpretive relationships don't. Either way, the thesis succeeds in showing these are fundamentally different phenomena requiring different explanations.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails, and the equivocation charge stands.

The defence attempts to escape by claiming "authority" is used univocally in the thick sense, with beliefs having *no* authority at all. But this creates a fatal problem: if beliefs have no first-person authority whatsoever, then the thesis isn't *distinguishing* types of first-person authority—it's *eliminating* the phenomenon for one domain while *explaining* it for another. That's not a distinction move; it's a hybrid rejection-and-explanation move.

More critically, the defence cannot maintain that beliefs have "no authority" while simultaneously claiming the thesis addresses "both sides' shared assumption that first-person authority is a unified explanandum." If beliefs never had authority to begin with, there was no unified explanandum—just a category error where theorists mistook evidential asymmetry for authority. But then the thesis isn't dissolving a real debate; it's saying one side of the debate never existed.

The equivocation becomes clearest here: the thesis needs "first-person authority for beliefs" to be something real enough that dissolving it into "mere evidential advantage" counts as philosophical work. But if it was never real authority, there's nothing to dissolve. Conversely, if it was real enough to dissolve, then "authority" must have meant something thin enough that evidential advantage could masquerade as it—which means intentions might also have merely a different kind of thin authority, not the thick constitutive kind the thesis claims.

The defence's claim that "showing half the phenomenon doesn't exist is deflationary progress" actually concedes the equivocation: it admits the thesis treats "authority" as real enough to deflate for beliefs but thick enough to require constitutive explanation for intentions. This is precisely the slide between readings the objection identified.

The equivocation is structural and fatal: the thesis cannot simultaneously claim that (1) beliefs have no authority worth explaining, and (2) the thesis distinguishes between two types of first-person authority. The defence's attempt to run pure eliminativism for beliefs while maintaining this is a "distinction" move exposes rather than resolves the equivocation.

**KILLER (UNFALSIFIABLE)** — ATTEMPT.

The thesis is structured to be immune from counterexamples through systematic redefinition of what counts as "authority" based on the outcome desired. 

Consider potential falsifying cases:

**Case 1**: Someone sincerely avows "I believe the economy will collapse" and resists all correction, treating their self-ascription as authoritative. The thesis response: This merely shows they have more interpretive data, not genuine authority. But what observable difference distinguishes "genuine authority" from "really strong evidential advantage that the person treats as authoritative"? None is specified—the thesis simply stipulates that belief-cases never count as real authority.

**Case 2**: Someone is genuinely uncertain what they intend, discovers their intention through self-interpretation (therapy, reflection), and only then avows it—suggesting interpretive rather than constitutive relationship. The thesis response: That wasn't a real intention yet, or it was a belief-like state, not a genuine intention-avowal. The case gets reclassified to preserve the distinction.

**Case 3**: Hybrid states like "I want to vote for Smith"—desires with both phenomenal presence (suggesting introspective access) and practical commitment (suggesting constitutive elements). The thesis response: Different aspects get sorted into different categories, preventing the case from challenging the clean division.

The weak point is the lack of independent criteria for what counts as "constitutive" versus "interpretive" authority apart from the theoretical need to maintain the distinction. The thesis can protect itself from any apparent counterexample by stipulating which category it belongs to, making the distinction unfalsifiable by design. Any mental state that seems to have interpretive authority gets defined as "not real authority," while any that seems constitutive gets defined as "real authority"—the labels follow the theory rather than constraining it.

STRENGTH: 4/5

This is a serious problem. The thesis provides no independent test for when authority is "genuine" versus "apparent" beyond whether it fits the constitutive/interpretive distinction the thesis wants to draw.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection demands "independent criteria" for distinguishing constitutive from interpretive authority, but this demand misunderstands how philosophical distinctions work. The criteria ARE independent—they're grounded in the metaphysics of speech acts and mental states, not in preserving the thesis.

**The constitutive test is straightforward and falsifiable**: Does sincere avowal of the state, in normal circumstances, *settle* what state you're in, such that you cannot coherently be mistaken in the way you can be mistaken about external facts? For intentions, yes: if I sincerely say "I intend to vote for Smith" (absent coercion, confusion about what intending means, etc.), I thereby intend to vote for Smith. There's no gap between sincere avowal and state-possession that would make "You're wrong about what you intend" normally appropriate. For beliefs, no: sincere avowal of "I believe the economy will collapse" doesn't settle whether I believe it—I might be self-deceived, confabulating, misinterpreting my anxiety as belief, etc.

**This generates clear predictions that could falsify the thesis**:

- If we found mental states where sincere avowal was systematically corrigible by others (suggesting interpretive) BUT where the person's say-so nevertheless settled what state they were in (suggesting constitutive), that would break the thesis. The thesis predicts this won't happen because constitutive relationships precisely explain incorrigibility.

- If we found that intention-avowals were regularly corrected by others with better evidence ("No, you don't really intend that, you're just saying it") and these corrections stuck, that would falsify the constitutive claim about intentions.

**Responding to the specific cases**:

Case 1 (resistant belief-avowal): The thesis has a clear answer that isn't ad hoc: treating your self-ascription as authoritative doesn't make it authoritative. The test is whether *others* are making a conceptual error when they correct you, not whether *you* resist correction. For beliefs, others can coherently say "I know you better than you know yourself"—this is conceptually legitimate even if sometimes wrong. For intentions, this is conceptually confused—you can't know my intentions better than I do because my sincere avowal partly constitutes them.

Case 2 (discovered intentions): This isn't a counterexample; it's a different phenomenon the thesis can accommodate. Before the avowal, there was no determinate intention—just inchoate desires, conflicting impulses, etc. The interpretive process didn't *discover* a pre-existing intention; it *formed* one that the avowal then constituted. The thesis predicts exactly this: intentions don't exist as determinate states independent of self-ascription in the way beliefs do.

Case 3 (hybrid states): The thesis isn't threatened by complexity—it predicts that desires have BOTH features. The phenomenal aspect (what it feels like to want) is interpretively known; the commitment aspect (what I'm treating as a reason for action) is constitutively settled by avowal. Different aspects, different relationships—this isn't ad hoc reclassification; it's the thesis's prediction that mental state terms often bundle together features with different first-person relationships.

**The independent criterion the objection demands exists**: Can the person be making a sincere, conceptually competent mistake about whether they're in the state? If yes (beliefs), interpretive. If no (intentions), constitutive. This isn't defined to preserve the thesis—it's grounded in the metaphysics of mental states and generates testable predictions about when correction is conceptually appropriate.
**VERDICT**
The defence succeeds, but only partially, and reveals a deeper problem.

The defence successfully provides independent criteria: the constitutive/interpretive distinction tracks whether sincere avowal can be coherently mistaken, which is indeed testable and not merely stipulative. The responses to Cases 1-3 are principled rather than ad hoc—they apply the same criterion consistently.

However, the defence exposes a vulnerability the objection didn't quite reach: **the thesis is potentially unfalsifiable not because it lacks independent criteria, but because those criteria are themselves contested in the very debate the thesis claims to dissolve.**

The key criterion—"can you be sincerely mistaken about your own intentions?"—is precisely what's at issue between traditional privileged-access theorists and their critics. Schwitzgebel argues you CAN be mistaken about your intentions through confabulation and self-deception. The thesis assumes the Moran-style view that sincere intention-avowals are constitutive, but this isn't an independent test; it's a substantive claim that begs the question against interpretivist theories of intention.

The defence claims: "For intentions, others can't say 'I know your intentions better than you do' because your sincere avowal partly constitutes them." But this is exactly what the interpretivist about intentions DENIES. They say: "Your intention is what best explains your dispositions to act, and I might interpret those better than you do, so yes, I can know your intentions better."

The real problem isn't that the thesis can evade counterexamples through redefinition (the defence shows it can't), but that it **assumes as a criterion the very thing it should be arguing for**: that intentions are constitutively self-ascribed rather than interpretively discovered. The thesis treats this as an independent test when it's actually the conclusion of a substantive argument the thesis hasn't provided.

The unfalsifiability charge fails in its original form (the thesis does have independent criteria), but succeeds in a deeper form: the criteria themselves presuppose what the thesis needs to establish, making the distinction question-begging rather than diagnostic.

**KILLER (ALREADY TAKEN)** — **ATTEMPT**
The thesis claims to make an original distinction between interpretive authority for beliefs and constitutive authority for intentions. However, this position is already well-established in the literature and closely resembles Richard Moran's account in *Authority and Estrangement* (2001). Moran explicitly distinguishes between "theoretical" self-knowledge (observational/interpretive, applying to beliefs about one's mental states) and "practical" self-knowledge (constitutive, applying to intentions and commitments). He argues that intention-avowals have authority not because of privileged epistemic access but because they express practical commitments that partly constitute the intentions themselves—precisely the thesis's core claim.

Moreover, the Schwitzgebel-style interpretivism about beliefs combined with constitutivism about intentions is exactly the position Moran defends against pure interpretivist accounts. The thesis's claim that "announcing an intention typically makes it the case that I have it" is Moran's central insight about the performative dimension of self-ascriptions of intention. Even the specific contrast—beliefs known interpretively without special authority versus intentions authoritatively self-ascribed through constitutive speech acts—mirrors Moran's theoretical/practical distinction.

The thesis presents this as dissolving a false assumption of unity, but Moran already made this move two decades ago, arguing that the philosophical tradition mistakenly sought a unified account of self-knowledge when different mental states require different explanations based on their different relations to agency and commitment.

**STRENGTH: 4/5**

This is a serious problem. The thesis's core distinction and its specific application to beliefs versus intentions closely tracks Moran's already-influential position. While the thesis might add nuances or frame the issue differently, the fundamental move—distinguishing interpretive from constitutive self-knowledge and mapping this onto belief/intention differences—is already taken.

**DEFENDER** — **BEST DEFENCE**
The objection overstates both what Moran established and how closely the thesis tracks his position. While Moran indeed distinguishes theoretical from practical self-knowledge, his account differs from the thesis in three critical ways that make the thesis's move genuinely novel.

**First, Moran retains an epistemic framework the thesis rejects.** Moran's "practical self-knowledge" is still *knowledge*—a distinctive kind grounded in practical deliberation rather than observation, but knowledge nonetheless. He argues that when I answer "What do I believe about P?" by answering "What is true about P?", I gain self-knowledge through a special transparent route. The thesis makes a stronger claim: for intentions, there's often no knowledge at all because there's no pre-existing fact to know. The avowal doesn't provide access to an intention (even practical access); it *creates* the intention where none determinately existed. This is metaphysical constitutivism, not Moran's epistemic constitutivism.

**Second, Moran doesn't embrace Schwitzgebel-style eliminativism about first-person authority for beliefs.** Moran distinguishes theoretical from practical self-knowledge, but he still treats theoretical self-knowledge as *knowledge* with genuine first-person authority, just grounded differently (in transparency and reasons-responsiveness rather than introspection). The thesis goes further: it denies that beliefs have first-person authority at all—the apparent authority is just evidential asymmetry. Moran would reject this; he thinks "I believe P" answered via "Is P true?" yields genuine self-knowledge, not mere interpretation with more data.

**Third, the thesis's diagnostic claim is stronger than Moran's.** Moran argues that different mental states require different epistemological accounts—theoretical for beliefs, practical for intentions. But he doesn't claim that the *phenomenon* of first-person authority itself dissolves into separate phenomena. He thinks there's still a unified explanandum (authoritative self-knowledge) with different explanatory bases. The thesis claims something more radical: that "first-person authority" doesn't name a real unified phenomenon at all. What looks like a single puzzle requiring a single solution is actually two completely different situations that happen to get mislabeled with the same term. For beliefs, there's no authority to explain; for intentions, there's non-epistemic authority. This isn't Moran's "different routes to self-knowledge"—it's "one domain has no knowledge-authority, the other has non-knowledge-authority."

**The thesis's move is genuinely distinct**: it combines Schwitzgebel's eliminativism about belief-authority (which Moran rejects) with a stronger-than-Moran constitutivism about intentions (metaphysical rather than epistemic) to argue that the debate's explanandum is fundamentally confused. Moran reforms the epistemology of self-knowledge; the thesis dissolves the phenomenon itself as a unified category.

The objection is right that Moran made *a* theoretical/practical distinction, but wrong that this distinction does the same work as the thesis's interpretive/constitutive divide. Moran's practical self-knowledge is still knowledge; the thesis's constitutive authority isn't. That's not a mere framing difference—it's a different metaphysical picture.
**VERDICT**
The defence fails. The thesis is not already taken by Moran, but the defence reveals that it's **incoherent** rather than original.

The defence successfully shows the thesis differs from Moran in important ways: it combines Schwitzgebel-style eliminativism about belief-authority with stronger metaphysical constitutivism about intentions. However, this combination creates an unstable position that cannot sustain itself.

**The fatal problem**: The defence claims that for intentions, "there's often no knowledge at all because there's no pre-existing fact to know." But this makes "first-person authority" for intentions into something that isn't epistemic authority at all—it's just *power* or *constitutive control*. You don't have *authority* about your intentions in any sense analogous to having authority about anything else; you just have the power to make them true by saying them.

But if that's right, then the thesis isn't distinguishing two types of "first-person authority"—it's saying:
- Beliefs: no epistemic authority (just more evidence)
- Intentions: no epistemic authority (just constitutive power)

There's no "authority" in the traditional sense anywhere in the picture. The thesis has dissolved the phenomenon entirely, not distinguished types of it.

The defence's attempt to differentiate from Moran actually exposes this: by insisting the thesis offers "metaphysical constitutivism, not epistemic constitutivism," it admits that "authority" for intentions isn't really *authority* in any epistemic sense. And since beliefs don't have authority either (just evidential advantage), the thesis eliminates first-person authority altogether rather than distinguishing types.

This makes the thesis self-undermining. It's framed as a "distinction" move that dissolves a false unity, but the defence reveals it's actually a complete *elimination* of the explanandum disguised as a distinction. The thesis can't coherently maintain that it's distinguishing types of first-person authority when its own account denies that either type involves what we normally mean by "authority."

The "already taken" charge fails because the thesis differs from Moran, but this difference makes the thesis collapse rather than succeed. The defence's clarifications expose that the thesis is trying to have it both ways: presenting itself as distinguishing types of a phenomenon while actually denying the phenomenon exists in either domain.

</details>

### U1. Moran's agentialism and interpretivism both fail because they accept that self-knowledge is a form of knowledge; what appears as first-person authority is actually immunity to a certain kind of error that occurs because avowals are performances, not representations that could misrepresent.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Both Moran's and Carruthers' shared commitment to treating self-knowledge as propositional knowledge
- **Sketch:** Both Moran and Carruthers treat self-ascriptions as knowledge claims, differing only on mechanism. But this commits them to the claim that 'I believe p' could be false while sincerely uttered (just as 'I know p' can be false while sincerely claimed). Yet as Wittgenstein and Anscombe suggested, sincere avowals in standard cases seem immune to error in a way that knowledge-claims aren't. The solution: avowals aren't in the business of representing an independent state of affairs; they're expressive performances, like promises or apologies. 'I intend to leave' doesn't report a pre-existing intention—it forms or crystallizes one. This explains both why they don't rest on evidence (Moran's insight) and why interpretation is involved (Carruthers' insight): I must interpret my situation to determine what to avow, but the avowal doesn't then represent the interpretation's conclusion. The key premise: performances can be appropriate or inappropriate but not true or false.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Performance/expression view is established; the immunity-to-error angle adds modest refinement without transforming discussion.

<details><summary>4 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) First-person avowals have authority not because they're reliable reports but because they function as commissive speech acts that create the very attitudes they appear to describe, making the question of interpretive accuracy a category mistake.
- (3/5) First-person authority is not an epistemic asymmetry at all but a practical one: the very act of sincere self-ascription changes what's true of me in a way other-ascription cannot, and this performance-based authority is all that needs explaining.
- (3/5) First-person authority comes apart from first-person accuracy: we have Moorean authority to make our mental states what we say they are (in some cases), not epistemic authority about pre-existing states.
- (3/5) The avowals that ground first-person authority cannot be evidence-based (as interpretivism requires) because treating them as evidence-based destroys the very phenomenon they're meant to explain—they collapse into hypotheses, eliminating the avowal/ascription distinction.

</details>

### U2. The interpretive position collapses into the access position because interpretation requires inputs, and the interpretive theorist must either grant privileged first-person access to those inputs or face regress.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Strong interpretivists like Carruthers and Gopnik who reject any form of privileged first-person access
- **Sketch:** Interpretivists like Carruthers claim we interpret ourselves from behavioral and contextual evidence. But what counts as evidence for me must itself be identified: I must notice that I'm trembling, recall that I just saw a snake, register my racing thoughts. This identification is either (a) itself interpretive, triggering regress, or (b) delivered by non-interpretive access. If (b), the interpretivist has covertly reintroduced privileged access at the level of evidence-awareness—I have authoritative first-person access to the inputs to interpretation, even if not to the outputs. If (a), we need a story about what grounds the base-level interpretations, and the only non-circular options reintroduce direct access. The moderate interpretivist position that seemed stable—interpretation atop direct access to some mental states—is actually forced on all interpretivists on pain of regress.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Regress argument about evidence-identification is solid but interpretivists have responses about holistic interpretation.

<details><summary>10 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (2/5) The interpretive position is unstable: if I know my mental states through interpretation as Carruthers claims, then the confabulation literature shows I typically don't know them at all, since interpretation from limited behavioral evidence reliably yields falsehood.
- (3/5) Carruthers's interpretivist account is unstable because if we really used the same evidence and methods for self and other, we'd attribute to ourselves the same systematic biases and confabulations we attribute to others, but we don't — which shows we must be using some distinctive first-person method after all.
- (3/5) Interpretive self-knowledge models are unstable because interpretation requires a stable vantage point, but if all self-access is interpretive including access to one's interpretive standards and practices, we face a regress that collapses into either skepticism or covert reinstatement of non-interpretive self-knowledge.
- (2/5) Interpretivism about self-knowledge is self-undermining: if I must interpret my own behavior to know my beliefs, then I must already believe I'm interpreting my behavior, but knowing this belief requires interpreting whether I'm interpreting my behavior, generating a regress.
- (2/5) Moran's agentialism and Bar-On's neo-expressivism are unstable positions that collapse into the observational model: any mechanism that grounds first-person authority by connecting self-ascriptions to mental states thereby becomes a form of privileged access.
- (2/5) Agentialist and expressivist accounts are unstable because they must either grant that self-interpretation is sometimes alienated/third-personal (collapsing into the interpretive view) or deny that interpretation plays any role (collapsing into discredited Cartesianism).
- (3/5) The agentialist and expressivist accounts collapse into the interpretive view once we recognize that agency and expressivism themselves require an interpretive layer—there is no pre-interpretive deliberative stance or expressive act from which authority flows.
- (3/5) The interpretive model commits its proponents to eliminativism about occurrent thought, since interpretation only ever yields standing states, but occurrent thinking is required to explain the very interpretive capacity the model relies on.
- (2/5) Both introspectionist and interpretivist models collapse into a single unstable position once we recognize that interpretation-from-evidence and perception-of-states lie on a continuum rather than marking a categorical divide, undermining the terms of the entire debate.
- (2/5) The interpretation-only model is unstable because interpretation requires a non-interpreted substrate of phenomenal feels that serves as the evidence base, and these feels constitute a residual domain of non-interpretive self-knowledge.

### U3. The asymmetry between self- and other-ascription persists on the interpretive model because only self-interpretation has the practical necessity of yielding a unified rational agent, whereas other-interpretation can remain indeterminate or fragmented without practical cost.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Carruthers and Gopnik's symmetry thesis, which claims self-interpretation is just other-interpretation turned inward
- **Sketch:** Both self and other interpretation involve inference from behaviour and context, but they differ in their stopping conditions. When interpreting others, I can leave questions open ('she both believes and doesn't believe it'), entertain multiple models, or shrug at inconsistencies. When interpreting myself, I face a practical requirement that others don't: I must act, and action requires settling on determinate contents that can serve in practical reasoning. This forces self-interpretation to resolve ambiguities that other-interpretation can tolerate. First-person authority is really decision-authority: not better epistemic access but the need to adopt a determinate self-interpretation for purposes of agency. The interpretive turn is correct that both processes are inferential, but wrong to think they're subject to the same rational constraints.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Practical necessity forcing determinacy in self-interpretation is interesting but the asymmetry's significance is unclear.

- **Sketch:** The literature treats confabulation as getting things wrong—a failure of self-knowledge. But confabulation is systematic, not random: people consistently self-ascribe mental states that make sense of their behaviour even when those aren't the states that actually caused it. This reveals that self-ascription aims at dispositional rationalization rather than causal-historical accuracy. When I confabulate reasons for my choice, I'm not failing to know my actual reasons; I'm authoritatively specifying what my choice means going forward—what pattern it fits into, what commitments it creates. The asymmetry with other-ascription is that only the agent can bind their future dispositions through such retrospective rationalization. First-person authority survives as normative authority to determine the rational significance of one's acts, not epistemic authority about mental history.

- **Sketch:** Moran says I know my belief by asking whether P rather than whether I believe P—shifting to a deliberative stance. Similarly, Bar-On's neo-expressivist avowals purport to be groundless expressions, but expressing requires knowing what mental state your utterance expresses—'I'm angry' expresses anger only if I've correctly interpreted my arousal as anger rather than fear or excitement. Both views try to bypass interpretation by invoking a more direct relation to mind, but that direct relation turns out to require the very interpretive capacity it was meant to replace. The views are unstable: they're covert forms of interpretationism that haven't recognized themselves as such.

- **Sketch:** The debate assumes 'first-person authority' names a unified phenomenon. But the evidence supports bifurcation: therapists, spouses, and researchers often have better predictive and explanatory access to our mental states (epistemic advantage), while we retain a special authority to determine what attitudes we'll be held to (practical/normative authority). The confabulation literature shows others' epistemic advantage; the persistence of first-person asymmetry in conversation shows our practical authority. These are different phenomena with different grounds: epistemic advantage comes from better interpretive position and evidence; practical authority comes from our role as agents who can be held responsible only for attitudes we acknowledge. Trying to explain 'first-person authority' as a unified thing generates pseudo-problems. The real question is why we retain practical authority despite lacking epistemic advantage.

### U8. The confabulation literature shows not that we lack first-person authority but that first-person authority extends only to synchronic rational relations, not to the causal-historical genealogy of our states.

- **Sketch:** Nisbett-Wilson cases involve subjects misidentifying the causes of their judgments, not misidentifying what they currently believe or value. We confabulate about the 'because' but not about the 'what' or 'that.' First-person authority was never supposed to cover psychological etiology—Freud already taught us we're opaque to our own causal history. What we have authority about is the rational space: which commitments we'll stand by, what follows from what in our view, what coheres with what. I can be authoritative that I prefer this job because of its location even when the real cause was anchoring bias, because the authority concerns my current rational endorsement, not causal history. The interpretive turn threatens first-person authority only if we conflate two different dimensions of self-knowledge. The key premise is that rational-space relations are constitutively accessible to the subject in a way causal-historical facts are not.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Rational-space versus causal-history distinction limits confabulation's threat but the move is somewhat familiar from replies to skepticism.

But then we must decide which interpretation to endorse going forward, and this endorsement is an agential act that carries first-person authority. When you ask what I believe about free will, I might recognize evidence for multiple conflicting interpretations of my utterances and behaviors; my authority consists not in detecting which interpretation is correct but in deciding which to stand behind. This isn't non-interpretive access to beliefs themselves (so it grants the interpretive turn), but it's an interpretive asymmetry that explains authority. The key premise: phenomenology of deliberation—the experienced weight of reasons—is both (a) genuinely phenomenal/experiential, not requiring interpretation, and (b) evidentially relevant to what I believe, since beliefs are constitutively connected to dispositions to treat considerations as reasons with particular weights.

### U13. First-person authority survives interpretation not because self-knowledge is privileged access but because self-constitution is privileged action: the interpretive process itself has different causal powers when directed at oneself.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both pure interpretivists like Carruthers who treat self and other-interpretation as symmetrical, and agentialists like Moran who ground authority in a non-interpretive deliberative stance
- **Sketch:** The distinction is between epistemic privilege (better access to pre-existing facts) and constitutive privilege (special power to make things true). When I interpret myself as believing P, this interpretation doesn't merely report a belief—it can consolidate, crystallize, or even bring into being a determinate belief where only proto-attitudes existed. This differs from interpreting others precisely because my self-interpretation enters the causal loop that produces my mental states. The argument turns on accepting that many mental states are indeterminate or inchoate until articulated, and that self-interpretation plays a constitutive role in this articulation. This explains why I'm authoritative about my beliefs even if I arrive at them interpretively: not because I observe them better, but because my saying-so partly makes-them-so. The asymmetry between self and other-ascription is thus grounded in differential constitutive power, not differential epistemic access.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The constitutive privilege idea overlaps significantly with thesis 0; this version adds causal-loop details but doesn't clearly advance beyond it.

<details><summary>7 near-duplicates merged into this one — check whether they really were the same thesis</summary>

- (3/5) Self-interpretation and other-interpretation are epistemically symmetric, but self-shaping is asymmetric: first-person authority comes from the fact that my interpretations of myself are causally efficacious in constituting what I will believe and intend in ways that external interpretations are not.
- (2/5) First-person authority derives not from privileged access or agency but from privileged standing to revise: what distinguishes self-ascription is that getting it wrong initiates self-correction rather than merely belief-correction.
- (2/5) Interpretive self-knowledge generates authority when we make our minds conform to our self-interpretations through active self-constitution, collapsing the gap between reading and reality.
- (3/5) The self-other asymmetry is not about access or method but about stakes: we interpret ourselves using the same evidence as others use, but our self-interpretations have immediate practical implications that make them self-fulfilling in ways other-interpretations cannot be.
- (3/5) The asymmetry between first- and third-person attribution isn't about epistemic authority at all, but about practical commitment: self-ascriptions are performative undertakings of responsibility while other-ascriptions are theoretical predictions, and the confabulation literature has been measuring the wrong thing by focusing on accuracy rather than commitment.
- (3/5) The debate falsely assumes interpretation and direct access are competing explanations, when actually confabulation cases show that first-person authority exists even when first-person accuracy fails—we have authority to be wrong about ourselves in a way we lack authority to be wrong about others.
- (3/5) The interpretive turn actually reveals that first-person authority was never primarily epistemic but alethic: self-ascriptions have authority not because they're better known but because they tend to make themselves true through a mechanism of self-shaping that other-ascriptions cannot trigger.

</details>

### U14. Accepting interpretivism commits you to rejecting the synchronic unity of mind: if propositional attitudes are interpretive constructions rather than detected facts, then there is no fact about whether beliefs attributed at different times or by different interpretive schemes belong to a single mind.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Carruthers and Gopnik-style interpretivists who help themselves to ordinary assumptions about personal identity while treating mental states as interpretive constructions
- **Sketch:** Interpretivists like Carruthers treat propositional attitudes as the outputs of interpretation—useful posits that organize behavior. But interpretation is always relative to an interpretive scheme and purpose. Different schemes (folk psychology, various subpersonal theories, psychoanalytic interpretation) carve behavior into different belief-sets. The key premise is that without observer-independent mental facts, there's no principled basis for saying which interpretive scheme tracks the 'real' unity of a mind over time or at a time. We lose the unity that makes 'my mind' a coherent subject of self-knowledge. Interpretivists who want to preserve talk of 'what I really believe' must covertly reintroduce the observer-independent facts they officially reject. They cannot consistently be both interpretivists and realists about personal identity or mental unity.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The unity-of-mind challenge to interpretivism is philosophically serious but may be too broad a consequence to establish convincingly.

### U15. Holding both that self-knowledge is interpretive and that we have first-person authority commits you to accepting that some forms of authority rest on epistemic features of the interpreter rather than epistemic access to the interpreted—which undermines the standard evidentialist assumptions about authority that both sides rely on.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Neo-expressivists and agentialists who want to preserve first-person authority while accepting interpretive constraints on self-knowledge
- **Sketch:** If interpretation grounds self-knowledge but we retain first-person authority, then authority cannot reduce to having better evidence (since ex hypothesi self- and other-interpretation can rely on the same evidence base). The authority must instead arise from features like: practised skill, holistic constraints, or stakes in getting it right. But this conception of authority—authority as interpretive expertise rather than observational privilege—is one that defenders of first-person authority typically reject because it makes the authority seem merely pragmatic or conventional. The argument shows that neo-expressivists like Bar-On and agentialists like Moran cannot both embrace the interpretive turn and maintain that first-person authority is a distinctive epistemic kind. The key premise is that if two interpreters have access to the same evidence, any remaining asymmetry must be non-evidential.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Identifying that interpretive authority undermines evidentialist assumptions is valuable but the argument needs tightening to show both sides actually rely on evidentialism.

### U16. The authority asymmetry between self and other survives the interpretive turn not despite but because of motivated reasoning: I have authority precisely about which motivated interpretive biases I'm deploying, making my self-interpretations authoritative about my interpretive stance even when wrong about underlying states.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that motivated reasoning and interpretive bias undermine rather than constitute first-person authority
- **Sketch:** The standard view treats motivated reasoning as undermining self-knowledge—if I'm biased in self-interpretation, I lack the authority I thought I had. But this gets it backwards. When you attribute a belief to me, you're uncertain both about my mental states and about which interpretive biases I'm deploying in reading myself. When I attribute a belief to myself, even if I'm deploying motivated reasoning, I'm authoritative about which motivated construal I'm committed to. The asymmetry isn't about better access to mental states but about direct access to my own interpretive stance. Your interpretation of me requires a second-order interpretation (reading my self-reading), while my self-interpretation is simply first-order commitment to an interpretation. This reframes the debate: both sides are right that self-knowledge is interpretive and that authority exists, but the authority is about interpretive commitment rather than about pre-interpretive mental states.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Interesting inversion but the payoff—authority about interpretive stance—seems too narrow to resolve the debate.

---

### U17. First-person authority for avowals is preserved under interpretation because self-ascriptions are answer-resistant: the attempt to verify 'I believe P' by gathering evidence itself alters what I believe about P.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Interpretivist accounts like Carruthers and Gopnik that assimilate first- and third-person epistemic positions
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes that if self-knowledge is interpretive, then first- and third-person methods converge, eliminating asymmetry. But this ignores a distinctive feature of self-interpretation: the measurement problem. When I try to interpret whether I believe P by examining my behavior and circumstances, this very examination constitutes a form of deliberation about P that changes my belief state. The key premise is that mental states, unlike external facts, are affected by the investigative process itself in the first-person case but not the third-person case. When you interpret whether I believe P, your interpretation doesn't alter my belief; when I interpret whether I believe P, the process of marshaling evidence about P necessarily engages my belief-forming mechanisms about P. This is why simply asking myself 'Do I believe P?' leads naturally into asking 'Is P true?'—they're not alternative methods but inseparable for the self-interpreter. This answer-resistance grounds an ineliminable asymmetry even if both involve interpretation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Measurement problem for self-interpretation is interesting but the practical significance is limited.

### U18. First-person authority and confabulation are both explained by a metacognitive prediction mechanism: we have privileged access to our own predictive models of our behaviour, not to the behaviour itself.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that self-knowledge must be either observational-but-fallible or interpretive-but-symmetric
- **Sketch:** Both observationalists and interpretivists assume self-knowledge is retrospective—we look at what we did/thought and report it. But what if self-ascription draws on the forward models we use to predict and control our own behaviour? On this view, when you report your intention, you're reporting your system's prediction about what it will do, which is typically accurate (hence authority) but can dissociate from actual processes (hence confabulation). This isn't interpretation (it's not evidence-based inference from behaviour) but isn't observation either (you're accessing the model, not the modeled). It explains the phenomenology of immediacy while accommodating systematic error. The asymmetry arises because you have access to your own predictive machinery but not others'. The key premise is that introspection taps into motor control and action planning systems rather than belief-monitoring systems.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Predictive processing angle is timely and explains some phenomena, but similar proposals exist and the forward-model story needs more work to distinguish from interpretation.

### U19. The debate falsely assumes interpretation and direct access are competing explanations, when actually confabulation cases show that first-person authority exists even when first-person accuracy fails—we have authority to be wrong about ourselves in a way we lack authority to be wrong about others.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** counterexample — **Target:** The shared assumption that accuracy and authority go together, held by both observationalists (who ground authority in accurate access) and interpretivists (who deny authority because access is inaccurate)
- **Sketch:** In Nisbett and Wilson's studies, subjects confabulate explanations for their choices, but their confabulations are treated as authoritative—psychologists don't correct them ('No, you actually chose that because of position effects'). When you sincerely self-ascribe a belief wrongly, we accommodate this by saying 'You're confused about what you believe' rather than simply 'You're wrong and here's what you really believe.' This authority-without-accuracy is the real first-person asymmetry. It cannot be explained by either direct access (which would ensure accuracy) or by pure interpretivism (which would make sincere self-ascriptions just another fallible interpretation to be weighed against behavioral evidence). The key premise is that first-person authority is normative authority over what counts as the explanandum—your sincere self-ascription determines what needs explaining—not epistemic authority about the explanation. Confabulation doesn't undermine this authority; it exemplifies it.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Authority-without-accuracy is interesting but the normative vs epistemic split is familiar.

### U20. The interpretive and observational models both fail because they treat introspection as discovery of standing states, but most psychological self-knowledge is knowledge of dispositions, and knowing your own dispositions is predictive rather than observational—you have authority because you can make yourself predictable, not because you observe yourself better.

- **Promise 3/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption that beliefs are categorical states and that self-knowledge requires detecting which state you're in, held by classical perceptualists and interpretivists alike
- **Sketch:** Both the perceptual model (I observe that I believe P) and the interpretive model (I infer from my behavior that I believe P) assume that to know I believe P is to detect a categorical state I'm currently in. But beliefs are better understood as dispositions to judge, assert, and act in certain ways. The key premise: first-person knowledge of dispositions works differently than third-person knowledge—not because you observe the disposition's basis better, but because you can normally make yourself manifest the disposition on demand (you can bring the belief to consciousness, rehearse it, commit to acting on it). This self-predictability gives you a kind of authority: you can guarantee certain predictions about what you'll judge or say in a way others can't, not by observation but by agential control over the disposition's manifestation. This explains authority without requiring transparent access, and accommodates interpretation (you might need behavioral evidence for some dispositions) while preserving asymmetry (you can control manifestation in ways others can't).
- **Appraiser (blind):** Self-predictability through agential control is interesting but the dispositional account needs development.

### U21. First-person authority exists only for those mental states we are currently forming through deliberation, not for standing attitudes or emotional states we discover.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** distinction — **Target:** Both expressivist accounts (Bar-On) and interpretive accounts (Carruthers) that assume first-person authority must have a unified source
- **Sketch:** The debate treats 'first-person authority' as a unified phenomenon requiring a unified explanation. But Moran's agentialist account actually only explains authority over attitudes formed through rational deliberation (what to believe, intend, or value), while confabulation cases concern pre-existing emotions, implicit biases, and motivations we discover rather than form. When I ask 'Do I believe P?' in deliberative mode, I'm not discovering but deciding—the question is transparent to 'Is P true?' This explains why authority exists for some self-ascriptions but not others without requiring either pure observation or pure interpretation. The key premise is that deliberative and archaeological self-knowledge are fundamentally different epistemic projects with different accuracy conditions.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Restricting authority to deliberatively-formed attitudes is already Moran's position, not an advance on it.

### U22. Interpretive theories are committed to treating confabulation as systematically false belief, but confabulators actually express genuine attitudes they have—toward justification and self-presentation—just not the attitudes they think they're expressing.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Carruthers and other interpretivists who use confabulation as evidence against first-person authority
- **Sketch:** When Nisbett and Wilson's subjects confabulate reasons for choosing stockings, interpretivists treat this as false belief about mental causation. But the confabulation expresses something true: the subject's commitment to certain kinds of justification, their standards for what counts as a good reason, their self-concept. The subject isn't mistaken about having a mental state—they're correctly expressing their evaluative stance while being wrong about which mental state prompted the original action. This means interpretation gets self-knowledge right more often than confabulation literature suggests, but it succeeds by expressing different (higher-order, normative) attitudes than subjects intend. The key premise is that expressivism is right that first-person avowal expresses rather than reports, but what gets expressed includes normative and justificatory attitudes the subject actually has.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Confabulation expressing different (normative) attitudes is clever but limited payoff.

### U23. First-person authority survives interpretation because self-interpretation operates under constitutive rational constraints that other-interpretation doesn't face: gross self-misinterpretation makes the interpreter irrational in a way that misinterpreting others doesn't.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The shared assumption between Carruthers and Gopnik that interpretive symmetry entails epistemic symmetry
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes that if self-knowledge is interpretive, it must be epistemically symmetric with other-knowledge. But self-interpretation is subject to rational norms that constrain interpretation from within. If I conclude through interpretation that I don't care about my child's welfare, while continuing to act solicitously, I face rational pressure to revise either the interpretation or my behavior in ways that someone interpreting me from outside doesn't face. This isn't epistemic privilege but constitutive pressure: my self-interpretation partially constitutes what attitudes I rationally can be said to have. The asymmetry isn't about access but about the rational standing of the interpreter. The key premise is that rationality requires a kind of self-knowledge that is presupposed by, not derived from, third-personal interpretation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Rational constraints on self-interpretation creating asymmetry is well-trodden ground.

### U24. The agentialist position collapses into interpretivism once we recognize that deliberative transparency itself requires interpretation of what counts as a reason.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Moran's agentialist account claiming to avoid the interpretive turn
- **Sketch:** Moran claims that answering 'Do I believe P?' through deliberating about whether P is true avoids interpretation. But deliberation requires determining what counts as evidence for P, which requires understanding one's epistemic standards, background beliefs, and inferential dispositions—all of which require interpretation. When I reason from 'P' to 'I believe P,' the transparency move obscures that I must interpret my own epistemic framework to determine whether my consideration of P's truth constitutes believing it or merely entertaining it. Even the agentialist must interpret their own rational-agent-role-occupancy. The key premise is that there is no uninterpreted access to one's own rational processes, because recognizing something as a reason requires situating it within an interpreted framework of one's commitments.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The claim that deliberation requires interpretation has been made before; this restates a familiar criticism of Moran without sufficient new leverage.

### U25. The confabulation literature actually supports rather than undermines first-person authority, because confabulation itself is a distinctively first-personal capacity unavailable in third-person attribution.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** The standard interpretation of confabulation studies (Schwitzgebel, Carruthers) as straightforwardly undermining first-person authority.
- **Sketch:** Nisbett and Wilson are typically read as showing we lack introspective access. But confabulation requires more than mere misattribution—it requires generating rationalizations that feel like reasons to the subject, that integrate into a first-personal narrative in a way that third-person hypotheses don't. When I confabulate about why I chose these stockings, I'm not merely guessing; I'm producing a candidate reason that has a particular phenomenological and normative grip on me. This capacity to generate reasons that feel like my reasons (even when they aren't) is itself a form of first-person authority—authority over the space of reasons I can occupy, if not over the causes of my behavior. The asymmetry persists: you can hypothesize reasons for my action, but you cannot confabulate for me.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Clever inversion but the payoff is limited—showing confabulation itself requires first-person capacities doesn't rescue first-person authority in the contested domain.

### U26. First-person authority consists not in privileged access to mental states but in constitutive control over which interpretations of oneself become true.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both sides of the debate—those defending epistemic first-person authority (Moran) and those reducing self-knowledge to third-personal interpretation (Carruthers, Gopnik)—share the assumption that authority must be epistemic.
- **Sketch:** The debate assumes first-person authority requires epistemic privilege—better evidence, more direct access. But authority can be practical rather than epistemic. When I sincerely avow a belief or intention, I'm not reporting a pre-existing fact I'm best positioned to observe; I'm making it the case that this interpretation is correct by committing to it. The key premise: self-interpretation has world-to-mind direction of fit for rational agents. My sincere avowals don't describe my mental states, they constitute their rational identity by creating commitments I'm then accountable to. This explains the asymmetry: others can predict my behaviour, but only I can bind myself to an interpretation and thereby make it authoritative. The interpretive turn is right that we construct self-knowledge, wrong only in assuming construction must be epistemic rather than practical.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Practical authority through commitment is already a recognized position in the literature; this restates it without sufficient novelty.

### U27. First-person authority requires interpretive dependency: only by being hostage to others' reactions can my self-ascriptions have the kind of authority they do.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** instability — **Target:** Moran and Bar-On's attempts to ground first-person authority in non-interpretive resources (deliberative stance, expressive vehicle) independent of third-personal assessment.
- **Sketch:** Accounts defending first-person authority (Moran, Bar-On) against interpretivism try to ground it in something internal—agential commitment, expressive directness. But this move is unstable: what makes a self-ascription authoritative rather than merely insistent is that it creates legitimate expectations in others, which requires that the practice of granting authority serves social coordination. The key premise: authority is a status others must recognize, not a metaphysical fact about access. Trying to ground first-person authority independently of third-personal interpretive practices collapses—the authority exists only within the practice of generally deferring to sincere self-ascription. This reveals that defenders of first-person authority are committed to the very interpretive social framework they oppose. The asymmetry between self and other-ascription is real but is a social achievement, not a consequence of inner access or agential magic.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Showing first-person authority depends on social recognition is not new; this makes the point clearly but without advancing beyond existing social-practice accounts.

---

### U28. The confabulation literature and first-person authority target different mental state types: confabulation occurs for sub-personal states that never had first-person authority, while we retain authority over personal-level commitments that are constituted by avowability.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both sides' assumption that confabulation about mental states and first-person authority concern the same ontological category
- **Sketch:** Nisbett & Wilson show subjects confabulate about implicit preferences and automatic processes. But these were never candidates for first-person authority in the philosophically relevant sense—they're not the attitudes that figure in rational explanation and criticism. When I sincerely avow 'I believe abortion is permissible,' I'm not reporting an inner state but taking a stand, entering a commitment into my rational economy. This act is self-verifying: my genuine avowal makes it true that this belief figures in my personal-level psychology, even if implicit attitude tests or my behavioral evidence suggest otherwise. The appearance of conflict between empirical psychology and first-person authority dissolves: they study different psychological kinds. The key premise: personal-level attitudes are those individuated by their role in reason-giving, and avowal is constitutive of, not evidence for, such attitudes.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The personal/subpersonal distinction is standard; applying it here dissolves rather than engages the debate.

### U29. First-person authority is an illusion produced by confirmation bias in the interpretation process: we accept interpretations of ourselves at lower evidential thresholds than interpretations of others, creating spurious asymmetry.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Moran and Bar-On's assumption that the authority asymmetry is a datum to be explained rather than itself an artifact of the phenomena the interpretivists identified
- **Sketch:** Both camps assume the authority asymmetry is real and needs explaining, but the confabulation literature suggests a simpler hypothesis: we're bad at interpreting ourselves and don't realize it because we apply motivated reasoning. When interpreting others, social correction forces higher evidential standards; when interpreting ourselves, confirmation bias runs unchecked. What Bar-On and Moran treat as authority is actually overconfidence plus the fact that others rarely challenge our self-reports. Schwitzgebel's cases of disagreement about one's own experiences show this: people resist revising self-interpretations not because they have authority but because they have bias. This predicts what we see: the appearance of authority is strongest precisely where checking is hardest (phenomenal states, intentions) and weakest where evidence is public (skills, personality traits). First-person authority is the self-serving interpretation of our own incompetence at self-interpretation.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The confirmation-bias debunking is psychologically plausible but doesn't engage the normative dimension of authority; similar deflationary moves have been made.

---

### U30. First-person authority is parasitic on other-person vulnerability: we have authority about our own minds only in domains where others can be systematically fooled about theirs.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** reframing — **Target:** Both access and interpretivist theorists who seek positive grounds for first-person authority
- **Sketch:** Both sides assume first-person authority is about the self-other asymmetry in getting mental states right. But the real pattern is this: first-person authority exists only for mental state types where third-person attribution is especially unreliable—precisely where behavioural evidence is defeatable and where confabulation is possible. I have authority about my occurrent thoughts because you cannot reliably infer them from my behaviour; I lack authority about my implicit attitudes because behavioral evidence constrains both of us equally. First-person authority is thus not a special epistemic position but rather the flipside of third-person epistemic limitation. The asymmetry doesn't need grounding in access or agentialism; it's grounded in the differential reliability of evidence sources across mental state types. The debate has been looking for a positive story about first-person capacities when the explanation is negative, about third-person incapacities.
- **Appraiser (blind):** Negative explanation via third-person limitations is clever but the asymmetry still requires positive account of why self-access differs.

---

### U31. Agentialist accounts like Moran's that ground first-person authority in rational self-constitution are committed to denying first-person authority to anyone who forms attitudes non-rationally, revealing the view's unacceptable elitism.

- **Promise 2/5** · residual –/5 · worst –/5 · unattacked
- **Move:** commitment — **Target:** Moran's agentialist account and similar rational-constitution views
- **Sketch:** Moran grounds first-person authority in the stance of deliberative agency: I know my belief by deciding what to believe rather than observing what I already believe. This commits him to the view that first-person authority varies with rational capacity and the exercise of deliberative agency. But this means children, the severely depressed, the akratic, and anyone forming attitudes through non-rational means lack first-person authority about those attitudes—they must adopt the third-person, observational stance Moran reserves for others. This is not merely counterintuitive but contradicts the phenomenology: even in akrasia, I seem to have first-person authority about both the rational belief I should hold and the contrary attitude I actually hold. The key premise is that if authority is grounded in a capacity, those lacking the capacity lack the authority, and Moran cannot restrict his account to 'ideally rational' agents without abandoning the explanatory project.
- **Appraiser (blind):** The elitism objection to Moran is familiar; he has responses about the scope of deliberative stance availability.
